Conventions. Schemes are noetherian where the text assumes it; the base field is not assumed algebraically closed unless an exercise says so. Results of the text are cited by their numbers in Hartshorne (“Thm. 5.2”, “Prop. 9.7”, …), and results of other chapters as “I, …” or “II, …”. The numbering and statements of the exercises follow the edition scanned in this repository (in particular Section 1 has no exercises in this edition); each exercise is restated before its solution, and starred exercises are marked “Starred in the book” at the end of the statement.

Honest notes. Several printed statements need an extra hypothesis or correction, and the solutions say what is assumed or proved instead: \(\operatorname{char}\neq2\) in Ex. 10.6 and 10.8; the printed values in Ex. 12.3 are false for the untwisted ideal sheaf; Ex. 10.7(b) omits a third case in its classification; Ex. 12.5 needs rank \(\ge2\). Ex. 7.4 depends on a choice of normalization of the trace maps (stated as an assumption), and a few solutions use standard facts beyond the text (Matsumura's local flatness criteria, comparison theorems), which are named where used.

1. Derived functors

Section 1 of Chapter III (pp. 202–206 of the book) contains no exercises: the exercises of this chapter begin at the end of Section 2 (numbered 2.1–2.7) and are solved in the next section. The results of this section that are used below are cited by their numbers: Thm. 1.1A (existence of the right derived functors \(R^iF\) of a left exact functor \(F\) on a category with enough injectives, and the long exact sequences), Prop. 1.2A (acyclic resolutions compute \(R^iF\)), Thm. 1.3A (effaceable \(\delta\)-functors are universal) and Cor. 1.4.

2. Cohomology of sheaves

Conventions. \(\mathrm{Ab}(X)\) is the category of sheaves of abelian groups on \(X\); it has enough injectives (Cor. 2.3). For an open set \(U\subseteq X\) with inclusion \(i\), and a sheaf \(\mathcal F\), \(\mathcal F_U=i_!(\mathcal F|_U)\) is the extension by zero (II, Ex. 1.19); for a closed set \(Y\) with inclusion \(j\), \(\mathcal F_Y=j_*(\mathcal F|_Y)\). For every sheaf \(\mathcal G\) one has \(\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbf Z_U,\mathcal G)=\mathcal G(U)\) (this was used in the proof of Lemma 2.4). We use freely: injective sheaves are flasque (Lemma 2.4); flasque sheaves are acyclic for \(\Gamma(X,\cdot)\) (Prop. 2.5), hence cohomology can be computed from flasque resolutions (Prop. 1.2A); if \(0\to\mathcal F'\to\mathcal F\to\mathcal F''\to0\) is exact with \(\mathcal F',\mathcal F\) flasque then so is \(\mathcal F''\), and if \(\mathcal F'\) is flasque then \(\Gamma(X,\mathcal F)\to\Gamma(X,\mathcal F'')\) is surjective (II, Ex. 1.16(b),(c)); restrictions of flasque sheaves to open subsets are flasque; a constant sheaf on an irreducible space is flasque (II, Ex. 1.16(a)).

Exercise 2.1

Problem
  1. (a)

    Let \(X=\mathbf A^1_k\) be the affine line over an infinite field \(k\). Let \(P,Q\) be distinct closed points of \(X\), and let \(U=X-\{P,Q\}\). Show that \(H^1(X,\mathbf Z_U)\neq0\).

  2. (b)

    More generally, let \(Y\subseteq X=\mathbf A^n_k\) be the union of \(n+1\) hyperplanes in suitably general position, and let \(U=X-Y\). Show that \(H^n(X,\mathbf Z_U)\neq0\). Thus the result of (2.7) is the best possible.

(Part (b) is starred in the book.)

Solution

(a) Let \(Y=\{P,Q\}\) (closed, discrete). By II, Ex. 1.19 there is an exact sequence \(0\to\mathbf Z_U\to\mathbf Z\to\mathbf Z_Y\to0\) (stalkwise: at \(x\in U\) it reads \(0\to\mathbf Z\to\mathbf Z\to0\to0\), at \(P\) it reads \(0\to0\to\mathbf Z\to\mathbf Z\to0\), similarly at \(Q\), and at other points everything is \(0\) in the last two places). Here \(\mathbf Z_Y=\mathbf Z_P\oplus\mathbf Z_Q\) is a sum of two skyscraper sheaves, so \(\Gamma(X,\mathbf Z_Y)=\mathbf Z\oplus\mathbf Z\), and \(\Gamma(X,\mathbf Z)\to\Gamma(X,\mathbf Z_Y)\) is the diagonal map \(\mathbf Z\to\mathbf Z\oplus\mathbf Z\). The space \(X\) is irreducible, so \(\mathbf Z\) is flasque and \(H^1(X,\mathbf Z)=0\) (Prop. 2.5). The long exact sequence gives

\[ \Gamma(X,\mathbf Z)\xrightarrow{\ \Delta\ }\Gamma(X,\mathbf Z_Y)\to H^1(X,\mathbf Z_U)\to H^1(X,\mathbf Z)=0, \]

so \(H^1(X,\mathbf Z_U)\cong(\mathbf Z\oplus\mathbf Z)/\Delta(\mathbf Z)\cong\mathbf Z\neq0\). (Only irreducibility of \(X\) was used; the hypothesis that \(k\) is infinite is not needed.)

(b) Reading of “suitably general position”. Let \(H_0,\dots,H_n\subseteq X=\mathbf A^n_k\) be hyperplanes such that for every \(I\subseteq\{0,\dots,n\}\) with \(1\le|I|\le n\) the intersection \(Y_I=\bigcap_{i\in I}H_i\) is nonempty (it is then an affine linear subspace, in particular irreducible), while \(\bigcap_{i=0}^nH_i=\emptyset\). Such hyperplanes exist: take \(H_i=\{x_i=0\}\) for \(1\le i\le n\) and \(H_0=\{x_1+\dots+x_n=1\}\). (Indeed for \(0\notin I\), \(Y_I\) is a coordinate subspace; for \(0\in I\), \(Y_I\) is defined by \(x_i=0\) (\(i\in I-\{0\}\)) and \(\sum_{j\notin I}x_j=1\), which is nonempty since \(n-(|I|-1)\ge1\) coordinates remain; and all \(n+1\) together would force \(0=1\).) For \(n=1\) this is the situation of (a). Put \(Y=\bigcup H_i\), \(U=X-Y\). We assume \(n\ge2\); the case \(n=1\) is (a).

For a nonempty closed subvariety \(W\) that is irreducible, the sheaf \(\mathbf Z_W\) has \(\mathbf Z_W(V)=\mathbf Z\) if \(V\cap W\neq\emptyset\) (a locally constant function on the irreducible space \(V\cap W\) is constant) and \(0\) otherwise, with restriction maps identity or zero. Hence \(\mathbf Z_W\) is flasque and \(\Gamma(X,\mathbf Z_W)=\mathbf Z\). For \(I\subseteq J\) there is the natural restriction map \(\mathbf Z_{Y_I}\to\mathbf Z_{Y_J}\) (as \(Y_J\subseteq Y_I\)); on global sections it is the identity of \(\mathbf Z\) when \(Y_J\ne\emptyset\).

Define a complex of sheaves \(C^p=\bigoplus_{|I|=p+1}\mathbf Z_{Y_I}\) for \(0\le p\le n-1\) (the term with \(|I|=n+1\) is \(\mathbf Z_\emptyset=0\)), with the usual alternating-sign (Cech-type) differential, and an augmentation \(\mathbf Z_Y\to C^0=\bigoplus_i\mathbf Z_{H_i}\) given by the restriction maps. We claim

\[ 0\to\mathbf Z_Y\to C^0\to C^1\to\dots\to C^{n-1}\to0 \]

is exact. Check on stalks at \(x\in X\). If \(x\notin Y\) all stalks are \(0\). If \(x\in Y\), let \(S=\{i:x\in H_i\}\); then \(S\neq\emptyset\) and \(|S|\le n\). The stalk of \(\mathbf Z_{Y_I}\) at \(x\) is \(\mathbf Z\) if \(I\subseteq S\) and \(0\) otherwise, so the stalk complex is \(\mathbf Z\to\bigoplus_{i\in S}\mathbf Z\to\bigoplus_{i<j\in S}\mathbf Z\to\cdots\), the augmented simplicial cochain complex of the full simplex on the vertex set \(S\neq\emptyset\) (all subsets \(I\subseteq S\) have \(|I|\le n\), so no term is lost), which is exact (it has a contracting homotopy: fix \(s\in S\), and let \(h\) delete \(s\) from a set containing \(s\), up to sign). So the sequence is exact.

All \(C^p\) are flasque, so this is a flasque resolution of \(\mathbf Z_Y\), and \(H^p(X,\mathbf Z_Y)=h^p(\Gamma(X,C^\bullet))\) (Prop. 1.2A and Prop. 2.5). Now \(\Gamma(X,C^p)=K^p:=\bigoplus_{|I|=p+1}\mathbf Z\), and the differentials are the simplicial coboundaries. Let \(K^\bullet\) be the full complex \(K^p\), \(0\le p\le n\), where \(K^n=\mathbf Z\) corresponds to \(I=\{0,\dots,n\}\), augmented by \(\mathbf Z\to K^0\); it is the augmented cochain complex of the \(n\)-simplex, hence exact. Our complex \(\Gamma(X,C^\bullet)\) is its truncation \(K^0\to\dots\to K^{n-1}\). Therefore, for \(n\ge2\): \(h^p=0\) for \(1\le p\le n-2\) by exactness of \(K^\bullet\), \(h^0=\ker(K^0\to K^1)\cong\mathbf Z\), and

\[ H^{n-1}(X,\mathbf Z_Y)=h^{n-1}=K^{n-1}/\operatorname{im}(K^{n-2}\to K^{n-1})=K^{n-1}/\ker(K^{n-1}\to K^n)\cong K^n=\mathbf Z, \]

using exactness of \(K^\bullet\) at \(K^{n-1}\) and surjectivity of \(K^{n-1}\to K^n\) (exactness at \(K^n\)).

Now use \(0\to\mathbf Z_U\to\mathbf Z\to\mathbf Z_Y\to0\). Since \(X\) is irreducible, \(H^i(X,\mathbf Z)=0\) for \(i>0\), and \(n-1\ge1\), so the long exact sequence gives

\[ 0=H^{n-1}(X,\mathbf Z)\to H^{n-1}(X,\mathbf Z_Y)\to H^n(X,\mathbf Z_U)\to H^n(X,\mathbf Z)=0, \]

hence \(H^n(X,\mathbf Z_U)\cong H^{n-1}(X,\mathbf Z_Y)\cong\mathbf Z\neq0\). Since \(\dim X=n\), Grothendieck's vanishing theorem (2.7) says \(H^i=0\) for \(i>n\) and this shows that \(n\) cannot be lowered. ∎

Exercise 2.2

Problem

Let \(X=\mathbf P^1_k\) be the projective line over an algebraically closed field \(k\). Show that the exact sequence \(0\to\mathcal O\to\mathcal K\to\mathcal K/\mathcal O\to0\) of (II, Ex. 1.21d) is a flasque resolution of \(\mathcal O\). Conclude from (II, Ex. 1.21e) that \(H^i(X,\mathcal O)=0\) for all \(i>0\).

Solution

Recall from II, Ex. 1.21(d),(e): \(\mathcal K\) is the constant sheaf associated with the function field \(K\) of \(X\), \(\mathcal O\to\mathcal K\) is injective, \(\mathcal K/\mathcal O\cong\bigoplus_{P}i_P(I_P)\) where \(P\) runs over the closed points, \(I_P=K/\mathcal O_P\) and \(i_P(I_P)\) is the skyscraper sheaf, and \(0\to\Gamma(X,\mathcal O)\to\Gamma(X,\mathcal K)\to\Gamma(X,\mathcal K/\mathcal O)\to0\) is exact.

\(\mathcal K\) is a constant sheaf on the irreducible space \(X\), hence flasque (II, Ex. 1.16(a)).

\(\mathcal K/\mathcal O\) is flasque. Let \(\mathcal S=\bigoplus_Pi_P(I_P)\) (sheaf direct sum). Consider the presheaf \(\mathcal S'(V)=\bigoplus_{P\in V}I_P\) (direct sum of abelian groups over the closed points of \(V\)). It is a sheaf: locality is clear, and if \(V=\bigcup V_\lambda\) and sections \(s_\lambda\in\mathcal S'(V_\lambda)\) agree on overlaps, they define a family \(s=(s_P)_{P\in V}\); if all \(V_\lambda\) are empty there is nothing to prove, and otherwise some \(V_{\lambda_0}\neq\emptyset\) is cofinite in \(X\) (open nonempty subsets of \(\mathbf P^1\) are complements of finite sets), so \(V-V_{\lambda_0}\) is finite, and \(s\) has finite support because \(s|_{V_{\lambda_0}}=s_{\lambda_0}\) does. Since \(\mathcal S'\) is a sheaf which is the direct sum of the sheaves \(i_P(I_P)\) (it satisfies the universal property: sheaf homomorphisms from \(\mathcal S'\) correspond to compatible families on the summands), \(\mathcal S=\mathcal S'\). Its restriction maps for \(W\subseteq V\) are the projections \(\bigoplus_{P\in V}I_P\to\bigoplus_{P\in W}I_P\), which are surjective. So \(\mathcal K/\mathcal O\cong\mathcal S\) is flasque.

Thus \(0\to\mathcal O\to\mathcal K\to\mathcal K/\mathcal O\to0\) is a flasque resolution of \(\mathcal O\) (Prop. 2.5, Rem. 2.5.1 and Prop. 1.2A), and \(H^i(X,\mathcal O)=h^i\) of the complex \(0\to\Gamma(X,\mathcal K)\to\Gamma(X,\mathcal K/\mathcal O)\to0\) placed in degrees \(0,1\). In degrees \(i\ge2\) this complex is \(0\), so \(H^i(X,\mathcal O)=0\) for \(i\ge2\). In degree \(1\), \(H^1(X,\mathcal O)=\operatorname{coker}(\Gamma(X,\mathcal K)\to\Gamma(X,\mathcal K/\mathcal O))=0\) by the surjectivity in II, Ex. 1.21(e). ∎

Exercise 2.3

Problem

Cohomology with Supports (Grothendieck [7]). Let \(X\) be a topological space, let \(Y\) be a closed subset, and let \(\mathcal F\) be a sheaf of abelian groups on \(X\). Let \(\Gamma_Y(X,\mathcal F)\) denote the group of sections of \(\mathcal F\) with support in \(Y\) (II, Ex. 1.20).

  1. (a)

    Show that \(\Gamma_Y(X,\cdot)\) is a left exact functor from \(\mathrm{Ab}(X)\) to \(\mathrm{Ab}\). We denote the right derived functors by \(H^i_Y(X,\cdot)\). They are the cohomology groups of \(X\) with supports in \(Y\), and coefficients in a given sheaf.

  2. (b)

    If \(0\to\mathcal F'\to\mathcal F\to\mathcal F''\to0\) is an exact sequence of sheaves, with \(\mathcal F'\) flasque, show that \(0\to\Gamma_Y(X,\mathcal F')\to\Gamma_Y(X,\mathcal F)\to\Gamma_Y(X,\mathcal F'')\to0\) is exact.

  3. (c)

    Show that if \(\mathcal F\) is flasque, then \(H^i_Y(X,\mathcal F)=0\) for all \(i>0\).

  4. (d)

    If \(\mathcal F\) is flasque, show that the sequence \(0\to\Gamma_Y(X,\mathcal F)\to\Gamma(X,\mathcal F)\to\Gamma(X-Y,\mathcal F)\to0\) is exact.

  5. (e)

    Let \(U=X-Y\). Show that for any \(\mathcal F\), there is a long exact sequence of cohomology groups

    \[ 0\to H^0_Y(X,\mathcal F)\to H^0(X,\mathcal F)\to H^0(U,\mathcal F|_U)\to H^1_Y(X,\mathcal F)\to H^1(X,\mathcal F)\to H^1(U,\mathcal F|_U)\to H^2_Y(X,\mathcal F)\to\cdots. \]
  6. (f)

    Excision. Let \(V\) be an open subset of \(X\) containing \(Y\). Then there are natural functorial isomorphisms, for all \(i\) and \(\mathcal F\), \(H^i_Y(X,\mathcal F)\cong H^i_Y(V,\mathcal F|_V)\).

Solution

Throughout \(U=X-Y\), and for a section \(s\in\mathcal F(W)\) its support is the closed subset of \(W\) of points where the germ is nonzero; thus \(\Gamma_Y(X,\mathcal F)=\{s\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal F):s|_U=0\}=\ker\bigl(\Gamma(X,\mathcal F)\to\Gamma(U,\mathcal F)\bigr)\) (a section vanishing on \(U\) has all germs at points of \(U\) zero, i.e. support in \(Y\), and conversely, by the sheaf axiom).

(a) \(\Gamma_Y(X,\cdot)\) is an additive functor, the kernel of the natural transformation \(\Gamma(X,\cdot)\to\Gamma(U,\cdot|_U)\). Let \(0\to\mathcal F'\xrightarrow{\alpha}\mathcal F\xrightarrow{\beta}\mathcal F''\) be exact. Since \(\Gamma(X,\alpha)\) is injective, so is \(\Gamma_Y(\alpha)\). Clearly \(\Gamma_Y(\beta)\Gamma_Y(\alpha)=0\). If \(s\in\Gamma_Y(X,\mathcal F)\) has \(\beta(s)=0\), then \(s=\alpha(t)\) for a unique \(t\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal F')\); and \(\alpha(t|_U)=s|_U=0\) gives \(t|_U=0\) by injectivity of \(\Gamma(U,\alpha)\). So \(t\in\Gamma_Y(X,\mathcal F')\). Hence \(\Gamma_Y\) is left exact, and its derived functors \(H^i_Y(X,\cdot)\) exist by Cor. 2.3 and Thm. 1.1A.

(b) Exactness on the left and in the middle is (a). Let \(s''\in\Gamma_Y(X,\mathcal F'')\). As \(\mathcal F'\) is flasque, \(\Gamma(X,\mathcal F)\to\Gamma(X,\mathcal F'')\) is surjective, so \(s''=\beta(s)\) for some \(s\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal F)\). Now \(\beta(s|_U)=s''|_U=0\), so \(s|_U=\alpha(t)\) with \(t\in\Gamma(U,\mathcal F')\). Since \(\mathcal F'\) is flasque, \(t\) extends to \(\tilde t\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal F')\). Put \(s_1=s-\alpha(\tilde t)\). Then \(\beta(s_1)=s''\) and \(s_1|_U=s|_U-\alpha(t)=0\), i.e. \(s_1\in\Gamma_Y(X,\mathcal F)\) is a preimage of \(s''\).

(c) Embed \(\mathcal F\hookrightarrow\mathcal I\) with \(\mathcal I\) injective (Cor. 2.3), and let \(\mathcal G=\mathcal I/\mathcal F\). Then \(\mathcal I\) is flasque (Lemma 2.4), hence \(\mathcal G\) is flasque (II, Ex. 1.16(c)). By (b), \(0\to\Gamma_Y(\mathcal F)\to\Gamma_Y(\mathcal I)\to\Gamma_Y(\mathcal G)\to0\) is exact. As \(H^i_Y(X,\mathcal I)=0\) for \(i>0\) (Thm. 1.1A(e)), the long exact sequence gives \(H^1_Y(X,\mathcal F)=\operatorname{coker}(\Gamma_Y(\mathcal I)\to\Gamma_Y(\mathcal G))=0\), and \(H^i_Y(X,\mathcal F)\cong H^{i-1}_Y(X,\mathcal G)\) for \(i\ge2\). Since \(\mathcal G\) is again flasque, induction on \(i\) gives \(H^i_Y(X,\mathcal F)=0\) for all \(i>0\), exactly as in Prop. 2.5.

(d) Injectivity on the left is the definition of \(\Gamma_Y\), exactness in the middle is the description \(\Gamma_Y=\ker(\Gamma(X,\cdot)\to\Gamma(U,\cdot))\), and surjectivity on the right is the flasque property of \(\mathcal F\).

(e) Let \(\mathcal F\to\mathcal I^\bullet\) be an injective resolution in \(\mathrm{Ab}(X)\). Then \(H^i_Y(X,\mathcal F)=h^i(\Gamma_Y(X,\mathcal I^\bullet))\) and \(H^i(X,\mathcal F)=h^i(\Gamma(X,\mathcal I^\bullet))\). Restriction to \(U\) is exact, so \(\mathcal F|_U\to\mathcal I^\bullet|_U\) is a resolution, by sheaves \(\mathcal I^j|_U\) which are flasque (restriction of flasque sheaves), hence acyclic for \(\Gamma(U,\cdot)\) (Prop. 2.5). By Prop. 1.2A, \(H^i(U,\mathcal F|_U)=h^i(\Gamma(U,\mathcal I^\bullet|_U))\). By (d), applied to each flasque \(\mathcal I^j\), we have a short exact sequence of complexes

\[ 0\to\Gamma_Y(X,\mathcal I^\bullet)\to\Gamma(X,\mathcal I^\bullet)\to\Gamma(U,\mathcal I^\bullet|_U)\to0, \]

whose long exact cohomology sequence is the required one. (It starts with \(0\) because \(h^0\) of the left complex is \(H^0_Y=\Gamma_Y\) and the complexes live in degrees \(\ge0\); the maps \(H^i(X,\mathcal F)\to H^i(U,\mathcal F|_U)\) are the restriction maps.)

(f) Restriction of sections gives a map \(\rho:\Gamma_Y(X,\mathcal G)\to\Gamma_Y(V,\mathcal G|_V)\), natural in \(\mathcal G\) (\(Y\) is also closed in \(V\)). It is an isomorphism for every sheaf \(\mathcal G\): if \(s\in\Gamma_Y(X,\mathcal G)\) and \(s|_V=0\), then \(s\) vanishes on \(V\) and on \(X-Y\) (by definition), and these two open sets cover \(X\) because \(Y\subseteq V\), so \(s=0\); if \(t\in\Gamma_Y(V,\mathcal G|_V)\), then \(t\) and \(0\in\Gamma(X-Y,\mathcal G)\) agree on \(V\cap(X-Y)=V-Y\), so they glue to \(s\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal G)\) with \(s|_{X-Y}=0\), i.e. \(s\in\Gamma_Y(X,\mathcal G)\), and \(\rho(s)=t\).

Now take an injective resolution \(\mathcal F\to\mathcal I^\bullet\) in \(\mathrm{Ab}(X)\). Then \(\mathcal F|_V\to\mathcal I^\bullet|_V\) is a resolution of \(\mathcal F|_V\) by flasque sheaves on \(V\), which are acyclic for \(\Gamma_Y(V,\cdot)\) by (c) (applied to \(V\) and its closed subset \(Y\)). Prop. 1.2A is stated for an arbitrary left exact functor on an abelian category with enough injectives, so it applies to \(\Gamma_Y(V,\cdot)\) on \(\mathrm{Ab}(V)\) (acyclic meaning \(H^i_Y(V,\cdot)=0\) for \(i>0\)) and gives \(H^i_Y(V,\mathcal F|_V)=h^i(\Gamma_Y(V,\mathcal I^\bullet|_V))\). By \(\rho\), the complexes \(\Gamma_Y(X,\mathcal I^\bullet)\) and \(\Gamma_Y(V,\mathcal I^\bullet|_V)\) are isomorphic, whence \(H^i_Y(X,\mathcal F)\cong H^i_Y(V,\mathcal F|_V)\). The isomorphism is induced by the restriction maps, which are natural in \(\mathcal F\) (use a morphism of injective resolutions over a given \(\mathcal F\to\mathcal F'\)), and independent of the resolution because any two resolutions are related by comparison maps. ∎

Exercise 2.4

Problem

Mayer–Vietoris Sequence. Let \(Y_1,Y_2\) be two closed subsets of \(X\). Then there is a long exact sequence of cohomology with supports

\[ \cdots\to H^i_{Y_1\cap Y_2}(X,\mathcal F)\to H^i_{Y_1}(X,\mathcal F)\oplus H^i_{Y_2}(X,\mathcal F)\to H^i_{Y_1\cup Y_2}(X,\mathcal F)\to H^{i+1}_{Y_1\cap Y_2}(X,\mathcal F)\to\cdots. \]
Solution

Let \(\mathcal F\to\mathcal I^\bullet\) be an injective resolution in \(\mathrm{Ab}(X)\); all \(\mathcal I^j\) are flasque (Lemma 2.4). For any closed \(Z\), \(H^i_Z(X,\mathcal F)=h^i(\Gamma_Z(X,\mathcal I^\bullet))\). Notice that for a section \(s\) of any sheaf, \(\operatorname{supp}s\subseteq Y_1\cap Y_2\) iff \(\operatorname{supp}s\subseteq Y_1\) and \(\subseteq Y_2\), so \(\Gamma_{Y_1\cap Y_2}=\Gamma_{Y_1}\cap\Gamma_{Y_2}\) inside \(\Gamma(X,\cdot)\); also \(\Gamma_{Y_i}\subseteq\Gamma_{Y_1\cup Y_2}\). Claim: for every flasque sheaf \(\mathcal I\),

\[ 0\to\Gamma_{Y_1\cap Y_2}(X,\mathcal I)\xrightarrow{\ a\mapsto(a,a)\ }\Gamma_{Y_1}(X,\mathcal I)\oplus\Gamma_{Y_2}(X,\mathcal I)\xrightarrow{\ (b,c)\mapsto b-c\ }\Gamma_{Y_1\cup Y_2}(X,\mathcal I)\to0 \]

is exact. The first map is injective, the composite is \(0\), and if \(b-c=0\) then \(b=c\in\Gamma_{Y_1}\cap\Gamma_{Y_2}=\Gamma_{Y_1\cap Y_2}\), proving exactness in the middle. Surjectivity: let \(s\in\Gamma_{Y_1\cup Y_2}(X,\mathcal I)\), and put \(U_i=X-Y_i\). Then \(s|_{U_1\cap U_2}=0\) since \(U_1\cap U_2=X-(Y_1\cup Y_2)\). Hence there is a section \(b'\) of \(\mathcal I\) over \(U_1\cup U_2\) with \(b'|_{U_1}=0\) and \(b'|_{U_2}=s|_{U_2}\) (they agree on \(U_1\cap U_2\)). As \(\mathcal I\) is flasque, \(b'\) extends to \(b\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal I)\). Then \(b|_{U_1}=0\), i.e. \(b\in\Gamma_{Y_1}\); and \(c:=b-s\) satisfies \(c|_{U_2}=b'|_{U_2}-s|_{U_2}=0\), i.e. \(c\in\Gamma_{Y_2}\), and \(s=b-c\).

Applying this to each \(\mathcal I^j\) gives a short exact sequence of complexes, whose long exact cohomology sequence is the required one (the maps being induced by the inclusions \(\Gamma_{Y_1\cap Y_2}\subseteq\Gamma_{Y_i}\subseteq\Gamma_{Y_1\cup Y_2}\)). ∎

Exercise 2.5

Problem

Let \(X\) be a Zariski space (II, Ex. 3.17). Let \(P\in X\) be a closed point, and let \(X_P\) be the subset of \(X\) consisting of all points \(Q\in X\) such that \(P\in\overline{\{Q\}}\). We call \(X_P\) the local space of \(X\) at \(P\), and give it the induced topology. Let \(j:X_P\to X\) be the inclusion, and for any sheaf \(\mathcal F\) on \(X\), let \(\mathcal F_P=j^{-1}\mathcal F\). Show that for all \(i\), \(\mathcal F\), we have

\[ H^i_P(X,\mathcal F)=H^i_P(X_P,\mathcal F_P). \]
Solution

Recall that a Zariski space is a noetherian topological space in which every nonempty irreducible closed subset has a unique generic point. Here \(H^i_P\) denotes cohomology with supports in the closed set \(\{P\}\) (closed in \(X\), hence in \(X_P\)). The subspace \(X_P\) is noetherian. The sheaf \(\mathcal F_P=j^{-1}\mathcal F\) is the sheaf associated to the presheaf \(W\mapsto\varinjlim_{V\supseteq W}\mathcal F(V)\) (\(W\subseteq X_P\) open, \(V\) ranging over open subsets of \(X\) containing \(W\)). Note that if \(V\subseteq X\) is open and contains a point of \(\overline{\{Q\}}\), then \(Q\in V\); hence every open neighborhood \(V\) of \(P\) contains \(X_P\), and the open sets of \(X\) containing \(X_P\) are exactly the open neighborhoods of \(P\).

Step 1: the presheaf \(G(W)=\varinjlim_{V\supseteq W}\mathcal F(V)\) is a sheaf on \(X_P\), so \(\Gamma(W,\mathcal F_P)=G(W)\) for all open \(W\subseteq X_P\). We know \(G\) is a presheaf with \(G(\emptyset)=0\). Locality: let \(W=\bigcup W_\lambda\) and \(s\in G(W)\) with \(s|_{W_\lambda}=0\) for all \(\lambda\). Represent \(s\) by \(s\in\mathcal F(V)\), \(V\supseteq W\). Then \(s|_{V_\lambda}=0\) for some open \(V_\lambda\) with \(W_\lambda\subseteq V_\lambda\subseteq V\), so \(s\) vanishes on \(\bigcup V_\lambda\supseteq W\) and the germ is \(0\). Gluing for two sets: let \(W_1,W_2\subseteq X_P\) be open and \(s_i\in\mathcal F(V_i)\) germs on \(W_i\) that agree on \(W_1\cap W_2\). Choose open \(V_i^0\subseteq X\) with \(V_i^0\cap X_P=W_i\) and replace \(V_i\) by \(V_i\cap V_i^0\); so we may assume \(V_i\cap X_P=W_i\). Put \(V_{12}=V_1\cap V_2\), so \(V_{12}\cap X_P=W_1\cap W_2\). By hypothesis there is an open \(N\subseteq V_{12}\) with \(N\supseteq W_1\cap W_2\) and \(s_1|_N=s_2|_N\). Let \(Z=V_{12}-N\), closed in \(V_{12}\); then \(Z\cap X_P=\emptyset\). Let \(\overline Z\) be the closure of \(Z\) in \(X\) and let \(Z_1,\dots,Z_m\) be its irreducible components with generic points \(\zeta_k\). Then \(\overline Z\cap V_{12}=Z\). If \(Z_k\cap V_{12}\ne\emptyset\), this is a nonempty open subset of \(Z_k\), so it contains \(\zeta_k\); thus \(\zeta_k\in Z\), so \(\zeta_k\notin X_P\), i.e. \(P\notin\overline{\{\zeta_k\}}=Z_k\). Let \(Z''\) be the union of those \(Z_k\) with \(Z_k\cap V_{12}\ne\emptyset\). Then \(Z''\) is closed, \(Z\subseteq Z''\), \(P\notin Z''\), and \(Z''\cap X_P=\emptyset\) (if \(Q\in X_P\cap Z_k\) then \(P\in\overline{\{Q\}}\subseteq Z_k\)). Put \(U_i=V_i-Z''\). Then \(U_i\supseteq V_i\cap X_P=W_i\), and \(U_1\cap U_2\subseteq V_{12}-Z\subseteq N\), so \(s_1|_{U_1}\) and \(s_2|_{U_2}\) agree on \(U_1\cap U_2\) and glue to a section over \(U_1\cup U_2\supseteq W_1\cup W_2\), whose germ restricts to \(s_1\), \(s_2\). Gluing in general: let \(W=\bigcup W_\lambda\) with germs \(t_\lambda\) agreeing on overlaps. \(W\) is quasi-compact (noetherian), so \(W=W_1\cup\dots\cup W_r\) for finitely many of the \(W_\lambda\). By induction on \(r\) using the two-set case and locality, the \(t_i\) (\(i\le r\)) glue to \(t\in G(W)\): glue \(W_1,\dots,W_{r-1}\) to \(t'\) on \(W'=\bigcup_{i<r}W_i\), then \(t'\) and \(t_r\) agree on \(W'\cap W_r=\bigcup_{i<r}(W_i\cap W_r)\) by locality, and apply the two-set case. For any \(\lambda\), \(t|_{W_\lambda}\) and \(t_\lambda\) agree on each \(W_\lambda\cap W_i\), hence on \(W_\lambda\) by locality. So \(G\) is a sheaf.

Two consequences. (i) \(\Gamma(X_P,\mathcal F_P)=\varinjlim_{V\ni P}\mathcal F(V)\) is the stalk \(\mathcal F_P\) at \(P\) (so the notation is consistent). (ii) If \(\mathcal I\) is a flasque sheaf on \(X\) then \(\mathcal I_P:=j^{-1}\mathcal I\) is flasque on \(X_P\). Indeed let \(W'\subseteq W\) be open in \(X_P\) and let \(s\in\mathcal I(V'')\) represent a germ on \(W'\) (\(V''\supseteq W'\) open in \(X\)). Choose \(V\) open in \(X\) with \(V\cap X_P=W\). Since \(\mathcal I\) is flasque, \(s\) extends to \(\hat s\in\mathcal I(V''\cup V)\), and \(V''\cup V\supseteq W'\cup W=W\); the germ of \(\hat s\) on \(W\) restricts to the germ of \(s\) on \(W'\).

Step 2: cofinality. If \(V'\subseteq X\) is open and \(V'\supseteq X_P-\{P\}\), then there is an open \(V\ni P\) with \(V-\{P\}\subseteq V'\). Proof: let \(C=X-V'\), closed, with \(C\cap X_P\subseteq\{P\}\). Let \(C_1,\dots,C_m\) be its irreducible components with generic points \(\gamma_k\). If \(P\in C_k\), then \(\gamma_k\in X_P\cap C\subseteq\{P\}\), so \(\gamma_k=P\) and \(C_k=\{P\}\). Put \(V=X-\bigcup\{C_k:P\notin C_k\}\). This is an open neighborhood of \(P\), and \(V\cap C\subseteq\{P\}\), i.e. \(V-\{P\}\subseteq V'\).

Step 3: \(\Gamma_P(X,\mathcal F)\cong\Gamma_P(X_P,\mathcal F_P)\), naturally in \(\mathcal F\). The map \(\theta\) sends \(s\in\Gamma_P(X,\mathcal F)\) to the section of \(\mathcal F_P\) over \(X_P\) given by the germ of \(s\) at \(P\); its restriction to \(X_P-\{P\}\) is the germ of \(s|_{X-P}=0\), so \(\theta(s)\in\Gamma_P(X_P,\mathcal F_P)\). Injective: if \(\theta(s)=0\) then \(s|_V=0\) for an open \(V\ni P\), and \(s|_{X-\{P\}}=0\); since \(V\cup(X-\{P\})=X\), \(s=0\). Surjective: let \(\sigma\in\Gamma_P(X_P,\mathcal F_P)\), represented by \(s\in\mathcal F(V)\) for an open neighborhood \(V\) of \(P\) (consequence (i)). Its restriction to \(X_P-\{P\}\) is zero in \(G(X_P-\{P\})=\varinjlim_{V'\supseteq X_P-P}\mathcal F(V')\), so \(s|_{V'}=0\) for some open \(V'\supseteq X_P-\{P\}\) with \(V'\subseteq V\). By Step 2 there is an open \(V_1\ni P\) with \(V_1-\{P\}\subseteq V'\); shrinking, \(V_1\subseteq V\). Then \(s|_{V_1-\{P\}}=0\). So \(s|_{V_1}\) and \(0\in\mathcal F(X-\{P\})\) agree on \(V_1\cap(X-\{P\})\) and glue to \(\tilde s\in\Gamma_P(X,\mathcal F)\) with \(\theta(\tilde s)=\sigma\).

Step 4: conclusion. Let \(\mathcal F\to\mathcal I^\bullet\) be an injective resolution in \(\mathrm{Ab}(X)\). Then \(H^i_P(X,\mathcal F)=h^i(\Gamma_P(X,\mathcal I^\bullet))\). The functor \(j^{-1}\) is exact (it does not change stalks), so \(\mathcal F_P\to\mathcal I^\bullet_P\) is a resolution on \(X_P\), by flasque sheaves by Step 1(ii). By Ex. 2.3(c) and Prop. 1.2A applied to \(\Gamma_P(X_P,\cdot)\) (as in 2.3(f)), \(H^i_P(X_P,\mathcal F_P)=h^i(\Gamma_P(X_P,\mathcal I^\bullet_P))\). The maps \(\theta\) of Step 3 are isomorphisms commuting with the differentials (naturality), so they induce an isomorphism of complexes, hence \(H^i_P(X,\mathcal F)\cong H^i_P(X_P,\mathcal F_P)\), natural in \(\mathcal F\). ∎

Exercise 2.6

Problem

Let \(X\) be a noetherian topological space, and let \(\{\mathcal I_\alpha\}_{\alpha\in A}\) be a direct system of injective sheaves of abelian groups on \(X\). Then \(\varinjlim\mathcal I_\alpha\) is also injective. [Hints: First show that a sheaf \(\mathcal I\) is injective if and only if for every open set \(U\subseteq X\), and for every subsheaf \(\mathcal R\subseteq\mathbf Z_U\), and for every map \(f:\mathcal R\to\mathcal I\), there exists an extension of \(f\) to a map \(\mathbf Z_U\to\mathcal I\). Secondly, show that any such sheaf \(\mathcal R\) is finitely generated, so any map \(\mathcal R\to\varinjlim\mathcal I_\alpha\) factors through one of the \(\mathcal I_\alpha\).]

Solution

Write \(\mathcal I=\varinjlim\mathcal I_\alpha\). We use two standard facts: \(\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbf Z_V,\mathcal G)=\mathcal G(V)\) for any open \(V\) and sheaf \(\mathcal G\) (via \(\varphi\mapsto\varphi(1)\), \(1\in\mathbf Z_V(V)\)); and, since \(X\) is noetherian, \(\mathcal I(V)=\varinjlim_\alpha\mathcal I_\alpha(V)\) for every open \(V\) (II, Ex. 1.11).

Step 1 (criterion). Let \(\mathcal I\) be a sheaf satisfying the extension property of the hint. We show it is injective, i.e. every map \(f:\mathcal A\to\mathcal I\) from a subsheaf \(\mathcal A\subseteq\mathcal B\) extends to \(\mathcal B\) (the converse direction of the equivalence is trivial, since \(\mathbf Z_U\) is a sheaf and \(\mathcal R\subseteq\mathbf Z_U\)). Consider the set of pairs \((\mathcal A',f')\) with \(\mathcal A\subseteq\mathcal A'\subseteq\mathcal B\) and \(f'|_{\mathcal A}=f\), ordered by extension. A chain has an upper bound: the union of the subsheaves (the subsheaf of \(\mathcal B\) generated by their sections, i.e. the sheaf associated to the union presheaf), with the map induced from the \(f'\) via the universal property of the associated sheaf. By Zorn's lemma there is a maximal \((\mathcal A',f')\). Suppose \(\mathcal A'\ne\mathcal B\); there are an open \(U\) and \(s\in\mathcal B(U)-\mathcal A'(U)\). Let \(\varphi:\mathbf Z_U\to\mathcal B\) be the map with \(\varphi(1)=s\), and let \(\mathcal R=\varphi^{-1}(\mathcal A')=\ker(\mathbf Z_U\to\mathcal B/\mathcal A')\subseteq\mathbf Z_U\). By hypothesis \(f'\circ\varphi|_{\mathcal R}:\mathcal R\to\mathcal I\) extends to \(g:\mathbf Z_U\to\mathcal I\). Let \(\mathcal A''=\mathcal A'+\varphi(\mathbf Z_U)\), a subsheaf of \(\mathcal B\) with \(\mathcal A''(U)\ni s\), so \(\mathcal A''\supsetneq\mathcal A'\). The map \(\psi:\mathcal A'\oplus\mathbf Z_U\to\mathcal A''\), \((a,t)\mapsto a+\varphi(t)\), is an epimorphism with kernel \(L=\{(-\varphi(t),t):t\in\mathcal R\}\), and \(\theta(a,t)=f'(a)+g(t)\) vanishes on \(L\) because \(g|_{\mathcal R}=f'\varphi|_{\mathcal R}\). So \(\theta\) factors through \(\mathcal A''\), giving an extension of \(f'\) to \(\mathcal A''\): contradicting maximality. Hence \(\mathcal A'=\mathcal B\).

Step 2 (finite generation). Say that a sheaf \(\mathcal R\) is finitely generated if there are finitely many opens \(O_1,\dots,O_N\) and sections \(\sigma_k\in\mathcal R(O_k)\) such that the induced map \(\bigoplus_k\mathbf Z_{O_k}\to\mathcal R\) is an epimorphism. Claim: every subsheaf \(\mathcal R\) of \(\mathcal P=\bigoplus_{k=1}^r\mathbf Z_{V_k}\) (\(V_k\) open) is finitely generated.

Case \(r=1\). Let \(\mathcal R\subseteq\mathbf Z_V\). For \(y\in V\) write \(\mathcal R_y=d(y)\mathbf Z\subseteq\mathbf Z\) with \(d(y)\ge0\); for \(y\notin V\), \(\mathcal R_y=0\). For an integer \(m\ge1\) let \(D_m=\{y\in V:m\in\mathcal R_y\}=\{y\in V:d(y)\mid m\}\), where \(m\) denotes the constant section of \(\mathbf Z_V\) over \(V\). \(D_m\) is open: if \(m_y\in\mathcal R_y\), there is an open \(V_0\ni y\) and \(r\in\mathcal R(V_0)\) with \(r_y=m_y\), and after shrinking \(V_0\subseteq V\), \(r=m|_{V_0}\), so \(m_z\in\mathcal R_z\) for all \(z\in V_0\). Moreover \(m|_{D_m}\in\mathcal R(D_m)\), since a section of \(\mathbf Z_V\) all of whose germs lie in \(\mathcal R\) lies in \(\mathcal R\). The open set \(V_+=\{y\in V:d(y)>0\}\) equals \(\bigcup_{m\ge1}D_m\) (as \(y\in D_{d(y)}\) if \(d(y)>0\)). Since \(X\) is noetherian, among the finite unions of the \(D_m\) there is a maximal one, and it must equal \(V_+\): \(V_+=D_{m_1}\cup\dots\cup D_{m_s}\). For \(y\in D_{m_i}\) we have \(d(y)\mid m_i\), so \(d\) takes only finitely many values \(e_1,\dots,e_t>0\) on \(V_+\). Let \(\mathcal R'\subseteq\mathcal R\) be the image of \(\bigoplus_l\mathbf Z_{D_{e_l}}\to\mathcal R\) given by the sections \(e_l|_{D_{e_l}}\in\mathcal R(D_{e_l})\). For \(y\in V_+\), \(d(y)=e_l\) for some \(l\) and \(y\in D_{e_l}\), so \(\mathcal R'_y\ni e_l\) and \(\mathcal R'_y=\mathcal R_y\); for other \(y\), \(\mathcal R_y=0\). By stalks, \(\mathcal R'=\mathcal R\).

Induction on \(r\). Let \(r\ge2\), \(\mathcal A=\mathbf Z_{V_r}\), \(\mathcal B=\bigoplus_{k<r}\mathbf Z_{V_k}\), \(\pi:\mathcal P=\mathcal A\oplus\mathcal B\to\mathcal B\), and \(\mathcal R\subseteq\mathcal P\). By the case \(r=1\), \(\mathcal R\cap\mathcal A\) is generated by finitely many sections \(a_l\in\mathcal R(O_l)\), and by the induction hypothesis the subsheaf \(\pi(\mathcal R)\subseteq\mathcal B\) is generated by finitely many \(b_j\in\pi(\mathcal R)(W_j)\). As \(\mathcal R\to\pi(\mathcal R)\) is an epimorphism, each \(W_j\) is covered by opens on which \(b_j\) lifts to \(\mathcal R\); \(W_j\) is quasi-compact, so finitely many such opens \(W_{ji}\) and lifts \(c_{ji}\in\mathcal R(W_{ji})\) suffice. Let \(\mathcal R'\) be the subsheaf generated by the \(a_l\) and the \(c_{ji}\). For \(y\in X\) and \(x\in\mathcal R_y\), \(\pi(x)=\sum n_j(b_j)_y\) with \(n_j\in\mathbf Z\) and \((b_j)_y=\pi((c_{ji})_y)\) for some \(i\) (depending on \(j\)); then \(x-\sum n_j(c_{ji})_y\in\ker\pi\cap\mathcal R_y=(\mathcal R\cap\mathcal A)_y\subseteq\mathcal R'_y\). So \(\mathcal R'=\mathcal R\), proving the claim.

Step 3 (factoring through some \(\mathcal I_\alpha\)). Let \(\mathcal R\) be a subsheaf of \(\mathbf Z_U\) and \(f:\mathcal R\to\mathcal I=\varinjlim\mathcal I_\alpha\). By Step 2 there is an epimorphism \(\varepsilon:\mathcal P=\bigoplus_{k=1}^r\mathbf Z_{V_k}\to\mathcal R\), and its kernel \(\mathcal K\subseteq\mathcal P\) is again finitely generated by Step 2: there are opens \(O_1,\dots,O_q\) and \(\kappa_j\in\mathcal K(O_j)\subseteq\mathcal P(O_j)\) with \(\bigoplus\mathbf Z_{O_j}\to\mathcal K\) onto. The map \(g=f\varepsilon:\mathcal P\to\mathcal I\) is given by sections \(g_k\in\mathcal I(V_k)=\varinjlim_\alpha\mathcal I_\alpha(V_k)\); choose \(\alpha_0\) and \(t_k\in\mathcal I_{\alpha_0}(V_k)\) mapping to \(g_k\) (take \(\alpha_0\) above the finitely many indices needed), and let \(\psi:\mathcal P\to\mathcal I_{\alpha_0}\) be the corresponding map. Then \(\lambda_{\alpha_0}\psi=g\), where \(\lambda_\alpha:\mathcal I_\alpha\to\mathcal I\) is the canonical map. Now \(\lambda_{\alpha_0}(\psi(\kappa_j))=g(\kappa_j)=0\) in \(\mathcal I(O_j)=\varinjlim\mathcal I_\alpha(O_j)\), so there is \(\beta_j\ge\alpha_0\) such that \(\psi(\kappa_j)\mapsto0\) in \(\mathcal I_{\beta_j}(O_j)\). Take \(\beta\ge\) all \(\beta_j\) and set \(\psi_\beta:\mathcal P\to\mathcal I_{\alpha_0}\to\mathcal I_\beta\). Then \(\psi_\beta(\kappa_j)=0\) for all \(j\), so \(\psi_\beta\) kills the subsheaf \(\mathcal K\) they generate, hence factors as \(\psi_\beta=f_\beta\varepsilon\) with \(f_\beta:\mathcal R\to\mathcal I_\beta\) (\(\varepsilon\) is an epimorphism with kernel \(\mathcal K\)). And \(\lambda_\beta f_\beta\varepsilon=\lambda_\beta\psi_\beta=\lambda_{\alpha_0}\psi=g=f\varepsilon\), so \(\lambda_\beta f_\beta=f\) because \(\varepsilon\) is an epimorphism.

Step 4. Let \(U\) be open, \(\mathcal R\subseteq\mathbf Z_U\) and \(f:\mathcal R\to\mathcal I\). By Step 3, \(f=\lambda_\beta f_\beta\) with \(f_\beta:\mathcal R\to\mathcal I_\beta\). As \(\mathcal I_\beta\) is injective, \(f_\beta\) extends to \(F_\beta:\mathbf Z_U\to\mathcal I_\beta\), and then \(\lambda_\beta F_\beta\) extends \(f\). By Step 1, \(\mathcal I=\varinjlim\mathcal I_\alpha\) is injective. ∎

Exercise 2.7

Problem

Let \(S^1\) be the circle (with its usual topology), and let \(\mathbf Z\) be the constant sheaf \(\mathbf Z\).

  1. (a)

    Show that \(H^1(S^1,\mathbf Z)\cong\mathbf Z\), using our definition of cohomology.

  2. (b)

    Now let \(\mathcal R\) be the sheaf of germs of continuous real-valued functions on \(S^1\). Show that \(H^1(S^1,\mathcal R)=0\).

Solution

We prove (b) first, then use it for (a).

(b) Let \(\mathcal G\) be the sheaf of discontinuous sections of \(\mathcal R\): \(\mathcal G(W)=\prod_{x\in W}\mathcal R_x\) (II, Ex. 1.16(e)). It is flasque, and \(\mathcal R\to\mathcal G\) (\(s\mapsto\) its family of germs) is injective. Let \(\mathcal Q=\mathcal G/\mathcal R\). The long exact sequence for \(0\to\mathcal R\to\mathcal G\to\mathcal Q\to0\) and \(H^1(S^1,\mathcal G)=0\) (Prop. 2.5) give \(H^1(S^1,\mathcal R)\cong\operatorname{coker}(\Gamma(S^1,\mathcal G)\to\Gamma(S^1,\mathcal Q))\). So we must show that every \(q\in\Gamma(S^1,\mathcal Q)\) lifts to \(\Gamma(S^1,\mathcal G)\).

Locally \(q\) lifts: there is an open cover of \(S^1\) and \(g_i\in\mathcal G(U_i)\) mapping to \(q|_{U_i}\); since \(S^1\) is compact we may take the cover finite, \(U_1,\dots,U_m\). Then \(c_{ij}:=g_i-g_j\in\mathcal G(U_{i}\cap U_j)\) maps to \(0\) in \(\mathcal Q\), hence lies in \(\mathcal R(U_i\cap U_j)\), i.e. is a continuous function on \(U_i\cap U_j\), and \(c_{ij}+c_{jk}=c_{ik}\), \(c_{ij}=-c_{ji}\). Choose a continuous partition of unity \(\varphi_1,\dots,\varphi_m\) subordinate to the cover, i.e. \(\varphi_k:S^1\to[0,1]\) continuous, \(\operatorname{supp}\varphi_k\subseteq U_k\) (closed in \(S^1\)), \(\sum\varphi_k=1\) (it exists since \(S^1\) is compact Hausdorff, hence normal). For each \(k\), the function \(\varphi_kc_{ik}\) on \(U_i\cap U_k\) extends by \(0\) to a continuous function on \(U_i\) (it vanishes on \(U_i-\operatorname{supp}\varphi_k\), and \(U_i=(U_i\cap U_k)\cup(U_i-\operatorname{supp}\varphi_k)\) is an open cover of \(U_i\) on which the definitions agree). Put \(b_i=\sum_k\varphi_kc_{ik}\in\mathcal R(U_i)\). Then on \(U_i\cap U_j\)

\[ b_i-b_j=\sum_k\varphi_k(c_{ik}-c_{jk})=\sum_k\varphi_k(c_{ik}+c_{kj})=\sum_k\varphi_kc_{ij}=c_{ij}. \]

Hence \(g_i-b_i=g_j-b_j\) on \(U_i\cap U_j\), so the sections \(g_i-b_i\in\mathcal G(U_i)\) glue to \(g\in\Gamma(S^1,\mathcal G)\). Its image in \(\mathcal Q\) restricts on \(U_i\) to the class of \(g_i-b_i\), which equals that of \(g_i\) because \(b_i\in\mathcal R(U_i)\); so \(g\) maps to \(q\). Hence \(H^1(S^1,\mathcal R)=0\).

(a) Write \(S^1=\mathbf R/\mathbf Z\) and let \(\mathcal S\) be the sheaf of continuous functions to the group \(\mathbf R/\mathbf Z\) (a sheaf of abelian groups). Consider \(0\to\mathbf Z\xrightarrow{\ \iota\ }\mathcal R\xrightarrow{\ p\ }\mathcal S\to0\), where \(\iota\) regards a locally constant integer-valued function as a continuous real function, and \(p\) composes with \(\mathbf R\to\mathbf R/\mathbf Z\). Exactness: \(\ker p\) consists of the continuous functions with values in \(\mathbf Z\subset\mathbf R\), which are the locally constant integer-valued functions, i.e. the constant sheaf \(\mathbf Z\); and \(p\) is surjective on stalks, because \(\mathbf R\to\mathbf R/\mathbf Z\) is a covering map, so any continuous map from a small enough (connected, simply connected) neighborhood of a point to \(\mathbf R/\mathbf Z\) lifts to \(\mathbf R\). The long exact sequence and (b) give

\[ \Gamma(S^1,\mathcal R)\xrightarrow{\ p\ }\Gamma(S^1,\mathcal S)\to H^1(S^1,\mathbf Z)\to H^1(S^1,\mathcal R)=0, \]

so \(H^1(S^1,\mathbf Z)\cong C(S^1,\mathbf R/\mathbf Z)/p\bigl(C(S^1,\mathbf R)\bigr)\), where \(C(\cdot,\cdot)\) denotes continuous maps.

We compute this quotient via the degree. Let \(\pi:\mathbf R\to S^1=\mathbf R/\mathbf Z\) be the quotient map. For \(h\in C(S^1,\mathbf R/\mathbf Z)\), the map \(h\pi:\mathbf R\to\mathbf R/\mathbf Z\) lifts (path lifting for the covering, \(\mathbf R\) simply connected) to a continuous \(H:\mathbf R\to\mathbf R\) with \(H\bmod\mathbf Z=h\pi\), unique up to an additive integer constant. The function \(t\mapsto H(t+1)-H(t)\) is continuous with values in \(\mathbf Z\) (as \(h\pi(t+1)=h\pi(t)\)), hence a constant \(\deg h\in\mathbf Z\), independent of the choice of \(H\). If \(H_1,H_2\) lift \(h_1,h_2\) then \(H_1+H_2\) lifts \(h_1+h_2\), so \(\deg:C(S^1,\mathbf R/\mathbf Z)\to\mathbf Z\) is a homomorphism. It is surjective: \(h_n(t)=nt\bmod\mathbf Z\) (well defined on \(\mathbf R/\mathbf Z\)) has lift \(H(t)=nt\) and \(\deg h_n=n\). Its kernel is \(p(C(S^1,\mathbf R))\): if \(\deg h=0\) then \(H(t+1)=H(t)\), so \(H\) descends to a continuous \(\tilde H:S^1\to\mathbf R\) with \(p(\tilde H)=h\); conversely \(p(f)\) has lift \(f\pi\) of degree \(0\). Therefore \(C(S^1,\mathbf R/\mathbf Z)/p(C(S^1,\mathbf R))\cong\mathbf Z\), i.e. \(H^1(S^1,\mathbf Z)\cong\mathbf Z\). ∎

3. Cohomology of a noetherian affine scheme

Conventions and results used. For an ideal \(\fa\) of a ring \(A\) and an \(A\)-module \(M\), \(\Gamma_{\fa}(M)=\{m\in M\mid \fa^nm=0\text{ for some }n>0\}\) (II, Ex. 5.6). Open subschemes and closed subschemes of noetherian schemes are noetherian. If \(i:Y\to X\) is a closed immersion, then \(H^p(X,i_*\mathcal G)=H^p(Y,\mathcal G)\) (Lemma 2.10), and a quasi-coherent \(\mathcal O_X\)-module annihilated by the ideal sheaf of \(Y\) is of the form \(i_*\mathcal G\) with \(\mathcal G\) quasi-coherent on \(Y\) (II, Ex. 5.10). A closed subscheme of an affine scheme is affine. Throughout, “Thm. 3.5” means that quasi-coherent sheaves on a noetherian affine scheme have no higher cohomology, and Thm. 3.7 is Serre's criterion: a noetherian scheme \(X\) is affine if and only if \(H^1(X,\mathcal I)=0\) for every coherent sheaf of ideals \(\mathcal I\).

Exercise 3.1

Problem

Let \(X\) be a noetherian scheme. Show that \(X\) is affine if and only if \(X_{\mathrm{red}}\) is affine (II, Ex. 2.3). [Hint: Use (3.7), and for every coherent sheaf \(\mathcal F\) on \(X\), consider the filtration \(\mathcal F\supseteq\mathcal N\cdot\mathcal F\supseteq\mathcal N^2\cdot\mathcal F\supseteq\dots\), where \(\mathcal N\) is the sheaf of nilpotent elements on \(X\).]

Solution

(\(\Rightarrow\)) \(X_{\mathrm{red}}\) is a closed subscheme of \(X\), hence affine if \(X\) is.

(\(\Leftarrow\)) Let \(i:X_{\mathrm{red}}\to X\) be the closed immersion, \(\mathcal N\) the nilradical sheaf (the ideal sheaf of \(X_{\mathrm{red}}\)); it is coherent because \(X\) is noetherian. On each affine open \(\operatorname{Spec}A_j\) of a finite affine cover the nilradical of the noetherian ring \(A_j\) is nilpotent, say \(\mathcal N^{n_j}=0\) there; so \(\mathcal N^n=0\) for \(n=\max n_j\).

Let \(\mathcal F\) be a coherent sheaf on \(X\). We claim \(H^p(X,\mathcal F)=0\) for all \(p>0\). Consider the filtration \(\mathcal F\supseteq\mathcal N\mathcal F\supseteq\dots\supseteq\mathcal N^n\mathcal F=0\). Its quotients \(\mathcal Q_k=\mathcal N^k\mathcal F/\mathcal N^{k+1}\mathcal F\) are coherent and annihilated by \(\mathcal N\), hence \(\mathcal Q_k=i_*\mathcal G_k\) with \(\mathcal G_k\) quasi-coherent on \(X_{\mathrm{red}}\). Since \(X_{\mathrm{red}}\) is a noetherian affine scheme, Lemma 2.10 and Thm. 3.5 give \(H^p(X,\mathcal Q_k)=H^p(X_{\mathrm{red}},\mathcal G_k)=0\) for \(p>0\). Now show by descending induction on \(k\) that \(H^p(X,\mathcal N^k\mathcal F)=0\) for \(p>0\): it is clear for \(k=n\); and the exact sequence \(0\to\mathcal N^{k+1}\mathcal F\to\mathcal N^k\mathcal F\to\mathcal Q_k\to0\) and the long exact sequence give it for \(k\) from \(k+1\). For \(k=0\) we get the claim.

In particular \(H^1(X,\mathcal I)=0\) for all coherent ideal sheaves \(\mathcal I\), so \(X\) is affine by Thm. 3.7. ∎

Exercise 3.2

Problem

Let \(X\) be a reduced noetherian scheme. Show that \(X\) is affine if and only if each irreducible component of \(X\) is affine.

Solution

Irreducible components are given their reduced induced structure (they are then integral closed subschemes).

(\(\Rightarrow\)) Closed subschemes of affine schemes are affine.

(\(\Leftarrow\)) Induction on the number \(n\) of irreducible components \(X_1,\dots,X_n\) of \(X\) (all affine). If \(n=1\), \(X=X_1\). Let \(n\ge2\), \(Y=X_1\) and \(Z=X_2\cup\dots\cup X_n\) with the reduced structure. Then \(Z\) is a reduced noetherian scheme whose irreducible components are \(X_2,\dots,X_n\), so \(Z\) is affine by induction; \(Y\) is affine by hypothesis. Let \(\mathcal I_Y,\mathcal I_Z\subseteq\mathcal O_X\) be their ideal sheaves.

Claim: \(\mathcal I_Y\cap\mathcal I_Z=0\). On an affine open \(\operatorname{Spec}A\) of \(X\) (\(A\) a reduced noetherian ring), \(\mathcal I_Y,\mathcal I_Z\) correspond to the radical ideals \(\fa=\bigcap_{\fp\in Y}\fp\), \(\fb=\bigcap_{\fp\in Z}\fp\), so \(\fa\cap\fb=\bigcap_{\fp\in Y\cup Z}\fp=\bigcap_{\fp\in\operatorname{Spec}A}\fp=\Nil(A)=0\).

Now let \(\mathcal J\subseteq\mathcal O_X\) be any coherent ideal sheaf. We show \(H^1(X,\mathcal J)=0\); then \(X\) is affine by Thm. 3.7. Consider \(0\to\mathcal J\cap\mathcal I_Y\to\mathcal J\to\mathcal J/(\mathcal J\cap\mathcal I_Y)\to0\).

  • •

    \(\mathcal J/(\mathcal J\cap\mathcal I_Y)\cong(\mathcal J+\mathcal I_Y)/\mathcal I_Y\) is a coherent sheaf killed by \(\mathcal I_Y\), i.e. it is \(i_{Y*}\) of a quasi-coherent sheaf on \(Y\). As \(Y\) is affine, its \(H^1\) vanishes by Lemma 2.10 and Thm. 3.5.

  • •

    \(\mathcal J\cap\mathcal I_Y\subseteq\mathcal I_Y\) is coherent and killed by \(\mathcal I_Z\), since \(\mathcal I_Z\cdot(\mathcal J\cap\mathcal I_Y)\subseteq\mathcal I_Z\mathcal I_Y\subseteq\mathcal I_Z\cap\mathcal I_Y=0\). So it is \(i_{Z*}\) of a quasi-coherent sheaf on the affine scheme \(Z\), and its \(H^1\) vanishes in the same way.

The long exact sequence now gives \(H^1(X,\mathcal J)=0\). ∎

Exercise 3.3

Problem

Let \(A\) be a noetherian ring, and let \(\fa\) be an ideal of \(A\).

  1. (a)

    Show that \(\Gamma_{\fa}(\cdot)\) (II, Ex. 5.6) is a left-exact functor from the category of \(A\)-modules to itself. We denote its right derived functors, calculated in \(\mathfrak{Mod}(A)\), by \(H^i_{\fa}(\cdot)\).

  2. (b)

    Now let \(X=\operatorname{Spec}A\), \(Y=V(\fa)\). Show that for any \(A\)-module \(M\), \(H^i_{\fa}(M)=H^i_Y(X,\widetilde M)\), where \(H^i_Y(X,\cdot)\) denotes cohomology with supports in \(Y\) (Ex. 2.3).

  3. (c)

    For any \(i\), show that \(\Gamma_{\fa}(H^i_{\fa}(M))=H^i_{\fa}(M)\).

Solution

(a) \(\Gamma_{\fa}\) is an additive functor (a homomorphism sends \(\fa\)-power torsion elements to such). Let \(0\to M'\xrightarrow{u}M\xrightarrow{v}M''\) be exact. If \(m'\in\Gamma_{\fa}(M')\) with \(\fa^nm'=0\) then \(\fa^nu(m')=0\); \(u\) is injective, so \(\Gamma_{\fa}(u)\) is injective. If \(m\in\Gamma_{\fa}(M)\) and \(v(m)=0\), then \(m=u(m')\) and \(u(\fa^nm')=\fa^nm=0\), so \(\fa^nm'=0\), i.e. \(m'\in\Gamma_{\fa}(M')\). Hence \(0\to\Gamma_{\fa}(M')\to\Gamma_{\fa}(M)\to\Gamma_{\fa}(M'')\) is exact. (Also \(\Gamma_{\fa}(\cdot)\) is \(A\)-linear.) The category of \(A\)-modules has enough injectives, so the right derived functors \(H^i_{\fa}\) exist.

(b) First, for any \(A\)-module \(M\) we have \(\Gamma_Y(X,\widetilde M)=\Gamma_{\fa}(M)\) as submodules of \(M=\Gamma(X,\widetilde M)\). Indeed, for \(m\in M\) the support of the section \(m\) is \(\{\fp\mid m/1\neq0\text{ in }M_{\fp}\}=\{\fp\mid\Ann(m)\subseteq\fp\}=V(\Ann m)\). So \(\operatorname{Supp}(m)\subseteq V(\fa)\) iff \(\fa\subseteq\rad(\Ann m)\) iff \(\fa^n\subseteq\Ann(m)\) for some \(n\) (\(\fa\) is finitely generated), i.e. iff \(m\in\Gamma_{\fa}(M)\).

Let \(0\to M\to I^\bullet\) be an injective resolution in \(\mathfrak{Mod}(A)\). The functor \(\widetilde{\ }\) is exact, so \(0\to\widetilde M\to\widetilde{I^\bullet}\) is a resolution, and each \(\widetilde{I^p}\) is flasque by Prop. 3.4. By Ex. 2.3(c), \(H^i_Y(X,\widetilde{I^p})=0\) for \(i>0\), so flasque sheaves are acyclic for \(\Gamma_Y(X,\cdot)\) and this resolution computes \(H^i_Y(X,\widetilde M)\) (Prop. 1.2A). Thus

\[ H^i_Y(X,\widetilde M)=h^i(\Gamma_Y(X,\widetilde{I^\bullet}))=h^i(\Gamma_{\fa}(I^\bullet))=H^i_{\fa}(M). \]

(c) Let \(M\to I^\bullet\) be an injective resolution. Then \(H^i_{\fa}(M)=\ker(\Gamma_{\fa}(I^i)\to\Gamma_{\fa}(I^{i+1}))/\operatorname{im}\), a subquotient of \(\Gamma_{\fa}(I^i)\). Every element \(z\) of the kernel satisfies \(\fa^nz=0\) for some \(n\), hence so does its class \([z]\). So every element of \(H^i_{\fa}(M)\) is killed by a power of \(\fa\), i.e. \(\Gamma_{\fa}(H^i_{\fa}(M))=H^i_{\fa}(M)\). ∎

Exercise 3.4

Problem

Cohomological interpretation of depth. If \(A\) is a ring, \(\fa\) an ideal, and \(M\) an \(A\)-module, then \(\operatorname{depth}_{\fa}M\) is the maximum length of an \(M\)-regular sequence \(x_1,\dots,x_r\), with all \(x_i\in\fa\). This generalizes the notion of depth introduced in (II, Section 8).

  1. (a)

    Assume that \(A\) is noetherian. Show that if \(\operatorname{depth}_{\fa}M\ge1\), then \(\Gamma_{\fa}(M)=0\), and the converse is true if \(M\) is finitely generated. [Hint: When \(M\) is finitely generated, both conditions are equivalent to saying that \(\fa\) is not contained in any associated prime of \(M\).]

  2. (b)

    Show inductively, for \(M\) finitely generated, that for any \(n\ge0\), the following conditions are equivalent: (i) \(\operatorname{depth}_{\fa}M\ge n\); (ii) \(H^i_{\fa}(M)=0\) for all \(i<n\).

For more details, and related results, see Grothendieck [7].

Solution

Convention. Here \(x_1,\dots,x_r\in\fa\) is called \(M\)-regular if \(x_i\) is a nonzerodivisor on \(M/(x_1,\dots,x_{i-1})M\) for each \(i\). The extra requirement \(M/(x_1,\dots,x_r)M\neq0\) that is often added is not imposed (and it must not be, in order that (b) be true: for \(\fa=A\) one has \(\Gamma_{\fa}=0\), so \(H^i_{\fa}(M)=0\) for all \(i\), while \(A\) contains regular sequences \(1,1,1,\dots\) of any length). Depth may be \(\infty\); “\(\operatorname{depth}_{\fa}M\ge n\)” means that an \(M\)-regular sequence of length \(n\) in \(\fa\) exists.

(a) Suppose \(x\in\fa\) is \(M\)-regular and \(m\in\Gamma_{\fa}(M)\), \(\fa^nm=0\). Then \(x^nm=0\), so \(m=0\) as \(x\) is a nonzerodivisor on \(M\). Hence \(\Gamma_{\fa}(M)=0\).

Conversely let \(M\) be finitely generated and \(\Gamma_{\fa}(M)=0\). We prove: \(\Gamma_{\fa}(M)=0\) iff \(\fa\not\subseteq\fp\) for every \(\fp\in\operatorname{Ass}M\). If \(\fa\subseteq\fp=\Ann(m)\) with \(0\ne m\), then \(\fa m=0\), so \(0\neq m\in\Gamma_{\fa}(M)\). Conversely if \(0\ne m\in\Gamma_{\fa}(M)\), the nonzero module \(Am\subseteq\Gamma_{\fa}(M)\) has an associated prime \(\fp=\Ann(m')\), \(m'\in Am\); as \(\fa^nm'=0\) for some \(n\), \(\fa^n\subseteq\fp\) hence \(\fa\subseteq\fp\), and \(\fp\in\operatorname{Ass}(M)\). Now \(\operatorname{Ass}M\) is finite and the set of zerodivisors of \(M\) is \(\bigcup_{\fp\in\operatorname{Ass}M}\fp\) (Matsumura, Thm. 6.1). If \(\fa\not\subseteq\fp\) for all \(\fp\in\operatorname{Ass}M\), prime avoidance gives \(x\in\fa\) not in any associated prime, i.e. \(x\) is \(M\)-regular, so \(\operatorname{depth}_{\fa}M\ge1\).

(b) We prove by induction on \(n\): \(P(n)\): for every finitely generated \(M\), \(\operatorname{depth}_{\fa}M\ge n\iff H^i_{\fa}(M)=0\) for \(i<n\). \(P(0)\) is trivial and \(P(1)\) is (a) (\(H^0_{\fa}=\Gamma_{\fa}\)). Assume \(P(n)\) for some \(n\ge1\); we prove \(P(n+1)\) for any finitely generated \(M\). For an \(M\)-regular \(x\in\fa\) the sequence \(0\to M\xrightarrow{x}M\to M/xM\to0\) gives, by the long exact sequence of the \(\delta\)-functor \(H^\bullet_{\fa}\),

\[ \dots\to H^{i-1}_{\fa}(M/xM)\to H^i_{\fa}(M)\xrightarrow{\ x\ }H^i_{\fa}(M)\to H^i_{\fa}(M/xM)\to H^{i+1}_{\fa}(M)\to\dots\tag{$*$} \]

(the map induced by multiplication by \(x\) on \(M\) is multiplication by \(x\) on \(H^i_{\fa}(M)\), because \(\Gamma_{\fa}\) is \(A\)-linear; lift \(x\cdot\) to the injective resolution as \(x\cdot\)).

(\(\Leftarrow\)) Suppose \(H^i_{\fa}(M)=0\) for \(i<n+1\). In particular \(\Gamma_{\fa}(M)=0\), so by (a) there is an \(M\)-regular \(x\in\fa\). For \(i<n\) we have \(H^i_{\fa}(M)=0\) and \(H^{i+1}_{\fa}(M)=0\), so by \((*)\) \(H^i_{\fa}(M/xM)=0\). By \(P(n)\) applied to the finitely generated module \(M/xM\), \(\operatorname{depth}_{\fa}M/xM\ge n\), so \(\operatorname{depth}_{\fa}M\ge n+1\).

(\(\Rightarrow\)) Suppose \(x_1,\dots,x_{n+1}\in\fa\) is \(M\)-regular. Put \(x=x_1\), \(M'=M/xM\); then \(x_2,\dots,x_{n+1}\) is \(M'\)-regular, so \(\operatorname{depth}_{\fa}M'\ge n\) and by \(P(n)\) \(H^i_{\fa}(M')=0\) for \(i<n\). By \((*)\), for \(i\le n\) the map \(x:H^i_{\fa}(M)\to H^i_{\fa}(M)\) is injective (its kernel is the image of \(H^{i-1}_{\fa}(M')=0\)). But every element of \(H^i_{\fa}(M)\) is killed by a power of \(\fa\), hence by a power of \(x\) (3.3(c)); so an injective \(x\) forces \(H^i_{\fa}(M)=0\). Thus \(H^i_{\fa}(M)=0\) for \(i\le n\), i.e. for \(i<n+1\). ∎

Exercise 3.5

Problem

Let \(X\) be a noetherian scheme, and let \(P\) be a closed point of \(X\). Show that the following conditions are equivalent: (i) \(\operatorname{depth}\mathcal O_P\ge2\); (ii) if \(U\) is any open neighborhood of \(P\), then every section of \(\mathcal O_X\) over \(U-P\) extends uniquely to a section of \(\mathcal O_X\) over \(U\). This generalizes (I, Ex. 3.20), in view of (II, 8.22A).

Solution

Here \(\operatorname{depth}\mathcal O_P\) is the depth of the noetherian local ring \(R=\mathcal O_{X,P}\) with respect to its maximal ideal \(\fm_P\), i.e. \(\operatorname{depth}_{\fm_P}R\) in the sense of 3.4.

Step 1: reduction to affine neighbourhoods. For an open \(U\ni P\) let \(\rho_U:\mathcal O_X(U)\to\mathcal O_X(U-P)\) be the restriction (\(U-P\) is open as \(P\) is closed). Claim: (ii) holds iff \(\rho_U\) is bijective for every affine open neighbourhood \(U\) of \(P\). One direction is trivial. Conversely, let \(U\ni P\) be open and consider the morphism of sheaves \(\varphi:\mathcal O_U\to j_*(\mathcal O_{U-P})\) on \(U\) (\(j:U-P\to U\)). Let \(\mathcal B\) be the collection of opens \(W\subseteq U\) that either do not contain \(P\) or are affine neighbourhoods of \(P\). This is a basis of the topology of \(U\): a neighbourhood of \(x\neq P\) can be shrunk inside \(U-P\), and every neighbourhood of \(P\) contains an affine neighbourhood. For \(W\in\mathcal B\) not containing \(P\), \(\varphi(W)\) is the identity; for \(W\) affine containing \(P\), \(\varphi(W)=\rho_W\) is bijective by assumption. A morphism of sheaves which is bijective on a basis is an isomorphism of sheaves, hence bijective on all open sets, in particular \(\rho_U\) is bijective.

Step 2: the affine case. Let \(U=\operatorname{Spec}A\) be an affine open neighbourhood of \(P\) and \(\fm\subset A\) the maximal ideal of \(P\) (the point \(P\) is closed in \(U\)). Ex. 2.3(e) for \(Y=\{P\}\) gives an exact sequence

\[ 0\to H^0_P(U,\mathcal O_U)\to A\xrightarrow{\rho_U}\mathcal O(U-P)\to H^1_P(U,\mathcal O_U)\to H^1(U,\mathcal O_U)=0, \]

the last group vanishing by Thm. 3.5. By 3.3(b), \(H^i_P(U,\mathcal O_U)=H^i_{\fm}(A)\). Hence \(\rho_U\) is bijective iff \(H^0_{\fm}(A)=H^1_{\fm}(A)=0\).

Step 3: localization. Let \(R=A_{\fm}=\mathcal O_P\). Claim: \(H^i_{\fm}(A)\cong H^i_{\fm R}(R)\). For a multiplicative set \(S\subset A\), we have \(S^{-1}\Gamma_{\fa}(M)=\Gamma_{S^{-1}\fa}(S^{-1}M)\) (\(\subseteq\) is clear; if \(z/s\) is killed by \((S^{-1}\fa)^n\), then with \(\fa^n=(g_1,\dots,g_k)\) there are \(t_l\in S\) with \(t_lg_lz=0\), and \(t=\prod t_l\) gives \(\fa^n(tz)=0\) and \(z/s=tz/ts\)). Moreover localization preserves injectives over the noetherian ring \(A\): if \(I\) is injective and \(\fb'=S^{-1}\fb\) (\(\fb\) a finitely generated ideal) is an ideal of \(S^{-1}A\), then since \(\fb\) is finitely presented, every \(\varphi:S^{-1}\fb\to S^{-1}I\) has the form \(\psi/s\) with \(\psi\in\Hom_A(\fb,I)\); \(\psi\) extends to \(\tilde\psi:A\to I\) and \(\tilde\psi/s\) extends \(\varphi\); Baer's criterion applies. Thus \(S^{-1}\) of an injective resolution of \(M\) is an injective resolution of \(S^{-1}M\) over \(S^{-1}A\), and since \(S^{-1}\) is exact, \(S^{-1}H^i_{\fa}(M)=H^i_{S^{-1}\fa}(S^{-1}M)\). Take \(S=A-\fm\). By 3.3(c) \(H^i_{\fm}(A)\) is \(\fm\)-power torsion, and then every \(s\notin\fm\) acts bijectively on it: if \(z\) is killed by \(\fm^k\) then \(s\) is a unit modulo \(\fm^k\), \(gs\equiv1\pmod{\fm^k}\), so \(z=gsz\); this shows \(sz=0\Rightarrow z=0\) and \(z=s(gz)\). Therefore \(H^i_{\fm}(A)=H^i_{\fm}(A)_{\fm}=H^i_{\fm R}(R)\).

Conclusion. By Steps 2 and 3, for an affine neighbourhood \(U\): \(\rho_U\) is bijective iff \(H^0_{\fm R}(R)=H^1_{\fm R}(R)=0\). This condition depends only on \(R\). By 3.4(b) with \(n=2\) (applied to the noetherian local ring \(R\), ideal \(\fm R\), \(M=R\)) it is equivalent to \(\operatorname{depth}_{\fm R}R\ge2\). (For the maximal ideal of a noetherian local ring, \(R/(x_1,\dots,x_r)R\neq0\) automatically by Nakayama, so the convention of 3.4 agrees with the usual definition of the depth of a local ring, II, Section 8.) Together with Step 1, (ii) holds iff \(\operatorname{depth}\mathcal O_P\ge2\). (Existence of an affine neighbourhood \(U\) is clear.) ∎

Exercise 3.6

Problem

Let \(X\) be a noetherian scheme.

  1. (a)

    Show that the sheaf \(\mathcal G\) constructed in the proof of (3.6) is an injective object in the category \(\mathfrak{Qco}(X)\) of quasi-coherent sheaves on \(X\). Thus \(\mathfrak{Qco}(X)\) has enough injectives.

  2. (b)

    Show that any injective object of \(\mathfrak{Qco}(X)\) is flasque. [Hints: The method of proof of (2.4) will not work, because \(\mathcal O_U\) is not quasi-coherent on \(X\) in general. Instead, use (II, Ex. 5.15) to show that if \(\mathcal I\in\mathfrak{Qco}(X)\) is injective, and if \(U\subseteq X\) is an open subset, then \(\mathcal I|_U\) is an injective object of \(\mathfrak{Qco}(U)\). Then cover \(X\) with open affines…]

  3. (c)

    Conclude that one can compute cohomology as the derived functors of \(\Gamma(X,\cdot)\), considered as a functor from \(\mathfrak{Qco}(X)\) to \(\mathfrak{Ab}\).

(Part (b) is starred in the book.)

Solution

Recall the construction in 3.6: a finite affine open cover \(X=\bigcup U_i\), \(U_i=\operatorname{Spec}A_i\), \(f_i:U_i\to X\) the inclusions, \(\mathcal F|_{U_i}=\widetilde{M_i}\), \(M_i\hookrightarrow I_i\) an injective \(A_i\)-module, and \(\mathcal G=\bigoplus_if_{i*}(\widetilde{I_i})\), with the monomorphism \(\mathcal F\to\mathcal G\). The sheaf \(\mathcal G\) is quasi-coherent (II, 5.8) and flasque (3.4, II Ex. 1.16(d)).

(a) \(\mathfrak{Qco}(X)\) is a full subcategory of \(\mathfrak{Mod}(X)\), closed under kernels and cokernels (II, 5.7); so a monomorphism in it is an injective sheaf map. Let \(\mathcal A\hookrightarrow\mathcal B\) be a monomorphism in \(\mathfrak{Qco}(X)\); we must show \(\Hom(\mathcal B,\mathcal G)\to\Hom(\mathcal A,\mathcal G)\) is surjective. Since the sum is finite, it suffices to treat each \(f_{i*}\widetilde{I_i}\). By the adjunction between \(f_i^*\) (restriction to \(U_i\)) and \(f_{i*}\), and by the equivalence \(\mathfrak{Qco}(U_i)\simeq\mathfrak{Mod}(A_i)\) (II, 5.5),

\[ \Hom_{\mathcal O_X}(\mathcal A,f_{i*}\widetilde{I_i})\cong\Hom_{\mathcal O_{U_i}}(\mathcal A|_{U_i},\widetilde{I_i})\cong\Hom_{A_i}(\Gamma(U_i,\mathcal A),I_i), \]

naturally in \(\mathcal A\). The map \(\Gamma(U_i,\mathcal A)\to\Gamma(U_i,\mathcal B)\) is injective (\(\Gamma\) is left exact), so, \(I_i\) being injective, \(\Hom_{A_i}(\Gamma(U_i,\mathcal B),I_i)\to\Hom_{A_i}(\Gamma(U_i,\mathcal A),I_i)\) is surjective. Hence \(\mathcal G\) is injective in \(\mathfrak{Qco}(X)\). Since every quasi-coherent \(\mathcal F\) embeds in such a \(\mathcal G\) (3.6), \(\mathfrak{Qco}(X)\) has enough injectives.

(b) (We do not need the hinted route: (a) gives a short proof.) Let \(\mathcal J\) be an injective object of \(\mathfrak{Qco}(X)\). By (3.6) there is a monomorphism \(u:\mathcal J\to\mathcal G\) in \(\mathfrak{Qco}(X)\) with \(\mathcal G\) as above, which is flasque. Since \(\mathcal J\) is injective in \(\mathfrak{Qco}(X)\), the identity of \(\mathcal J\) extends along \(u\): there is \(r:\mathcal G\to\mathcal J\) with \(ru=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal J}\). Now let \(V\subseteq X\) be open and \(s\in\mathcal J(V)\). Then \(u(s)\in\mathcal G(V)\) lifts to \(t\in\mathcal G(X)\) since \(\mathcal G\) is flasque, and \(r(t)\in\mathcal J(X)\) satisfies \(r(t)|_V=r(t|_V)=r(u(s))=s\). So \(\mathcal J(X)\to\mathcal J(V)\) is surjective for all \(V\): \(\mathcal J\) is flasque (a direct summand of a flasque sheaf is flasque).

(c) Let \(\mathcal F\) be quasi-coherent and \(0\to\mathcal F\to\mathcal J^\bullet\) an injective resolution in \(\mathfrak{Qco}(X)\) (exists by (a)). Since \(\mathfrak{Qco}(X)\) is closed under kernels and cokernels in \(\mathfrak{Mod}(X)\) (II, 5.7), the sequence is exact as a sequence of sheaves, and by (b) each \(\mathcal J^p\) is flasque. By Prop. 2.5 (flasque resolutions compute cohomology), \(H^p(X,\mathcal F)=h^p(\Gamma(X,\mathcal J^\bullet))\), which is by definition the \(p\)-th right derived functor of \(\Gamma(X,\cdot):\mathfrak{Qco}(X)\to\mathfrak{Ab}\) evaluated at \(\mathcal F\). (The identification is natural in \(\mathcal F\), and compatible with connecting morphisms for short exact sequences of quasi-coherent sheaves.) ∎

Exercise 3.7

Problem

Let \(A\) be a noetherian ring, let \(X=\operatorname{Spec}A\), let \(\fa\subseteq A\) be an ideal, and let \(U\subseteq X\) be the open set \(X-V(\fa)\).

  1. (a)

    For any \(A\)-module \(M\), establish the following formula of Deligne:

    \[ \Gamma(U,\widetilde M)\cong\varinjlim_n\Hom_A(\fa^n,M). \]
  2. (b)

    Apply this in the case of an injective \(A\)-module \(I\), to give another proof of (3.4).

Solution

Choose generators \(\fa=(f_1,\dots,f_r)\) (\(A\) is noetherian). Then \(U=\bigcup_iD(f_i)\) and \(D(f)\subseteq U\) for every \(f\in\fa\), and \(\{D(f)\}_{f\in\fa}\) covers \(U\).

(a) The map. For \(\varphi\in\Hom_A(\fa^n,M)\) and \(f\in\fa\), put \(s_f=\varphi(f^n)/f^n\in M_f=\widetilde M(D(f))\). For \(f,g\in\fa\), in \(M_{fg}\) we have \(s_f=s_g\), because \(g^n\varphi(f^n)=\varphi(f^ng^n)=f^n\varphi(g^n)\). So the \(s_f\) glue to a section \(\sigma(\varphi)\in\Gamma(U,\widetilde M)\) with \(\sigma(\varphi)|_{D(f)}=\varphi(f^n)/f^n\) for all \(f\in\fa\). If \(\varphi'=\varphi|_{\fa^{n+1}}\) then \(\varphi'(f^{n+1})/f^{n+1}=f\varphi(f^n)/f^{n+1}=\varphi(f^n)/f^n\), so \(\sigma(\varphi')=\sigma(\varphi)\). Hence \(\sigma\) induces an \(A\)-linear map \(\sigma:\varinjlim_n\Hom_A(\fa^n,M)\to\Gamma(U,\widetilde M)\).

Injectivity. Let \(\sigma(\varphi)=0\), \(\varphi\in\Hom(\fa^n,M)\). Then \(\varphi(f_i^n)/f_i^n=0\) in \(M_{f_i}\), so there is \(k\) (uniform in \(i\)) with \(f_i^k\varphi(f_i^n)=0\), i.e. \(\varphi(f_i^{n+k})=0\) for all \(i\). Put \(N=r(n+k)\). The ideal \(\fa^N\) is generated by the monomials \(f^\alpha=\prod f_i^{\alpha_i}\) with \(|\alpha|=N\); each has some \(\alpha_i\ge n+k\), so \(f^\alpha=gf_i^{n+k}\) with \(g\in A\) and \(\varphi(f^\alpha)=g\varphi(f_i^{n+k})=0\). So \(\varphi|_{\fa^N}=0\): the class of \(\varphi\) in the limit is \(0\).

Surjectivity. Let \(s\in\Gamma(U,\widetilde M)\). Then \(s|_{D(f_i)}=m_i/f_i^{n}\) for some \(m_i\in M\) and a common \(n\ge1\) (we may always increase \(n\)). On \(D(f_if_j)\), \(m_i/f_i^n=m_j/f_j^n\), so there is \(k\) with \((f_if_j)^k(f_j^nm_i-f_i^nm_j)=0\) for all \(i,j\). Replacing \(n\) by \(n+k\) and \(m_i\) by \(f_i^km_i\) (which does not change \(m_i/f_i^n\)) we may assume

\[ f_j^nm_i=f_i^nm_j\ \text{ in }M\quad\text{for all }i,j.\tag{1} \]

Let \(F=A^r\) with basis \(e_1,\dots,e_r\), let \(\pi:F\to\fa^{(n)}:=(f_1^n,\dots,f_r^n)\), \(e_i\mapsto f_i^n\), with kernel \(R\) (finitely generated, \(A\) noetherian), and let \(\psi:F\to M\), \(e_i\mapsto m_i\). If \(\rho=\sum c_ie_i\in R\), then \(z=\psi(\rho)=\sum c_im_i\) satisfies, in \(M_{f_j}\), using (1) (\(m_i/1=f_i^nm_j/f_j^n\)):

\[ z/1=\Big(\sum c_if_i^n\Big)\frac{m_j}{f_j^n}=0 . \]

Thus every element of \(\psi(R)\) is killed by a power of each \(f_j\), hence by a power of \(\fa\); since \(\psi(R)\) is finitely generated there is \(T\) with \(\fa^T\psi(R)=0\). By Krull's theorem (3.1A) applied to \(R\subseteq F\) there is \(T'\ge T\) with \(R\cap\fa^{T'}F\subseteq\fa^TR\), so \(\psi(R\cap\fa^{T'}F)=0\). Hence \(\psi\) restricted to \(\fa^{T'}F\) factors through \(\fa^{T'}F/(R\cap\fa^{T'}F)\cong\pi(\fa^{T'}F)=\fa^{T'}\fa^{(n)}\): we get \(\varphi_0:\fa^{T'}\fa^{(n)}\to M\) with \(\varphi_0(\sum c_if_i^n)=\sum c_im_i\) for \(c_i\in\fa^{T'}\).

Now \(\fa^{N_0}\subseteq\fa^{(n)}\) for \(N_0=r(n-1)+1\) (pigeonhole on monomials), hence \(\fa^{T'+N_0}\subseteq\fa^{T'}\fa^{(n)}\). Let \(N=T'+N_0\) and \(\varphi=\varphi_0|_{\fa^N}\in\Hom(\fa^N,M)\). For \(j\) we have \(N\ge T'+n\) (as \(N_0\ge n\)) and \(f_j^N=f_j^{N-n}\cdot f_j^n\) with \(f_j^{N-n}\in\fa^{T'}\), so \(\varphi(f_j^N)=f_j^{N-n}m_j\) and

\[ \sigma(\varphi)|_{D(f_j)}=\varphi(f_j^N)/f_j^N=m_j/f_j^n=s|_{D(f_j)}. \]

The \(D(f_j)\) cover \(U\), so \(\sigma(\varphi)=s\).

(b) Let \(I\) be an injective \(A\)-module, \(\fa\) any ideal. Since \(I\) is injective, restriction \(I=\Hom_A(A,I)\to\Hom_A(\fa^n,I)\) is surjective for each \(n\): every \(\varphi\in\Hom(\fa^n,I)\) is \(x\mapsto xm\) for some \(m\in I\). For such \(\varphi\), \(\sigma(\varphi)|_{D(f)}=f^nm/f^n=m|_{D(f)}\), so \(\sigma(\varphi)=m|_U\), the restriction to \(U\) of the global section \(m\in I=\Gamma(X,\widetilde I)\). By (a), \(\sigma\) is surjective, so \(\Gamma(X,\widetilde I)\to\Gamma(U,\widetilde I)\) is surjective. Every open subset \(U\) of \(X\) is of the form \(X-V(\fa)\) for some ideal \(\fa\). Thus \(\widetilde I(X)\to\widetilde I(U)\) is surjective for every open \(U\); if \(V\subseteq U\) are open and \(s\in\widetilde I(V)\), lift \(s\) to \(t\in\widetilde I(X)\), and then \(t|_U\) extends \(s\). So \(\widetilde I\) is flasque, which is Prop. 3.4. (This proof uses Krull's theorem 3.1A, as did Lemma 3.2, but not Lemmas 3.2 and 3.3 or noetherian induction.) ∎

Exercise 3.8

Problem

Without the noetherian hypothesis, (3.3) and (3.4) are false. Let \(A=k[x_0,x_1,x_2,\dots]\) with the relations \(x_0^nx_n=0\) for \(n=1,2,\dots\). Let \(I\) be an injective \(A\)-module containing \(A\). Show that \(I\to I_{x_0}\) is not surjective.

Solution

Let \(\theta:I\to I_{x_0}\), \(z\mapsto z/1\), and regard \(A\subseteq I\). Consider the element \(1/x_0\in I_{x_0}\) (the class of \(1\in A\subseteq I\)). Suppose \(\theta(z)=1/x_0\) for some \(z\in I\). Then \(z/1=1/x_0\) in \(I_{x_0}\) means that for some \(m\ge0\), \(x_0^m(x_0z-1)=0\) in \(I\), i.e.

\[ x_0^{m+1}z=x_0^m\cdot1\quad\text{in }I. \]

Multiply by \(x_{m+1}\in A\) and use \(x_0^{m+1}x_{m+1}=0\) in \(A\):

\[ x_{m+1}x_0^m\cdot1=x_{m+1}x_0^{m+1}z=0\quad\text{in }I. \]

Since \(A\subseteq I\), this says \(x_0^mx_{m+1}=0\) in \(A\). But \(A=k[x_0,x_1,\dots]/\mathfrak b\) with \(\mathfrak b=(x_0^nx_n\mid n\ge1)\) a monomial ideal, and the monomial \(x_0^mx_{m+1}\) is divisible by no generator \(x_0^nx_n\) (it involves \(x_{m+1}\), so we would need \(n=m+1\), but then \(x_0\) would have to occur to the power \(\ge m+1\)). Hence \(x_0^mx_{m+1}\notin\mathfrak b\), i.e. it is nonzero in \(A\), a contradiction. Therefore \(1/x_0\notin\theta(I)\) and \(I\to I_{x_0}\) is not surjective.

(Remark: the argument works for any \(A\)-module \(I\) containing \(A\); the failure is caused by the strictly increasing chain \(\Ann(x_0)\subsetneq\Ann(x_0^2)\subsetneq\cdots\), \(x_n\in\Ann(x_0^n)\setminus\Ann(x_0^{n-1})\), which violates the stabilization used in Lemma 3.3. Consequently the proof of Prop. 3.4 does not go through; in fact for this \(I\) the surjectivity \(\widetilde I(X)\to\widetilde I(D(x_0))=I_{x_0}\) fails, so \(\widetilde I\) is not flasque.) ∎

4. Cech cohomology

Conventions and results used. For a topological space \(X\), an open covering \(\mathfrak U=(U_i)_{i\in I}\) with \(I\) well-ordered, and a sheaf \(\mathcal F\) of abelian groups, \(C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\) is the Cech complex and \(\check H^p(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\) its cohomology. By Remark 4.0.1 we may regard a cochain \(\alpha\in C^p(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\) as a family \(\alpha_{i_0\dots i_p}\in\mathcal F(U_{i_0\dots i_p})\) indexed by all \((p+1)\)-tuples, alternating (zero if two indices coincide, changing sign under a transposition), and the formula for \(d\) is then valid for all \((p+2)\)-tuples. We use: Lemma 4.1 (\(\check H^0(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)=\Gamma(X,\mathcal F)\)), Lemma 4.2 (the sheafified complex \(\mathcal C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\) is a resolution of \(\mathcal F\)), Prop. 4.3 (flasque sheaves have \(\check H^p=0\), \(p>0\)), the natural maps \(\check H^p(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\to H^p(X,\mathcal F)\) of Lemma 4.4, and Thm. 4.5. In a separated scheme the intersection of two open affines is affine (II, Ex. 4.3). If \(X\) is noetherian and separated, \(\mathfrak U\) an open affine cover and \(\mathcal F\) quasi-coherent, then \(\check H^p(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)=H^p(X,\mathcal F)\) (Thm. 4.5).

Exercise 4.1

Problem

Let \(f:X\to Y\) be an affine morphism of noetherian separated schemes (II, Ex. 5.17). Show that for any quasi-coherent sheaf \(\mathcal F\) on \(X\), there are natural isomorphisms for all \(i\ge0\),

\[ H^i(X,\mathcal F)\cong H^i(Y,f_*\mathcal F). \]

[Hint: Use (II, 5.8).]

Solution

Let \(\mathfrak V=(V_j)_{j\in J}\) be a finite open affine cover of \(Y\) and \(U_j=f^{-1}(V_j)\); since \(f\) is affine, each \(U_j\) is affine, so \(\mathfrak U=(U_j)\) is an open affine cover of \(X\). Moreover \(U_{j_0\dots j_p}=f^{-1}(V_{j_0\dots j_p})\). The sheaf \(f_*\mathcal F\) is quasi-coherent on \(Y\) (II, 5.8(c); \(X\) is noetherian), and

\[ \mathcal F(U_{j_0\dots j_p})=\mathcal F(f^{-1}V_{j_0\dots j_p})=(f_*\mathcal F)(V_{j_0\dots j_p}). \]

Hence (with the same ordering of \(J\)) \(C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)=C^\bullet(\mathfrak V,f_*\mathcal F)\) as complexes. Applying Thm. 4.5 to \(X\) and to \(Y\),

\[ H^i(X,\mathcal F)\cong\check H^i(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)=\check H^i(\mathfrak V,f_*\mathcal F)\cong H^i(Y,f_*\mathcal F). \]

(Naturality in \(\mathcal F\) is clear since the Cech isomorphisms are natural in the sheaf.) ∎

Exercise 4.2

Problem

Prove Chevalley's theorem: Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a finite surjective morphism of noetherian separated schemes, with \(X\) affine. Then \(Y\) is affine.

  1. (a)

    Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a finite surjective morphism of integral noetherian schemes. Show that there is a coherent sheaf \(\mathcal M\) on \(X\), and a morphism of sheaves \(\alpha:\mathcal O_Y^r\to f_*\mathcal M\) for some \(r>0\), such that \(\alpha\) is an isomorphism at the generic point of \(Y\).

  2. (b)

    For any coherent sheaf \(\mathcal F\) on \(Y\), show that there is a coherent sheaf \(\mathcal G\) on \(X\), and a morphism \(\beta:f_*\mathcal G\to\mathcal F^r\) which is an isomorphism at the generic point of \(Y\). [Hint: Apply \(\mathcal Hom(\cdot,\mathcal F)\) to \(\alpha\) and use (II, Ex. 5.17e).]

  3. (c)

    Now prove Chevalley's theorem. First use (Ex. 3.1) and (Ex. 3.2) to reduce to the case \(X\) and \(Y\) integral. Then use (3.7), (Ex. 4.1), consider \(\ker\beta\) and \(\operatorname{coker}\beta\), and use noetherian induction on \(Y\).

Solution

(a) Let \(\eta\) and \(\xi\) be the generic points of \(Y\) and \(X\), \(K(Y)=\mathcal O_{Y,\eta}\), \(K(X)=\mathcal O_{X,\xi}\). Let \(V=\operatorname{Spec}A\subseteq Y\) be an affine open; \(f^{-1}V=\operatorname{Spec}B\) with \(B\) a domain which is a finite \(A\)-module, \(A\) a domain. Since \(f\) is surjective, \(A\to B\) is injective: if \(a\in\ker\), then \(a\) lies in every prime of \(A\) (every prime of \(A\) is the contraction of a prime of \(B\)), so \(a\in\Nil A=0\). Hence \(B\otimes_AK(Y)=B_{(A-0)}\) is a domain finite over the field \(K(Y)\), hence a field, and therefore equal to \(\operatorname{Frac}B=K(X)\). So \(K(X)\) is a finite extension of \(K(Y)\); let \(r=[K(X):K(Y)]\ge1\), and choose a basis \(s_1,\dots,s_r\) of \(K(X)\) over \(K(Y)\). For a quasi-coherent \(\mathcal M\) on \(X\), with \(N=\Gamma(f^{-1}V,\mathcal M)\) (a \(B\)-module), \((f_*\mathcal M)_\eta=N\otimes_AK(Y)=N\otimes_B(B\otimes_AK(Y))=N\otimes_BK(X)=\mathcal M_\xi\).

Let \(\mathcal K\) be the constant sheaf \(K(X)\) on \(X\); it is quasi-coherent (on an affine open \(\operatorname{Spec}B\) it is \(\widetilde{K(X)}\) with \(K(X)=\operatorname{Frac}B\), a \(B\)-module). Let \(\mathcal M\subseteq\mathcal K\) be the image of \(\mathcal O_X^r\to\mathcal K\), \(e_i\mapsto s_i\). It is quasi-coherent and finitely generated, hence coherent, and the \(s_i\) are global sections of \(\mathcal M\). Define \(\alpha:\mathcal O_Y^r\to f_*\mathcal M\) by \(e_i\mapsto s_i\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal M)=\Gamma(Y,f_*\mathcal M)\). At the generic point, \(\alpha_\eta:K(Y)^r\to(f_*\mathcal M)_\eta=\mathcal M_\xi=\sum K(X)s_i=K(X)\) sends \(e_i\mapsto s_i\), an isomorphism because \(s_1,\dots,s_r\) is a basis.

(b) The sheaf \(f_*\mathcal M\) is a coherent \(\mathcal O_Y\)-module (finite morphism), and a module over the sheaf of algebras \(\mathcal A=f_*\mathcal O_X\). Hence \(\mathcal Hom_{\mathcal O_Y}(f_*\mathcal M,\mathcal F)\) is a coherent \(\mathcal O_Y\)-module with a natural \(\mathcal A\)-module structure (\((a\cdot\varphi)(m)=\varphi(am)\)). By II, Ex. 5.17(e) (equivalence between quasi-coherent \(\mathcal O_X\)-modules and quasi-coherent \(\mathcal A\)-modules, via \(f_*\); \(f\) is affine, II Ex. 5.17(b)) there is a quasi-coherent \(\mathcal O_X\)-module \(\mathcal G\) with \(f_*\mathcal G\cong\mathcal Hom_{\mathcal O_Y}(f_*\mathcal M,\mathcal F)\) as \(\mathcal A\)-modules; \(\mathcal G\) is coherent since \(f_*\mathcal G\) is coherent over \(\mathcal O_Y\) (locally \(\mathcal G=\widetilde N\) with \(N\) a \(B\)-module finitely generated over \(A\), hence over \(B\)). Applying \(\mathcal Hom_{\mathcal O_Y}(\cdot,\mathcal F)\) to \(\alpha\) we get

\[ \beta:\ f_*\mathcal G\cong\mathcal Hom(f_*\mathcal M,\mathcal F)\xrightarrow{\ \alpha^*\ }\mathcal Hom(\mathcal O_Y^r,\mathcal F)=\mathcal F^r . \]

Since \(f_*\mathcal M\) is coherent, the stalk of \(\mathcal Hom(f_*\mathcal M,\mathcal F)\) at \(\eta\) is \(\Hom_{K(Y)}((f_*\mathcal M)_\eta,\mathcal F_\eta)\), and \(\beta_\eta=\Hom(\alpha_\eta,\mathcal F_\eta)\) is an isomorphism because \(\alpha_\eta\) is.

(c) Let \(f:X\to Y\) be finite and surjective, \(X\) affine, \(X,Y\) noetherian separated. Suppose \(Y\) is not affine; we derive a contradiction.

Reduction to a minimal counterexample. Among the closed subsets \(Z\subseteq Y\) such that \(Z\) (with its reduced structure) is not affine – a nonempty family, since \(Y\) belongs to it – choose a minimal one, \(Z_0\) (the space \(Y\) is noetherian). Let \(Y_0=Z_0\) with the reduced structure and \(X_0=(f^{-1}Z_0)_{\mathrm{red}}\), a closed subscheme of the affine \(X\), hence affine. Since \(f\) is surjective, \(f(f^{-1}Z_0)=Z_0\). The reduced scheme \(X_0\) maps into \(Y_0\) (a morphism from a reduced scheme to \(Y\) with set-theoretic image in the closed subset \(Z_0\) factors through \(Y_0\)), and \(X_0\to Y_0\) is finite (a closed subscheme of \(X\times_YY_0\), which is finite over \(Y_0\)) and surjective. So replacing \(f\) by \(X_0\to Y_0\) we may assume: \(Y\) is reduced, noetherian, separated, not affine, and every proper closed subset of \(Y\), with reduced structure, is affine; \(X\) is affine; \(f\) finite surjective.

\(Y\) is integral. If \(Y\) had several irreducible components, each would be a proper closed subset, hence affine, and then \(Y\) would be affine by Ex. 3.2: contradiction. So \(Y\) is irreducible, hence integral.

\(X\) may be taken integral. \(f(X)=Y\) is the finite union of the closed irreducible sets \(f(X_j)\) (\(X_j\) the irreducible components of \(X\); \(f\) is closed as it is finite), so \(Y=f(X_j)\) for some \(j\) (\(Y\) is irreducible). Replace \(X\) by \(X_j\) with the reduced structure: it is integral, affine (closed in \(X\)) and \(X_j\to Y\) is finite and surjective.

The cohomological argument. Now \(f:X\to Y\) is a finite surjective morphism of integral noetherian separated schemes, \(X\) affine, \(Y\) not affine, all proper closed subsets of \(Y\) (reduced structure) affine. Let \(\mathcal F\) be any coherent sheaf on \(Y\). By (b) there are \(r>0\), a coherent sheaf \(\mathcal G\) on \(X\) and \(\beta:f_*\mathcal G\to\mathcal F^r\) which is an isomorphism at the generic point \(\eta\). Let \(\mathcal K=\ker\beta\), \(\mathcal B=\operatorname{im}\beta\), \(\mathcal C=\operatorname{coker}\beta\); these are coherent, and

\[ 0\to\mathcal K\to f_*\mathcal G\to\mathcal B\to0,\qquad 0\to\mathcal B\to\mathcal F^r\to\mathcal C\to0 \]

are exact. As \(\mathcal K_\eta=0=\mathcal C_\eta\), the supports of \(\mathcal K\) and \(\mathcal C\) are closed subsets of \(Y\) not containing \(\eta\), so are proper closed subsets.

Claim: \(H^p(Y,\mathcal L)=0\) for \(p>0\) if \(\mathcal L\) is coherent with \(\operatorname{Supp}\mathcal L\ne Y\). Let \(\mathcal J=\operatorname{Ann}\mathcal L\), a coherent ideal sheaf with \(V(\mathcal J)=\operatorname{Supp}\mathcal L\), and \(Z'\) the closed subscheme defined by \(\mathcal J\). Its reduced scheme \(Z'_{\mathrm{red}}\) is the proper closed subset \(\operatorname{Supp}\mathcal L\) with reduced structure, hence affine by minimality. By Ex. 3.1, the noetherian scheme \(Z'\) is affine. Then \(\mathcal L=i_*\mathcal L'\) with \(\mathcal L'\) coherent on \(Z'\) (II, Ex. 5.10), and \(H^p(Y,\mathcal L)=H^p(Z',\mathcal L')=0\) for \(p>0\) by Lemma 2.10 and Thm. 3.5.

Now \(f\) is finite, hence affine, so \(H^p(Y,f_*\mathcal G)=H^p(X,\mathcal G)=0\) for \(p>0\) by Ex. 4.1 and Thm. 3.5 (\(X\) is affine). Thus: from the first sequence, \(H^1(Y,\mathcal B)\hookrightarrow H^2(Y,\mathcal K)=0\) (as \(H^1(f_*\mathcal G)=0\)), so \(H^1(Y,\mathcal B)=0\); from the second, \(H^1(Y,\mathcal B)\to H^1(Y,\mathcal F)^r\to H^1(Y,\mathcal C)=0\), so \(H^1(Y,\mathcal F)^r=0\) and \(H^1(Y,\mathcal F)=0\). This holds for every coherent \(\mathcal F\), in particular for every coherent ideal sheaf; by Serre's criterion (3.7), \(Y\) is affine. This contradicts the assumption. Hence \(Y\) is affine. ∎

Exercise 4.3

Problem

Let \(X=\mathbf A^2_k=\operatorname{Spec}k[x,y]\), and let \(U=X-\{(0,0)\}\). Using a suitable cover of \(U\) by open affine subsets, show that \(H^1(U,\mathcal O_U)\) is isomorphic to the \(k\)-vector space spanned by \(\{x^iy^j\mid i,j<0\}\). In particular, it is infinite-dimensional. (Using (3.5), this provides another proof that \(U\) is not affine — cf. (I, Ex. 3.6).)

Solution

Put \(A=k[x,y]\), \(U_1=D(x)\), \(U_2=D(y)\), with \(U_1\cup U_2=X-V(x,y)=U\). These are affine, \(U_1\cap U_2=D(xy)\) is affine, and \(U\) is noetherian and separated. By Thm. 4.5, \(H^i(U,\mathcal O_U)=\check H^i(\mathfrak U,\mathcal O_U)\) for \(\mathfrak U=(U_1,U_2)\). The Cech complex has only two nonzero terms:

\[ 0\to A_x\oplus A_y\xrightarrow{\ d\ }A_{xy}\to0,\qquad d(a,b)=b-a . \]

\(A_{xy}=k[x,y,x^{-1},y^{-1}]\) has \(k\)-basis the monomials \(x^iy^j\), \((i,j)\in\mathbf Z^2\); \(A_x\) is spanned by those with \(j\ge0\), \(A_y\) by those with \(i\ge0\). Hence \(\operatorname{im}d\) is spanned by the monomials with \(i\ge0\) or \(j\ge0\) and

\[ H^1(U,\mathcal O_U)=\operatorname{coker}d\cong\bigoplus_{i<0,\,j<0}k\cdot x^iy^j, \]

an infinite-dimensional vector space (\(H^0=\ker d=A\), and \(H^p=0\) for \(p\ge2\)). If \(U\) were affine, \(H^1(U,\mathcal O_U)=0\) by Thm. 3.5; hence \(U\) is not affine. ∎

Exercise 4.4

Problem

On an arbitrary topological space \(X\) with an arbitrary abelian sheaf \(\mathcal F\), Cech cohomology may not give the same result as the derived functor cohomology. But here we show that for \(H^1\), there is an isomorphism if one takes the limit over all coverings.

  1. (a)

    Let \(\mathfrak U=(U_i)_{i\in I}\) be an open covering of the topological space \(X\). A refinement of \(\mathfrak U\) is a covering \(\mathfrak V=(V_j)_{j\in J}\), together with a map \(\lambda:J\to I\) of the index sets, such that for each \(j\in J\), \(V_j\subseteq U_{\lambda(j)}\). If \(\mathfrak V\) is a refinement of \(\mathfrak U\), show that there is a natural induced map on Cech cohomology, for any abelian sheaf \(\mathcal F\), and for each \(i\),

    \[ \lambda^i:\check H^i(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\to\check H^i(\mathfrak V,\mathcal F). \]

    The coverings of \(X\) form a partially ordered set under refinement, so we can consider the Cech cohomology in the limit

    \[ \varinjlim_{\mathfrak U}\check H^i(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F). \]
  2. (b)

    For any abelian sheaf \(\mathcal F\) on \(X\), show that the natural maps (4.4) for each covering

    \[ \check H^i(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\to H^i(X,\mathcal F) \]

    are compatible with the refinement maps above.

  3. (c)
