    Now prove the following theorem. Let \(X\) be a topological space, \(\mathcal F\) a sheaf of abelian groups. Then the natural map

    \[ \varinjlim_{\mathfrak U}\check H^1(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\to H^1(X,\mathcal F) \]

    is an isomorphism. [Hint: Embed \(\mathcal F\) in a flasque sheaf \(\mathcal G\), and let \(\mathcal R=\mathcal G/\mathcal F\), so that we have an exact sequence \(0\to\mathcal F\to\mathcal G\to\mathcal R\to0\). Define a complex \(D^\bullet(\mathfrak U)\) by \(0\to C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\to C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\mathcal G)\to D^\bullet(\mathfrak U)\to0\). Then use the exact cohomology sequence of this sequence of complexes, and the natural map of complexes \(D^\bullet(\mathfrak U)\to C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\mathcal R)\), and see what happens under refinement.]

Solution

(a) The map. Let \(\mathfrak V=(V_j)\) refine \(\mathfrak U=(U_i)\) via \(\lambda\). Use alternating cochains (Remark 4.0.1) and define \(\lambda^\#:C^p(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\to C^p(\mathfrak V,\mathcal F)\) by

\[ (\lambda^\#\alpha)_{j_0\dots j_p}=\alpha_{\lambda(j_0)\dots\lambda(j_p)}\big|_{V_{j_0\dots j_p}} \]

(note \(V_{j_0\dots j_p}\subseteq U_{\lambda(j_0)\dots\lambda(j_p)}\)). This is again alternating (if two \(j\)'s coincide so do two \(\lambda(j)\)'s, and a transposition of the \(j\)'s transposes the \(\lambda(j)\)'s). It commutes with \(d\):

\[ (d\lambda^\#\alpha)_{j_0\dots j_{p+1}}=\sum_k(-1)^k\alpha_{\lambda(j_0)\dots\widehat{\lambda(j_k)}\dots\lambda(j_{p+1})}\Big|=(\lambda^\#d\alpha)_{j_0\dots j_{p+1}}, \]

using that the formula for \(d\) holds for arbitrary tuples. So \(\lambda^\#\) is a morphism of complexes, natural in \(\mathcal F\), and induces \(\lambda^i:\check H^i(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\to\check H^i(\mathfrak V,\mathcal F)\).

Independence of \(\lambda\). Let \(\mu:J\to I\) be another map with \(V_j\subseteq U_{\mu(j)}\). Then \(\lambda^i=\mu^i\). Indeed define \(h:C^p(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\to C^{p-1}(\mathfrak V,\mathcal F)\) by

\[ (h\alpha)_{j_0\dots j_{p-1}}=\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}(-1)^k\alpha_{\lambda(j_0)\dots\lambda(j_k)\mu(j_k)\dots\mu(j_{p-1})}\big|_{V_{j_0\dots j_{p-1}}} \]

(meaningful, since \(V_{j_0\dots j_{p-1}}\) lies in \(U_{\lambda j_0}\cap\dots\cap U_{\lambda j_k}\cap U_{\mu j_k}\cap\dots\cap U_{\mu j_{p-1}}\)). We define \(h\alpha\) by this formula for \(j_0<\dots<j_{p-1}\) and extend it to all tuples by alternation (the formula itself need not be alternating). To verify the identity below it suffices to take \(j_0<\dots<j_p\), since both sides are alternating cochains; then every sub-tuple of \((j_0,\dots,j_p)\) is increasing, so \(h\alpha\) is only evaluated on increasing tuples, while \(d\alpha\) may be evaluated on the non-increasing tuples \(w_k\), which is legitimate by Remark 4.0.1. We claim \(dh+hd=\mu^\#-\lambda^\#\). Fix \(j_0,\dots,j_p\) and put \(w_k=(\lambda j_0,\dots,\lambda j_k,\mu j_k,\dots,\mu j_p)\), \(0\le k\le p\) (a \((p+2)\)-tuple). Then

\[ (hd\alpha)_{j_0\dots j_p}=\sum_{k=0}^p(-1)^k(d\alpha)_{w_k}=\sum_{k=0}^p\sum_{l=0}^{p+1}(-1)^{k+l}\alpha_{w_k\setminus l}, \]

where \(w_k\setminus l\) omits the entry in position \(l\); and \((dh\alpha)_{j_0\dots j_p}=\sum_{l=0}^p(-1)^l(h\alpha)_{j_0\dots\widehat{j_l}\dots j_p}=\sum_{l=0}^p\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}(-1)^{l+k}\alpha_{t(l,k)}\), where \(t(l,k)\) is the tuple built from the list \(j_0,\dots,\widehat{j_l},\dots,j_p\) by applying \(\lambda\) to its first \(k+1\) entries and \(\mu\) to its entries number \(k,\dots,p-1\). Sort the terms of \(hd\alpha\) by the position \(l\) relative to \(k\) (in \(w_k\), the positions \(0,\dots,k\) hold \(\lambda j_0,\dots,\lambda j_k\) and positions \(k+1,\dots,p+1\) hold \(\mu j_k,\dots,\mu j_p\)).

  • •

    \(l=k+1\) (omit \(\mu j_k\)) and \(l=k\) (omit \(\lambda j_k\)): \(w_k\setminus(k+1)=(\lambda j_0,\dots,\lambda j_k,\mu j_{k+1},\dots,\mu j_p)=w_{k+1}\setminus(k+1)\), with signs \(-1\) and \(+1\) respectively, so for \(0\le k\le p-1\) the term \((k,k+1)\) cancels the term \((k+1,k+1)\). What is left of these two kinds are \((k,l)=(0,0)\), giving \(+\alpha_{\mu j_0\dots\mu j_p}\), and \((k,l)=(p,p+1)\), giving \(-\alpha_{\lambda j_0\dots\lambda j_p}\).

  • •

    \(l<k\) (omit \(\lambda j_l\)): \(w_k\setminus l=(\lambda j_0,..,\widehat{\lambda j_l},..,\lambda j_k,\mu j_k,\dots,\mu j_p)=t(l,k-1)\) (here \(l\le k-1\), so the \((k-1)\)-st entry of the shortened list is \(j_k\)), and the sign \((-1)^{k+l}\) is opposite to \((-1)^{l+k-1}\). So these cancel exactly the terms of \(dh\alpha\) with inner index \(k'\ge l\).

  • •

    \(l\ge k+2\) (omit \(\mu j_{l-1}\)): \(w_k\setminus l=(\lambda j_0,\dots,\lambda j_k,\mu j_k,..,\widehat{\mu j_{l-1}},..,\mu j_p)=t(l-1,k)\) (inner index \(k<l-1\)), with sign \((-1)^{k+l}\) opposite to \((-1)^{(l-1)+k}\). So these cancel exactly the terms of \(dh\alpha\) with inner index \(k'<\) omitted index.

Every term of \(dh\alpha\) is matched exactly once (a pair \((l',k')\) has either \(k'\ge l'\) or \(k'<l'\)). The only terms left are those displayed in the first item. Hence \(dh+hd=\mu^\#-\lambda^\#\), so \(\lambda^i=\mu^i\) on cohomology. Consequently the maps \(\lambda^i\) depend only on the refinement relation, they compose (\((\lambda\mu')^\#=\mu'^\#\lambda^\#\)), and since any two coverings have a common refinement (\((U_i\cap U'_{i'})_{(i,i')}\)), the groups \(\check H^i(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\) form a direct system over the directed set of coverings, whose limit \(\varinjlim_{\mathfrak U}\check H^i(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\) is what is considered. (To avoid set-theoretic problems one may restrict to coverings indexed by subsets of the power set of \(X\).)

(b) Choose an injective resolution \(\mathcal F\to\mathcal I^\bullet\) in \(\mathfrak{Ab}(X)\). The natural map of (4.4) is obtained as follows: by Lemma 4.2, \(\mathcal C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\) is a resolution of \(\mathcal F\), so there is a morphism of complexes \(\theta_{\mathfrak U}:\mathcal C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\to\mathcal I^\bullet\) over \(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal F}\), unique up to homotopy; one applies \(\Gamma(X,\cdot)\) and takes \(h^p\), using \(\Gamma(X,\mathcal C^p(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F))=C^p(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\). Let \(\mathfrak V\) refine \(\mathfrak U\) via \(\lambda\). The formula for \(\lambda^\#\) defines a morphism of sheaf complexes \(\lambda^\#:\mathcal C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\to\mathcal C^\bullet(\mathfrak V,\mathcal F)\) (on an open \(W\): \((\lambda^\#\alpha)_{j_0\dots j_p}=\alpha_{\lambda j_0\dots\lambda j_p}|_{W\cap V_{j_0\dots j_p}}\), the same computation as in (a)), which commutes with the augmentations \(\varepsilon:\mathcal F\to\mathcal C^0\), since \(\varepsilon(s)_j=s|_{V_j}\) and \((\lambda^\#\varepsilon s)_j=s|_{U_{\lambda j}}|_{V_j}=s|_{V_j}\). Therefore \(\theta_{\mathfrak V}\circ\lambda^\#\) and \(\theta_{\mathfrak U}\) are two morphisms of complexes from the resolution \(\mathcal C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\) of \(\mathcal F\) to the injective resolution \(\mathcal I^\bullet\) lying over \(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal F}\); hence they are homotopic (Hilton–Stammbach IV.4.4). Applying \(\Gamma(X,\cdot)\) (and \(\Gamma(X,\lambda^\#)=\lambda^\#\) on Cech cochains) we get that on cohomology the composite \(\check H^i(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\xrightarrow{\lambda^i}\check H^i(\mathfrak V,\mathcal F)\to H^i(X,\mathcal F)\) equals the natural map for \(\mathfrak U\). This is the compatibility. In particular there is a well-defined map \(\varinjlim_{\mathfrak U}\check H^i(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\to H^i(X,\mathcal F)\).

(c) Embed \(\mathcal F\hookrightarrow\mathcal G\) with \(\mathcal G\) flasque (e.g. injective; Lemma 2.4) and put \(\mathcal R=\mathcal G/\mathcal F\). Fix a covering \(\mathfrak U\). The maps \(C^p(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\to C^p(\mathfrak U,\mathcal G)\) are injective (products of the injections \(\mathcal F(W)\hookrightarrow\mathcal G(W)\)), so with \(D^p=D^p(\mathfrak U)=C^p(\mathfrak U,\mathcal G)/C^p(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)=\prod\mathcal G(U_{i_0\dots i_p})/\mathcal F(U_{i_0\dots i_p})\) there is a short exact sequence of complexes

\[ 0\to C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\to C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\mathcal G)\to D^\bullet(\mathfrak U)\to0 . \]

Since \(\mathcal G(W)/\mathcal F(W)\hookrightarrow\mathcal R(W)\) (left exactness of sections), \(D^p\hookrightarrow C^p(\mathfrak U,\mathcal R)\), and this is a morphism (injective) of complexes. Because \(D^1\to C^1(\mathfrak U,\mathcal R)\) is injective,

\[ H^0(D^\bullet(\mathfrak U))=\ker(D^0\to D^1)=D^0\cap\ker(d:C^0(\mathfrak U,\mathcal R)\to C^1(\mathfrak U,\mathcal R))=\{t\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal R)\mid t|_{U_i}\in\operatorname{im}(\mathcal G(U_i)\to\mathcal R(U_i))\ \forall i\}=:\Gamma_{\mathfrak U}, \]

using Lemma 4.1 (\(\ker d=\Gamma(X,\mathcal R)\)). So \(j:H^0(D^\bullet(\mathfrak U))\hookrightarrow\Gamma(X,\mathcal R)\) is injective with image \(\Gamma_{\mathfrak U}\). By Lemma 4.1, \(\check H^0(\mathfrak U,\mathcal G)=\Gamma(X,\mathcal G)\) and, as \(\mathcal G\) is flasque, \(\check H^1(\mathfrak U,\mathcal G)=0\) (Prop. 4.3). The long exact sequence of the sequence of complexes therefore reads

\[ \Gamma(X,\mathcal G)\to H^0(D^\bullet(\mathfrak U))\xrightarrow{\ \delta_{\check C}\ }\check H^1(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\to0, \]

exact, where the first map followed by \(j\) is the map \(\Gamma(X,\mathcal G)\to\Gamma(X,\mathcal R)\). On the other side, the sequence \(0\to\mathcal F\to\mathcal G\to\mathcal R\to0\) and \(H^1(X,\mathcal G)=0\) (Prop. 2.5) give an exact sequence

\[ \Gamma(X,\mathcal G)\to\Gamma(X,\mathcal R)\xrightarrow{\ \delta\ }H^1(X,\mathcal F)\to0 . \]

Claim: \(\theta_{\mathfrak U}\circ\delta_{\check C}=\delta\circ j\), where \(\theta_{\mathfrak U}:\check H^1(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\to H^1(X,\mathcal F)\) is the natural map. Granting it, we have a commutative diagram with exact rows

\[ \begin{array}{ccccccc} \Gamma(X,\mathcal G)&\to&H^0(D^\bullet(\mathfrak U))&\xrightarrow{\delta_{\check C}}&\check H^1(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)&\to&0\\ \Vert&&\downarrow j&&\downarrow\theta_{\mathfrak U}&&\\ \Gamma(X,\mathcal G)&\to&\Gamma(X,\mathcal R)&\xrightarrow{\ \delta\ }&H^1(X,\mathcal F)&\to&0 \end{array} \]

Proof of the claim. Take injective resolutions \(\mathcal I_F^\bullet\) of \(\mathcal F\) and \(\mathcal I_R^\bullet\) of \(\mathcal R\); by the horseshoe lemma there is an injective resolution \(\mathcal I_G^\bullet=\mathcal I_F^\bullet\oplus\mathcal I_R^\bullet\) of \(\mathcal G\) with \(0\to\mathcal I_F^p\to\mathcal I_G^p\to\mathcal I_R^p\to0\) split exact and compatible with \(0\to\mathcal F\to\mathcal G\to\mathcal R\to0\). After applying \(\Gamma\) one still has short exact sequences, and the connecting homomorphism \(H^0(\Gamma(\mathcal I_R^\bullet))=\Gamma(X,\mathcal R)\to H^1(\Gamma(\mathcal I_F^\bullet))=H^1(X,\mathcal F)\) is \(\delta\). Choose \(\theta_F:\mathcal C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\to\mathcal I_F^\bullet\) as in 4.4 and let \(\iota:\mathcal I_F^\bullet\to\mathcal I_G^\bullet\). Choose any morphism \(\theta_G:\mathcal C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\mathcal G)\to\mathcal I_G^\bullet\) over \(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal G}\). The two morphisms \(\theta_G|_{\mathcal C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)}\) and \(\iota\theta_F\) from the resolution \(\mathcal C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\) of \(\mathcal F\) to the injective resolution \(\mathcal I_G^\bullet\) of \(\mathcal G\) lie over \(\mathcal F\to\mathcal G\), so they are homotopic, by some \(s^p:\mathcal C^p(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\to\mathcal I_G^{p-1}\) with \(\theta_G|_{\mathcal C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)}-\iota\theta_F=ds+sd\). As \(\mathcal I_G^{p-1}\) is injective and \(\mathcal C^p(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\to\mathcal C^p(\mathfrak U,\mathcal G)\) is a monomorphism, \(s\) extends to \(\tilde s^p:\mathcal C^p(\mathfrak U,\mathcal G)\to\mathcal I_G^{p-1}\); replacing \(\theta_G\) by \(\theta_G-d\tilde s-\tilde sd\) (still a morphism of complexes, and still over \(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal G}\): \(\tilde s^0=0\), and \(d\circ\varepsilon=0\) for the augmentation \(\varepsilon:\mathcal G\to\mathcal C^0(\mathfrak U,\mathcal G)\)) we may assume \(\theta_G|_{\mathcal C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)}=\iota\theta_F\). Applying \(\Gamma\) gives a morphism of short exact sequences of complexes

\[ \begin{array}{ccccccccc} 0&\to&C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)&\to&C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\mathcal G)&\to&D^\bullet(\mathfrak U)&\to&0\\ &&\downarrow\theta_F&&\downarrow\theta_G&&\downarrow\bar\theta&&\\ 0&\to&\Gamma(\mathcal I_F^\bullet)&\to&\Gamma(\mathcal I_G^\bullet)&\to&\Gamma(\mathcal I_R^\bullet)&\to&0 \end{array} \]

where \(\bar\theta\) is induced on quotients. By naturality of connecting homomorphisms, \(\delta\circ H^0(\bar\theta)=H^1(\theta_F)\circ\delta_{\check C}=\theta_{\mathfrak U}\circ\delta_{\check C}\). It remains to see \(H^0(\bar\theta)=j\) (identifying \(H^0(\Gamma(\mathcal I_R^\bullet))=\Gamma(X,\mathcal R)\) via the augmentation \(\mathcal R\to\mathcal I_R^0\)). Let \(z\in H^0(D^\bullet)\) be represented by \((g_i)\in\prod\mathcal G(U_i)\), with \(t_i\in\mathcal R(U_i)\) the image of \(g_i\) and \(t\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal R)\) the section glued from the \(t_i\), so \(j(z)=t\). Let \(g=(g_i)\in C^0(\mathfrak U,\mathcal G)\) and \(t'=\theta_G^0(g)\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal I_G^0)\); then \(H^0(\bar\theta)(z)\) is the image of \(t'\) in \(\Gamma(\mathcal I_R^0)\). On \(U_i\): \(g|_{U_i}=(g_k|_{U_{ik}})_k\) differs from \(\varepsilon(g_i)=(g_i|_{U_{ik}})_k\) by \(((g_k-g_i)|_{U_{ik}})_k\in\mathcal C^0(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)(U_i)\) (because \(g_k-g_i\mapsto t_k-t_i=0\) on \(U_{ik}\)). Since \(\theta_G^0\) maps \(\mathcal C^0(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\) into \(\mathcal I_F^0\) and \(\theta_G^0\varepsilon=\eta_G\) (the augmentation of \(\mathcal I_G^\bullet\)), \(t'|_{U_i}\equiv\eta_G(g_i)\pmod{\mathcal I_F^0(U_i)}\), so the image of \(t'\) in \(\mathcal I_R^0(U_i)\) is \(\eta_R(t_i)=\eta_R(t)|_{U_i}\). Hence the image of \(t'\) in \(\Gamma(\mathcal I_R^0)\) is \(\eta_R(t)\), i.e. \(H^0(\bar\theta)(z)=t=j(z)\). This proves the claim.

Conclusion. For every covering \(\mathfrak U\), \(\theta_{\mathfrak U}\) is injective: if \(c=\delta_{\check C}(z)\) and \(\theta_{\mathfrak U}(c)=\delta(j(z))=0\), then \(j(z)\) is the image of some \(g\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal G)\); the image \(z'\) of \(g\) in \(H^0(D^\bullet(\mathfrak U))\) has \(j(z')=j(z)\), hence \(z'=z\) (\(j\) injective), and \(c=\delta_{\check C}(z')=0\) by exactness of the top row. As a direct limit of injective maps (over a directed set, compatibly by (b)) is injective, \(\varinjlim_{\mathfrak U}\check H^1(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\to H^1(X,\mathcal F)\) is injective. It is surjective: let \(c'\in H^1(X,\mathcal F)\); by the bottom row \(c'=\delta(t)\) for some \(t\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal R)\). Since \(\mathcal G\to\mathcal R\) is a surjection of sheaves, there is an open covering \(\mathfrak U_t\) on which \(t\) lifts locally: \(t|_{U_i}\in\operatorname{im}(\mathcal G(U_i)\to\mathcal R(U_i))\). Then \(t\in\Gamma_{\mathfrak U_t}=j(H^0(D^\bullet(\mathfrak U_t)))\), say \(t=j(z)\), and \(c'=\delta(j(z))=\theta_{\mathfrak U_t}(\delta_{\check C}z)\) lies in the image of \(\theta_{\mathfrak U_t}\). Hence the limit map is an isomorphism. ∎

Exercise 4.5

Problem

For any ringed space \((X,\mathcal O_X)\), let \(\operatorname{Pic}X\) be the group of isomorphism classes of invertible sheaves on \(X\) (II, Section 6). Show that \(\operatorname{Pic}X\cong H^1(X,\mathcal O_X^*)\), where \(\mathcal O_X^*\) denotes the sheaf whose sections over an open set \(U\) are the units in the ring \(\Gamma(U,\mathcal O_X)\), with multiplication as the group operation. [Hint: For any invertible sheaf \(\mathcal L\) on \(X\), cover \(X\) by open sets \(U_i\) on which \(\mathcal L\) is free, and fix isomorphisms \(\varphi_i:\mathcal O_{U_i}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal L|_{U_i}\). Then on \(U_i\cap U_j\), we get an isomorphism \(\varphi_i^{-1}\circ\varphi_j\) of \(\mathcal O_{U_i\cap U_j}\) with itself. These isomorphisms give an element of \(\check H^1(\mathfrak U,\mathcal O_X^*)\). Now use (Ex. 4.4).]

Solution

Write the group law of \(\mathcal O_X^*\) multiplicatively; its Cech complex \(C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\mathcal O_X^*)\) then has \((d\alpha)_{ijk}=\alpha_{jk}\alpha_{ik}^{-1}\alpha_{ij}\) for \(p=1\) and \((d\beta)_{ij}=\beta_j\beta_i^{-1}\) for \(p=0\). Note that an automorphism of the \(\mathcal O_W\)-module \(\mathcal O_W\) is multiplication by a unit of \(\Gamma(W,\mathcal O_X)\), i.e. \(\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal O_W)=\Gamma(W,\mathcal O_X^*)\).

The map. Let \(\mathcal L\) be invertible, \(\mathfrak U=(U_i)\) a covering with isomorphisms \(\varphi_i:\mathcal O_{U_i}\to\mathcal L|_{U_i}\) (a “trivialization”). Define \(g_{ij}=\varphi_i^{-1}\varphi_j\in\Gamma(U_{ij},\mathcal O_X^*)\). Then \(g_{ij}g_{jk}=\varphi_i^{-1}\varphi_j\varphi_j^{-1}\varphi_k=g_{ik}\) on \(U_{ijk}\), so, putting \(\alpha_{ij}=g_{ij}\) for \(i<j\), we have \((d\alpha)_{ijk}=g_{jk}g_{ik}^{-1}g_{ij}=1\): \(\alpha\) is a cocycle (and \(g_{ji}=g_{ij}^{-1}\), \(g_{ii}=1\), consistent with Remark 4.0.1). If we change the trivializations to \(\varphi_i'=\varphi_iu_i\), \(u_i\in\Gamma(U_i,\mathcal O_X^*)\), then \(g'_{ij}=u_i^{-1}g_{ij}u_j=g_{ij}\cdot(du)_{ij}\), so the class \([\alpha]\in\check H^1(\mathfrak U,\mathcal O_X^*)\) is independent of the choice of the \(\varphi_i\) for fixed \(\mathfrak U\). If \(\mathfrak V\) refines \(\mathfrak U\) via \(\lambda\), the trivializations \(\varphi_{\lambda(j)}|_{V_j}\) of \(\mathcal L\) on \(\mathfrak V\) give the cocycle \(\lambda^\#\alpha\); any trivializing covering has a common refinement with any other (take \((U_i\cap U'_{i'})\), trivialized by restriction), and the class in \(\check H^1(\mathfrak V,\mathcal O_X^*)\) is independent of trivialization. Thus \(\mathcal L\) determines a well-defined element \(c(\mathcal L)\) of \(\varinjlim_{\mathfrak U}\check H^1(\mathfrak U,\mathcal O_X^*)\), which equals \(H^1(X,\mathcal O_X^*)\) by Ex. 4.4(c) (\(\mathcal O_X^*\) is a sheaf of abelian groups on the topological space \(X\)). If \(\psi:\mathcal L\to\mathcal L'\) is an isomorphism, then \(\psi\varphi_i\) are trivializations of \(\mathcal L'\) with the same \(g_{ij}\); so \(c\) is defined on \(\operatorname{Pic}X\).

Homomorphism. If \(\mathcal L,\mathcal L'\) are trivialized by \(\varphi_i,\varphi'_i\) on the same covering (use a common refinement), then \(\varphi_i\otimes\varphi'_i:\mathcal O_{U_i}=\mathcal O\otimes\mathcal O\to(\mathcal L\otimes\mathcal L')|_{U_i}\) has transition functions \(g_{ij}g'_{ij}\). So \(c(\mathcal L\otimes\mathcal L')=c(\mathcal L)c(\mathcal L')\).

Injective. Suppose \(c(\mathcal L)=0\). Then for some covering \(\mathfrak U\) with trivializations \(\varphi_i\) the cocycle \(\alpha=(g_{ij})\) is a coboundary: there are \(u_i\in\Gamma(U_i,\mathcal O_X^*)\) with \(g_{ij}=u_ju_i^{-1}\) (after replacing the covering by a refinement, using the definition of the direct limit). Put \(\varphi_i'=\varphi_i\circ(u_i^{-1})\). Then \(\varphi_i'^{-1}\varphi_j'=u_ig_{ij}u_j^{-1}=1\), i.e. \(\varphi_i'=\varphi_j'\) on \(U_{ij}\). By the sheaf property of \(\mathcal Hom(\mathcal O_X,\mathcal L)\) the \(\varphi_i'\) glue to a global isomorphism \(\mathcal O_X\to\mathcal L\). So \(\mathcal L\cong\mathcal O_X\).

Surjective. Let \(\xi\in H^1(X,\mathcal O_X^*)\). By Ex. 4.4(c) it comes from some \(\check H^1(\mathfrak U,\mathcal O_X^*)\), i.e. is represented by a cocycle \(\alpha=(\alpha_{ij})_{i<j}\), \(\alpha_{ij}\in\Gamma(U_{ij},\mathcal O_X^*)\), with \(\alpha_{jk}\alpha_{ik}^{-1}\alpha_{ij}=1\) for \(i<j<k\). Extend alternatingly (\(\alpha_{ii}=1\), \(\alpha_{ji}=\alpha_{ij}^{-1}\)); then \(\alpha_{ij}\alpha_{jk}=\alpha_{ik}\) for all \(i,j,k\) (Remark 4.0.1). Glue the sheaves \(\mathcal O_{U_i}\) along the isomorphisms \(\psi_{ij}:\mathcal O_{U_j}|_{U_{ij}}\to\mathcal O_{U_i}|_{U_{ij}}\), “multiplication by \(\alpha_{ij}\)”: \(\psi_{ii}=\mathrm{id}\) and \(\psi_{ik}=\psi_{ij}\psi_{jk}\) on \(U_{ijk}\). By the gluing of sheaves (II, Ex. 1.22) there is a sheaf of \(\mathcal O_X\)-modules \(\mathcal L\) with isomorphisms \(\varphi_i:\mathcal O_{U_i}\to\mathcal L|_{U_i}\) such that \(\varphi_i^{-1}\varphi_j=\psi_{ij}\). Then \(\mathcal L\) is invertible with \(c(\mathcal L)=[\alpha]=\xi\).

Thus \(c:\operatorname{Pic}X\to H^1(X,\mathcal O_X^*)\) is an isomorphism of groups. ∎

Exercise 4.6

Problem

Let \((X,\mathcal O_X)\) be a ringed space, let \(\mathcal I\) be a sheaf of ideals with \(\mathcal I^2=0\), and let \(X_0\) be the ringed space \((X,\mathcal O_X/\mathcal I)\). Show that there is an exact sequence of sheaves of abelian groups on \(X\),

\[ 0\to\mathcal I\to\mathcal O_X^*\to\mathcal O_{X_0}^*\to0, \]

where \(\mathcal O_X^*\) (respectively \(\mathcal O_{X_0}^*\)) denotes the sheaf of (multiplicative) groups of units in the sheaf of rings \(\mathcal O_X\) (respectively \(\mathcal O_{X_0}\)); the map \(\mathcal I\to\mathcal O_X^*\) is defined by \(a\mapsto1+a\), and \(\mathcal I\) has its usual (additive) group structure. Conclude there is an exact sequence of abelian groups

\[ \dots\to H^1(X,\mathcal I)\to\operatorname{Pic}X\to\operatorname{Pic}X_0\to H^2(X,\mathcal I)\to\dots. \]
Solution

The map \(\mathcal I\to\mathcal O_X^*\). For a local section \(a\) of \(\mathcal I\), \((1+a)(1-a)=1-a^2=1\), so \(1+a\) is a unit. And \((1+a)(1+b)=1+a+b+ab=1+a+b\) since \(ab\in\mathcal I^2=0\). So \(a\mapsto1+a\) is a homomorphism of sheaves of groups \(\mathcal I\to\mathcal O_X^*\), clearly injective.

The map \(\mathcal O_X^*\to\mathcal O_{X_0}^*\) is induced by the quotient map \(\pi:\mathcal O_X\to\mathcal O_X/\mathcal I\), a ring homomorphism, and it is a group homomorphism. Its kernel consists of the units \(u\) with \(\pi(u)=1\), i.e. \(u-1\in\mathcal I\), i.e. \(u=1+a\) with \(a\in\mathcal I\); this is the image of \(\mathcal I\). So the sequence is exact at \(\mathcal I\) and at \(\mathcal O_X^*\).

Surjectivity of \(\mathcal O_X^*\to\mathcal O_{X_0}^*\) (as sheaves): it suffices to check on stalks. The stalk of \(\mathcal O_{X_0}^*\) at \(x\) is the group of units of \(\mathcal O_{X,x}/\mathcal I_x\) (a germ of a section is a unit iff it is invertible in the stalk, since an inverse of the germ is defined near \(x\) and is an inverse on a neighbourhood). Let \(\bar u\) be such a unit with inverse \(\bar v\), and lift to \(u,v\in\mathcal O_{X,x}\). Then \(uv=1+a\) with \(a\in\mathcal I_x\), and \(1+a\) is a unit with inverse \(1-a\). So \(u\cdot v(1-a)=1\), and \(u\) is a unit of \(\mathcal O_{X,x}\) mapping to \(\bar u\).

The cohomology sequence. The long exact cohomology sequence of this short exact sequence of sheaves of abelian groups on \(X\) reads

\[ \dots\to H^1(X,\mathcal I)\to H^1(X,\mathcal O_X^*)\to H^1(X,\mathcal O_{X_0}^*)\to H^2(X,\mathcal I)\to\dots \]

(\(X_0\) has the same underlying space.) By Ex. 4.5 applied to the ringed spaces \(X\) and \(X_0\), \(H^1(X,\mathcal O_X^*)\cong\operatorname{Pic}X\) and \(H^1(X,\mathcal O_{X_0}^*)\cong\operatorname{Pic}X_0\), which gives the desired sequence (it can be continued to the left by \(H^0\)-terms). ∎

Exercise 4.7

Problem

Let \(X\) be a subscheme of \(\mathbf P^2_k\) defined by a single homogeneous equation \(f(x_0,x_1,x_2)=0\) of degree \(d\). (Do not assume \(f\) is irreducible.) Assume that the point \((1,0,0)\) is not on \(X\). Then show that \(X\) can be covered by the two open affine subsets \(U=X\cap\{x_1\neq0\}\) and \(V=X\cap\{x_2\neq0\}\). Now calculate the Cech complex

\[ \Gamma(U,\mathcal O_X)\oplus\Gamma(V,\mathcal O_X)\to\Gamma(U\cap V,\mathcal O_X) \]

explicitly, and thus show that

\[ \dim_kH^0(X,\mathcal O_X)=1,\qquad\dim_kH^1(X,\mathcal O_X)=\tfrac12(d-1)(d-2). \]
Solution

Here \(k\) is a field (arbitrary), \(S=k[x_0,x_1,x_2]\), \(T=S/(f)\), \(X=\operatorname{Proj}T\), and \(d\ge1\).

Covering. The closed subset \(\{x_1=x_2=0\}\) of \(\mathbf P^2_k\) is the single rational point \((1,0,0)\). It is not on \(X\), so \(X\cap\{x_1=x_2=0\}=\emptyset\) and \(X=U\cup V\), where \(U=D_+(x_1)\cap X=\operatorname{Spec}(T_{x_1})_0\) and \(V=D_+(x_2)\cap X=\operatorname{Spec}(T_{x_2})_0\) are affine; \(U\cap V=\operatorname{Spec}(T_{x_1x_2})_0\). Also \(X\) is noetherian and separated (closed in \(\mathbf P^2\)), so Thm. 4.5 gives \(H^i(X,\mathcal O_X)=\check H^i(\mathfrak U,\mathcal O_X)\) for \(\mathfrak U=(U,V)\).

Structure of \(T\). Since \(f(1,0,0)\ne0\), the coefficient of \(x_0^d\) in \(f\) is a nonzero constant; scaling, \(f\) is monic of degree \(d\) in \(x_0\) over \(k[x_1,x_2]\). Hence \(T=k[x_1,x_2][x_0]/(f)\) is a free \(k[x_1,x_2]\)-module with basis \(1,x_0,\dots,x_0^{d-1}\), and these elements are homogeneous. Localizing, \(T_{x_1x_2}\) is free over \(k[x_1^{\pm1},x_2^{\pm1}]\), \(T_{x_1}\) free over \(k[x_1^{\pm1},x_2]\), \(T_{x_2}\) free over \(k[x_1,x_2^{\pm1}]\), all with the same basis \(x_0^m\) (\(0\le m\le d-1\)); the maps \(T_{x_1}\to T_{x_1x_2}\leftarrow T_{x_2}\) are injective, the inclusions of these free modules coefficientwise. An element of degree \(0\) is \(\sum_mx_0^mr_m\) with \(r_m\) homogeneous of degree \(-m\) in the Laurent ring. A \(k\)-basis of the degree-\(0\) parts is therefore:

  • •

    \(\Gamma(U,\mathcal O_X)=(T_{x_1})_0\): the elements \(x_0^mx_1^px_2^q\) with \(0\le m\le d-1\), \(p\in\mathbf Z\), \(q\ge0\), \(p+q=-m\);

  • •

    \(\Gamma(V,\mathcal O_X)=(T_{x_2})_0\): the elements \(x_0^mx_1^px_2^q\) with \(p\ge0\), \(q\in\mathbf Z\), \(p+q=-m\);

  • •

    \(\Gamma(U\cap V,\mathcal O_X)=(T_{x_1x_2})_0\): the elements \(x_0^mx_1^px_2^q\) with \(p,q\in\mathbf Z\), \(p+q=-m\).

The Cech differential is \((a,b)\mapsto b-a\), so its image is spanned by the basis elements with \(q\ge0\) or \(p\ge0\), and its kernel (the elements \(a=b\)) by the basis elements common to \(U\) and \(V\), i.e. \(p,q\ge0\) and \(p+q=-m\le0\), which forces \(m=p=q=0\).

Conclusion. \(H^0(X,\mathcal O_X)=\ker d=k\cdot1\), of dimension \(1\). And \(H^1(X,\mathcal O_X)=\operatorname{coker}d\) has basis \(x_0^mx_1^{-s}x_2^{-t}\) with \(0\le m\le d-1\), \(s,t\ge1\), \(s+t=m\). For given \(m\) there are \(m-1\) pairs \((s,t)\) if \(m\ge2\), and none if \(m\le1\). So

\[ \dim_kH^1(X,\mathcal O_X)=\sum_{m=2}^{d-1}(m-1)=\frac{(d-2)(d-1)}2, \]

(which is \(0\) for \(d=1,2\)). ∎

Exercise 4.8

Problem

Cohomological Dimension (Hartshorne [3]). Let \(X\) be a noetherian separated scheme. We define the cohomological dimension of \(X\), denoted \(\operatorname{cd}(X)\), to be the least integer \(n\) such that \(H^i(X,\mathcal F)=0\) for all quasi-coherent sheaves \(\mathcal F\) and all \(i>n\). Thus for example, Serre's theorem (3.7) says that \(\operatorname{cd}(X)=0\) if and only if \(X\) is affine. Grothendieck's theorem (2.7) implies that \(\operatorname{cd}(X)\le\dim X\).

  1. (a)

    In the definition of \(\operatorname{cd}(X)\), show that it is sufficient to consider only coherent sheaves \(\mathcal F\). Use (II, Ex. 5.15) and (2.9).

  2. (b)

    If \(X\) is quasi-projective over a field \(k\), then it is even sufficient to consider only locally free coherent sheaves \(\mathcal F\) on \(X\). Use (II, 5.18).

  3. (c)

    Suppose \(X\) has a covering by \(r+1\) open affine subsets. Use Cech cohomology to show that \(\operatorname{cd}(X)\le r\).

  4. (d)

    If \(X\) is a quasi-projective scheme of dimension \(r\) over a field \(k\), then \(X\) can be covered by \(r+1\) open affine subsets. Conclude (independently of (2.7)) that \(\operatorname{cd}(X)\le\dim X\). (Starred in the book.)

  5. (e)

    Let \(Y\) be a set-theoretic complete intersection (I, Ex. 2.17) of codimension \(r\) in \(X=\mathbf P^n_k\). Show that \(\operatorname{cd}(X-Y)\le r-1\).

Solution

(a) Suppose \(n\ge0\) is such that \(H^i(X,\mathcal F)=0\) for all coherent \(\mathcal F\) and all \(i>n\); we show the same for quasi-coherent \(\mathcal F\). By II, Ex. 5.15(e), \(\mathcal F\) is the union of its coherent subsheaves \(\mathcal F_\alpha\); this family is directed under inclusion (the sum of two coherent subsheaves is a coherent subsheaf), so \(\mathcal F=\varinjlim\mathcal F_\alpha\). As \(X\) is a noetherian topological space, cohomology commutes with direct limits (2.9), so \(H^i(X,\mathcal F)=\varinjlim H^i(X,\mathcal F_\alpha)=0\) for \(i>n\). Hence \(\operatorname{cd}(X)\) is the least \(n\) with vanishing for coherent sheaves.

(b) Let \(X\) be quasi-projective over \(k\), with \(\mathcal O_X(1)\) very ample; \(N=\dim X<\infty\). Suppose \(H^i(X,\mathcal E)=0\) for all locally free coherent \(\mathcal E\) and all \(i>n\). We prove by descending induction on \(i\) that \(H^j(X,\mathcal F)=0\) for all coherent \(\mathcal F\) and all \(j\ge i\), for each \(i\ge n+1\). For \(i=N+1\) this is Grothendieck vanishing (2.7). Suppose it holds for \(i+1\), where \(n+1\le i\le N\). Given coherent \(\mathcal F\), by II, 5.18 there is a surjection \(\mathcal E\to\mathcal F\) with \(\mathcal E=\bigoplus\mathcal O_X(-m_l)\) (finite sum), locally free; let \(\mathcal G\) be its kernel (coherent). The exact sequence \(H^i(X,\mathcal E)\to H^i(X,\mathcal F)\to H^{i+1}(X,\mathcal G)\) has outer terms \(0\) (the first since \(i>n\), the second by induction), so \(H^i(X,\mathcal F)=0\). For \(i=n+1\) we get the claim; with (a), \(\operatorname{cd}(X)\le n\).

(c) Let \(X=U_0\cup\dots\cup U_r\) with \(U_l\) open affine. Since \(X\) is noetherian and separated, by Thm. 4.5 for any quasi-coherent \(\mathcal F\), \(H^p(X,\mathcal F)=\check H^p(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\) with \(\mathfrak U=(U_0,\dots,U_r)\). The Cech complex \(C^p(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)=\prod_{i_0<\dots<i_p}\mathcal F(U_{i_0\dots i_p})\) is zero for \(p>r\) (there are only \(r+1\) indices). So \(H^p(X,\mathcal F)=0\) for \(p>r\), i.e. \(\operatorname{cd}(X)\le r\).

(d) Let \(X\) be an open subscheme of a closed subscheme \(P\) of \(\mathbf P^n_k\), of dimension \(r\), \(X\neq\emptyset\) (this is what quasi-projective means); let \(\bar X\) be the closure of \(X\) in \(\mathbf P^n_k\) (a closed subset; the construction below only uses underlying sets until the last paragraph), so \(X\) is open in \(\bar X\), \(\dim\bar X=r\) (each irreducible component of \(\bar X\) meets the dense open subset \(X\) in a dense open subset of equal dimension), and \(Z=\bar X-X\) is closed. Let \(S=k[x_0,\dots,x_n]\) and let \(J\subseteq S\) be the ideal generated by all homogeneous \(f\) of positive degree with \(Z\subseteq V_+(f)\). Then \(V_+(J)=Z\): “\(\supseteq\)” is clear, and if \(Z=V_+(\mathfrak a)\), \(\mathfrak a\) homogeneous, then \(\mathfrak a\cap S_+\subseteq J\) and \(V_+(\mathfrak a\cap S_+)=Z\). If \(W\subseteq\mathbf P^n\) is an irreducible closed subset with homogeneous prime \(\mathfrak p_W\) and \(W\not\subseteq Z\), then \(J\not\subseteq\mathfrak p_W\) (else \(W=V_+(\mathfrak p_W)\subseteq V_+(J)=Z\)).

Graded prime avoidance. Let \(\mathfrak p_1,\dots,\mathfrak p_s\) be homogeneous primes with \(J\not\subseteq\mathfrak p_j\) for all \(j\). Then there is a homogeneous \(y\in J\) (of positive degree, as \(J\subseteq S_+\)) with \(y\notin\mathfrak p_j\) for all \(j\). Proof: discarding primes contained in others we may assume there are no inclusions among the \(\mathfrak p_j\). For \(s=1\) pick a homogeneous generator of \(J\) outside \(\mathfrak p_1\). For \(s\ge2\) and each \(j\) the homogeneous ideal \(J\prod_{i\neq j}\mathfrak p_i\) is not contained in \(\mathfrak p_j\) (as \(\mathfrak p_j\) is prime, \(J\not\subseteq\mathfrak p_j\) and \(\mathfrak p_i\not\subseteq\mathfrak p_j\)), so it contains a homogeneous element \(y_j\notin\mathfrak p_j\); thus \(y_j\in J\), \(y_j\in\mathfrak p_i\) (\(i\neq j\)), \(\deg y_j=e_j>0\). With \(e\) a common multiple of the \(e_j\), \(y=\sum_jy_j^{e/e_j}\in J\) is homogeneous and \(y\equiv y_i^{e/e_i}\not\equiv0\pmod{\mathfrak p_i}\) for each \(i\).

Construction. We choose homogeneous \(f_0,\dots,f_r\in J\) of positive degree inductively. Put \(Y_0=\bar X\) and \(Y_{i+1}=Y_i\cap V_+(f_i)\). Given \(Y_i\), let \(W_1,\dots,W_s\) be the irreducible components of \(Y_i\) not contained in \(Z\), and choose by graded prime avoidance a homogeneous \(f_i\in J\) of positive degree not in any \(\mathfrak p_{W_l}\) (if \(s=0\), take any \(f_i\in J\) of positive degree). Let \(d_i\) be the maximal dimension of an irreducible component of \(Y_i\) not contained in \(Z\) (\(d_i=-\infty\) if none); \(d_0=r\). We claim \(d_{i+1}\le d_i-1\). Let \(V'\) be an irreducible component of \(Y_{i+1}\) not contained in \(Z\). It lies in some component \(W\) of \(Y_i\), and \(W\not\subseteq Z\); \(V'\) is an irreducible component of \(W\cap V_+(f_i)\) (it is maximal irreducible in the bigger set \(Y_{i+1}\supseteq W\cap V_+(f_i)\supseteq V'\)). Since \(f_i\notin\mathfrak p_W\), \(f_i\) does not vanish identically on the integral scheme \(W\), and by Krull's Hauptidealsatz (and the dimension formula for finitely generated \(k\)-algebras, applied on an affine chart \(D_+(x_l)\cap W\) meeting \(V'\)) \(\dim V'=\dim W-1\le d_i-1\). So \(d_{r+1}\le r-(r+1)<0\): every irreducible component of \(Y_{r+1}=\bar X\cap V_+(f_0,\dots,f_r)\) lies in \(Z\), i.e. \(Y_{r+1}\subseteq Z\).

Conclusion. Put \(U_i=X\cap D_+(f_i)\), an open subscheme of \(X\). As \(f_i\) vanishes on \(Z\), \(\bar X\cap D_+(f_i)\subseteq\bar X-Z=X\), so as a set \(U_i=\bar X\cap D_+(f_i)\). Now \(P\cap D_+(f_i)=\operatorname{Spec}C\) is a closed subscheme of the affine scheme \(D_+(f_i)\), and \(U_i\) is a subset of it which is open (it is \(X\cap D_+(f_i)\)) and closed (it is \(\bar X\cap D_+(f_i)\)). A clopen subset of \(\operatorname{Spec}C\) is \(D(e)\) for an idempotent \(e\), so \(U_i=\operatorname{Spec}C_e\) is affine. Thus \(U_i\) is an open affine subscheme of \(X\). Finally \(X\cap V_+(f_0,\dots,f_r)\subseteq Y_{r+1}\cap X\subseteq Z\cap X=\emptyset\), so \(X=\bigcup_{i=0}^rU_i\). By (c), \(\operatorname{cd}(X)\le r=\dim X\) (this uses only Cech cohomology, Thm. 4.5, not (2.7)).

(e) “\(Y\) is a set-theoretic complete intersection of codimension \(r\) in \(\mathbf P^n_k\)” means \(Y=H_1\cap\dots\cap H_r\) with \(H_l=V_+(f_l)\) hypersurfaces, \(f_l\) homogeneous of positive degree. Then \(X-Y=\mathbf P^n_k-\bigcap H_l=\bigcup_{l=1}^rD_+(f_l)\), a union of \(r\) open affine subsets. \(X-Y\) is noetherian and separated. By (c) (with \(r-1\) in place of \(r\)), \(\operatorname{cd}(X-Y)\le r-1\). ∎

Exercise 4.9

Problem

Let \(X=\operatorname{Spec}k[x_1,x_2,x_3,x_4]\) be affine four-space over a field \(k\). Let \(Y_1\) be the plane \(x_1=x_2=0\) and let \(Y_2\) be the plane \(x_3=x_4=0\). Show that \(Y=Y_1\cup Y_2\) is not a set-theoretic complete intersection in \(X\). Therefore the projective closure \(\bar Y\) in \(\mathbf P^4_k\) is also not a set-theoretic complete intersection. [Hints: Use an affine analogue of (Ex. 4.8e). Then show that \(H^2(X-Y,\mathcal O_X)\neq0\), by using (Ex. 2.3) and (Ex. 2.4). If \(P=Y_1\cap Y_2\), imitate (Ex. 4.3) to show \(H^3(X-P,\mathcal O_X)\ne0\).]

Solution

Put \(A=k[x_1,\dots,x_4]\). All cohomology below is of \(\mathcal O_X\) (or its restriction).

Step 1: affine analogue of 4.8(e). Suppose \(Y=V(h_1,h_2)\) for two polynomials \(h_1,h_2\in A\) (this is what “set-theoretic complete intersection” means here, \(Y\) having codimension \(2\); fewer equations are covered by allowing some \(h_l=0\)). Then \(X-Y=D(h_1)\cup D(h_2)\) is a union of two open affines, so by 4.8(c) (the scheme \(X-Y\) is noetherian and separated) \(H^2(X-Y,\mathcal O_X)=0\). So it suffices to show \(H^2(X-Y,\mathcal O_X)\ne0\).

Step 2: cohomology with supports. By Ex. 2.3(e), for a closed subset \(W\subseteq X\) we have an exact sequence \(H^{i-1}(X,\mathcal O)\to H^{i-1}(X-W,\mathcal O)\to H^i_W(X,\mathcal O)\to H^i(X,\mathcal O)\). As \(X\) is affine, \(H^i(X,\mathcal O)=0\) for \(i>0\) (Thm. 3.5), so

\[ H^{i-1}(X-W,\mathcal O)\cong H^i_W(X,\mathcal O)\qquad(i\ge2).\tag{1} \]

(a) \(W=Y_1\): \(X-Y_1=D(x_1)\cup D(x_2)\), covered by two open affines; its Cech complex (for this cover, Thm. 4.5) has no terms in degrees \(\ge2\), so \(H^q(X-Y_1,\mathcal O)=0\) for \(q\ge2\), and by (1) \(H^i_{Y_1}(X,\mathcal O)=0\) for \(i\ge3\). Likewise \(H^i_{Y_2}(X,\mathcal O)=0\) for \(i\ge3\) (cover \(X-Y_2=D(x_3)\cup D(x_4)\)).

(b) \(W=P=Y_1\cap Y_2=V(x_1,\dots,x_4)\), the origin. The Mayer–Vietoris sequence with supports (Ex. 2.4), for the closed sets \(Y_1,Y_2\), contains

\[ H^3_{Y_1}(X,\mathcal O)\oplus H^3_{Y_2}(X,\mathcal O)\to H^3_Y(X,\mathcal O)\to H^4_P(X,\mathcal O)\to H^4_{Y_1}(X,\mathcal O)\oplus H^4_{Y_2}(X,\mathcal O). \]

By (a) both outer groups are \(0\), so \(H^3_Y(X,\mathcal O)\cong H^4_P(X,\mathcal O)\). Using (1) three times,

\[ H^2(X-Y,\mathcal O)\cong H^3_Y(X,\mathcal O)\cong H^4_P(X,\mathcal O)\cong H^3(X-P,\mathcal O). \]

Step 3: \(H^3(X-P,\mathcal O)\ne0\). The open sets \(U_l=D(x_l)\), \(l=1,\dots,4\), are affine, cover \(X-P=X-V(x_1,\dots,x_4)\), and all their intersections are affine; \(X-P\) is noetherian separated, so \(H^3(X-P,\mathcal O)=\check H^3(\mathfrak U,\mathcal O)\) (Thm. 4.5). The Cech complex has \(C^3=A_{x_1x_2x_3x_4}\) (one term) and \(C^2=\bigoplus_{l=1}^4A_{x_1\cdots\widehat{x_l}\cdots x_4}\), and, writing \(\alpha_{\hat l}\) for the component omitting the index \(l\), \(d(\alpha)=\alpha_{\hat1}-\alpha_{\hat2}+\alpha_{\hat3}-\alpha_{\hat4}\) (restricted to \(U_{1234}\)). \(A_{x_1x_2x_3x_4}\) has \(k\)-basis the Laurent monomials \(x^a=x_1^{a_1}\cdots x_4^{a_4}\), \(a\in\mathbf Z^4\), and the summand \(A_{x_1\cdots\widehat{x_l}\cdots x_4}\) is spanned by those with \(a_l\ge0\). Hence the image of \(d\) is spanned by the monomials with some \(a_l\ge0\), and

\[ H^3(X-P,\mathcal O)=\operatorname{coker}d\cong\bigoplus_{a_1,\dots,a_4<0}k\,x^a\ne0 \]

(for example \((x_1x_2x_3x_4)^{-1}\) gives a nonzero class). Hence \(H^2(X-Y,\mathcal O)\neq0\) by Step 2, and by Step 1, \(Y\) is not a set-theoretic complete intersection in \(X\).

The projective closure. Identify \(X=D_+(x_0)\subseteq\mathbf P^4_k\). The closure \(\bar Y\) of \(Y\) satisfies \(\bar Y\cap X=Y\) (as \(Y\) is closed in \(X\)); \(\bar Y\) has dimension \(2\), so codimension \(2\) in \(\mathbf P^4_k\). If \(\bar Y=V_+(g_1,g_2)\) for homogeneous \(g_1,g_2\), then \(Y=\bar Y\cap D_+(x_0)=V(g_1(1,x_1,\dots,x_4),g_2(1,x_1,\dots,x_4))\) would be cut out in \(X\) by two polynomials (possibly zero or constant ones), which is impossible by what we proved. So \(\bar Y\) is not a set-theoretic complete intersection in \(\mathbf P^4_k\). ∎

Exercise 4.10

Problem

Let \(X\) be a nonsingular variety over an algebraically closed field \(k\), and let \(\mathcal F\) be a coherent sheaf on \(X\). Show that there is a one-to-one correspondence between the set of infinitesimal extensions of \(X\) by \(\mathcal F\) (II, Ex. 8.7), up to isomorphism, and the group \(H^1(X,\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal T)\), where \(\mathcal T\) is the tangent sheaf of \(X\) (II, Section 8). [Hint: Use (II, Ex. 8.6) and (4.5).] (Starred in the book.)

Solution

Definitions and conventions. An infinitesimal extension of \(X\) by \(\mathcal F\) (II, Ex. 8.7) is a scheme \(X'\) over \(k\) together with a sheaf of ideals \(\mathcal J\subseteq\mathcal O_{X'}\) with \(\mathcal J^2=0\), an identification of \(k\)-schemes \((X',\mathcal O_{X'}/\mathcal J)=(X,\mathcal O_X)\) (so \(X'\) and \(X\) have the same underlying space and \(\mathcal O_{X'}\to\mathcal O_X\) is the quotient map), and an isomorphism of \(\mathcal O_X\)-modules \(\mathcal J\cong\mathcal F\) (\(\mathcal J\) is an \(\mathcal O_{X'}/\mathcal J\)-module because \(\mathcal J^2=0\)). I use the (standard) notion of isomorphism: \(X',X''\) are isomorphic extensions if there is an isomorphism of \(k\)-schemes \(X'\to X''\) (the identity on the space) which is compatible with the identifications \(\mathcal O_{X'}/\mathcal J'=\mathcal O_X=\mathcal O_{X''}/\mathcal J''\) and with \(\mathcal J'\cong\mathcal F\cong\mathcal J''\). The trivial extension is \(\mathcal O_X\oplus\mathcal F\) with \((a,f)(a',f')=(aa',af'+a'f)\).

Since \(X\) is a nonsingular variety, \(\Omega=\Omega_{X/k}\) is locally free of finite rank (II, 8.15), so \(\mathcal T=\mathcal Hom(\Omega,\mathcal O_X)\) is locally free and \(\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal T\cong\mathcal Hom(\Omega,\mathcal F)=:\mathcal H\) (canonical map, iso for \(\Omega\) locally free of finite rank). Also \(\mathcal H(W)=\operatorname{Der}_k(\mathcal O_W,\mathcal F|_W)\) for open \(W\) (universal property of \(\Omega\), II, Section 8, applied on affine opens: \(\Hom_B(\Omega_{B/k},M)=\operatorname{Der}_k(B,M)\)). \(\mathcal H\) is coherent. Also \(X\) is separated.

Step 1: local triviality. Let \((X',\mathcal J)\) be an infinitesimal extension, and \(V=\operatorname{Spec}A\subseteq X\) an affine open. Then \(V'=(V,\mathcal O_{X'}|_V)\) is an open subscheme of \(X'\). It is noetherian (locally \(\operatorname{Spec}B'\) with \(B'/I=A_\alpha\) noetherian and \(I=\mathcal J(\cdot)\) a finitely generated module over \(A_\alpha\), so \(B'\) is noetherian) and \(V'_{\mathrm{red}}=V_{\mathrm{red}}\) is affine; hence \(V'\) is affine by Ex. 3.1. Let \(B'=\Gamma(V',\mathcal O_{X'})\), \(I=\Gamma(V,\mathcal J)\); \(\mathcal J\) is quasi-coherent on \(V'\) (ideal sheaf of the closed subscheme \(V\subseteq V'\)), so \(0\to I\to B'\to A\to0\) is exact, \(I^2=0\), and \(B'\) is a \(k\)-algebra. Since \(A\) is the coordinate ring of a nonsingular affine variety, the infinitesimal lifting property (II, Ex. 8.6) applied to \(\mathrm{id}:A\to A\) gives a \(k\)-algebra homomorphism \(s:A\to B'\) with \(\pi s=\mathrm{id}\) (\(\pi:B'\to A\)). So every affine open \(V\) has a \(k\)-algebra section \(s\); it extends to a morphism of sheaves of \(k\)-algebras \(\sigma:\mathcal O_V\to\mathcal O_{X'}|_V\) with \(\pi\sigma=\mathrm{id}\) (for \(a\in A\), \(s(a)\) restricts to a unit on \(D(a)\) since its image in \(\mathcal O_X(D(a))\) is a unit and \(\mathcal J\) is nilpotent, so \(s\) extends to \(A_a\to\mathcal O_{X'}(D(a))\) by the universal property of localization). Call such \(\sigma\) a splitting over \(V\).

Step 2: the class. Let \(\sigma,\sigma'\) be two splittings over an open \(W\). Then \(\sigma'-\sigma:\mathcal O_W\to\mathcal J|_W\) (values in \(\mathcal J\) as both lift the identity), and it is a \(k\)-derivation: for local sections \(a,b\),

\[ \sigma'(ab)-\sigma(ab)=\sigma'(a)\big(\sigma'(b)-\sigma(b)\big)+\big(\sigma'(a)-\sigma(a)\big)\sigma(b)=a\,(\sigma'-\sigma)(b)+b\,(\sigma'-\sigma)(a), \]

because \(\mathcal J\) is an \(\mathcal O_{X'}/\mathcal J=\mathcal O_X\)-module (so \(\sigma'(a)\cdot j=a\,j\) for \(j\in\mathcal J\)). Thus \(\sigma'-\sigma\in\Gamma(W,\mathcal H)\), \(\mathcal H\cong\mathcal Hom(\Omega,\mathcal J)=\mathcal Hom(\Omega,\mathcal F)\) via \(\mathcal J\cong\mathcal F\). Let \(\mathfrak U=(U_i)\) be a cover of \(X\) by open affines, with splittings \(\sigma_i\) over \(U_i\) (Step 1), and put \(\theta_{ij}=\sigma_j-\sigma_i\in\Gamma(U_{ij},\mathcal H)\). Then \(\theta_{ij}+\theta_{jk}=\theta_{ik}\), i.e. \(\theta=(\theta_{ij})_{i<j}\) is a Cech 1-cocycle (\((d\theta)_{ijk}=\theta_{jk}-\theta_{ik}+\theta_{ij}=0\)). Changing \(\sigma_i\) to \(\sigma_i+\eta_i\), \(\eta_i\in\Gamma(U_i,\mathcal H)\) (every splitting over \(U_i\) is of this form), changes \(\theta\) to \(\theta+d\eta\) (as \((d\eta)_{ij}=\eta_j-\eta_i\)). So we get a class \(c_{\mathfrak U}(X')\in\check H^1(\mathfrak U,\mathcal H)\). Since \(X\) is noetherian and separated and \(\mathcal H\) is quasi-coherent, \(\check H^1(\mathfrak U,\mathcal H)\cong H^1(X,\mathcal H)\) (Thm. 4.5). If \(\mathfrak V\) is another open affine cover, then \(\mathfrak W=(U_i\cap V_j)\) is an open affine cover refining both; restricting splittings gives the restricted cocycles, i.e. \(c_{\mathfrak U}\) maps to \(c_{\mathfrak W}\) under the refinement map (and likewise for \(\mathfrak V\)), and by Ex. 4.4(b) the images in \(H^1(X,\mathcal H)\) coincide. Hence we get a well-defined class \(\Psi(X')\in H^1(X,\mathcal H)\). If \(\phi:X'\to X''\) is an isomorphism of extensions, then \(\phi^\flat\circ\sigma''_i\) (with \(\phi^\flat:\mathcal O_{X''}\to\mathcal O_{X'}\)) are splittings of \(X'\) whose differences are the same \(\theta_{ij}\) (as \(\phi^\flat\) is the identity on \(\mathcal J\cong\mathcal F\)), so \(\Psi(X')=\Psi(X'')\). We get a well-defined map \(\Psi:\{\text{extensions}\}/\!\cong\ \to H^1(X,\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal T)\).

Step 3: \(\Psi\) is injective. Let \(X',X''\) be extensions with \(\Psi(X')=\Psi(X'')\). Choose an affine cover \(\mathfrak U\) and splittings \(\sigma_i\) of \(X'\), \(\tau_i\) of \(X''\) over \(U_i\) (Step 1); with \(\theta'_{ij}=\sigma_j-\sigma_i\), \(\theta''_{ij}=\tau_j-\tau_i\), the classes of \(\theta',\theta''\) coincide in \(\check H^1(\mathfrak U,\mathcal H)=H^1(X,\mathcal H)\) (Thm. 4.5 for injectivity), so \(\theta'-\theta''=d\eta\) and replacing \(\tau_i\) by \(\tau_i+\eta_i\) (\(\eta=(\eta_i)\)) we may assume \(\theta'_{ij}=\theta''_{ij}\). Over \(U_i\), \(\mathcal O_{X'}|_{U_i}=\sigma_i(\mathcal O_{U_i})\oplus\mathcal J'\) (every \(u=\sigma_i(\pi u)+(u-\sigma_i\pi u)\)), with product \((\sigma_i(a)+x)(\sigma_i(b)+y)=\sigma_i(ab)+ay+bx\), and similarly for \(X''\) with \(\tau_i\). So \(\Phi_i:\sigma_i(a)+x\mapsto\tau_i(a)+x\) (using \(\mathcal J'\cong\mathcal F\cong\mathcal J''\)) is an isomorphism of sheaves of \(k\)-algebras \(\mathcal O_{X'}|_{U_i}\to\mathcal O_{X''}|_{U_i}\), the identity on \(\mathcal J\) and on the quotients \(\mathcal O_X\). On \(U_{ij}\): \(\Phi_i(\sigma_j(a)+x)=\Phi_i(\sigma_i(a)+\theta_{ij}(a)+x)=\tau_i(a)+\theta_{ij}(a)+x=\tau_j(a)+x=\Phi_j(\sigma_j(a)+x)\). So the \(\Phi_i\) glue to an isomorphism of sheaves of rings \(\mathcal O_{X'}\to\mathcal O_{X''}\), i.e. (same underlying space) an isomorphism of \(k\)-schemes \(X''\to X'\) (and its inverse \(X'\to X''\)) compatible with all the identifications: \(X'\cong X''\) as extensions.

Step 4: \(\Psi\) is surjective. Let \(\xi\in H^1(X,\mathcal H)\). Take any open affine cover \(\mathfrak U\); by Thm. 4.5 \(\xi\) is represented by a cocycle \(\theta=(\theta_{ij})_{i<j}\), \(\theta_{ij}\in\Gamma(U_{ij},\mathcal H)=\operatorname{Der}_k(\mathcal O_{U_{ij}},\mathcal F)\); extend alternatingly (\(\theta_{ii}=0\), \(\theta_{ji}=-\theta_{ij}\)), so that \(\theta_{ij}+\theta_{jk}=\theta_{ik}\) for all \(i,j,k\). On \(U_i\) let \(\mathcal A_i=\mathcal O_{U_i}\oplus\mathcal F|_{U_i}\) be the trivial extension; \((U_i,\mathcal A_i)\) is a scheme: over an affine open \(V=\operatorname{Spec}A'\subseteq U_i\) it is \(\operatorname{Spec}(A'\oplus\mathcal F(V))\), because the localization of \(A'\oplus M\) at \((a,0)\) is \(A'_a\oplus M_a\), and \(D((a,m))=D((a,0))\) since \((0,m)\) is nilpotent. On \(U_{ij}\) define \(g_{ij}:\mathcal A_j|_{U_{ij}}\to\mathcal A_i|_{U_{ij}}\), \(g_{ij}(a,f)=(a,f+\theta_{ij}(a))\). It is a ring isomorphism: \(g_{ij}(a,f)\,g_{ij}(b,h)=(ab,a(h+\theta_{ij}b)+b(f+\theta_{ij}a))=(ab,ah+bf+\theta_{ij}(ab))=g_{ij}\big((a,f)(b,h)\big)\), using the Leibniz rule; it is \(k\)-linear, kills nothing, and has inverse \(g_{ji}\). It is the identity on \(0\oplus\mathcal F\) and on the quotient \(\mathcal O_X\). Moreover \(g_{ij}g_{jk}=g_{ik}\) by the cocycle condition and \(g_{ii}=\mathrm{id}\). Gluing (II, Ex. 1.22, for sheaves of rings) we get a sheaf of rings \(\mathcal O_{X'}\) on \(X\), locally isomorphic to the \(\mathcal A_i\), so \(X'=(X,\mathcal O_{X'})\) is a scheme; it is a \(k\)-scheme (\(k\to\mathcal O_X\to\mathcal A_i\), \(c\mapsto(c,0)\), is preserved by \(g_{ij}\) since derivations over \(k\) kill \(k\)). The ideal \(\mathcal J=0\oplus\mathcal F\) glues to a sheaf of ideals with \(\mathcal J^2=0\), \(\mathcal O_{X'}/\mathcal J=\mathcal O_X\) and \(\mathcal J\cong\mathcal F\) as \(\mathcal O_X\)-modules. So \(X'\) is an infinitesimal extension of \(X\) by \(\mathcal F\). The maps \(\sigma_i:a\mapsto(a,0)\in\mathcal A_i\) are splittings over \(U_i\), and on \(U_{ij}\), \(\sigma_j(a)=g_{ij}(a,0)=(a,\theta_{ij}(a))\), so \(\sigma_j-\sigma_i=\theta_{ij}\). Hence \(\Psi(X')=\xi\).

Thus \(\Psi\) is a bijection between isomorphism classes of infinitesimal extensions of \(X\) by \(\mathcal F\) and \(H^1(X,\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal T)\) (the trivial extension corresponding to \(0\)). ∎

Exercise 4.11

Problem

This exercise shows that Cech cohomology will agree with the usual cohomology whenever the sheaf has no cohomology on any of the open sets. More precisely, let \(X\) be a topological space, \(\mathcal F\) a sheaf of abelian groups, and \(\mathfrak U=(U_i)\) an open cover. Assume for any finite intersection \(V=U_{i_0}\cap\dots\cap U_{i_p}\) of open sets of the covering, and for any \(k>0\), that \(H^k(V,\mathcal F|_V)=0\). Then prove that for all \(p\ge0\), the natural maps

\[ \check H^p(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\to H^p(X,\mathcal F) \]

of (4.4) are isomorphisms. Show also that one can recover (4.5) as a corollary of this more general result.

Solution

Choose an injective resolution \(0\to\mathcal F\to\mathcal I^\bullet\) in \(\mathfrak{Ab}(X)\). Injective sheaves are flasque (Lemma 2.4) and flasque sheaves restrict to flasque sheaves, so for every open \(V\), \(\mathcal I^\bullet|_V\) is a flasque resolution of \(\mathcal F|_V\) and computes \(H^q(V,\mathcal F|_V)\) (Prop. 2.5). Form the double complex of abelian groups

\[ K^{p,q}=C^p(\mathfrak U,\mathcal I^q)=\prod_{i_0<\dots<i_p}\mathcal I^q(U_{i_0\dots i_p}), \]

with horizontal differential the Cech \(d\) (\(p\)-direction) and vertical differential induced by \(\mathcal I^q\to\mathcal I^{q+1}\) (\(q\)-direction); they commute. Let \(\operatorname{Tot}K\) be its total complex (first quadrant, so finite sums in each degree). There are two augmentations:

  • •

    \(\alpha:C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\to\operatorname{Tot}K\), induced by \(\mathcal F\to\mathcal I^0\), landing in \(K^{p,0}\);

  • •

    \(\beta:\Gamma(X,\mathcal I^\bullet)\to\operatorname{Tot}K\), induced by restriction \(\Gamma(X,\mathcal I^q)\to C^0(\mathfrak U,\mathcal I^q)=K^{0,q}\).

Both are quasi-isomorphisms. For fixed \(p\), the augmented row \(0\to C^p(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\to K^{p,0}\to K^{p,1}\to\cdots\) is the product over \(i_0<\dots<i_p\) of the complexes \(0\to\mathcal F(V)\to\mathcal I^0(V)\to\mathcal I^1(V)\to\cdots\) with \(V=U_{i_0\dots i_p}\), whose cohomology is \(H^q(V,\mathcal F|_V)=0\) for \(q>0\) by the hypothesis, and \(\mathcal F(V)\) in degree \(0\); products of exact sequences are exact, so all augmented rows are exact. For fixed \(q\), the augmented column \(0\to\Gamma(X,\mathcal I^q)\to C^0(\mathfrak U,\mathcal I^q)\to C^1(\mathfrak U,\mathcal I^q)\to\cdots\) is exact by Lemma 4.1 and Prop. 4.3 (\(\mathcal I^q\) is flasque). The standard lemma on first-quadrant double complexes with exact augmented rows (resp. columns) (Weibel, Lemma 2.7.3, “acyclic assembly”) says that the augmentation of the rows (resp. columns) to the total complex is a quasi-isomorphism. So \(\alpha_*:\check H^p(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\to h^p(\operatorname{Tot}K)\) and \(\beta_*:H^p(X,\mathcal F)=h^p(\Gamma(X,\mathcal I^\bullet))\to h^p(\operatorname{Tot}K)\) are isomorphisms.

Identification with the natural map of (4.4). The natural map \(\theta:\check H^p(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\to H^p(X,\mathcal F)\) is \(h^p(\Gamma(\phi))\) where \(\phi:\mathcal C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\to\mathcal I^\bullet\) is a morphism of complexes over \(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal F}\). We show \(\beta_*\theta=\alpha_*\), whence \(\theta=\beta_*^{-1}\alpha_*\) is an isomorphism. Consider the sheafified double complex \(\mathcal K^{p,q}=\mathcal C^p(\mathfrak U,\mathcal I^q)=\prod_{i_0<\dots<i_p}f_*(\mathcal I^q|_{U_{i_0\dots i_p}})\) (with \(f\) the inclusion of \(U_{i_0\dots i_p}\)), so \(\Gamma(X,\mathcal K^{p,q})=K^{p,q}\). Each \(\mathcal K^{p,q}\) is an injective sheaf (\(f_*\) of an injective sheaf under an open immersion is injective since it is right adjoint to the exact restriction functor; products of injectives are injective), hence so is each (finite sum) term of \(\operatorname{Tot}\mathcal K\). For fixed \(q\), \(\mathcal C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\mathcal I^q)\) is a resolution of \(\mathcal I^q\) (Lemma 4.2), so \(\varepsilon:\mathcal I^\bullet\to\operatorname{Tot}\mathcal K\), induced by \(\mathcal I^q\to\mathcal C^0(\mathfrak U,\mathcal I^q)\), is a quasi-isomorphism; therefore \(\operatorname{Tot}\mathcal K\) is an injective resolution of \(\mathcal F\) (via \(\mathcal F\to\mathcal I^0\to\mathcal K^{0,0}\)). The two morphisms of complexes \(\tilde\alpha:\mathcal C^\bullet(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\to\operatorname{Tot}\mathcal K\) (induced by \(\mathcal F\to\mathcal I^0\)) and \(\varepsilon\phi\) both lie over \(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal F}\) (that is, they are compatible with the augmentations from \(\mathcal F\)), the source is a resolution of \(\mathcal F\) (Lemma 4.2) and the target an injective resolution; so they are homotopic. Applying \(\Gamma(X,\cdot)\), \(\alpha=\tilde\alpha(X)\) and \(\beta\circ\Gamma(\phi)=(\varepsilon\phi)(X)\) are homotopic, i.e. \(\alpha_*=\beta_*\theta\).

Hence \(\theta:\check H^p(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\to H^p(X,\mathcal F)\) is an isomorphism for all \(p\ge0\).

Recovering (4.5). Let \(X\) be a noetherian separated scheme, \(\mathfrak U\) an open affine cover and \(\mathcal F\) quasi-coherent. Every finite intersection \(V=U_{i_0\dots i_p}\) is affine (II, Ex. 4.3) and noetherian, and \(\mathcal F|_V\) is quasi-coherent, so \(H^k(V,\mathcal F|_V)=0\) for \(k>0\) by Thm. 3.5. The hypothesis of the exercise holds, hence \(\check H^p(\mathfrak U,\mathcal F)\cong H^p(X,\mathcal F)\) for all \(p\), which is Thm. 4.5 (and without using the embedding in flasque quasi-coherent sheaves). (The same argument applies to any topological space and covering by open sets whose finite intersections are acyclic for \(\mathcal F\); e.g. for Example 4.0.4, the circle covered by two semicircles, whose intersection and the semicircles have no higher cohomology for the constant sheaf.) ∎

5. Cohomology of projective space

Throughout, “Thm. 5.1”, “Thm. 5.2”, “Prop. 5.3” refer to the results of Chapter III, Section 5 of the text: Thm. 5.1 computes \(H^i(\mathbf P^r_A,\mathcal O(n))\), Thm. 5.2 (Serre) says that for \(\mathcal F\) coherent on a projective scheme over a noetherian ring \(A\) the groups \(H^i(X,\mathcal F)\) are finitely generated \(A\)-modules and \(H^i(X,\mathcal F(n))=0\) for \(i>0\), \(n\gg0\), and Prop. 5.3 says that, for \(X\) proper over \(\operatorname{Spec}A\), an invertible sheaf \(\mathcal L\) is ample iff for every coherent \(\mathcal F\) one has \(H^i(X,\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal L^n)=0\) for all \(i>0\) and \(n\ge n_0(\mathcal F)\). If \(i\colon Y\to X\) is a closed immersion and \(\mathcal G\) is a sheaf of abelian groups on \(Y\) then \(H^i(Y,\mathcal G)=H^i(X,i_*\mathcal G)\) (Lemma 2.10), and we use this tacitly. If \(X\subseteq\mathbf P^r_k\) is a closed subscheme then \(H^i(X,\mathcal F)=0\) for \(i>r\) (Ex. 4.8(c), or Thm. 4.5 with the cover by the \(r+1\) standard affines).

Exercise 5.1

Problem

Let \(X\) be a projective scheme over a field \(k\), and let \(\mathcal F\) be a coherent sheaf on \(X\). We define the Euler characteristic of \(\mathcal F\) by

\[ \chi(\mathcal F)=\sum_{i\ge0}(-1)^i\dim_kH^i(X,\mathcal F). \]

If \(0\to\mathcal F'\to\mathcal F\to\mathcal F''\to0\) is a short exact sequence of coherent sheaves on \(X\), show that \(\chi(\mathcal F)=\chi(\mathcal F')+\chi(\mathcal F'')\).

Solution

By Thm. 5.2(a) every \(H^i(X,\mathcal G)\), \(\mathcal G\) coherent, is a finitely generated \(k\)-module, i.e. a finite dimensional vector space, and \(H^i(X,\mathcal G)=0\) for \(i>r\) if \(X\subseteq\mathbf P^r_k\). So \(\chi\) is well defined for coherent sheaves (\(\mathcal F',\mathcal F''\) are coherent by hypothesis). The long exact cohomology sequence

\[ 0\to H^0(\mathcal F')\to H^0(\mathcal F)\to H^0(\mathcal F'')\to H^1(\mathcal F')\to\cdots\to H^r(\mathcal F')\to H^r(\mathcal F)\to H^r(\mathcal F'')\to0 \]

is a finite exact sequence of finite dimensional \(k\)-vector spaces (it ends with \(0\) because \(H^{r+1}(\mathcal F')=0\)). For an exact sequence \(0\to V_0\to V_1\to\dots\to V_m\to0\) of finite dimensional vector spaces one has \(\sum(-1)^j\dim V_j=0\) (rank–nullity, applied to each map). Grouping the terms in threes, this reads \(\sum_i(-1)^i\bigl(\dim H^i(\mathcal F')-\dim H^i(\mathcal F)+\dim H^i(\mathcal F'')\bigr)=0\), i.e. \(\chi(\mathcal F)=\chi(\mathcal F')+\chi(\mathcal F'')\). ∎

Exercise 5.2

Problem
  1. (a)

    Let \(X\) be a projective scheme over a field \(k\), let \(\mathcal O_X(1)\) be a very ample invertible sheaf on \(X\) over \(k\), and let \(\mathcal F\) be a coherent sheaf on \(X\). Show that there is a polynomial \(P(z)\in\mathbf Q[z]\), such that \(\chi(\mathcal F(n))=P(n)\) for all \(n\in\mathbf Z\). We call \(P\) the Hilbert polynomial of \(\mathcal F\) with respect to the sheaf \(\mathcal O_X(1)\). [Hints: Use induction on \(\dim\operatorname{Supp}\mathcal F\), general properties of numerical polynomials (I, 7.3), and suitable exact sequences \(0\to\mathcal R\to\mathcal F(-1)\to\mathcal F\to\mathcal Q\to0\).]

  2. (b)

    Now let \(X=\mathbf P^r_k\), and let \(M=\Gamma_*(\mathcal F)\), considered as a graded \(S=k[x_0,\dots,x_r]\)-module. Use (5.2) to show that the Hilbert polynomial of \(\mathcal F\) just defined is the same as the Hilbert polynomial of \(M\) defined in (I, Section 7).

Solution

(a) Fix a closed immersion \(i\colon X\to\mathbf P^r_k\) with \(i^*\mathcal O(1)\cong\mathcal O_X(1)\) (II, 5.16.1).

Step 0: reduction to \(k\) infinite. Let \(K\supseteq k\) be an infinite field extension (e.g. an algebraic closure), \(X_K=X\times_kK\), \(\mathcal F_K\) the pull-back of \(\mathcal F\). Then \(X_K\) is a projective scheme over \(K\) (closed in \(\mathbf P^r_K\)), \(\mathcal O_{X_K}(1)\) is very ample, \(\mathcal F_K\) is coherent, and \((\mathcal F(n))_K=\mathcal F_K(n)\). I claim \(H^i(X_K,\mathcal F_K)\cong H^i(X,\mathcal F)\otimes_kK\). Indeed let \(U_j=X\cap D_+(x_j)\); these are affine, finitely many, and all finite intersections are affine (\(X\) is separated). If \(U=\operatorname{Spec}B\) and \(\mathcal F|_U=\tilde M\) then \(U_K=\operatorname{Spec}(B\otimes_kK)\) and \(\Gamma(U_K,\mathcal F_K)=M\otimes_kK\). Hence the Cech complex of \(\mathcal F_K\) for the cover \((U_{j,K})\) is the Cech complex of \(\mathcal F\) for \((U_j)\) tensored with \(K\). As \(K\) is flat over \(k\), cohomology commutes with \(\otimes_kK\), and Thm. 4.5 (for \(X\) and for \(X_K\), both noetherian separated) gives the claim. Consequently \(\chi_K(\mathcal F_K(n))=\chi_k(\mathcal F(n))\) for all \(n\). So it suffices to prove the assertion over \(K\) for all coherent sheaves on \(X_K\), i.e. we may and do assume \(k\) is infinite.

Step 1: a lemma on polynomials. Let \(f\colon\mathbf Z\to\mathbf Q\) be a function such that \(f(n)-f(n-1)=g(n)\) for all \(n\in\mathbf Z\), where \(g\in\mathbf Q[z]\). Then \(f\) is a polynomial function. Indeed, the polynomials \(\binom{z+m}{m+1}\), \(m\ge0\), satisfy \(\binom{z+m}{m+1}-\binom{z+m-1}{m+1}=\binom{z+m-1}{m}\), and the polynomials \(\binom{z+m-1}{m}\) (of degree \(m\), \(m\ge0\)) form a \(\mathbf Q\)-basis of \(\mathbf Q[z]\), so there is \(h\in\mathbf Q[z]\) with \(h(z)-h(z-1)=g(z)\). Then \(f-h\) is constant on \(\mathbf Z\), so \(f=h+c\).

Step 2: induction. We prove: for every coherent \(\mathcal F\) on \(X\) the function \(n\mapsto\chi(\mathcal F(n))\) is a polynomial, by induction on \(d=\dim\operatorname{Supp}\mathcal F\in\{-1,0,1,\dots\}\) (\(d=-1\) meaning \(\mathcal F=0\), where \(P=0\)). Let \(d\ge0\). We use the following standard facts about the finite set \(\operatorname{Ass}\mathcal F\) of associated points of a coherent sheaf on a noetherian scheme (\(p\in\operatorname{Ass}\mathcal F\) iff \(\mathfrak m_p\in\operatorname{Ass}(\mathcal F_p)\)): the generic point of each irreducible component of \(\operatorname{Supp}\mathcal F\) lies in \(\operatorname{Ass}\mathcal F\); a nonzero coherent subsheaf \(\mathcal R\subseteq\mathcal F\) has \(\emptyset\ne\operatorname{Ass}\mathcal R\subseteq\operatorname{Ass}\mathcal F\).

For \(\ell\in V=\Gamma(\mathbf P^r_k,\mathcal O(1))=\bigoplus kx_i\) and \(p\in X\), let \(\mathfrak q_p\subset S\) be the homogeneous prime of \(i(p)\); the section \(i^*\ell\) of \(\mathcal O_X(1)\) does not vanish at \(p\) (i.e. generates the stalk \(\mathcal O_X(1)_p\)) iff \(\ell\notin\mathfrak q_p\). Since \(\mathfrak q_p\) does not contain all \(x_i\), \(V\cap\mathfrak q_p\) is a proper subspace of \(V\). As \(k\) is infinite, \(V\) is not a finite union of proper subspaces, so there is \(\ell\) with \(x:=i^*\ell\) nonvanishing at every point of the finite set \(\operatorname{Ass}\mathcal F\). Multiplication by \(x\) gives \(x\colon\mathcal F(-1)\to\mathcal F\) and an exact sequence

\[ 0\to\mathcal R\to\mathcal F(-1)\to\mathcal F\to\mathcal Q\to0 \]

with \(\mathcal R,\mathcal Q\) coherent. At \(p\in\operatorname{Ass}\mathcal F\) the map \(x_p\) is an isomorphism, so \(\mathcal R_p=0\); if \(\mathcal R\neq0\) it would have an associated point in \(\operatorname{Ass}\mathcal F(-1)=\operatorname{Ass}\mathcal F\), a contradiction. So \(\mathcal R=0\). Moreover \(\operatorname{Supp}\mathcal Q\subseteq\operatorname{Supp}\mathcal F\cap Z(x)\), where \(Z(x)\) is the closed set where \(x\) vanishes (\(x_p\) is an isomorphism off \(Z(x)\)). Let \(T\) be an irreducible component of \(\operatorname{Supp}\mathcal F\) with generic point \(\eta\in\operatorname{Ass}\mathcal F\); then \(x(\eta)\ne0\), so \(T\cap Z(x)\) is a proper closed subset of \(T\), hence has dimension \(<\dim T\le d\) (a chain of irreducible closed subsets in it can be prolonged by \(T\)). Thus \(\dim\operatorname{Supp}\mathcal Q<d\) and by induction \(\chi(\mathcal Q(n))=P_{\mathcal Q}(n)\) for a polynomial \(P_{\mathcal Q}\).

Twisting \(0\to\mathcal F(-1)\to\mathcal F\to\mathcal Q\to0\) by \(n\) and using Ex. 5.1 we get \(\chi(\mathcal F(n))-\chi(\mathcal F(n-1))=P_{\mathcal Q}(n)\) for all \(n\in\mathbf Z\), and Step 1 shows \(n\mapsto\chi(\mathcal F(n))\) is polynomial. Its polynomial is unique since it is prescribed on infinitely many integers. ∎

(b) For \(n\gg0\), Thm. 5.2(b) gives \(H^i(\mathbf P^r_k,\mathcal F(n))=0\) for \(i>0\), so \(\chi(\mathcal F(n))=\dim_kH^0(\mathcal F(n))=\dim_kM_n\). Thus the Hilbert polynomial \(P_{\mathcal F}\) of (a) satisfies \(P_{\mathcal F}(n)=\dim_kM_n\) for all \(n\gg0\), which is exactly the property defining the Hilbert polynomial of \(M\) (I, Section 7: the unique polynomial with \(\varphi_M(n)=P_M(n)\) for \(n\gg0\)). A remark on finite generation: \(\Gamma_*(\mathcal F)\) need not be finitely generated (for a skyscraper sheaf at a rational point, \(\Gamma_*(\mathcal F)_n=k\) for all \(n\in\mathbf Z\)), but (II, 5.15) gives a finitely generated graded \(N\) with \(\tilde N\cong\mathcal F\), and then \(N_n\to\Gamma(\mathcal F(n))\) is an isomorphism for \(n\gg0\) (II, Ex. 5.9); so \(N\) has the same Hilbert polynomial \(P_{\mathcal F}\), and this is the finitely generated module to which (I, 7.5) literally applies. ∎

Exercise 5.3

Problem

Arithmetic Genus. Let \(X\) be a projective scheme of dimension \(r\) over a field \(k\). We define the arithmetic genus \(p_a\) of \(X\) by \(p_a(X)=(-1)^r(\chi(\mathcal O_X)-1)\). Note that it depends only on \(X\), not on any projective embedding.

  1. (a)

    If \(X\) is integral, and \(k\) algebraically closed, show that \(H^0(X,\mathcal O_X)\cong k\), so that \(p_a(X)=\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}(-1)^i\dim_kH^{r-i}(X,\mathcal O_X)\). In particular, if \(X\) is a curve, we have \(p_a(X)=\dim_kH^1(X,\mathcal O_X)\). [Hint: Use (I, 3.4).]

  2. (b)

    If \(X\) is a closed subvariety of \(\mathbf P^r_k\), show that this \(p_a(X)\) coincides with the one defined in (I, Ex. 7.2), which apparently depended on the projective embedding.

  3. (c)

    If \(X\) is a nonsingular projective curve over an algebraically closed field \(k\), show that \(p_a(X)\) is in fact a birational invariant. Conclude that a nonsingular plane curve of degree \(d\ge3\) is not rational. (This gives another proof of (II, 8.20.3) where we used the geometric genus.)

Solution

(a) \(\Gamma(X,\mathcal O_X)\) is an integral domain (\(X\) integral) and a finite dimensional \(k\)-algebra (Thm. 5.2(a), or Remark 5.2.1). A domain finite over \(k\) is a field finite over \(k\), hence equals \(k\) since \(k\) is algebraically closed. So \(h^0:=\dim H^0(X,\mathcal O_X)=1\). Since \(\dim X=r\), \(H^i(X,\mathcal O_X)=0\) for \(i>r\) (Grothendieck, Thm. 2.7). Hence

\[ \chi(\mathcal O_X)-1=\sum_{i=1}^{r}(-1)^i\dim H^i(X,\mathcal O_X),\qquad p_a(X)=\sum_{i=1}^r(-1)^{r+i}\dim H^i. \]

Putting \(i=r-j\) (\(0\le j\le r-1\)), \((-1)^{r+i}=(-1)^{2r-j}=(-1)^j\), which is the stated formula. For \(r=1\) it reads \(p_a=\dim H^1(X,\mathcal O_X)\).

(b) Let \(X\subseteq\mathbf P^r_k\) be a variety of dimension \(s\) with homogeneous ideal \(I\subset S=k[x_0,\dots,x_r]\). In (I, Ex. 7.2) \(p_a(X)=(-1)^s(P_{S/I}(0)-1)\), where \(P_{S/I}\) is the Hilbert polynomial of the graded module \(S/I\) (I, Section 7). Now \(X=\operatorname{Proj}S/I\) and \(i_*\mathcal O_X=(S/I)^\sim\) (II, Section 5), so by Ex. 5.2(b) (with the finitely generated module \(N=S/I\), \(\tilde N=i_*\mathcal O_X\)) \(P_{S/I}(n)=\chi(X,\mathcal O_X(n))\) for all \(n\). Evaluating at \(n=0\) gives \(P_{S/I}(0)=\chi(\mathcal O_X)\), so the two definitions agree.

(c) If \(X,X'\) are birational nonsingular projective curves over the algebraically closed field \(k\), then \(X\cong X'\) (I, Cor. 6.12; II, 6.7). Since \(p_a(X)=(-1)^1(\chi(\mathcal O_X)-1)\) is defined from the scheme \(X\) alone, \(p_a(X)=p_a(X')\), i.e. \(p_a\) is a birational invariant. (Here, as in Ch. I–II, a curve is integral.)

Now let \(X\subset\mathbf P^2_k\) be a nonsingular plane curve of degree \(d\ge3\), \(X=\operatorname{Proj}k[x_0,x_1,x_2]/(f)\), \(\deg f=d\) (it is connected by Ex. 5.5(b) below, hence irreducible since it is nonsingular: its local rings are domains, so distinct components cannot meet; so it is an integral curve). Since \(f\) is a nonzerodivisor in \(S\), we have the exact sequence \(0\to\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^2}(-d)\to\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^2}\to\mathcal O_X\to0\). By Thm. 5.1, \(H^0\) and \(H^1\) of \(\mathcal O(-d)\) vanish, \(H^2(\mathcal O(-d))\cong H^0(\mathcal O(d-3))^\vee\) has dimension \(\binom{d-1}{2}=\frac{(d-1)(d-2)}2\), and \(\chi(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^2})=1\). Hence \(\chi(\mathcal O_X)=1-\frac{(d-1)(d-2)}2\) and \(p_a(X)=\frac{(d-1)(d-2)}2\ge1\) for \(d\ge3\). If \(X\) were rational, it would be birational, hence isomorphic, to \(\mathbf P^1\), which has \(p_a=\dim H^1(\mathbf P^1,\mathcal O)=0\) by Thm. 5.1. This contradiction shows \(X\) is not rational. ∎

Exercise 5.4

Problem

Recall from (II, Ex. 6.10) the definition of the Grothendieck group \(K(X)\) of a noetherian scheme \(X\).

  1. (a)

    Let \(X\) be a projective scheme over a field \(k\), and let \(\mathcal O_X(1)\) be a very ample invertible sheaf on \(X\). Show that there is a (unique) additive homomorphism \(P\colon K(X)\to\mathbf Q[z]\) such that for each coherent sheaf \(\mathcal F\) on \(X\), \(P(\gamma(\mathcal F))\) is the Hilbert polynomial of \(\mathcal F\) (Ex. 5.2).

  2. (b)

    Now let \(X=\mathbf P^r_k\). For each \(i=0,1,\dots,r\), let \(L_i\) be a linear space of dimension \(i\) in \(X\). Then show that (1) \(K(X)\) is the free abelian group generated by \(\{\gamma(\mathcal O_{L_i})\mid i=0,\dots,r\}\), and (2) the map \(P\colon K(X)\to\mathbf Q[z]\) is injective. [Hint: Show (1) \(\Rightarrow\) (2). Then prove (1) and (2) simultaneously by induction on \(r\), using (II, Ex. 6.10c).]

Solution

Recall: \(K(X)\) is the free abelian group on the coherent sheaves modulo the subgroup generated by \(\mathcal F-\mathcal F'-\mathcal F''\) for all exact \(0\to\mathcal F'\to\mathcal F\to\mathcal F''\to0\); \(\gamma(\mathcal F)\) is the class of \(\mathcal F\).

(a) Let \(P_{\mathcal F}\) be the Hilbert polynomial (Ex. 5.2(a)). Isomorphic sheaves have the same \(P_{\mathcal F}\). If \(0\to\mathcal F'\to\mathcal F\to\mathcal F''\to0\) is exact, so is its twist by \(n\) for every \(n\), and Ex. 5.1 gives \(\chi(\mathcal F(n))=\chi(\mathcal F'(n))+\chi(\mathcal F''(n))\) for all \(n\in\mathbf Z\); two polynomials agreeing at all integers are equal, so \(P_{\mathcal F}=P_{\mathcal F'}+P_{\mathcal F''}\). Hence \(\mathcal F\mapsto P_{\mathcal F}\) extends to a homomorphism from the free abelian group on coherent sheaves to \(\mathbf Q[z]\) killing all relations, thus descending to \(P\colon K(X)\to\mathbf Q[z]\). Uniqueness: \(K(X)\) is generated by the \(\gamma(\mathcal F)\) and \(P(\gamma(\mathcal F))\) is prescribed.

(b) Let \(L\subseteq X=\mathbf P^r_k\) be a linear subspace of dimension \(i\), \(L\cong\mathbf P^i_k\) with \(\mathcal O_X(1)|_L=\mathcal O_L(1)\). By Lemma 2.10 and Thm. 5.1, for \(n\ge0\) and \(i\ge1\): \(H^j(L,\mathcal O(n))=0\) for \(j>0\) (for \(0<j<i\) by 5.1(b), for \(j=i\) since \(H^i(\mathcal O(n))\cong H^0(\mathcal O(-n-i-1))^\vee=0\)), and \(\dim H^0(\mathcal O_L(n))=\binom{n+i}{i}\); for \(i=0\) this is trivial. Hence

\[ P(\gamma(\mathcal O_L))=\binom{z+i}{i}, \]

a polynomial of degree exactly \(i\), independent of the choice of \(L\).

(1) \(\Rightarrow\) (2). If \(K(X)\) is free on the \(\gamma(\mathcal O_{L_i})\), then \(P\) maps this basis to the polynomials \(\binom{z+i}i\), \(0\le i\le r\), which have distinct degrees and so are linearly independent over \(\mathbf Q\); hence \(P\) is injective on \(K(X)\).

Generation, by induction on \(r\). Claim: \(K(\mathbf P^r_k)\) is generated by \(\gamma(\mathcal O_{L_i})\), \(0\le i\le r\), for some choice of linear spaces \(L_i\) (\(L_r=X\)). For \(r=0\), \(X=\operatorname{Spec}k\), a coherent sheaf is a finite dimensional vector space \(V\), which has a filtration with \(\dim V\) quotients \(k\), so \(\gamma(V)=\dim V\cdot\gamma(\mathcal O_X)\) and \(L_0=X\).

Let \(r\ge1\) and assume the claim for \(\mathbf P^{r-1}\). Let \(H=\{x_r=0\}\cong\mathbf P^{r-1}\), \(U=X\setminus H\cong\mathbf A^r_k\). By (II, Ex. 6.10c) the sequence \(K(H)\to K(X)\to K(U)\to0\) is exact (first map: extension by zero, second: restriction). I claim \(K(U)=\mathbf Z\cdot\gamma(\mathcal O_U)\): a coherent sheaf on \(U=\operatorname{Spec}A\), \(A=k[t_1,\dots,t_r]\), is \(\tilde M\) with \(M\) finitely generated, and by Hilbert's syzygy theorem \(M\) has a finite free resolution \(0\to F_m\to\dots\to F_0\to M\to0\), so \(\gamma(\tilde M)=\sum_j(-1)^j\operatorname{rank}(F_j)\gamma(\mathcal O_U)\) (the functor \(\tilde{\ }\) is exact). Now let \(\mathcal F\) be coherent on \(X\); then \(\gamma(\mathcal F|_U)=m\gamma(\mathcal O_U)\) for some \(m\in\mathbf Z\), so \(\gamma(\mathcal F)-m\gamma(\mathcal O_X)\) restricts to \(0\) in \(K(U)\), hence comes from \(K(H)\). By the induction hypothesis \(K(H)\) is generated by \(\gamma(\mathcal O_{L_i})\), \(0\le i\le r-1\), for linear subspaces \(L_i\subseteq H\) of dimension \(i\) (which are linear subspaces of \(X\)); their images in \(K(X)\) are the classes of the same sheaves viewed on \(X\). So \(K(X)\) is generated by \(\gamma(\mathcal O_X)\) and \(\gamma(\mathcal O_{L_i})\), \(0\le i\le r-1\), proving the claim.

Freeness. For these \(L_i\) the homomorphism \(\mathbf Z^{r+1}\to K(X)\), \(e_i\mapsto\gamma(\mathcal O_{L_i})\), is surjective, and its composite with \(P\) is injective (images of \(e_i\) are \(\binom{z+i}i\), linearly independent over \(\mathbf Q\)). Hence \(\mathbf Z^{r+1}\to K(X)\) is an isomorphism, and \(P\) is injective. Finally, for an arbitrary choice of linear subspaces \(L'_i\) of dimension \(i\), \(P(\gamma(\mathcal O_{L'_i}))=\binom{z+i}i=P(\gamma(\mathcal O_{L_i}))\), so by injectivity of \(P\), \(\gamma(\mathcal O_{L'_i})=\gamma(\mathcal O_{L_i})\). This proves (1) and (2) for every choice of the \(L_i\). ∎

Exercise 5.5

Problem

Let \(k\) be a field, let \(X=\mathbf P^r_k\), and let \(Y\) be a closed subscheme of dimension \(q\ge1\), which is a complete intersection (II, Ex. 8.4). Then:

  1. (a)

    for all \(n\in\mathbf Z\), the natural map \(H^0(X,\mathcal O_X(n))\to H^0(Y,\mathcal O_Y(n))\) is surjective. (This gives a generalization and another proof of (II, Ex. 8.4c), where we assumed \(Y\) was normal.)

  2. (b)

    \(Y\) is connected;

  3. (c)

    \(H^i(Y,\mathcal O_Y(n))=0\) for \(0<i<q\) and all \(n\in\mathbf Z\);

  4. (d)

    \(p_a(Y)=\dim_kH^q(Y,\mathcal O_Y)\).

[Hint: Use exact sequences and induction on the codimension, starting from the case \(Y=X\) which is (5.1).]

Solution

Write \(S=k[x_0,\dots,x_r]\) and \(c=r-q\) for the codimension. By definition (II, Ex. 8.4), the homogeneous ideal \(I\) of \(Y\) is generated by \(c\) elements; since \(I\) is homogeneous we may take them homogeneous, \(f_1,\dots,f_c\) of degrees \(d_j\) (graded Nakayama), and \(d_j\ge1\) because \(Y\ne\emptyset\) (\(q\ge1\)). As \(S\) is a finitely generated domain over \(k\) of dimension \(r+1\), \(\operatorname{ht}J+\dim S/J=r+1\) for every proper ideal \(J\) (apply \(\operatorname{ht}P+\dim S/P=r+1\) to the minimal primes of \(J\)), and \(\dim S/(J+(g))\ge\dim S/J-1\) for every \(g\). Here \(\dim S/I=q+1\) (as \(\dim Y=q\)), so \(\operatorname{ht}I=r-q=c\). Then \(\operatorname{ht}(f_1,\dots,f_j)=j\) for all \(j\): Krull's height theorem gives \(\le j\), and \(q+1=\dim S/I\ge\dim S/(f_1,\dots,f_j)-(c-j)\) gives \(\operatorname{ht}(f_1,\dots,f_j)=r+1-\dim S/(f_1,\dots,f_j)\ge r+1-(q+1)-(c-j)=j\). Since \(S\) is Cohen–Macaulay, \(f_1,\dots,f_c\) is an \(S_{\mathfrak m}\)-regular sequence (\(\mathfrak m=(x_0,\dots,x_r)\); Matsumura, Thm. 17.4; this is the unmixedness theorem of the hint to Ex. 8.4(a)), and then it is \(S\)-regular, because for a homogeneous element \(f\) and a graded module \(N\) the kernel of \(f\) on \(N\) is a graded submodule, which vanishes iff it vanishes after localizing at \(\mathfrak m\).

Let \(Y_j=\operatorname{Proj}S/(f_1,\dots,f_j)\), \(Y_0=X\), \(Y_c=Y\), with \(\mathcal O_{Y_j}=(S/(f_1,\dots,f_j))^\sim\). Regularity gives exact sequences of graded modules \(0\to (S/(f_1,\dots,f_{j-1}))(-d_j)\xrightarrow{f_j}S/(f_1,\dots,f_{j-1})\to S/(f_1,\dots,f_j)\to0\), hence exact sequences of sheaves on \(X\)

\[ 0\to\mathcal O_{Y_{j-1}}(m-d_j)\to\mathcal O_{Y_{j-1}}(m)\to\mathcal O_{Y_j}(m)\to0\qquad(m\in\mathbf Z).\tag{$*$} \]

Claim \((A_j)\), \(0\le j\le c\): \(H^i(Y_j,\mathcal O_{Y_j}(m))=0\) for \(0<i<r-j\) and all \(m\in\mathbf Z\), and \(H^0(X,\mathcal O_X(m))\to H^0(Y_j,\mathcal O_{Y_j}(m))\) is surjective for all \(m\). For \(j=0\) this is Thm. 5.1(a),(b). Assume \(A_{j-1}\) and \(j\le c\); then \(s:=r-j+1\ge q+1\ge2\). The long exact sequence of \((*)\) gives

\[ H^i(Y_{j-1},\mathcal O(m))\to H^i(Y_j,\mathcal O(m))\to H^{i+1}(Y_{j-1},\mathcal O(m-d_j)). \]

For \(0<i<r-j=s-1\) both outer terms vanish by \(A_{j-1}\) (as \(0<i\) and \(i+1<s\)), so \(H^i(Y_j,\mathcal O(m))=0\). For \(i=0\) the right-hand term is \(H^1(Y_{j-1},\mathcal O(m-d_j))\), which vanishes since \(0<1<s\); hence \(H^0(Y_{j-1},\mathcal O(m))\to H^0(Y_j,\mathcal O(m))\) is surjective, and composing with the surjection from \(A_{j-1}\) gives the surjectivity for \(Y_j\). This proves \(A_j\) for all \(j\le c\).

Taking \(j=c\) (\(r-c=q\)) gives (a) and (c).

(b) By (a) with \(n=0\), \(\Gamma(Y,\mathcal O_Y)\) is a quotient of \(\Gamma(X,\mathcal O_X)=k\); it is nonzero as \(Y\neq\emptyset\). So \(\Gamma(Y,\mathcal O_Y)=k\). If \(Y=Y'\sqcup Y''\) with \(Y',Y''\) nonempty open and closed, then \(\Gamma(Y,\mathcal O_Y)=\Gamma(Y',\mathcal O)\times\Gamma(Y'',\mathcal O)\) has dimension \(\ge2\), a contradiction. So \(Y\) is connected.

(d) \(Y\) has dimension \(q\ge1\), so \(H^i(Y,\mathcal O_Y)=0\) for \(i>q\) (Thm. 2.7), \(H^i=0\) for \(0<i<q\) by (c), and \(\dim H^0=1\) by (b). Hence \(\chi(\mathcal O_Y)=1+(-1)^q\dim H^q(Y,\mathcal O_Y)\) and \(p_a(Y)=(-1)^q(\chi(\mathcal O_Y)-1)=\dim_kH^q(Y,\mathcal O_Y)\). ∎

Exercise 5.6

Problem

Curves on a Nonsingular Quadric Surface. Let \(Q\) be the nonsingular quadric surface \(xy=zw\) in \(X=\mathbf P^3_k\) over a field \(k\). We will consider locally principal subschemes \(Y\) of \(Q\). These correspond to Cartier divisors on \(Q\) by (II, 6.17.1). On the other hand, we know that \(\operatorname{Pic}Q\cong\mathbf Z\oplus\mathbf Z\), so we can talk about the type \((a,b)\) of \(Y\) (II, 6.16) and (II, 6.6.1). Let us denote the invertible sheaf \(\mathcal L(Y)\) by \(\mathcal O_Q(a,b)\). Thus for any \(n\in\mathbf Z\), \(\mathcal O_Q(n)=\mathcal O_Q(n,n)\).

  1. (a)

    Use the special cases \((q,0)\) and \((0,q)\), with \(q>0\), when \(Y\) is a disjoint union of \(q\) lines \(\mathbf P^1\) in \(Q\), to show: (1) if \(|a-b|\le1\), then \(H^1(Q,\mathcal O_Q(a,b))=0\); (2) if \(a,b<0\), then \(H^1(Q,\mathcal O_Q(a,b))=0\); (3) if \(a\le-2\), then \(H^1(Q,\mathcal O_Q(a,0))\ne0\).

  2. (b)

    Now use these results to show: (1) if \(Y\) is a locally principal closed subscheme of type \((a,b)\), with \(a,b>0\), then \(Y\) is connected; (2) now assume \(k\) is algebraically closed. Then for any \(a,b>0\), there exists an irreducible nonsingular curve \(Y\) of type \((a,b)\). Use (II, 7.6.2) and (II, 8.18); (3) an irreducible nonsingular curve \(Y\) of type \((a,b)\), \(a,b>0\) on \(Q\) is projectively normal (II, Ex. 5.14) if and only if \(|a-b|\le1\). In particular, this gives lots of examples of nonsingular, but not projectively normal curves in \(\mathbf P^3\). The simplest is the one of type \((1,3)\), which is just the rational quartic curve (I, Ex. 3.18).

  3. (c)

    If \(Y\) is a locally principal subscheme of type \((a,b)\) in \(Q\), show that \(p_a(Y)=ab-a-b+1\). [Hint: Calculate Hilbert polynomials of suitable sheaves, and again use the special case \((q,0)\) which is a disjoint union of \(q\) copies of \(\mathbf P^1\). See (V, 1.5.2) for another method.]

Solution

Conventions. The Segre embedding \(\mathbf P^1\times_k\mathbf P^1\to\mathbf P^3\), \(((s:t),(s':t'))\mapsto(ss':tt':st':ts')\) (coordinates \(x,y,z,w\)) identifies \(\mathbf P^1\times\mathbf P^1\) with \(Q\) over any field. We write \(p_1,p_2\) for the projections and \(\mathcal O_Q(a,b)=p_1^*\mathcal O(a)\otimes p_2^*\mathcal O(b)\); then \(\mathcal O_Q(1)=\mathcal O_Q(1,1)\), \(\operatorname{Pic}Q=\mathbf Z\oplus\mathbf Z\) via \((a,b)\) (II, 6.6.1), and a locally principal \(Y\) has type \((a,b)\) iff its ideal sheaf is \(\mathcal L(Y)^{-1}\cong\mathcal O_Q(-a,-b)\), i.e. there is an exact sequence \(0\to\mathcal O_Q(-a,-b)\to\mathcal O_Q\to\mathcal O_Y\to0\). The automorphism of \(Q\) swapping the factors interchanges \((a,b)\) and \((b,a)\); so every statement below is symmetric in the two factors (the labelling of the rulings is immaterial). We use two facts on \(Q\), both from Ex. 5.5 (applied to the hypersurface \(Q\subset\mathbf P^3\), a complete intersection of dimension \(2\)): (i) \(H^1(Q,\mathcal O_Q(n))=0\) for all \(n\); (ii) \(H^0(\mathbf P^3,\mathcal O(n))\to H^0(Q,\mathcal O_Q(n))\) is surjective for all \(n\) and \(H^0(Q,\mathcal O_Q)=k\).

The curves of type \((q,0)\), \((0,q)\). Let \(p_1,\dots,p_q\in\mathbf P^1(k)\) be distinct rational points, \(Z\subset\mathbf P^1\) the reduced subscheme \(\{p_1,\dots,p_q\}\); its ideal sheaf is \(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^1}(-q)\) (the zero scheme of a product of \(q\) distinct linear forms). Put \(Y=p_1^{-1}(Z)=Z\times\mathbf P^1=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^qL_i\), \(L_i=\{p_i\}\times\mathbf P^1\), a disjoint union of \(q\) lines of \(Q\). As \(p_1\) is flat, \(\mathcal I_Y=p_1^*\mathcal O(-q)=\mathcal O_Q(-q,0)\), so \(Y\) has type \((q,0)\). Also \(\mathcal O_Q(n,n)|_{L_i}=\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^1}(n)\) (\(p_1^*\mathcal O(n)\) is trivial on \(L_i\)). So twisting \(0\to\mathcal O_Q(-q,0)\to\mathcal O_Q\to\mathcal O_Y\to0\) by \(\mathcal O_Q(n)\) gives
