\[ 0\to\mathcal O_Q(n-q,n)\to\mathcal O_Q(n)\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^q\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^1}(n)\to0. \]

Using (i) the long exact sequence yields

\[ H^0(Q,\mathcal O_Q(n))\xrightarrow{\ \rho\ }\bigoplus_{i=1}^qH^0(\mathbf P^1,\mathcal O(n))\to H^1(Q,\mathcal O_Q(n-q,n))\to H^1(Q,\mathcal O_Q(n))=0.\tag{$**$} \]

Hence \(H^1(Q,\mathcal O_Q(n-q,n))\cong\operatorname{coker}\rho\), a quotient of \(\bigoplus_iH^0(\mathbf P^1,\mathcal O(n))\). Similarly, with \(p_2\) in place of \(p_1\) we get \(H^1(Q,\mathcal O_Q(n,n-q))\cong\operatorname{coker}\rho'\) with the analogous \(\rho'\).

Since cohomology commutes with the flat base change \(k\to\bar k\) (proof as in Ex. 5.2, Step 0, and \(\mathcal O_Q(a,b)\) is compatible with base change), the dimensions of \(H^1(Q,\mathcal O_Q(a,b))\) do not change; so for \(q\ge2\) below we may assume \(k\) has at least \(q\) rational points (e.g. \(k\) algebraically closed).

(a)(1). If \(a=b\), use (i). Let \(a-b=-1\), i.e. \((a,b)=(n-1,n)\), \(n=b\). Take \(q=1\): \(Y=L\) is a line in \(\mathbf P^3\), and \(\rho\) is the composite \(H^0(Q,\mathcal O_Q(n))\to H^0(L,\mathcal O_L(n))\). The restriction \(H^0(\mathbf P^3,\mathcal O(n))\to H^0(L,\mathcal O_L(n))\) is surjective (\(L\) is a linear subspace, \(S\to S/(\ell_1,\ell_2)\cong k[s,t]\) is onto, and \(H^0(L,\mathcal O(n))=k[s,t]_n\) for \(n\ge0\) by Thm. 5.1(a), zero for \(n<0\)), and it factors through \(\rho\). So \(\rho\) is surjective and \(H^1(Q,\mathcal O_Q(n-1,n))=0\) by \((**)\). The case \(a-b=1\) is symmetric (use \(\rho'\)).

(a)(2). Let \(a,b<0\). If \(a\le b\), put \(n=b<0\), \(q=b-a\ge0\) (\(q=0\) is (i)). In \((**)\), \(H^0(\mathbf P^1,\mathcal O(n))=0\) since \(n<0\), so \(H^1(Q,\mathcal O_Q(a,b))=H^1(Q,\mathcal O_Q(n-q,n))=0\). If \(a\ge b\), put \(n=a<0\), \(q=a-b\) and use \(\rho'\) in the same way.

(a)(3). Let \(a\le-2\), \(q=-a\ge2\), \(n=0\). Then \(H^0(\mathbf P^1,\mathcal O)^{\oplus q}=k^q\), and the image of \(\rho\colon H^0(Q,\mathcal O_Q)=k\to k^q\) has dimension \(\le1\). By \((**)\), \(\dim H^1(Q,\mathcal O_Q(-q,0))=\dim\operatorname{coker}\rho\ge q-1\ge1\). So \(H^1(Q,\mathcal O_Q(a,0))\ne0\); by symmetry also \(H^1(Q,\mathcal O_Q(0,b))\ne0\) for \(b\le-2\).

(b)(1). Let \(Y\) be locally principal of type \((a,b)\), \(a,b>0\). From \(0\to\mathcal O_Q(-a,-b)\to\mathcal O_Q\to\mathcal O_Y\to0\) and \(H^1(Q,\mathcal O_Q(-a,-b))=0\) (by (a)(2)), \(H^0(Q,\mathcal O_Q)=k\to H^0(Y,\mathcal O_Y)\) is surjective. \(Y\ne\emptyset\): otherwise \(\mathcal O_Q(-a,-b)\cong\mathcal O_Q\), but restricted to the line \(\mathbf P^1\times\{pt\}\) this is \(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^1}(-a)\not\cong\mathcal O\). So \(\Gamma(Y,\mathcal O_Y)=k\), and as in Ex. 5.5(b), \(Y\) is connected (a decomposition \(Y=Y'\sqcup Y''\) would give \(\dim\Gamma(\mathcal O_Y)\ge2\)).

(b)(2). Let \(k\) be algebraically closed, \(a,b>0\). The sheaf \(\mathcal O_Q(a,b)\) is very ample: it is the pull-back of \(\mathcal O(1)\) under the closed immersion \(\mathbf P^1\times\mathbf P^1\to\mathbf P^a\times\mathbf P^b\to\mathbf P^N\), the product of the Veronese embeddings (II, Ex. 5.13; \(v_a^*\mathcal O(1)=\mathcal O(a)\)) followed by the Segre embedding (II, Ex. 2.14, 5.11); cf. (II, 7.6.2). So \(Q\) is embedded in \(\mathbf P^N\) with \(\mathcal O_Q(1)_{\mathbf P^N}=\mathcal O_Q(a,b)\). By Bertini (II, 8.18) there is a hyperplane \(H\subset\mathbf P^N\), not containing \(Q\), such that \(Y=H\cap Q\) is regular at every point. \(Y\) is the zero scheme of a nonzero section \(s\) of \(\mathcal O_Q(a,b)\) on the integral scheme \(Q\), so it is a locally principal subscheme with ideal sheaf \(\cong\mathcal O_Q(-a,-b)\), i.e. of type \((a,b)\) (and of pure dimension \(1\)). By (b)(1), \(Y\) is connected. A nonsingular connected scheme is irreducible (the local rings are regular, hence domains, so every point lies on a unique component; the finitely many components are then disjoint closed sets, so connectedness leaves one). So \(Y\) is an irreducible nonsingular (integral) curve of type \((a,b)\).

(b)(3). Let \(k\) be algebraically closed and \(Y\subset Q\subset\mathbf P^3\) be an irreducible nonsingular curve of type \((a,b)\), \(a,b>0\). \(Y\) is normal and connected (b)(1), so by (II, Ex. 5.14(d)) \(Y\) is projectively normal iff \(H^0(\mathbf P^3,\mathcal O(n))\to H^0(Y,\mathcal O_Y(n))\) is surjective for every \(n\ge0\). Twist \(0\to\mathcal O_Q(-a,-b)\to\mathcal O_Q\to\mathcal O_Y\to0\) by \(\mathcal O_Q(n)\):

\[ 0\to\mathcal O_Q(n-a,n-b)\to\mathcal O_Q(n)\to\mathcal O_Y(n)\to0. \]

By (ii) the map \(H^0(\mathbf P^3,\mathcal O(n))\to H^0(Q,\mathcal O_Q(n))\) is onto, so our map is onto iff \(\sigma\colon H^0(Q,\mathcal O_Q(n))\to H^0(Y,\mathcal O_Y(n))\) is onto, iff the connecting map \(\partial\colon H^0(\mathcal O_Y(n))\to H^1(Q,\mathcal O_Q(n-a,n-b))\) is zero. But \(\operatorname{im}\partial=\ker(H^1(\mathcal O_Q(n-a,n-b))\to H^1(\mathcal O_Q(n)))\) and \(H^1(\mathcal O_Q(n))=0\), so \(\partial\) is onto. Therefore

\[ Y\text{ is projectively normal}\iff H^1(Q,\mathcal O_Q(n-a,n-b))=0\ \text{for all }n\ge0. \]

If \(|a-b|\le1\) then \(|(n-a)-(n-b)|\le1\) and the right side holds by (a)(1). If \(|a-b|\ge2\): when \(a\ge b+2\), take \(n=b>0\): \(H^1(\mathcal O_Q(b-a,0))\ne0\) by (a)(3); when \(b\ge a+2\), take \(n=a\): \(H^1(\mathcal O_Q(0,a-b))\ne0\) by (a)(3) (symmetric form). So \(Y\) is not projectively normal. This proves (3). For type \((1,3)\): \(|1-3|=2\), so the curve (which exists and is nonsingular by (2), has degree \((1,3)\cdot(1,1)=4\) and \(p_a=(1-1)(3-1)=0\), i.e. is the rational quartic) is nonsingular but not projectively normal.

(c) Let \(Y\ne\emptyset\) be locally principal of type \((a,b)\) (so \(a,b\ge0\), not both \(0\), and \(Y\) is an effective Cartier divisor on the integral surface \(Q\), of dimension \(1\)). Let \(\chi(m,n)=\chi(Q,\mathcal O_Q(m,n))\). First \(\chi(0,0)=1\): from \(0\to\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^3}(-2)\to\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^3}\to\mathcal O_Q\to0\) and Ex. 5.1, \(\chi(\mathcal O_Q)=\chi(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^3})-\chi(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^3}(-2))=1-0\), since all cohomology of \(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^3}(-2)\) vanishes by Thm. 5.1 (\(H^3(\mathcal O(-2))\cong H^0(\mathcal O(-2))^\vee=0\)). Next let \(L=\{p\}\times\mathbf P^1\) be a rational line of type \((1,0)\); twisting \(0\to\mathcal O_Q(-1,0)\to\mathcal O_Q\to\mathcal O_L\to0\) by \(\mathcal O_Q(m,n)\) and using \(\mathcal O_Q(m,n)|_L=\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^1}(n)\), Ex. 5.1 and Thm. 5.1 (\(\chi(\mathbf P^1,\mathcal O(n))=n+1\) for all \(n\in\mathbf Z\)) give \(\chi(m,n)-\chi(m-1,n)=n+1\) for all \(m,n\); symmetrically \(\chi(m,n)-\chi(m,n-1)=m+1\). Hence \(\chi(0,n)=\chi(0,0)+n=n+1\) and \(\chi(m,n)=\chi(0,n)+m(n+1)=(m+1)(n+1)\) for all \(m,n\in\mathbf Z\). From \(0\to\mathcal O_Q(-a,-b)\to\mathcal O_Q\to\mathcal O_Y\to0\), \(\chi(\mathcal O_Y)=1-(1-a)(1-b)=a+b-ab\), so \(p_a(Y)=(-1)^1(\chi(\mathcal O_Y)-1)=ab-a-b+1\). (E.g. type \((1,3)\) gives \(0\), type \((2,2)\) gives \(1\).) ∎

Exercise 5.7

Problem

Let \(X\) (respectively, \(Y\)) be proper schemes over a noetherian ring \(A\). We denote by \(\mathcal L\) an invertible sheaf.

  1. (a)

    If \(\mathcal L\) is ample on \(X\), and \(Y\) is any closed subscheme of \(X\), then \(i^*\mathcal L\) is ample on \(Y\), where \(i\colon Y\to X\) is the inclusion.

  2. (b)

    \(\mathcal L\) is ample on \(X\) if and only if \(\mathcal L_{\mathrm{red}}=\mathcal L\otimes\mathcal O_{X_{\mathrm{red}}}\) is ample on \(X_{\mathrm{red}}\).

  3. (c)

    Suppose \(X\) is reduced. Then \(\mathcal L\) is ample on \(X\) if and only if \(\mathcal L\otimes\mathcal O_{X_i}\) is ample on \(X_i\), for each irreducible component \(X_i\) of \(X\).

  4. (d)

    Let \(f\colon X\to Y\) be a finite surjective morphism, and let \(\mathcal L\) be an invertible sheaf on \(Y\). Then \(\mathcal L\) is ample on \(Y\) if and only if \(f^*\mathcal L\) is ample on \(X\). [Hints: Use (5.3) and compare (Ex. 3.1, Ex. 3.2, Ex. 4.1, Ex. 4.2). See also Hartshorne [5, Ch. I Section 4] for more details.]

Solution

We use Prop. 5.3 throughout; note that closed subschemes of proper \(A\)-schemes are proper.

(a) \(Y\) is proper over \(A\). Let \(\mathcal G\) be coherent on \(Y\); then \(i_*\mathcal G\) is coherent on \(X\) and, by the projection formula (II, Ex. 5.1(d)) and Lemma 2.10, \(H^j(Y,\mathcal G\otimes i^*\mathcal L^n)=H^j(X,i_*(\mathcal G\otimes i^*\mathcal L^n))=H^j(X,i_*\mathcal G\otimes\mathcal L^n)\), which vanishes for \(j>0\), \(n\ge n_0\) because \(\mathcal L\) is ample on \(X\) (5.3(i)\(\Rightarrow\)(ii)). So \(i^*\mathcal L\) is ample by 5.3(ii)\(\Rightarrow\)(i).

(b) (\(\Rightarrow\)) is (a) with \(Y=X_{\mathrm{red}}\). (\(\Leftarrow\)) Let \(\mathcal N\) be the nilradical, \(\mathcal N^m=0\). Let \(\mathcal F\) be coherent on \(X\) and consider \(\mathcal F\supseteq\mathcal N\mathcal F\supseteq\dots\supseteq\mathcal N^m\mathcal F=0\). Each quotient \(\mathcal G_j=\mathcal N^j\mathcal F/\mathcal N^{j+1}\mathcal F\) is coherent and killed by \(\mathcal N\), so \(\mathcal G_j=i_*\mathcal G'_j\) for a coherent \(\mathcal G'_j\) on \(X_{\mathrm{red}}\) (\(i\colon X_{\mathrm{red}}\to X\)). As above, \(H^l(X,\mathcal G_j\otimes\mathcal L^n)=H^l(X_{\mathrm{red}},\mathcal G'_j\otimes\mathcal L_{\mathrm{red}}^n)=0\) for \(l>0\), \(n\ge n_j\), since \(\mathcal L_{\mathrm{red}}\) is ample on the proper scheme \(X_{\mathrm{red}}\). Descending induction on \(j\), using \(0\to\mathcal N^{j+1}\mathcal F\to\mathcal N^j\mathcal F\to\mathcal G_j\to0\) tensored with \(\mathcal L^n\), shows \(H^l(X,\mathcal N^j\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal L^n)=0\) for \(l>0\) and \(n\ge\max_{j'\ge j}n_{j'}\); for \(j=0\) this is the claim for \(\mathcal F\). By 5.3, \(\mathcal L\) is ample.

(c) (\(\Rightarrow\)) is (a) (with \(X_i\) reduced closed subschemes). (\(\Leftarrow\)) By induction on the number \(m\) of components; \(m=1\) is trivial. Let \(Z=X_m\) and \(W=X_1\cup\dots\cup X_{m-1}\) with reduced structures, with ideal sheaves \(\mathcal I_Z,\mathcal I_W\). Since \(X\) is reduced, \(\mathcal I_Z\cap\mathcal I_W=0\) (a section in both has zero value at all points of \(Z\cup W=X\), hence is nilpotent, hence \(0\)), so \(\mathcal I_Z\mathcal I_W=0\). For \(\mathcal F\) coherent, \(\mathcal I_W\mathcal F\) is killed by \(\mathcal I_Z\), hence is a coherent \(\mathcal O_Z\)-module, and \(\mathcal F/\mathcal I_W\mathcal F\) is a coherent \(\mathcal O_W\)-module. By induction \(\mathcal L|_W\) is ample on \(W\) (its components are \(X_1,\dots,X_{m-1}\)) and \(\mathcal L|_Z\) is ample, so as in (b) \(H^l(\mathcal I_W\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal L^n)=0=H^l((\mathcal F/\mathcal I_W\mathcal F)\otimes\mathcal L^n)\) for \(l>0\), \(n\gg0\), and the sequence \(0\to\mathcal I_W\mathcal F\to\mathcal F\to\mathcal F/\mathcal I_W\mathcal F\to0\) gives the same for \(\mathcal F\). Conclude by 5.3.

(d) (\(\Rightarrow\)) Let \(\mathcal L\) be ample on \(Y\). \(f\) is finite, hence affine, and \(X\) is proper over \(A\). For \(\mathcal F\) coherent on \(X\), \(f_*\mathcal F\) is coherent (finiteness of \(f\)) and by Ex. 4.1 and the projection formula

\[ H^l(X,\mathcal F\otimes f^*\mathcal L^n)=H^l(Y,f_*(\mathcal F\otimes f^*\mathcal L^n))=H^l(Y,f_*\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal L^n)=0 \]

for \(l>0\), \(n\gg0\) (5.3). So \(f^*\mathcal L\) is ample. (Surjectivity is not needed here.)

(\(\Leftarrow\)) Assume \(f^*\mathcal L\) ample, \(f\) finite and surjective. We show that \(\mathcal L\) satisfies 5.3(ii). Call a closed subset \(W\subseteq Y\) good if for every coherent \(\mathcal F\) on \(Y\) with \(\operatorname{Supp}\mathcal F\subseteq W\) there is \(n_0\) with \(H^l(Y,\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal L^n)=0\) for \(l>0\), \(n\ge n_0\). \(\emptyset\) is good. Suppose some closed subset is not good; by noetherian induction pick a minimal non-good \(W\), so every proper closed subset of \(W\) is good.

\(W\) is irreducible. If \(W=W_1\cup W_2\) with \(W_1,W_2\subsetneq W\) closed, let \(\mathcal I_1\) be the ideal sheaf of \(W_1\) (reduced structure), and \(\mathcal F\) coherent supported in \(W\). On the open set \(Y\setminus W_2\) the support of \(\mathcal F\) lies in \(W_1\), so \(\mathcal I_1^m\mathcal F=0\) there for \(m\gg0\) (\(Y\setminus W_2\) is quasi-compact). Thus \(\operatorname{Supp}(\mathcal I_1^m\mathcal F)\subseteq W_2\), while \(\mathcal F/\mathcal I_1^m\mathcal F\) is supported in \(W_1\). The sequence \(0\to\mathcal I_1^m\mathcal F\to\mathcal F\to\mathcal F/\mathcal I_1^m\mathcal F\to0\) shows \(\mathcal F\) has the vanishing property, i.e. \(W\) is good, contradiction.

Reduction to \(\mathcal O_W\)-modules. Let \(j\colon W\to Y\) be the inclusion with reduced structure and \(\mathcal F\) coherent supported in \(W\) and not having the vanishing property. Then \(\mathcal I_W^m\mathcal F=0\) for some \(m\), and \(\mathcal F\supseteq\mathcal I_W\mathcal F\supseteq\dots\) has quotients that are coherent \(\mathcal O_W\)-modules; one of them fails the vanishing property (else \(\mathcal F\) would have it). So there is a coherent \(\mathcal O_W\)-module \(\mathcal E\) with \(j_*\mathcal E\) failing it. Since \(H^l(Y,j_*\mathcal E\otimes\mathcal L^n)=H^l(W,\mathcal E\otimes(\mathcal L|_W)^n)\), we get a contradiction as follows.

\(W\) is integral. Since \(f\) is surjective, \(f^{-1}(W)\to W\) is surjective; \(f^{-1}(W)_{\mathrm{red}}\) has finitely many irreducible components, whose images (closed, since \(f\) is finite) cover \(W\), so one of them, \(X'\), maps onto \(W\) (irreducibility of \(W\)). With the reduced structure, \(g=f|_{X'}\colon X'\to W\) is a finite surjective morphism of integral schemes, \(X'\) is proper over \(A\), and \(g^*(\mathcal L|_W)=(f^*\mathcal L)|_{X'}\) is ample by (a). Put \(\mathcal B=g_*\mathcal O_{X'}\), a coherent \(\mathcal O_W\)-algebra; at the generic point \(\eta\) of \(W\), \(\mathcal B_\eta=K(X')\) is a field extension of \(K=K(W)\) of finite degree \(\varrho\) (a finite \(K\)-algebra which is a domain). Put \(\mathcal M=g^*(\mathcal L|_W)\), ample on \(X'\). By (II, 7.6) and properness some \(\mathcal M^s\) gives a closed immersion \(X'\to\mathbf P^M_A\); let \(s_0,\dots,s_M\in\Gamma(X',\mathcal M^s)\) be the pulled-back coordinates, indexed so that \(s_0\ne0\) (as \(X'\) is integral, \(s_0\) is then a generator of \(\mathcal M^s\) at the generic point of \(X'\)). Put \(u_i=s_i/s_0\in K(X')\). The ring \(K[u_1,\dots,u_M]\subseteq K(X')\) is a domain finite-dimensional over \(K\), hence a field; it contains the coordinate ring \(A[u_1,\dots,u_M]\) of the nonempty affine open \(X'\cap D_+(x_0)\), hence its fraction field \(K(X')\). So \(K(X')=K[u_1,\dots,u_M]\), and for \(t\gg0\) the monomials \(u^J\), \(|J|\le t\), span \(K(X')\) over \(K\). Now \(s^Js_0^{t-|J|}\in\Gamma(X',\mathcal M^{st})=\Gamma(W,\mathcal B\otimes\mathcal L^{st})\) (projection formula) has generic germ \(u^J\) after trivializing by \(s_0^t\). Choose \(\varrho\) of these whose germs form a \(K\)-basis of \(K(X')=\mathcal B_\eta\), and put \(m=st\). This gives a global map \(\alpha\colon\mathcal O_W^{\varrho}\to\mathcal B\otimes\mathcal L^m\), an isomorphism at \(\eta\). Let \(\mathcal H=\mathcal Hom_{\mathcal O_W}(\mathcal B,\mathcal E)\), a coherent \(\mathcal B\)-module via \((b\varphi)(x)=\varphi(bx)\); since \(g\) is affine, \(\mathcal H=g_*\mathcal G\) for a coherent \(\mathcal O_{X'}\)-module \(\mathcal G\) (II, Ex. 5.17). Put \(\mathcal H'=\mathcal Hom(\mathcal B\otimes\mathcal L^m,\mathcal E)=\mathcal H\otimes\mathcal L^{-m}=g_*(\mathcal G\otimes\mathcal M^{-m})\). The map \(\mathcal Hom(\alpha,\mathcal E)\colon\mathcal H'\to\mathcal E^{\varrho}\) is an isomorphism at \(\eta\) (Hom of coherent sheaves commutes with localization). Let \(\mathcal K\) and \(\mathcal C\) be its kernel and cokernel; they are coherent and vanish at \(\eta\), so their supports are proper closed subsets of \(W\), which are good by minimality; and (viewed on \(Y\) via \(j_*\)) \(\mathcal K,\mathcal C\) have the vanishing property. Moreover by Ex. 4.1 and the projection formula

\[ H^l(W,\mathcal H'\otimes\mathcal L^n)=H^l(X',\mathcal G\otimes\mathcal M^{n-m})=0\qquad(l>0,\ n\gg0) \]

because \(\mathcal M\) is ample on \(X'\) (5.3). Let \(\mathcal B'=\operatorname{im}(\mathcal H'\to\mathcal E^\varrho)\). From \(0\to\mathcal K\to\mathcal H'\to\mathcal B'\to0\): \(H^l(\mathcal B'\otimes\mathcal L^n)\) sits between \(H^l(\mathcal H'\otimes\mathcal L^n)=0\) and \(H^{l+1}(\mathcal K\otimes\mathcal L^n)=0\), so vanishes for \(l>0\), \(n\gg0\); from \(0\to\mathcal B'\to\mathcal E^\varrho\to\mathcal C\to0\), \(H^l(\mathcal E^\varrho\otimes\mathcal L^n)=H^l(\mathcal E\otimes\mathcal L^n)^{\varrho}\) lies between \(H^l(\mathcal B'\otimes\mathcal L^n)\) and \(H^l(\mathcal C\otimes\mathcal L^n)\), both \(0\) for \(l>0\), \(n\gg0\). So \(j_*\mathcal E\) does have the vanishing property: contradiction. Hence every closed subset, in particular \(Y\), is good, and \(\mathcal L\) is ample by 5.3(ii)\(\Rightarrow\)(i). (Compare EGA II, 6.6.2, and Hartshorne [5, Ch. I, Section 4].) ∎

Exercise 5.8

Problem

Prove that every one-dimensional proper scheme \(X\) over an algebraically closed field \(k\) is projective.

  1. (a)

    If \(X\) is irreducible and nonsingular, then \(X\) is projective by (II, 6.7).

  2. (b)

    If \(X\) is integral, let \(\tilde X\) be its normalization (II, Ex. 3.8). Show that \(\tilde X\) is complete and nonsingular, hence projective by (a). Let \(f\colon\tilde X\to X\) be the projection. Let \(\mathcal L\) be a very ample invertible sheaf on \(\tilde X\). Show that there is an effective divisor \(D=\sum P_i\) on \(\tilde X\) with \(\mathcal L(D)\cong\mathcal L\), and such that \(f(P_i)\) is a nonsingular point of \(X\), for each \(i\). Conclude that there is an invertible sheaf \(\mathcal L_0\) on \(X\) with \(f^*\mathcal L_0\cong\mathcal L\). Then use (Ex. 5.7d), (II, 7.6) and (II, 5.16.1) to show that \(X\) is projective.

  3. (c)

    If \(X\) is reduced, but not necessarily irreducible, let \(X_1,\dots,X_r\) be the irreducible components of \(X\). Use (Ex. 4.5) to show \(\operatorname{Pic}X\to\bigoplus\operatorname{Pic}X_i\) is surjective. Then use (Ex. 5.7c) to show \(X\) is projective.

  4. (d)

    Finally, if \(X\) is any one-dimensional proper scheme over \(k\), use (2.7) and (Ex. 4.6) to show that \(\operatorname{Pic}X\to\operatorname{Pic}X_{\mathrm{red}}\) is surjective. Then use (Ex. 5.7b) to show that \(X\) is projective.

Solution

(a) A proper scheme that is irreducible and nonsingular (regular local rings are domains) is an integral nonsingular complete curve; by (II, 6.7) it is projective. (If \(\dim X=0\) it is a point \(\operatorname{Spec}k\).)

Preliminary. If \(\mathcal M\) is an ample invertible sheaf on a proper \(k\)-scheme \(X\), then by (II, 7.6) some \(\mathcal M^m\) (\(m>0\)) is very ample over \(k\), i.e. gives an immersion \(X\to\mathbf P^N_k\), which is a closed immersion since \(X\) is proper (II, 4.9; cf. II, 5.16.1). So a proper \(X\) with an ample invertible sheaf is projective.

(b) \(f\colon\tilde X\to X\) is finite (II, Ex. 3.8), so \(\tilde X\) is proper over \(k\); it is integral, of dimension \(1\), normal, so its local rings at closed points are normal one-dimensional noetherian local domains, i.e. DVRs: \(\tilde X\) is nonsingular, hence projective by (a). Let \(\mathcal L\) be very ample on \(\tilde X\), giving \(\tilde X\subseteq\mathbf P^N_k\).

Let \(\Sigma\subset X\) be the set of singular (closed) points; it is finite, being a proper closed subset of the integral curve \(X\) (II, 8.16). Over the open set \(U=X\setminus\Sigma\) of nonsingular points (local rings DVRs, integrally closed), \(\mathcal O_X\to f_*\mathcal O_{\tilde X}\) is an isomorphism, so \(f^{-1}(U)\to U\) is an isomorphism. The set \(f^{-1}(\Sigma)\) is finite. Choose a hyperplane \(H\subset\mathbf P^N\) containing none of the finitely many points of \(f^{-1}(\Sigma)\) (the hyperplanes through a given point form a proper closed subset of the dual space, and \(k\) is infinite); then \(H\not\supseteq\tilde X\), and \(H\cap\tilde X\) is the zero scheme of a nonzero section of \(\mathcal L\), an effective divisor \(D=\sum P_i\) with \(\mathcal L(D)\cong\mathcal L\) and \(P_i\notin f^{-1}(\Sigma)\), i.e. \(f(P_i)\) nonsingular.

Put \(D_0=f(\operatorname{Supp}D)\subset U\). As \(f\) is an isomorphism over \(U\), \(f^{-1}(D_0)=\operatorname{Supp}D\), and the ideal sheaf \(\mathcal I_D\) of \(D\) corresponds on \(f^{-1}(U)\cong U\) to an invertible ideal sheaf \(\mathcal I_U\subseteq\mathcal O_U\). Define \(\mathcal I\subseteq\mathcal O_X\) by \(\mathcal I|_U=\mathcal I_U\) and \(\mathcal I|_{X\setminus D_0}=\mathcal O\); these agree on \(U\setminus D_0\) (both are \(\mathcal O\)), so \(\mathcal I\) is an invertible ideal sheaf. The map \(f^*\mathcal I\to\mathcal O_{\tilde X}\) is an isomorphism onto \(\mathcal I_D\): on \(f^{-1}(U)\) by construction and on \(\tilde X\setminus\operatorname{Supp}D=f^{-1}(X\setminus D_0)\) because both sides are \(\mathcal O\). Hence with \(\mathcal L_0=\mathcal I^{-1}\) we get \(f^*\mathcal L_0\cong\mathcal I_D^{-1}=\mathcal L(D)\cong\mathcal L\).

Now \(f\) is finite and surjective, and \(f^*\mathcal L_0\cong\mathcal L\) is ample on \(\tilde X\); by Ex. 5.7(d), \(\mathcal L_0\) is ample on \(X\), which is proper, so \(X\) is projective by the Preliminary.

(c) Let \(X\) be reduced, with irreducible components \(X_i\) (reduced structure). Each \(X_i\) is an integral proper scheme of dimension \(\le1\), so projective by (a), (b) (a \(0\)-dimensional component is a point). The sets \(X_i\cap X_j\) (\(i\ne j\)) are proper closed subsets of the irreducible \(X_i\) of dimension \(\le1\), hence finite sets of closed points; let \(T_0\) be the finite set of points lying on at least two components. Since \(X\) is reduced, \(\mathcal O_X\to\bigoplus_i(\iota_i)_*\mathcal O_{X_i}\) (\(\iota_i\colon X_i\to X\)) is injective (an element vanishing in each \(\mathcal O_{X_i}\) lies in all minimal primes, hence is \(0\)), so we get an exact sequence of sheaves of abelian groups

\[ 0\to\mathcal O_X^*\to\bigoplus_i(\iota_i)_*\mathcal O_{X_i}^*\to\mathcal T\to0. \]

At a point \(x\notin T_0\), lying on exactly one component \(X_i\), \(X\) coincides near \(x\) with \(X_i\) (both reduced), and the other summands are trivial near \(x\), so the first map is an isomorphism on stalks at \(x\): \(\mathcal T\) is supported on the finite set \(T_0\) of closed points, hence \(H^1(X,\mathcal T)=0\) (Thm. 2.7 applied to \(T_0\) of dimension \(0\), and Lemma 2.10). The long exact sequence gives that \(H^1(X,\mathcal O_X^*)\to\bigoplus H^1(X,(\iota_i)_*\mathcal O_{X_i}^*)=\bigoplus H^1(X_i,\mathcal O^*_{X_i})\) is surjective, and by Ex. 4.5 (compatibly with restriction of line bundles) this is \(\operatorname{Pic}X\to\bigoplus\operatorname{Pic}X_i\).

Choose very ample \(\mathcal L_i\) on each \(X_i\) and \(\mathcal L\) on \(X\) with \(\mathcal L|_{X_i}\cong\mathcal L_i\) for all \(i\). By Ex. 5.7(c) \(\mathcal L\) is ample on \(X\); \(X\) is proper, so projective.

(d) Let \(X\) be any one-dimensional proper scheme, \(\mathcal N\) its nilradical, \(\mathcal N^m=0\). Let \(X^{(j)}\) be the closed subscheme with ideal \(\mathcal N^j\), \(1\le j\le m\); \(X^{(1)}=X_{\mathrm{red}}\), \(X^{(m)}=X\), all with the same underlying space. \(X^{(j)}\) is a closed subscheme of \(X^{(j+1)}\) with ideal sheaf \(\mathcal J_j=\mathcal N^j/\mathcal N^{j+1}\), and \(\mathcal J_j^2=0\) (as \(2j\ge j+1\)). By Ex. 4.6 there is an exact sequence \(\operatorname{Pic}X^{(j+1)}\to\operatorname{Pic}X^{(j)}\to H^2(X,\mathcal J_j)\), and \(H^2(X,\mathcal J_j)=0\) by Grothendieck's theorem (2.7) since \(\dim X=1\). So every \(\operatorname{Pic}X^{(j+1)}\to\operatorname{Pic}X^{(j)}\) is surjective, and so is the composite \(\operatorname{Pic}X\to\operatorname{Pic}X_{\mathrm{red}}\). By (c) \(X_{\mathrm{red}}\) is projective; let \(\mathcal L'\) be an ample (e.g. very ample) invertible sheaf on it and \(\mathcal L\) on \(X\) with \(\mathcal L\otimes\mathcal O_{X_{\mathrm{red}}}\cong\mathcal L'\). By Ex. 5.7(b) \(\mathcal L\) is ample on \(X\), hence \(X\) is projective (Preliminary). ∎

Exercise 5.9

Problem

A Nonprojective Scheme. We show the result of (Ex. 5.8) is false in dimension \(2\). Let \(k\) be an algebraically closed field of characteristic \(0\), and let \(X=\mathbf P^2_k\). Let \(\omega\) be the sheaf of differential \(2\)-forms on \(X\) (II, Section 8). Define an infinitesimal extension \(X'\) of \(X\) by \(\omega\) by giving the element \(\xi\in H^1(X,\omega\otimes\mathcal T)\) defined as follows (Ex. 4.10). Let \(x_0,x_1,x_2\) be the homogeneous coordinates of \(X\), let \(U_0,U_1,U_2\) be the standard open covering, and let \(\xi_{ij}=(x_i/x_j)\,d(x_j/x_i)\). This gives a Cech \(1\)-cocycle with values in \(\Omega^1_X\), and since \(\dim X=2\), we have \(\omega\otimes\mathcal T\cong\Omega^1\) (II, Ex. 5.16b). Now use the exact sequence

\[ \dots\to H^1(X,\omega)\to\operatorname{Pic}X'\to\operatorname{Pic}X\xrightarrow{\ \delta\ }H^2(X,\omega)\to\dots \]

of (Ex. 4.6) and (4.5). We have \(\omega\cong\mathcal O_X(-3)\) (II, 8.20.1), so \(H^2(X,\omega)\cong k\). Since \(\operatorname{char}k=0\), you need only show \(\delta(\mathcal L(1))\ne0\), which can be done by calculating in Cech cohomology. Since \(H^1(X,\omega)=0\), we see that \(\operatorname{Pic}X'=0\). In particular, \(X'\) has no ample invertible sheaves, so is not projective.

Note. In fact, this result can be generalized to show that for any nonsingular projective surface \(X\) over an algebraically closed field \(k\) of characteristic \(0\), there is an infinitesimal extension \(X'\) of \(X\) by \(\omega\), such that \(X'\) is not projective over \(k\). Indeed, let \(D\) be an ample divisor on \(X\). Then \(D\) determines an element \(c_1(D)\in H^1(X,\Omega^1)\) which we use to define \(X'\), as above. Then for any divisor \(E\) on \(X\) one can show that \(\delta(\mathcal L(E))=(D.E)\), where \((D.E)\) is the intersection number (Chapter V), considered as an element of \(k\). Hence if \(E\) is ample, \(\delta(\mathcal L(E))\ne0\). Therefore \(X'\) has no ample divisors. On the other hand, over a field of characteristic \(p>0\), a proper scheme \(X\) is projective if and only if \(X_{\mathrm{red}}\) is!

Solution

Construction. On \(U_i\) (\(\cong\mathbf A^2\), affine) let \(\mathcal A_i=\mathcal O_{U_i}\oplus\omega|_{U_i}\) with multiplication \((f,a)(g,b)=(fg,fb+ga)\) (\(\omega^2=0\)); \((U_i,\mathcal A_i)\) is the affine scheme \(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal O(U_i)\oplus\omega(U_i))\) (same underlying space, localizations \(B_h\oplus M_h\)). We identify \(\Omega^1\cong\mathcal Hom(\Omega^1,\omega)=\omega\otimes\mathcal T\) by \(\Phi(\eta)(\nu)=\eta\wedge\nu\); this is an isomorphism (in a local basis \(e_1,e_2\) of \(\Omega^1\), \(\eta=ae_1+be_2\) goes to \(e_1\mapsto-be_{12}\), \(e_2\mapsto ae_{12}\)). (Any other identification differs by a unit of \(\mathcal O_X\), i.e. by a nonzero constant, which does not affect the argument.) Put \(\eta_{ij}=\xi_{ij}=\frac{x_i}{x_j}d\!\left(\frac{x_j}{x_i}\right)=d\log\frac{x_j}{x_i}\in\Omega^1(U_{ij})\); then \(\eta_{ij}+\eta_{jk}=\eta_{ik}\), so \((\eta_{ij})\) is a cocycle, and \(D_{ij}(f):=\eta_{ij}\wedge df\) is an \(\mathcal O\)-derivation \(\mathcal O_{U_{ij}}\to\omega|_{U_{ij}}\). The maps

\[ \varphi_{ij}\colon\mathcal A_j|_{U_{ij}}\to\mathcal A_i|_{U_{ij}},\qquad(f,a)\mapsto(f,\,a+D_{ij}(f)) \]

are ring isomorphisms (\((fg,fb+ga+D(fg))=(f,a+Df)(g,b+Dg)\) because \(D(fg)=fDg+gDf\) and \(\omega^2=0\)), preserve the ideal \(0\oplus\omega\) (acting as the identity on it) and the quotient \(\mathcal O\), and satisfy \(\varphi_{ij}\varphi_{jk}=\varphi_{ik}\), \(\varphi_{ii}=\mathrm{id}\) because \(D_{ij}+D_{jk}=D_{ik}\). Gluing gives a scheme \(X'\) with \(\mathcal O_{X'}|_{U_i}\cong\mathcal A_i\) (isomorphisms \(\psi_i\), \(\psi_i\psi_j^{-1}=\varphi_{ij}\)), the same underlying space as \(X\), and an ideal \(\omega\subset\mathcal O_{X'}\) with \(\omega^2=0\) and \(\mathcal O_{X'}/\omega=\mathcal O_X\): this is the infinitesimal extension of \(X\) by \(\omega\) with class \(\xi\) (Ex. 4.10, II Ex. 8.7). \(X'\) is of finite type over \(k\) (the rings \(\mathcal O(U_i)\oplus\omega(U_i)\) are finitely generated \(k\)-algebras) and \(X'_{\mathrm{red}}=X\). \(X'\) is proper over \(k\) by the valuative criteria (II, 4.3, 4.7): any morphism from the spectrum of a reduced ring (\(K\) a field or \(R\) a DVR) to \(X'\) factors through \(X'_{\mathrm{red}}=X\), where existence and uniqueness of liftings hold since \(X\) is proper.

The exact sequence. By Ex. 4.6, with \(\mathcal I=\omega\), \(X_0=X\), there is an exact sequence of sheaves \(0\to\omega\to\mathcal O_{X'}^*\to\mathcal O_X^*\to0\), \(a\mapsto1+a\), and so \(H^1(X,\omega)\to\operatorname{Pic}X'\to\operatorname{Pic}X\xrightarrow{\delta}H^2(X,\omega)\) exact. Here \(\omega\cong\mathcal O(-3)\), so by Thm. 5.1 \(H^1(X,\omega)=0\) and \(H^2(X,\omega)\cong k\). Hence \(\operatorname{Pic}X'\to\operatorname{Pic}X\) is injective with image \(\ker\delta\). Now \(\operatorname{Pic}X=\mathbf Z\cdot[\mathcal O(1)]\) (II, 6.4) and \(\delta\) is a homomorphism, so \(\delta(\mathcal O(m))=m\,\delta(\mathcal O(1))\); as \(\operatorname{char}k=0\) and \(H^2(X,\omega)\cong k\), this is nonzero for all \(m\ne0\) as soon as \(\delta(\mathcal O(1))\ne0\). Then \(\ker\delta=0\) and \(\operatorname{Pic}X'=0\).

Computation of \(\delta(\mathcal O(1))\) by Cech cochains. Use the open affine cover \(\mathfrak U=(U_i)\). All finite intersections \(V\) are affine, and \(\mathcal O_{X'}^*(V)\to\mathcal O_X^*(V)\) is onto (in a chart \(V\subseteq U_i\), \(\mathcal O_{X'}(V)=\mathcal O(V)\oplus\omega(V)\) and \((g,a)\) is a unit iff \(g\) is). So the sequence of Cech complexes of \(0\to\omega\to\mathcal O_{X'}^*\to\mathcal O_X^*\to0\) is exact, and the connecting homomorphism on Cech cohomology is compatible with that of derived-functor cohomology (cf. the proof of Thm. 4.5); moreover \(\check H^2(\mathfrak U,\omega)\cong H^2(X,\omega)\) by Thm. 4.5 (\(\omega\) quasi-coherent). So it suffices to show that a Cech \(2\)-cocycle representing \(\delta(\mathcal O(1))\) is not a coboundary.

\(\mathcal O(1)\) has the generators \(e_i=x_i\) on \(U_i\), \(e_j=g_{ij}e_i\) with \(g_{ij}=x_j/x_i\); \(g_{ik}=g_{ij}g_{jk}\). Lift \(g_{ij}\) to \(\tilde g_{ij}\in\mathcal O_{X'}^*(U_{ij})\) by \(\psi_i(\tilde g_{ij})=(g_{ij},0)\). Then \(\delta[g]\) is the class of the \(2\)-cocycle \(c_{ijk}=\tilde g_{ij}\tilde g_{jk}\tilde g_{ik}^{-1}-1\in\omega(U_{ijk})\). Compute in the chart \(i\): \(\psi_i(\tilde g_{jk})=\varphi_{ij}(g_{jk},0)=(g_{jk},D_{ij}(g_{jk}))\), so

\[ \psi_i\bigl(\tilde g_{ij}\tilde g_{jk}\tilde g_{ik}^{-1}\bigr)=(g_{ij},0)(g_{jk},D_{ij}g_{jk})(g_{ik}^{-1},0)=\Bigl(1,\ g_{ij}g_{ik}^{-1}D_{ij}(g_{jk})\Bigr)=\Bigl(1,\ \frac{D_{ij}(g_{jk})}{g_{jk}}\Bigr), \]

so \(c_{ijk}=\eta_{ij}\wedge d\log g_{jk}=\eta_{ij}\wedge\eta_{jk}\). For \((i,j,k)=(0,1,2)\) put \(u=x_1/x_0\), \(v=x_2/x_0\), coordinates on \(U_0\), units on \(U_{012}\). Then \(\eta_{01}=d\log u\), \(\eta_{12}=d\log(v/u)=d\log v-d\log u\), and

\[ c_{012}=d\log u\wedge d\log v=u^{-1}v^{-1}\,du\wedge dv\in\omega(U_{012})=k[u^{\pm1},v^{\pm1}]\,du\wedge dv. \]

(The other convention for \(\mathcal O(1)\) only changes the sign.) The Cech group \(C^2(\mathfrak U,\omega)=\omega(U_{012})\) and the coboundaries are \(\{\alpha_{12}-\alpha_{02}+\alpha_{01}\}\) with \(\alpha_{ij}\in\omega(U_{ij})\). Describe these in the monomial basis \(u^av^b\,du\wedge dv\) of \(\omega(U_{012})\):

  • •

    \(U_{01}=\{u\ne0\}\subset U_0\): \(\omega(U_{01})=k[u^{\pm1},v]\,du\wedge dv\): monomials with \(b\ge0\).

  • •

    \(U_{02}=\{v\ne0\}\subset U_0\): \(\omega(U_{02})=k[u,v^{\pm1}]\,du\wedge dv\): monomials with \(a\ge0\).

  • •

    \(U_{12}\subset U_1\) with coordinates \(s=x_0/x_1=u^{-1}\), \(t=x_2/x_1=v/u\), \(U_{12}=\{t\ne0\}\): \(\omega(U_{12})=k[s,t^{\pm1}]\,ds\wedge dt\). Since \(ds\wedge dt=-u^{-3}du\wedge dv\), \(s^mt^n\,ds\wedge dt=-u^{-m-n-3}v^n\,du\wedge dv\) (\(m\ge0\)): monomials with \(a+b=-m-3\le-3\).

Each of the three spaces is spanned by monomials, hence so is their sum, which is spanned by the monomials with \(b\ge0\) or \(a\ge0\) or \(a+b\le-3\). The monomial \(u^{-1}v^{-1}\) (\(a=b=-1\)) has \(b<0\), \(a<0\), \(a+b=-2>-3\), so it does not occur. Hence \(c_{012}\) is not a coboundary and \(\delta(\mathcal O(1))\ne0\) in \(\check H^2(\mathfrak U,\omega)=H^2(X,\omega)\).

Conclusion. By the above \(\operatorname{Pic}X'=0\), i.e. \(\mathcal O_{X'}\) is the only invertible sheaf on \(X'\). If \(X'\) were projective over \(k\), it would carry a very ample invertible sheaf \(\mathcal O_{X'}(1)\cong\mathcal O_{X'}\) defining a closed immersion \(X'\to\mathbf P^N_k\) by global sections \(s_0,\dots,s_N\in\Gamma(X',\mathcal O_{X'})\). Restricting to \(X'_{\mathrm{red}}=X=\mathbf P^2\), these become global sections of \(\mathcal O_X\), i.e. constants, so \(X=\mathbf P^2\to\mathbf P^N\) would be constant, while it is a closed immersion (composite of closed immersions \(X\to X'\to\mathbf P^N\)), hence injective on points: contradiction. So \(X'\) is a proper, two-dimensional, non-projective \(k\)-scheme, showing the result of Ex. 5.8 fails in dimension \(2\). ∎

Exercise 5.10

Problem

Let \(X\) be a projective scheme over a noetherian ring \(A\), and let \(\mathcal F^1\to\mathcal F^2\to\dots\to\mathcal F^r\) be an exact sequence of coherent sheaves on \(X\). Show that there is an integer \(n_0\), such that for all \(n\ge n_0\), the sequence of global sections

\[ \Gamma(X,\mathcal F^1(n))\to\Gamma(X,\mathcal F^2(n))\to\dots\to\Gamma(X,\mathcal F^r(n)) \]

is exact.

Solution

Let \(d_j\colon\mathcal F^j\to\mathcal F^{j+1}\) (\(1\le j\le r-1\)), \(\mathcal Z^j=\ker d_j\), \(\mathcal B^{j+1}=\operatorname{im}d_j\subseteq\mathcal F^{j+1}\); all are coherent. Exactness at \(\mathcal F^j\) for \(2\le j\le r-1\) means \(\mathcal B^j=\mathcal Z^j\). We have short exact sequences of coherent sheaves

\[ 0\to\mathcal Z^j\to\mathcal F^j\to\mathcal B^{j+1}\to0\qquad(1\le j\le r-1). \]

By Thm. 5.2(b) applied to the finitely many sheaves \(\mathcal Z^1,\dots,\mathcal Z^{r-1}\) there is \(n_0\) with \(H^1(X,\mathcal Z^j(n))=0\) for all \(j\) and all \(n\ge n_0\). Twisting by \(n\) (exact) and taking global sections, for \(n\ge n_0\) we get exact sequences

\[ 0\to\Gamma(\mathcal Z^j(n))\to\Gamma(\mathcal F^j(n))\to\Gamma(\mathcal B^{j+1}(n))\to0\qquad(1\le j\le r-1). \]

Fix \(2\le j\le r-1\) and \(n\ge n_0\). The map \(\Gamma(\mathcal F^{j-1}(n))\to\Gamma(\mathcal F^j(n))\) is the composite of the surjection \(\Gamma(\mathcal F^{j-1}(n))\to\Gamma(\mathcal B^j(n))=\Gamma(\mathcal Z^j(n))\) (the sequence for \(j-1\)) with the inclusion \(\Gamma(\mathcal Z^j(n))\hookrightarrow\Gamma(\mathcal F^j(n))\); so its image is \(\Gamma(\mathcal Z^j(n))\). The map \(\Gamma(\mathcal F^j(n))\to\Gamma(\mathcal F^{j+1}(n))\) is the composite of the surjection onto \(\Gamma(\mathcal B^{j+1}(n))\) with the injection \(\Gamma(\mathcal B^{j+1}(n))\hookrightarrow\Gamma(\mathcal F^{j+1}(n))\) (left exactness of \(\Gamma\)), so its kernel is the kernel of \(\Gamma(\mathcal F^j(n))\to\Gamma(\mathcal B^{j+1}(n))\), which is \(\Gamma(\mathcal Z^j(n))\). Image equals kernel at every interior spot, so the sequence of global sections is exact for all \(n\ge n_0\). ∎

6. Ext groups and sheaves

Throughout, \(\operatorname{Ext}^i(\mathcal F,\cdot)\) and \(\mathcal{E}xt^i(\mathcal F,\cdot)\) are the right derived functors of \(\Hom(\mathcal F,\cdot)\) and \(\mathcal{H}om(\mathcal F,\cdot)\) on \(\mathfrak{Mod}(X)\), as in the text. We use freely: the long exact sequences in the second variable and (Prop. 6.4) in the first variable, which are natural for morphisms of short exact sequences; \(\operatorname{Ext}^i(\mathcal F,\mathcal I)=0=\mathcal{E}xt^i(\mathcal F,\mathcal I)\) for \(i>0\) and \(\mathcal I\) injective; additivity of \(\operatorname{Ext}^i\) and \(\mathcal{E}xt^i\) in each variable; and, for an affine scheme \(\operatorname{Spec}A\) regarded as a ringed space, the module-theoretic \(\operatorname{Ext}^i_A\) (a ring \(A\) is the special case of a one-point space). For a ring \(A\) and \(A\)-modules the analogue of Prop. 6.4 (long exact sequence in the first variable) holds, with the same proof.

Exercise 6.1

Problem

Let \((X,\mathcal O_X)\) be a ringed space, and let \(\mathcal F',\mathcal F''\in\mathfrak{Mod}(X)\). An extension of \(\mathcal F''\) by \(\mathcal F'\) is a short exact sequence

\[ 0\to\mathcal F'\to\mathcal F\to\mathcal F''\to0 \]

in \(\mathfrak{Mod}(X)\). Two extensions are isomorphic if there is an isomorphism of the short exact sequences, inducing the identity maps on \(\mathcal F'\) and \(\mathcal F''\). Given an extension as above consider the long exact sequence arising from \(\Hom(\mathcal F'',\cdot)\), in particular the map

\[ \delta\colon\Hom(\mathcal F'',\mathcal F'')\to\operatorname{Ext}^1(\mathcal F'',\mathcal F'), \]

and let \(\xi\in\operatorname{Ext}^1(\mathcal F'',\mathcal F')\) be \(\delta(1_{\mathcal F''})\). Show that this process gives a one-to-one correspondence between isomorphism classes of extensions of \(\mathcal F''\) by \(\mathcal F'\), and elements of the group \(\operatorname{Ext}^1(\mathcal F'',\mathcal F')\). For more details, see, e.g., Hilton and Stammbach [1, Ch. III].

Solution

Write \(\iota\colon\mathcal F'\to\mathcal F\) and \(p\colon\mathcal F\to\mathcal F''\) for the maps of an extension \(E\), and let \(\delta_E\) be its connecting map. Fix once and for all an embedding of \(\mathcal F'\) in an injective object (Prop. 2.2 of the text; \(\mathfrak{Mod}(X)\) has enough injectives): an exact sequence

\[ 0\to\mathcal F'\xrightarrow{\ \jmath\ }\mathcal I\xrightarrow{\ \pi\ }\mathcal Q\to0,\qquad\mathcal I\text{ injective}. \]

Applying \(\Hom(\mathcal F'',\cdot)\) and using \(\operatorname{Ext}^1(\mathcal F'',\mathcal I)=0\) we get an exact sequence

\[ \Hom(\mathcal F'',\mathcal I)\xrightarrow{\pi_*}\Hom(\mathcal F'',\mathcal Q)\xrightarrow{\ \partial\ }\operatorname{Ext}^1(\mathcal F'',\mathcal F')\to0 .\tag{1} \]

So \(\partial\) identifies \(\operatorname{Ext}^1(\mathcal F'',\mathcal F')\) with \(\Hom(\mathcal F'',\mathcal Q)/\pi_*\Hom(\mathcal F'',\mathcal I)\).

Step 1: from an extension to a class. Let \(E\) be an extension. Since \(\mathcal I\) is injective, the monomorphism \(\iota\) and the map \(\jmath\colon\mathcal F'\to\mathcal I\) give \(g\colon\mathcal F\to\mathcal I\) with \(g\iota=\jmath\). Then \(g\) induces \(h\colon\mathcal F''=\mathcal F/\mathcal F'\to\mathcal I/\mathcal F'=\mathcal Q\) with \(hp=\pi g\), and \((1_{\mathcal F'},g,h)\) is a morphism of short exact sequences from \(E\) to \(E_{\mathcal I}\colon0\to\mathcal F'\to\mathcal I\to\mathcal Q\to0\). The connecting maps are natural for morphisms of short exact sequences (this is part of the statement that the long exact sequence is a morphism of \(\delta\)-functors); for the morphism \((1,g,h)\), with \(1\) on the first term, this gives the commutative square

\[ \delta_E=\partial\circ h_*\colon\Hom(\mathcal F'',\mathcal F'')\to\operatorname{Ext}^1(\mathcal F'',\mathcal F'), \]

where \(h_*\colon\Hom(\mathcal F'',\mathcal F'')\to\Hom(\mathcal F'',\mathcal Q)\) is composition with \(h\) and \(\partial=\delta_{E_{\mathcal I}}\). Evaluating at \(1_{\mathcal F''}\):

\[ \xi_E=\delta_E(1_{\mathcal F''})=\partial(h).\tag{2} \]

Another choice \(g'\) of the extension of \(\jmath\) differs from \(g\) by a map \(g'-g\colon\mathcal F\to\mathcal I\) vanishing on \(\mathcal F'\), hence of the form \(u p\) with \(u\colon\mathcal F''\to\mathcal I\); then \(h'=h+\pi u\), so \(h'\equiv h\) modulo \(\pi_*\Hom(\mathcal F'',\mathcal I)\), consistent with (2). If \(\theta\colon E\to E'\) is an isomorphism of extensions, then \(g'\theta\) is an admissible choice of \(g\) for \(E\) and gives the same \(h\); so \(\xi_E=\xi_{E'}\). Thus \(E\mapsto\xi_E\) is well defined on isomorphism classes.

Step 2: from \(h\) to an extension. Let \(h\in\Hom(\mathcal F'',\mathcal Q)\) be arbitrary. Define \(\mathcal F_h=\operatorname{Ker}(\mathcal I\oplus\mathcal F''\to\mathcal Q,\ (a,b)\mapsto\pi(a)-h(b))\) (the fibre product \(\mathcal I\times_{\mathcal Q}\mathcal F''\)), \(\iota_h(a')=(\jmath a',0)\), \(p_h(a,b)=b\). The sequence \(E_h\colon0\to\mathcal F'\to\mathcal F_h\to\mathcal F''\to0\) is exact: \(\iota_h\) is injective and lands in \(\mathcal F_h\) since \(\pi\jmath=0\); \(p_h\iota_h=0\) and \(\operatorname{Ker}p_h=\{(a,0):\pi(a)=0\}=\iota_h(\mathcal F')\) because \(\operatorname{Ker}\pi=\jmath(\mathcal F')\) and \(\jmath\) is injective; \(p_h\) is surjective on stalks: given \(b\in\mathcal F''_x\), \(h(b)\in\mathcal Q_x=\pi(\mathcal I_x)\), so \(h(b)=\pi(a)\) for some \(a\), and \((a,b)\in(\mathcal F_h)_x\). For this extension we may take \(g=\mathrm{pr}_1\colon\mathcal F_h\to\mathcal I\) (it satisfies \(g\iota_h=\jmath\)), and the induced map on quotients is \((a,b)\mapsto\pi(a)=h(b)\), i.e. \(h\) itself. By (2), \(\xi_{E_h}=\partial(h)\).

Surjectivity. By (1), every element of \(\operatorname{Ext}^1(\mathcal F'',\mathcal F')\) is \(\partial(h)\) for some \(h\), hence is \(\xi_{E_h}\).

Injectivity. Suppose \(\xi_E=\xi_{E'}\). Choose \(g,h\) for \(E\) and \(g',h'\) for \(E'\) as in Step 1. By (2) and (1), \(h'-h=\pi u\) for some \(u\colon\mathcal F''\to\mathcal I\). Replacing \(g\) by \(g+up\) (which still extends \(\jmath\), and changes \(h\) to \(h+\pi u=h'\)) we may assume \(h=h'\). Now \((g,p)\colon\mathcal F\to\mathcal I\oplus\mathcal F''\) lands in \(\mathcal F_h\) (because \(\pi g=hp\)) and gives a morphism of extensions \(E\to E_h\) which is the identity on \(\mathcal F'\) and \(\mathcal F''\); by the five lemma it is an isomorphism. Likewise \(E'\cong E_{h}\). Hence \(E\cong E'\).

Therefore \(E\mapsto\xi_E\) is a bijection from isomorphism classes of extensions onto \(\operatorname{Ext}^1(\mathcal F'',\mathcal F')\). ∎

Exercise 6.2

Problem

Let \(X=\mathbf P^1_k\), with \(k\) an infinite field.

  1. (a)

    Show that there does not exist a projective object \(\mathcal P\in\mathfrak{Mod}(X)\), together with a surjective map \(\mathcal P\to\mathcal O_X\to0\). [Hint: Consider surjections of the form \(\mathcal O_V\to k(x)\to0\), where \(x\in X\) is a closed point, \(V\) is an open neighborhood of \(x\), and \(\mathcal O_V=j_!(\mathcal O_X|_V)\), where \(j\colon V\to X\) is the inclusion.]

  2. (b)

    Show that there does not exist a projective object \(\mathcal P\) in either \(\mathfrak{Qco}(X)\) or \(\mathfrak{Coh}(X)\) together with a surjection \(\mathcal P\to\mathcal O_X\to0\). [Hint: Consider surjections of the form \(\mathcal L\to\mathcal L\otimes k(x)\to0\), where \(x\in X\) is a closed point, and \(\mathcal L\) is an invertible sheaf on \(X\).]

Solution

Here \(k(x)\) is the skyscraper sheaf at the closed point \(x\) with stalk the residue field \(\kappa(x)\); it is quasi-coherent and coherent, and \(\varepsilon\colon\mathcal O_X\to k(x)\), \(s\mapsto s(x)\), is a surjection. “Projective” means that \(\Hom(\mathcal P,\cdot)\) is exact on the category in question; in particular maps from \(\mathcal P\) lift along every surjection of sheaves in that category (a surjective map of sheaves in \(\mathfrak{Qco}\) or \(\mathfrak{Coh}\) is an epimorphism there). The hypothesis that \(k\) is infinite will not be needed: we only use that \(X\) is an integral scheme with infinitely many closed points (true for \(\mathbf P^1_k\) over any field).

(a) Suppose \(\pi\colon\mathcal P\to\mathcal O_X\) is surjective with \(\mathcal P\) projective in \(\mathfrak{Mod}(X)\). Fix a closed point \(x\). Since \(\pi_x\colon\mathcal P_x\to\mathcal O_{X,x}\) is surjective, there are an open neighborhood \(U\) of \(x\) and \(s\in\mathcal P(U)\) with \(\pi(s)=1|_U\). The set \(U\) is the complement of finitely many closed points, so it contains a closed point \(y\neq x\). Put \(V=U\setminus\{y\}\), an open neighborhood of \(x\), let \(j\colon V\to X\) be the inclusion, \(\mathcal O_V=j_!(\mathcal O_X|_V)\subseteq\mathcal O_X\) (extension by zero, Ex. 1.19 of Ch. II), with inclusion \(\iota\), and let \(u=\varepsilon\iota\colon\mathcal O_V\to k(x)\). It is surjective: at \(x\) the stalk map is \(\mathcal O_{X,x}\to\kappa(x)\), and at any other point the stalk of \(k(x)\) is \(0\).

The map \(f=\varepsilon\pi\colon\mathcal P\to k(x)\) lifts, by projectivity, to \(\tilde f\colon\mathcal P\to\mathcal O_V\) with \(u\tilde f=f\). Now \(\tilde f(s)\in\mathcal O_V(U)\) is zero: indeed, \(\iota\tilde f(s)\in\mathcal O_X(U)\) has germ at \(y\) equal to the image of \(\tilde f(s)_y\in(\mathcal O_V)_y=0\) (as \(y\notin V\)), so \(\iota\tilde f(s)\) has zero germ at \(y\); since \(X\) is integral, \(\mathcal O_X(U)\to\mathcal O_{X,y}\) is injective, hence \(\iota\tilde f(s)=0\), and \(\iota\) is injective, so \(\tilde f(s)=0\). Therefore \(f(s)=u(\tilde f(s))=0\). But \(f(s)=\varepsilon(\pi(s))=\varepsilon(1)=1\neq0\) in \(k(x)(U)=\kappa(x)\). This contradiction shows no such \(\mathcal P\) exists.

(b) Let \(\mathcal A\) be \(\mathfrak{Qco}(X)\) or \(\mathfrak{Coh}(X)\), suppose \(\mathcal P\in\mathcal A\) is projective in \(\mathcal A\) and \(\pi\colon\mathcal P\to\mathcal O_X\) is surjective.

Step 1: a coherent piece. There is a coherent subsheaf \(\mathcal C\subseteq\mathcal P\) with \(\pi(\mathcal C)=\mathcal O_X\). If \(\mathcal A=\mathfrak{Coh}(X)\) take \(\mathcal C=\mathcal P\). If \(\mathcal A=\mathfrak{Qco}(X)\), \(\mathcal P\) is the directed union of its coherent subsheaves (Ex. 5.15(e) of Ch. II). The subsheaves \(\pi(\mathcal C_\alpha)\) of \(\mathcal O_X\) are quasi-coherent, form a directed family, and their union is \(\pi(\mathcal P)=\mathcal O_X\) (a union of subsheaves is computed on stalks). Cover \(X\) by finitely many open affines \(U_i=\operatorname{Spec}A_i\) and write \(\pi(\mathcal C_\alpha)|_{U_i}=\widetilde{I^{(i)}_\alpha}\) with ideals \(I^{(i)}_\alpha\subseteq A_i\). The ideals \(I^{(i)}_\alpha\) form a directed family in the noetherian ring \(A_i\), so there is a maximal one, \(I^{(i)}_{\alpha_i}\); by directedness it contains all the others (for any \(\alpha\) pick \(\beta\ge\alpha,\alpha_i\); then \(I^{(i)}_\beta\supseteq I^{(i)}_{\alpha_i}\) forces equality, so \(I^{(i)}_\alpha\subseteq I^{(i)}_{\alpha_i}\)). Hence \(\widetilde{I^{(i)}_{\alpha_i}}=\mathcal O_{U_i}\). Taking \(\alpha\ge\alpha_1,\dots,\alpha_r\) gives \(\pi(\mathcal C_\alpha)=\mathcal O_X\).

Step 2: a global section. Let \(\mathcal K=\operatorname{Ker}(\pi|_{\mathcal C}\colon\mathcal C\to\mathcal O_X)\), coherent. Twisting by \(\mathcal O(n)\) is exact: \(0\to\mathcal K(n)\to\mathcal C(n)\to\mathcal O(n)\to0\). By Thm. 5.2(b) there is \(n\ge0\) with \(H^1(X,\mathcal K(n))=0\), so \(\Gamma(X,\mathcal C(n))\to\Gamma(X,\mathcal O(n))\) is surjective. Fix a closed point \(x\). As \(\mathcal O(n)\) (\(n\ge0\)) is generated by global sections, there is \(\sigma\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal O(n))\) with \(\sigma(x)\neq0\) in \(\mathcal O(n)\otimes k(x)\). Choose \(c\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal C(n))\subseteq\Gamma(X,\mathcal P(n))\) mapping to \(\sigma\).

Step 3: twisting is an autoequivalence. The functor \(\otimes\mathcal O(n)\) is an autoequivalence of \(\mathcal A\) (with inverse \(\otimes\mathcal O(-n)\)), so \(\mathcal P(n)=\mathcal P\otimes\mathcal O(n)\) is projective in \(\mathcal A\) and \(\pi(n)\colon\mathcal P(n)\to\mathcal O(n)\) is surjective.

Step 4: contradiction. Let \(\mathcal L=\mathcal O(-1)\); then \(\Gamma(X,\mathcal L)=0\) (Thm. 5.1). Choose an isomorphism \(\theta\colon\mathcal O(n)\otimes k(x)\to\mathcal L\otimes k(x)\) (both are skyscraper sheaves at \(x\) with one-dimensional \(\kappa(x)\)-vector space as stalk). The map \(f=\theta\circ(\mathcal O(n)\to\mathcal O(n)\otimes k(x))\circ\pi(n)\colon\mathcal P(n)\to\mathcal L\otimes k(x)\) lifts, by projectivity of \(\mathcal P(n)\) and surjectivity of \(q\colon\mathcal L\to\mathcal L\otimes k(x)\), to \(\tilde f\colon\mathcal P(n)\to\mathcal L\) with \(q\tilde f=f\). Then \(\tilde f(c)\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal L)=0\), so \(f(c)=q(\tilde f(c))=0\). But \(f(c)=\theta(\sigma(x))\neq0\). Contradiction. So no such \(\mathcal P\) exists in \(\mathfrak{Qco}(X)\) or in \(\mathfrak{Coh}(X)\). ∎

Exercise 6.3

Problem

Let \(X\) be a noetherian scheme, and let \(\mathcal F,\mathcal G\in\mathfrak{Mod}(X)\).

  1. (a)

    If \(\mathcal F,\mathcal G\) are both coherent, then \(\mathcal{E}xt^i(\mathcal F,\mathcal G)\) is coherent, for all \(i\ge0\).

  2. (b)

    If \(\mathcal F\) is coherent and \(\mathcal G\) is quasi-coherent, then \(\mathcal{E}xt^i(\mathcal F,\mathcal G)\) is quasi-coherent, for all \(i\ge0\).

Solution

Being coherent (resp. quasi-coherent) is a local property, and \(\mathcal{E}xt^i(\mathcal F,\mathcal G)|_U\cong\mathcal{E}xt^i_U(\mathcal F|_U,\mathcal G|_U)\) for \(U\) open (Prop. 6.2). Since \(X\) is noetherian it is covered by finitely many open affine noetherian subschemes, so we may assume \(X=\operatorname{Spec}A\) with \(A\) noetherian. Then \(\mathcal F=\widetilde M\) with \(M\) finitely generated; since \(A\) is noetherian there is a resolution \(\cdots\to L_1\to L_0\to M\to0\) by finitely generated free \(A\)-modules, and by exactness of \(\widetilde{\ }\) (Ch. II, Prop. 5.2) the sheaves \(\widetilde{L_j}\cong\mathcal O_X^{r_j}\) form a locally free resolution of \(\mathcal F\) of finite rank. By Prop. 6.5, \(\mathcal{E}xt^i(\mathcal F,\mathcal G)\cong h^i(\mathcal{H}om(\widetilde{L}_\bullet,\mathcal G))\). Now \(\mathcal{H}om(\widetilde{L_j},\mathcal G)\cong\mathcal G^{r_j}\), which is coherent (resp. quasi-coherent) when \(\mathcal G\) is. The differentials are \(\mathcal O_X\)-linear maps between such sheaves, and kernels, images and cokernels of morphisms of coherent (resp. quasi-coherent) sheaves on a noetherian scheme are coherent (resp. quasi-coherent) (Ch. II, Prop. 5.7). Hence \(h^i=\operatorname{Ker}d^i/\operatorname{Im}d^{i-1}\) is coherent (resp. quasi-coherent). ∎

Exercise 6.4

Problem

Let \(X\) be a noetherian scheme, and suppose that every coherent sheaf on \(X\) is a quotient of a locally free sheaf. In this case we say \(\mathfrak{Coh}(X)\) has enough locally frees. Then for any \(\mathcal G\in\mathfrak{Mod}(X)\), show that the \(\delta\)-functor \((\mathcal{E}xt^i(\cdot,\mathcal G))_{i\ge0}\) from \(\mathfrak{Coh}(X)\) to \(\mathfrak{Mod}(X)\) is a contravariant universal \(\delta\)-functor. [Hint: Show \(\mathcal{E}xt^i(\cdot,\mathcal G)\) is coeffaceable (Sect. 1) for \(i>0\).]

Solution

(Here “locally free” means locally free of finite rank, as in Prop. 6.5.)

It is a contravariant \(\delta\)-functor on \(\mathfrak{Coh}(X)\). A short exact sequence \(0\to\mathcal F'\to\mathcal F\to\mathcal F''\to0\) in \(\mathfrak{Coh}(X)\) is one in \(\mathfrak{Mod}(X)\), so by Prop. 6.4 it gives a long exact sequence

\[ 0\to\mathcal{H}om(\mathcal F'',\mathcal G)\to\mathcal{H}om(\mathcal F,\mathcal G)\to\mathcal{H}om(\mathcal F',\mathcal G)\to\mathcal{E}xt^1(\mathcal F'',\mathcal G)\to\cdots, \]

obtained from the short exact sequence of complexes \(0\to\mathcal{H}om(\mathcal F'',\mathcal I^\bullet)\to\mathcal{H}om(\mathcal F,\mathcal I^\bullet)\to\mathcal{H}om(\mathcal F',\mathcal I^\bullet)\to0\) for an injective resolution \(\mathcal G\to\mathcal I^\bullet\) (exact by 6.1, as in the proof of 6.4). This construction is functorial in the short exact sequence, so the connecting maps are natural; thus \((\mathcal{E}xt^i(\cdot,\mathcal G))_i\) is a contravariant \(\delta\)-functor.

Vanishing on locally free sheaves. Let \(\mathcal E\) be locally free of finite rank and \(i>0\). By Prop. 6.7 (with \(\mathcal F=\mathcal O_X\), \(\mathcal L=\mathcal E\)), \(\mathcal{E}xt^i(\mathcal E,\mathcal G)=\mathcal{E}xt^i(\mathcal O_X\otimes\mathcal E,\mathcal G)\cong\mathcal{E}xt^i(\mathcal O_X,\mathcal E^\vee\otimes\mathcal G)=0\) by Prop. 6.3(b).

Coeffaceability. Let \(\mathcal F\in\mathfrak{Coh}(X)\) and \(i>0\). By hypothesis there is a surjection \(u\colon\mathcal E\to\mathcal F\) with \(\mathcal E\) locally free, and \(\mathcal E\in\mathfrak{Coh}(X)\). Then \(\mathcal{E}xt^i(u,\mathcal G)\colon\mathcal{E}xt^i(\mathcal F,\mathcal G)\to\mathcal{E}xt^i(\mathcal E,\mathcal G)=0\) is zero. So the contravariant \(\delta\)-functor is coeffaceable for \(i>0\).

By the dual form of Thm. 1.3A (a contravariant \(\delta\)-functor that is coeffaceable in all degrees \(i>0\) is universal; the proof is the same as for the covariant, effaceable case, with arrows reversed), \((\mathcal{E}xt^i(\cdot,\mathcal G))_{i\ge0}\) is a universal \(\delta\)-functor on \(\mathfrak{Coh}(X)\): it is the universal contravariant \(\delta\)-functor extending \(\mathcal F\mapsto\mathcal{H}om(\mathcal F,\mathcal G)\). ∎

Exercise 6.5

Problem

Let \(X\) be a noetherian scheme, and assume that \(\mathfrak{Coh}(X)\) has enough locally frees (Ex. 6.4). Then for any coherent sheaf \(\mathcal F\) we define the homological dimension of \(\mathcal F\), denoted \(\operatorname{hd}(\mathcal F)\), to be the least length of a locally free resolution of \(\mathcal F\) (or \(+\infty\) if there is no finite one). Show:

  1. (a)

    \(\mathcal F\) is locally free \(\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{E}xt^1(\mathcal F,\mathcal G)=0\) for all \(\mathcal G\in\mathfrak{Mod}(X)\);

  2. (b)

    \(\operatorname{hd}(\mathcal F)\le n\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{E}xt^i(\mathcal F,\mathcal G)=0\) for all \(i>n\) and all \(\mathcal G\in\mathfrak{Mod}(X)\);

  3. (c)

    \(\operatorname{hd}(\mathcal F)=\sup_{x\in X}\operatorname{pd}_{\mathcal O_x}\mathcal F_x\).

Solution

(Resolutions are by locally free sheaves of finite rank, as in Prop. 6.5.) Since \(\mathfrak{Coh}(X)\) has enough locally frees, every coherent \(\mathcal F\) has a locally free resolution \(\mathcal E_\bullet\to\mathcal F\to0\): choose \(\mathcal E_0\to\mathcal F\) onto; the kernel is coherent, and repeat. Put \(\mathcal Z_0=\mathcal F\) and \(\mathcal Z_{j+1}=\operatorname{Ker}(\mathcal E_j\to\mathcal Z_j)\), coherent sheaves with exact sequences \(0\to\mathcal Z_{j+1}\to\mathcal E_j\to\mathcal Z_j\to0\).

Preliminary 1. If \(\mathcal K\) is coherent and \(\mathcal K_x\) is a free \(\mathcal O_x\)-module, then \(\mathcal K\) is free on a neighborhood of \(x\). Let \(r=\operatorname{rank}\mathcal K_x\); choose generators of \(\mathcal K_x\) lifting to sections of \(\mathcal K\) on an open \(W\ni x\), giving \(\varphi\colon\mathcal O_W^r\to\mathcal K|_W\) that is an isomorphism at \(x\). Kernel and cokernel are coherent with zero stalk at \(x\); the support of a coherent sheaf is closed (Ch. II, Ex. 5.6), so after shrinking \(W\) both vanish. In particular \(\{x:\mathcal K_x\text{ free}\}\) is open.

Preliminary 2 (dimension shifting). For \(\mathcal G\in\mathfrak{Mod}(X)\), \(i\ge1\) and \(j\ge0\), the long exact sequence of \(\mathcal{E}xt(\cdot,\mathcal G)\) for \(0\to\mathcal Z_{j+1}\to\mathcal E_j\to\mathcal Z_j\to0\) together with \(\mathcal{E}xt^q(\mathcal E_j,\mathcal G)=0\) for \(q>0\) (Ex. 6.4) gives \(\mathcal{E}xt^i(\mathcal Z_{j+1},\mathcal G)\cong\mathcal{E}xt^{i+1}(\mathcal Z_j,\mathcal G)\). Hence

\[ \mathcal{E}xt^1(\mathcal Z_n,\mathcal G)\cong\mathcal{E}xt^{n+1}(\mathcal F,\mathcal G)\qquad(n\ge0),\tag{1} \]

and more generally \(\mathcal{E}xt^i(\mathcal Z_n,\mathcal G)\cong\mathcal{E}xt^{n+i}(\mathcal F,\mathcal G)\) for \(i\ge1\). The same holds verbatim for modules over a ring (free modules have vanishing higher Ext).

(a) (\(\Rightarrow\)) This was shown in Ex. 6.4: \(\mathcal{E}xt^i(\mathcal F,\mathcal G)=0\) for \(i>0\) if \(\mathcal F\) is locally free.

(\(\Leftarrow\)) Suppose \(\mathcal{E}xt^1(\mathcal F,\mathcal G)=0\) for all \(\mathcal G\). Let \(x\in X\) and let \(N\) be any \(\mathcal O_x\)-module. Let \(\mathcal G=i_*N\) be the skyscraper sheaf: \(\mathcal G(U)=N\) if \(x\in U\) and \(0\) otherwise; it is an \(\mathcal O_X\)-module via \(\mathcal O_X(U)\to\mathcal O_x\), and \(\mathcal G_x=N\). By Prop. 6.8 (\(X\) noetherian, \(\mathcal F\) coherent), \(\operatorname{Ext}^1_{\mathcal O_x}(\mathcal F_x,N)=\mathcal{E}xt^1(\mathcal F,\mathcal G)_x=0\). As \(N\) was arbitrary, \(\mathcal F_x\) is projective (Prop. 6.10A(a)); it is finitely generated over a local ring, hence free. By Preliminary 1, \(\mathcal F\) is locally free near each \(x\).

(b) (\(\Rightarrow\)) Let \(0\to\mathcal E_n\to\cdots\to\mathcal E_0\to\mathcal F\to0\) be a locally free resolution of length \(\le n\) (pad with zeros). By Prop. 6.5, \(\mathcal{E}xt^i(\mathcal F,\mathcal G)=h^i(\mathcal{H}om(\mathcal E_\bullet,\mathcal G))=0\) for \(i>n\), since \(\mathcal{H}om(\mathcal E_j,\mathcal G)=0\) for \(j>n\).

(\(\Leftarrow\)) Assume \(\mathcal{E}xt^i(\mathcal F,\mathcal G)=0\) for all \(i>n\) and all \(\mathcal G\). Take any locally free resolution \(\mathcal E_\bullet\) and the sheaves \(\mathcal Z_j\) above. By (1), \(\mathcal{E}xt^1(\mathcal Z_n,\mathcal G)\cong\mathcal{E}xt^{n+1}(\mathcal F,\mathcal G)=0\) for all \(\mathcal G\), and \(\mathcal Z_n\) is coherent, so \(\mathcal Z_n\) is locally free by (a). Hence \(0\to\mathcal Z_n\to\mathcal E_{n-1}\to\cdots\to\mathcal E_0\to\mathcal F\to0\) (for \(n=0\): \(\mathcal Z_0=\mathcal F\) is locally free) is a locally free resolution of length \(n\), so \(\operatorname{hd}(\mathcal F)\le n\).

(c) Put \(m=\sup_x\operatorname{pd}_{\mathcal O_x}\mathcal F_x\) and \(h=\operatorname{hd}(\mathcal F)\).

\(m\le h\): if \(h<\infty\) take a locally free resolution of length \(h\). Taking stalks (exact) gives a resolution of \(\mathcal F_x\) by free \(\mathcal O_x\)-modules of finite rank, of length \(h\); so \(\operatorname{pd}\mathcal F_x\le h\) for all \(x\).

\(h\le m\): we may assume \(m<\infty\). For each \(x\) and \(i>m\), \(\operatorname{Ext}^i_{\mathcal O_x}(\mathcal F_x,N)=0\) for every \(\mathcal O_x\)-module \(N\) (Prop. 6.10A(b)). By Prop. 6.8, \(\mathcal{E}xt^i(\mathcal F,\mathcal G)_x=\operatorname{Ext}^i_{\mathcal O_x}(\mathcal F_x,\mathcal G_x)=0\) for every \(\mathcal G\in\mathfrak{Mod}(X)\), every \(x\) and every \(i>m\). So \(\mathcal{E}xt^i(\mathcal F,\mathcal G)=0\) for \(i>m\), whence \(h\le m\) by (b). ∎

Exercise 6.6

Problem

Let \(A\) be a regular local ring, and let \(M\) be a finitely generated \(A\)-module. In this case, strengthen the result (6.10A) as follows.

  1. (a)

    \(M\) is projective if and only if \(\operatorname{Ext}^i(M,A)=0\) for all \(i>0\). [Hint: Use (6.11A) and descending induction on \(i\) to show that \(\operatorname{Ext}^i(M,N)=0\) for all \(i>0\) and all finitely generated \(A\)-modules \(N\). Then show \(M\) is a direct summand of a free \(A\)-module (Matsumura [2, p. 129]).]

  2. (b)

    Use (a) to show that for any \(n\), \(\operatorname{pd}M\le n\) if and only if \(\operatorname{Ext}^i(M,A)=0\) for all \(i>n\).

Solution

Let \(d=\dim A\). All modules are over \(A\), which is noetherian, so kernels of maps between finitely generated modules are finitely generated.

(a) (\(\Rightarrow\)) If \(M\) is projective then \(\operatorname{pd}M=0\), so \(\operatorname{Ext}^i(M,N)=0\) for \(i>0\) and all \(N\) by Prop. 6.10A(b).

(\(\Leftarrow\)) Assume \(\operatorname{Ext}^i(M,A)=0\) for all \(i>0\); hence \(\operatorname{Ext}^i(M,A^r)=\operatorname{Ext}^i(M,A)^r=0\) for all \(r\). By Prop. 6.11A(a), \(\operatorname{pd}M\le d\), so \(\operatorname{Ext}^i(M,N)=0\) for all \(i>d\) and all \(N\) (Prop. 6.10A(b)).

Claim: \(\operatorname{Ext}^i(M,N)=0\) for all \(i\ge1\) and all finitely generated \(N\). We prove it by descending induction on \(i\); it holds for \(i>d\). Suppose it holds for \(i+1\) (where \(i\ge1\)) and let \(N\) be finitely generated. Choose an exact sequence \(0\to N'\to A^r\to N\to0\) with \(N'\) finitely generated. The exact sequence

\[ \operatorname{Ext}^i(M,A^r)\to\operatorname{Ext}^i(M,N)\to\operatorname{Ext}^{i+1}(M,N') \]

has both outer terms \(0\) (the left one by hypothesis, the right one by induction), so \(\operatorname{Ext}^i(M,N)=0\). This proves the claim.

Now choose a surjection \(q\colon A^s\to M\) with kernel \(K\), finitely generated. In the exact sequence \(\Hom(M,A^s)\to\Hom(M,M)\to\operatorname{Ext}^1(M,K)=0\) the identity of \(M\) lifts to \(\sigma\colon M\to A^s\) with \(q\sigma=1_M\). So \(M\) is a direct summand of the free module \(A^s\), hence projective.

(b) (\(\Rightarrow\)) If \(\operatorname{pd}M\le n\), then \(\operatorname{Ext}^i(M,A)=0\) for \(i>n\) by Prop. 6.10A(b).

(\(\Leftarrow\)) Suppose \(\operatorname{Ext}^i(M,A)=0\) for \(i>n\). Take a resolution \(\cdots\to F_1\to F_0\to M\to0\) by finitely generated free modules, \(K_0=M\), \(K_{j+1}=\operatorname{Ker}(F_j\to K_j)\) (finitely generated). Since \(\operatorname{Ext}^q(F_j,N)=0\) for \(q>0\), the sequences \(0\to K_{j+1}\to F_j\to K_j\to0\) give \(\operatorname{Ext}^i(K_{j+1},N)\cong\operatorname{Ext}^{i+1}(K_j,N)\) for \(i\ge1\) (Prop. 6.4 for modules). Hence \(\operatorname{Ext}^i(K_n,A)\cong\operatorname{Ext}^{i+n}(M,A)=0\) for \(i\ge1\). By (a), \(K_n\) is projective. Then \(0\to K_n\to F_{n-1}\to\cdots\to F_0\to M\to0\) is a projective resolution of length \(n\) (for \(n=0\) this says \(M=K_0\) projective), so \(\operatorname{pd}M\le n\). ∎

Exercise 6.7

Problem

Let \(X=\operatorname{Spec}A\) be an affine noetherian scheme. Let \(M,N\) be \(A\)-modules, with \(M\) finitely generated. Then

\[ \operatorname{Ext}^i_X(\widetilde M,\widetilde N)\cong\operatorname{Ext}^i_A(M,N) \]

and

\[ \mathcal{E}xt^i_X(\widetilde M,\widetilde N)\cong\operatorname{Ext}^i_A(M,N)^{\sim}. \]
Solution

Since \(A\) is noetherian and \(M\) finitely generated, choose a resolution \(L_\bullet\to M\to0\) by finitely generated free \(A\)-modules. As \(\widetilde{\ }\) is exact (Ch. II, Prop. 5.2), \(\widetilde{L}_\bullet\to\widetilde M\to0\) is a locally free resolution with \(\widetilde{L_j}\cong\mathcal O_X^{r_j}\). Also, by Ch. II, Cor. 5.5, \(\widetilde{\ }\) is fully faithful on modules: \(\Hom_X(\widetilde P,\widetilde N)=\Hom_A(P,N)\), compatibly with composition. For the module Ext, Prop. 6.5 applied to the one-point space with ring \(A\) gives \(\operatorname{Ext}^i_A(M,N)=h^i(\Hom_A(L_\bullet,N))\).

Sheaf Ext. By Prop. 6.5, \(\mathcal{E}xt^i_X(\widetilde M,\widetilde N)=h^i(\mathcal{H}om(\widetilde L_\bullet,\widetilde N))\). For \(L\) finitely generated free, the natural map \(\Hom_A(L,N)^\sim\to\mathcal{H}om(\widetilde L,\widetilde N)\) (on \(D(g)\) it is \(\Hom_A(L,N)_g=\Hom_{A_g}(L_g,N_g)=\Hom(\widetilde L|_{D(g)},\widetilde N|_{D(g)})\)) is an isomorphism (both sides are \(\widetilde N^{\,r}\)) and is functorial in \(L\) for maps between finite free modules. Hence \(\mathcal{H}om(\widetilde L_\bullet,\widetilde N)\cong\Hom_A(L_\bullet,N)^\sim\) as complexes, and since \(\widetilde{\ }\) is exact it commutes with taking cohomology: \(\mathcal{E}xt^i_X(\widetilde M,\widetilde N)\cong h^i(\Hom_A(L_\bullet,N))^\sim=\operatorname{Ext}^i_A(M,N)^\sim\).

Global Ext. Put \(\mathcal G=\widetilde N\). First, for \(j\ge0\) and \(i\ge1\),

\[ \operatorname{Ext}^i_X(\widetilde{L_j},\mathcal G)\cong\operatorname{Ext}^i_X(\mathcal O_X,\mathcal G)^{r_j}\cong H^i(X,\mathcal G)^{r_j}=0,\tag{1} \]

by additivity, Prop. 6.3(c), and Thm. 3.5 (\(X\) affine noetherian, \(\mathcal G\) quasi-coherent).

Let \(K_0=\widetilde M\) and \(K_{j+1}=\operatorname{Ker}(\widetilde{L_j}\to K_j)\), so \(0\to K_{j+1}\to\widetilde{L_j}\to K_j\to0\) is exact. By (1) and the long exact sequence in the first variable (Prop. 6.4), for \(i\ge1\) we get \(\operatorname{Ext}^i(K_{j+1},\mathcal G)\cong\operatorname{Ext}^{i+1}(K_j,\mathcal G)\), and, from the beginning of the sequence,

\[ \operatorname{Ext}^1(K_j,\mathcal G)\cong\operatorname{Coker}\big(\Hom(\widetilde{L_j},\mathcal G)\to\Hom(K_{j+1},\mathcal G)\big). \]

Hence for \(i\ge1\): \(\operatorname{Ext}^i(\widetilde M,\mathcal G)\cong\operatorname{Ext}^1(K_{i-1},\mathcal G)\cong\operatorname{Coker}\big(\Hom(\widetilde{L_{i-1}},\mathcal G)\to\Hom(K_i,\mathcal G)\big)\). Now \(\widetilde{L_i}\to K_i\) is onto, and \(K_{i+1}\) is the image of \(\widetilde{L_{i+1}}\), so \(\Hom(K_i,\mathcal G)\) is the subgroup of \(\Hom(\widetilde{L_i},\mathcal G)\) of maps killing \(K_{i+1}\), i.e. \(\operatorname{Ker}(d^i\colon\Hom(\widetilde{L_i},\mathcal G)\to\Hom(\widetilde{L_{i+1}},\mathcal G))\); and the image of \(\Hom(\widetilde{L_{i-1}},\mathcal G)\to\Hom(K_i,\mathcal G)\) is the image of \(d^{i-1}\). Therefore

\[ \operatorname{Ext}^i_X(\widetilde M,\widetilde N)\cong h^i(\Hom_X(\widetilde L_\bullet,\widetilde N))=h^i(\Hom_A(L_\bullet,N))=\operatorname{Ext}^i_A(M,N)\qquad(i\ge1). \]

For \(i=0\) this is the full faithfulness \(\Hom_X(\widetilde M,\widetilde N)=\Hom_A(M,N)\). ∎

Exercise 6.8

Problem

Prove the following theorem of Kleiman (see Borelli [1]): if \(X\) is a noetherian, integral, separated, locally factorial scheme, then every coherent sheaf on \(X\) is a quotient of a locally free sheaf (of finite rank).

  1. (a)

    First show that open sets of the form \(X_s\), for various \(s\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal L)\), and various invertible sheaves \(\mathcal L\) on \(X\), form a base for the topology of \(X\). [Hint: Given a closed point \(x\in X\) and an open neighborhood \(U\) of \(x\), to show there is an \(\mathcal L,s\) such that \(x\in X_s\subseteq U\), first reduce to the case that \(Z=X-U\) is irreducible. Then let \(\zeta\) be the generic point of \(Z\). Let \(f\in K(X)\) be a rational function with \(f\in\mathcal O_x\), \(f\notin\mathcal O_\zeta\). Let \(D=(f)_\infty\), and let \(\mathcal L=\mathcal L(D)\), \(s\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal L(D))\) correspond to \(D\) (II, Sect. 6).]

  2. (b)

    Now use (II, 5.14) to show that any coherent sheaf is a quotient of a direct sum \(\bigoplus\mathcal L_i^{\otimes n_i}\) for various invertible sheaves \(\mathcal L_i\) and various integers \(n_i\).

Solution

Recall \(X_s=\{z\in X: s_z\notin\mathfrak m_z\mathcal L_z\}\), the open set where \(s\) does not vanish. We have \(X_{s\otimes t}=X_s\cap X_t\) for \(s\in\Gamma(\mathcal L)\), \(t\in\Gamma(\mathcal L')\). Let \(K=K(X)\). Reading of the hint: the printed hint says “closed point \(x\)”; I will prove the statement for an arbitrary point \(y\) in place of \(x\), which is what a base of the topology requires (a non-closed point of \(U\) need not be handled by the closed-point case).

Facts used. (i) \(X\) is locally factorial, so each local ring \(\mathcal O_z\) is a UFD, hence a noetherian normal domain, and it is the intersection of its localizations at height one primes (Matsumura, Thm. 11.5). The local ring of a prime divisor \(Y\) (closure of a point \(\eta_Y\) of codimension one) is a one-dimensional UFD, hence a DVR, with valuation \(\nu_Y\). (ii) Every Weil divisor \(D\) on \(X\) is locally principal, and \(\mathcal L(D)\subseteq\mathcal K\), \(\mathcal L(D)(W)=\{g\in K:(g)|_W+D|_W\ge0\}\) is invertible (Ch. II, Prop. 6.11 and 6.13). If \(D=\sum n_YY\) is effective, then \(1\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal L(D))\) is a section \(s_D\) with \(X_{s_D}=X\setminus\operatorname{Supp}D\). Indeed, near a point \(z\) choose an open \(W\ni z\) and \(g\in K\) with \(D|_W=(g)|_W\); since \(D\ge0\) and \(X\) is normal, \(g\in\mathcal O(W)\), and multiplication by \(g\) gives \(\mathcal L(D)|_W\cong\mathcal O_W\) taking \(s_D\) to \(g\). So \(z\in X_{s_D}\) iff \(g\) is a unit in \(\mathcal O_z\). As \(\mathcal O_z\) is a UFD, \(g\) is a non-unit iff it is divisible by a prime element, i.e. iff \(\nu_Y(g)=n_Y>0\) for some prime divisor \(Y\ni z\) (the prime divisors through \(z\) correspond to the height one primes of \(\mathcal O_z\)). That is, iff \(z\in\operatorname{Supp}D\).

(a) Let \(y\in X\) and \(U\ni y\) open. We find \(\mathcal L\) and \(s\) with \(y\in X_s\subseteq U\). Let \(Z=X\setminus U=Z_1\cup\dots\cup Z_m\) be the decomposition into irreducible components. If for each \(\ell\) we find \((\mathcal L_\ell,s_\ell)\) with \(y\in X_{s_\ell}\subseteq X\setminus Z_\ell\), then \(\mathcal L=\bigotimes\mathcal L_\ell\), \(s=\bigotimes s_\ell\) satisfy \(y\in X_s=\bigcap_\ell X_{s_\ell}\subseteq\bigcap_\ell(X\setminus Z_\ell)=U\). (If \(Z=\emptyset\) take \(\mathcal L=\mathcal O_X\), \(s=1\).) So we may assume \(Z\) irreducible, with generic point \(\zeta\); \(y\notin Z\).

Claim 1: \(\mathcal O_y\not\subseteq\mathcal O_\zeta\) in \(K\). Suppose \(\mathcal O_y\subseteq\mathcal O_\zeta\). Consider the two morphisms \(\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal O_\zeta\to X\): the canonical one \(\phi\), and \(\psi\), the composite of \(\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal O_\zeta\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal O_y\) (from the inclusion) with the canonical \(\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal O_y\to X\). Both restrict on the dense open \(\operatorname{Spec}K\subseteq\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal O_\zeta\) to the canonical morphism to the generic point of \(X\). As \(\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal O_\zeta\) is reduced and \(X\) is separated, \(\phi=\psi\) (Ch. II, Ex. 4.2). The closed point of \(\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal O_\zeta\) goes to \(\zeta\) under \(\phi\), and under \(\psi\) to a point of the image of \(\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal O_y\), i.e. to a generization of \(y\). Thus \(\zeta\) is a generization of \(y\), i.e. \(y\in\overline{\{\zeta\}}=Z\), a contradiction.

Choose \(f\in\mathcal O_y\setminus\mathcal O_\zeta\), and let \(D=(f)_\infty=\sum_{\nu_Y(f)<0}(-\nu_Y(f))Y\) (the polar part of the Weil divisor \((f)\)), an effective divisor.

\(y\notin\operatorname{Supp}D\): if \(y\in Y\) with \(\nu_Y(f)<0\), then \(\mathcal O_{\eta_Y}\) is a localization of \(\mathcal O_y\) (as \(\eta_Y\) is a generization of \(y\)), so \(f\in\mathcal O_y\subseteq\mathcal O_{\eta_Y}\) and \(\nu_Y(f)\ge0\), a contradiction.

\(Z\subseteq\operatorname{Supp}D\): as \(f\notin\mathcal O_\zeta=\bigcap_{\mathfrak p}(\mathcal O_\zeta)_{\mathfrak p}\) (over height one primes \(\mathfrak p\) of \(\mathcal O_\zeta\)), there is such \(\mathfrak p\) with \(f\notin(\mathcal O_\zeta)_{\mathfrak p}\). This ring is the local ring \(\mathcal O_{\eta_Y}\) of a prime divisor \(Y\) through \(\zeta\), a DVR in which \(f\notin\), so \(\nu_Y(f)<0\) and \(\zeta\in Y\subseteq\operatorname{Supp}D\). Since \(\operatorname{Supp}D\) is closed, \(Z=\overline{\{\zeta\}}\subseteq\operatorname{Supp}D\).

With \(\mathcal L=\mathcal L(D)\) and \(s=s_D\) we get from (ii): \(y\in X_s=X\setminus\operatorname{Supp}D\subseteq X\setminus Z=U\). This proves (a).

(b) The sets \(X_s\) used are affine. By (a), each \(y\in X\) has an open affine neighborhood \(U_y\) and \((\mathcal L_y,s_y)\) with \(y\in X_{s_y}\subseteq U_y\). Then \(X_{s_y}\) is affine: cover \(U_y=\operatorname{Spec}B\) by finitely many \(D(g_\ell)\) on which \(\mathcal L_y\) is trivial; there \(s_y\) is a function \(h_\ell\) and \(X_{s_y}\cap D(g_\ell)=D(g_\ell h_\ell)\) is affine. So the open immersion \(X_{s_y}\to U_y\) is an affine morphism, hence \(X_{s_y}\) is affine. As \(X\) is noetherian, hence quasi-compact, finitely many \(X_{s_i}\), \(i=1,\dots,r\) (with \(s_i\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal L_i)\)) cover \(X\).

Let \(\mathcal F\) be coherent on \(X\). Since \(X_{s_i}\) is affine and \(\mathcal F|_{X_{s_i}}\) coherent, it is generated by finitely many sections \(t_{ij}\in\Gamma(X_{s_i},\mathcal F)\), \(j=1,\dots,r_i\). The hypotheses of Lemma 5.14(b) of Ch. II hold (\(X\) noetherian: take a finite affine cover on whose members \(\mathcal L_i\) is free; intersections are quasi-compact). Hence there are integers \(n_{ij}>0\) and global sections \(u_{ij}\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal L_i^{\otimes n_{ij}})\) whose restriction to \(X_{s_i}\) is \(t_{ij}\otimes s_i^{\,n_{ij}}\). Let \(\varphi_{ij}\colon\mathcal L_i^{\otimes-n_{ij}}\to\mathcal F\) be the morphism corresponding to \(u_{ij}\) under \(\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal L_i^{\otimes n}=\mathcal{H}om(\mathcal L_i^{\otimes-n},\mathcal F)\). On \(X_{s_i}\) the section \(s_i\) trivializes \(\mathcal L_i\), so \(\mathcal L_i^{\otimes-n_{ij}}|_{X_{s_i}}\) is generated by the dual generator \(s_i^{-n_{ij}}\), and \(\varphi_{ij}(s_i^{-n_{ij}})=t_{ij}\). Thus on \(X_{s_i}\) the image of \(\bigoplus_j\varphi_{ij}\) contains all \(t_{ij}\), hence is all of \(\mathcal F|_{X_{s_i}}\). Consequently

\[ \Phi=\bigoplus_{i,j}\varphi_{ij}\colon\bigoplus_{i=1}^r\bigoplus_{j=1}^{r_i}\mathcal L_i^{\otimes-n_{ij}}\to\mathcal F \]

is surjective on each \(X_{s_i}\), hence surjective. The source is a finite direct sum of invertible sheaves, so is locally free of finite rank. This proves (b), and with it Kleiman's theorem. ∎

Exercise 6.9

Problem

Let \(X\) be a noetherian, integral, separated, regular scheme. (We say a scheme is regular if all of its local rings are regular local rings.) Recall the definition of the Grothendieck group \(K(X)\) from (II, Ex. 6.10). We define similarly another group \(K_1(X)\) using locally free sheaves: it is the quotient of the free abelian group generated by all locally free (coherent) sheaves, by the subgroup generated by all expressions of the form \(\mathcal E-\mathcal E'-\mathcal E''\), whenever \(0\to\mathcal E'\to\mathcal E\to\mathcal E''\to0\) is a short exact sequence of locally free sheaves. Clearly there is a natural group homomorphism \(\varepsilon\colon K_1(X)\to K(X)\). Show that \(\varepsilon\) is an isomorphism (Borel and Serre [1, Sect. 4]) as follows.

  1. (a)

    Given a coherent sheaf \(\mathcal F\), use (Ex. 6.8) to show that it has a locally free resolution \(\mathcal E_\bullet\to\mathcal F\to0\). Then use (6.11A) and (Ex. 6.5) to show that it has a finite locally free resolution

    \[ 0\to\mathcal E_n\to\cdots\to\mathcal E_1\to\mathcal E_0\to\mathcal F\to0. \]
  2. (b)

    For each \(\mathcal F\), choose a finite locally free resolution \(\mathcal E_\bullet\to\mathcal F\to0\), and let \(\delta(\mathcal F)=\sum(-1)^i\gamma_1(\mathcal E_i)\) in \(K_1(X)\). Show that \(\delta(\mathcal F)\) is independent of the resolution chosen, that it defines a homomorphism of \(K(X)\) to \(K_1(X)\), and finally that it is an inverse to \(\varepsilon\).

Solution

Conventions. Locally free means locally free of finite rank. \(\gamma(\mathcal F)\in K(X)\) and \(\gamma_1(\mathcal E)\in K_1(X)\) denote classes; \(K(X)\) is generated by the classes of coherent sheaves, with relations \(\gamma(\mathcal F)=\gamma(\mathcal F')+\gamma(\mathcal F'')\) for short exact sequences, and \(\varepsilon\gamma_1(\mathcal E)=\gamma(\mathcal E)\). A regular local ring is a UFD (Auslander–Buchsbaum; a fact beyond the text), so \(X\) is locally factorial and Ex. 6.8 applies. We use the following three elementary facts.

(P1) A coherent sheaf \(\mathcal K\) with \(\mathcal K_x\) free is free near \(x\) (Preliminary 1 of Ex. 6.5); so \(\mathcal K\) is locally free iff all its stalks are free, and \(\{x:\mathcal K_x\text{ free}\}\) is open.

(P2) If \(0\to\mathcal A\to\mathcal B\to\mathcal C\to0\) is exact with \(\mathcal B,\mathcal C\) locally free, then \(\mathcal A\) is locally free (coherent, and stalks are kernels of surjections of free modules over a local ring, which split, so are free; use (P1)).

(P3) Exact finite complexes. If \(0\to\mathcal C_n\to\cdots\to\mathcal C_0\to0\) is an exact complex of locally free sheaves then \(\sum(-1)^j\gamma_1(\mathcal C_j)=0\) in \(K_1(X)\). By induction on \(n\): for \(n=0\), \(\mathcal C_0=0\). For \(n\ge1\), \(\mathcal C_1\to\mathcal C_0\) is onto with kernel \(\mathcal Z\) locally free (P2), so \(\gamma_1(\mathcal C_1)=\gamma_1(\mathcal Z)+\gamma_1(\mathcal C_0)\) and the alternating sum equals \(-[\gamma_1(\mathcal Z)-\gamma_1(\mathcal C_2)+\gamma_1(\mathcal C_3)-\cdots]\), where the bracket is the alternating sum of the exact complex \(0\to\mathcal C_n\to\cdots\to\mathcal C_2\to\mathcal Z\to0\) of length \(n-1\), zero by induction.

(a) By Ex. 6.8, every coherent sheaf is a quotient of a locally free one. So, as in Ex. 6.5, for coherent \(\mathcal F\) there is a locally free resolution \(\mathcal E_\bullet\to\mathcal F\to0\) (choose \(\mathcal E_0\to\mathcal F\) onto, then \(\mathcal E_1\) onto the (coherent) kernel, and so on). Let \(\mathcal Z_0=\mathcal F\) and \(\mathcal Z_{j+1}=\operatorname{Ker}(\mathcal E_j\to\mathcal Z_j)\).

Finiteness. Let \(V_m=\{x\in X:(\mathcal Z_m)_x\text{ is free}\}\), open by (P1). If \(x\in V_m\) then \(x\in V_{m+1}\), because \((\mathcal Z_{m+1})_x\) is the kernel of the surjection \((\mathcal E_m)_x\to(\mathcal Z_m)_x\) of free modules, which splits. For every \(x\), the local ring \(\mathcal O_x\) is regular, so \(\operatorname{pd}\mathcal F_x\le\dim\mathcal O_x<\infty\) by Prop. 6.11A(a); and, as in Ex. 6.5, if \(\operatorname{pd}\mathcal F_x\le m\) then by dimension shifting (Ex. 6.5, Preliminary 2, for modules) \(\operatorname{Ext}^1_{\mathcal O_x}((\mathcal Z_m)_x,N)\cong\operatorname{Ext}^{m+1}_{\mathcal O_x}(\mathcal F_x,N)=0\) for all \(N\), so \((\mathcal Z_m)_x\) is projective, hence free (Prop. 6.10A(a)). Thus \(\bigcup_mV_m=X\). As \(X\) is noetherian, hence quasi-compact, and \(V_0\subseteq V_1\subseteq\cdots\) are open, \(X=V_m\) for some \(m\) (in fact for all large \(m\)). (This is the content of Ex. 6.5(c): \(\operatorname{hd}\mathcal F=\sup_x\operatorname{pd}\mathcal F_x\), and the supremum is finite here by quasi-compactness even if \(\dim X=\infty\).) By (P1) \(\mathcal Z_m\) is locally free, and

\[ 0\to\mathcal Z_m\to\mathcal E_{m-1}\to\cdots\to\mathcal E_0\to\mathcal F\to0 \]

is a finite locally free resolution. Moreover this works for any locally free resolution: the syzygy \(\mathcal Z_m\) is locally free for all large \(m\). \(\quad(\ast)\)

(b) Step 1: comparison for surjective chain maps. Let \(\mathcal E_\bullet\to\mathcal F\) and \(\mathcal W_\bullet\to\mathcal F\) be finite locally free resolutions and \(p\colon\mathcal W_\bullet\to\mathcal E_\bullet\) a chain map over \(1_{\mathcal F}\) which is surjective in each degree. Then \(\sum(-1)^j\gamma_1(\mathcal W_j)=\sum(-1)^j\gamma_1(\mathcal E_j)\).

Indeed, let \(\mathcal C_j=\operatorname{Ker}p_j\), locally free by (P2). Then \(0\to\mathcal C_\bullet\to\mathcal W_\bullet\to\mathcal E_\bullet\to0\) is a short exact sequence of complexes. The complexes \(\mathcal W_\bullet,\mathcal E_\bullet\) have homology \(0\) in degrees \(>0\) and \(\mathcal F\) in degree \(0\), and \(p\) induces the identity on \(H_0=\mathcal F\). The long exact homology sequence shows \(\mathcal C_\bullet\) is exact in all degrees (in degree \(0\): \(0=H_1(\mathcal E)\to H_0(\mathcal C)\to H_0(\mathcal W)\xrightarrow{\sim}H_0(\mathcal E)\)). By (P3), \(\sum(-1)^j\gamma_1(\mathcal C_j)=0\), and \(\gamma_1(\mathcal W_j)=\gamma_1(\mathcal C_j)+\gamma_1(\mathcal E_j)\).

Step 2: any two finite resolutions are dominated by a third. Let \(\mathcal E_\bullet\), \(\mathcal E'_\bullet\) be locally free resolutions of \(\mathcal F\), of lengths \(\le n\) (\(\mathcal E_j=0=\mathcal E'_j\) for \(j>n\)). Let \(\mathcal B_j=\operatorname{Ker}(\mathcal E_{j-1}\to\mathcal E_{j-2})\) for \(j\ge1\) (with \(\mathcal E_{-1}=\mathcal F\)), so \(\mathcal B_1=\operatorname{Ker}(\mathcal E_0\to\mathcal F)\), and by exactness the differential \(\mathcal E_j\to\mathcal B_j\) is onto for \(j\ge1\) (with \(\mathcal B_{j+1}=\operatorname{Ker}(\mathcal E_j\to\mathcal E_{j-1})\)); define \(\mathcal B_j'\) likewise. We construct locally free \(\mathcal W_j\) and maps \(d\colon\mathcal W_j\to\mathcal W_{j-1}\), \(p_j\colon\mathcal W_j\to\mathcal E_j\), \(p'_j\colon\mathcal W_j\to\mathcal E_j'\), all surjective, compatible with the differentials and augmentations, such that \(\mathcal W_\bullet\to\mathcal F\) is a resolution. Put \(\mathcal Y_j=\operatorname{Ker}(\mathcal W_{j-1}\to\mathcal W_{j-2})\) (\(\mathcal W_{-1}=\mathcal F\)).

\(j=0\): the sheaf \(\mathcal Q_0=\mathcal E_0\times_{\mathcal F}\mathcal E'_0\) is coherent and maps onto \(\mathcal E_0\) and \(\mathcal E_0'\) (as both augmentations are onto).
