Choose a locally free \(\mathcal W_0\) onto \(\mathcal Q_0\) (Ex. 6.8) and let \(p_0,p_0'\) be the composites. The kernel of \(\mathcal Q_0\to\mathcal F\) is \(\mathcal B_1\oplus\mathcal B_1'\), and its preimage in \(\mathcal W_0\) is \(\mathcal Y_1\); so \((p_0,p_0')\colon\mathcal Y_1\to\mathcal B_1\oplus\mathcal B_1'\) is onto.

Inductive step: suppose \(\mathcal W_0,\dots,\mathcal W_{j-1}\) are constructed (\(j\ge1\)) and \(\mathcal Y_j\to\mathcal B_j\oplus\mathcal B_j'\) is onto. Let \(\mathcal Q_j=\mathcal Y_j\times_{\mathcal B_j\oplus\mathcal B_j'}(\mathcal E_j\oplus\mathcal E_j')\), coherent, mapping onto \(\mathcal Y_j\) (since \(\mathcal E_j\oplus\mathcal E'_j\to\mathcal B_j\oplus\mathcal B_j'\) is onto) and onto \(\mathcal E_j\oplus\mathcal E_j'\) (since \(\mathcal Y_j\to\mathcal B_j\oplus\mathcal B_j'\) is onto). Choose \(\mathcal W_j\) locally free onto \(\mathcal Q_j\), and define \(\mathcal W_j\to\mathcal Y_j\subseteq\mathcal W_{j-1}\) and \(p_j,p_j'\) via \(\mathcal Q_j\). All the squares commute by construction of the fibre product, and the maps are onto. The kernel of \(\mathcal Q_j\to\mathcal Y_j\) is \(\operatorname{Ker}(\mathcal E_j\oplus\mathcal E'_j\to\mathcal B_j\oplus\mathcal B'_j)=\mathcal B_{j+1}\oplus\mathcal B'_{j+1}\), and its preimage in \(\mathcal W_j\) is \(\mathcal Y_{j+1}\); hence \(\mathcal Y_{j+1}\to\mathcal B_{j+1}\oplus\mathcal B'_{j+1}\) is onto and the induction continues.

Since \(\mathcal W_j\to\mathcal Y_j\) is onto for every \(j\ge1\) and \(\mathcal W_0\to\mathcal F\) is onto, \(\mathcal W_\bullet\to\mathcal F\) is a locally free resolution. By \((\ast)\) choose \(m\ge n+2\) with \(\mathcal Y_m\) locally free, and set \(\mathcal W^{\mathrm{tr}}_j=\mathcal W_j\) for \(j<m\), \(\mathcal W^{\mathrm{tr}}_m=\mathcal Y_m\), \(\mathcal W_j^{\mathrm{tr}}=0\) for \(j>m\). This is a finite locally free resolution of \(\mathcal F\), and \(p,p'\) restrict to chain maps \(\mathcal W^{\mathrm{tr}}_\bullet\to\mathcal E_\bullet\), \(\to\mathcal E'_\bullet\) (in degrees \(\ge m-1\ge n+1\) the target is \(0\)), surjective in every degree.

Step 3: independence. By Steps 1 and 2, \(\sum(-1)^j\gamma_1(\mathcal E_j)=\sum(-1)^j\gamma_1(\mathcal W^{\mathrm{tr}}_j)=\sum(-1)^j\gamma_1(\mathcal E_j')\). So \(\delta(\mathcal F)\in K_1(X)\) is well defined (it clearly depends only on the isomorphism class of \(\mathcal F\)).

Step 4: additivity. Let \(0\to\mathcal F'\to\mathcal F\to\mathcal F''\to0\) be an exact sequence of coherent sheaves. Choose a finite locally free resolution \(\mathcal E''_\bullet\) of \(\mathcal F''\) of length \(n\), with syzygies \(\mathcal Z''_0=\mathcal F''\), \(\mathcal Z''_{j+1}=\operatorname{Ker}(\mathcal E''_j\to\mathcal Z''_j)\); so \(\mathcal Z''_{j}=0\) for \(j>n\). Put \(\mathcal A_0=\mathcal F\), \(\mathcal A_0'=\mathcal F'\), so that \(0\to\mathcal A'_0\to\mathcal A_0\to\mathcal Z''_0\to0\) is exact. Suppose we have an exact sequence \(0\to\mathcal A_j'\to\mathcal A_j\to\mathcal Z''_j\to0\) (\(j\ge0\)). Let \(\mathcal Q_j=\mathcal E_j''\times_{\mathcal Z''_j}\mathcal A_j\); it maps onto \(\mathcal E_j''\) and onto \(\mathcal A_j\) (as \(\mathcal A_j\to\mathcal Z''_j\) and \(\mathcal E''_j\to\mathcal Z''_j\) are onto). Choose a locally free \(\mathcal G_j\) onto \(\mathcal Q_j\); then \(\mathcal G_j\to\mathcal E''_j\) and \(\mathcal G_j\to\mathcal A_j\) are onto. Let \(\mathcal C_j=\operatorname{Ker}(\mathcal G_j\to\mathcal E''_j)\), locally free by (P2); it maps onto \(\mathcal A_j'\), because \(\operatorname{Ker}(\mathcal Q_j\to\mathcal E''_j)=\mathcal A_j'\). We have the commutative diagram with exact rows \(0\to\mathcal C_j\to\mathcal G_j\to\mathcal E''_j\to0\) and \(0\to\mathcal A'_j\to\mathcal A_j\to\mathcal Z_j''\to0\) and surjective vertical maps; by the snake lemma the kernels form an exact sequence \(0\to\mathcal A'_{j+1}\to\mathcal A_{j+1}\to\mathcal Z''_{j+1}\to0\), where \(\mathcal A_{j+1}=\operatorname{Ker}(\mathcal G_j\to\mathcal A_j)\), \(\mathcal A'_{j+1}=\operatorname{Ker}(\mathcal C_j\to\mathcal A_j')\). Continue. We obtain resolutions \(\mathcal G_\bullet\to\mathcal F\), \(\mathcal C_\bullet\to\mathcal F'\) by locally free sheaves with \(\mathcal A_j=\operatorname{Ker}(\mathcal G_{j-1}\to\mathcal G_{j-2})\), \(\mathcal A_j'=\operatorname{Ker}(\mathcal C_{j-1}\to\mathcal C_{j-2})\) and a short exact sequence of complexes \(0\to\mathcal C_\bullet\to\mathcal G_\bullet\to\mathcal E''_\bullet\to0\) (degreewise exact, with \(\mathcal C_\bullet\subseteq\mathcal G_\bullet\) a subcomplex). For \(j>n\) we have \(\mathcal E_j''=0=\mathcal Z''_j\), so \(\mathcal C_j=\mathcal G_j\) and \(\mathcal A'_j=\mathcal A_j\). Using \((\ast)\) choose \(m\ge n+2\) with \(\mathcal A_m=\mathcal A'_m\) locally free and truncate both \(\mathcal G_\bullet\) and \(\mathcal C_\bullet\) by replacing the degree \(m\) term by \(\mathcal A_m\) and omitting the higher terms. We get finite locally free resolutions \(\mathcal G^{\mathrm{tr}}_\bullet\) of \(\mathcal F\) and \(\mathcal C^{\mathrm{tr}}_\bullet\) of \(\mathcal F'\) with \(0\to\mathcal C^{\mathrm{tr}}_j\to\mathcal G^{\mathrm{tr}}_j\to\mathcal E''_j\to0\) exact for every \(j\) (for \(j\ge n+1\) it reads \(0\to\mathcal G_j\to\mathcal G_j\to0\to0\), resp. \(0\to\mathcal A_m\to\mathcal A_m\to0\to0\)). Hence \(\gamma_1(\mathcal G^{\mathrm{tr}}_j)=\gamma_1(\mathcal C^{\mathrm{tr}}_j)+\gamma_1(\mathcal E_j'')\) for all \(j\), and taking alternating sums and using Step 3 gives \(\delta(\mathcal F)=\delta(\mathcal F')+\delta(\mathcal F'')\).

Consequently \(\delta\) (defined on generators \(\gamma(\mathcal F)\mapsto\delta(\mathcal F)\)) kills the defining relations of \(K(X)\) and gives a group homomorphism \(\delta\colon K(X)\to K_1(X)\).

Step 5: \(\delta\) is inverse to \(\varepsilon\). For coherent \(\mathcal F\) with finite locally free resolution \(0\to\mathcal E_n\to\cdots\to\mathcal E_0\to\mathcal F\to0\), set \(\mathcal Z_0=\mathcal F\), \(\mathcal Z_{j+1}=\operatorname{Ker}(\mathcal E_j\to\mathcal Z_j)\), so \(\mathcal Z_n=\mathcal E_n\). The sequences \(0\to\mathcal Z_{j+1}\to\mathcal E_j\to\mathcal Z_j\to0\) of coherent sheaves give \(\gamma(\mathcal E_j)=\gamma(\mathcal Z_j)+\gamma(\mathcal Z_{j+1})\) in \(K(X)\) (\(j<n\)), whence

\[ \varepsilon\delta(\mathcal F)=\sum_{j=0}^n(-1)^j\gamma(\mathcal E_j)=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}(-1)^j\big(\gamma(\mathcal Z_j)+\gamma(\mathcal Z_{j+1})\big)+(-1)^n\gamma(\mathcal Z_n)=\gamma(\mathcal Z_0)=\gamma(\mathcal F). \]

Thus \(\varepsilon\circ\delta=\mathrm{id}_{K(X)}\) (on generators, hence everywhere). Conversely, for a locally free \(\mathcal E\) the one-term complex \(\mathcal E\to\mathcal E\) is a finite locally free resolution, so \(\delta\varepsilon(\gamma_1(\mathcal E))=\delta(\mathcal E)=\gamma_1(\mathcal E)\); as \(K_1(X)\) is generated by the \(\gamma_1(\mathcal E)\), \(\delta\circ\varepsilon=\mathrm{id}_{K_1(X)}\). Hence \(\varepsilon\) is an isomorphism with inverse \(\delta\). ∎

Exercise 6.10

Problem

Duality for a Finite Flat Morphism.

  1. (a)

    Let \(f\colon X\to Y\) be a finite morphism of noetherian schemes. For any quasi-coherent \(\mathcal O_Y\)-module \(\mathcal G\), \(\mathcal{H}om_Y(f_*\mathcal O_X,\mathcal G)\) is a quasi-coherent \(f_*\mathcal O_X\)-module, hence corresponds to a quasi-coherent \(\mathcal O_X\)-module, which we call \(f^!\mathcal G\) (II, Ex. 5.17e).

  2. (b)

    Show that for any coherent \(\mathcal F\) on \(X\) and any quasi-coherent \(\mathcal G\) on \(Y\), there is a natural isomorphism

    \[ f_*\mathcal{H}om_X(\mathcal F,f^!\mathcal G)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\mathcal{H}om_Y(f_*\mathcal F,\mathcal G). \]
  3. (c)

    For each \(i\ge0\), there is a natural map

    \[ \varphi_i\colon\operatorname{Ext}^i_X(\mathcal F,f^!\mathcal G)\to\operatorname{Ext}^i_Y(f_*\mathcal F,\mathcal G). \]

    [Hint: First construct a map \(\operatorname{Ext}^i_X(\mathcal F,f^!\mathcal G)\to\operatorname{Ext}^i_Y(f_*\mathcal F,f_*f^!\mathcal G)\). Then compose with a suitable map from \(f_*f^!\mathcal G\) to \(\mathcal G\).]

  4. (d)

    Now assume that \(X\) and \(Y\) are separated, \(\mathfrak{Coh}(X)\) has enough locally frees, and that \(f_*\mathcal O_X\) is locally free on \(Y\) (this is equivalent to saying that \(f\) is flat—see Sect. 9). Show that \(\varphi_i\) is an isomorphism for all \(i\), all \(\mathcal F\) coherent on \(X\), and all \(\mathcal G\) quasi-coherent on \(Y\). [Hints: First do \(i=0\). Then do \(\mathcal F=\mathcal O_X\), using (Ex. 4.1). Then do \(\mathcal F\) locally free. Do the general case by induction on \(i\), writing \(\mathcal F\) as a quotient of a locally free sheaf.]

Solution

Let \(\mathcal A=f_*\mathcal O_X\). As \(f\) is finite, it is affine and \(\mathcal A\) is a coherent \(\mathcal O_Y\)-module: over an open affine \(V=\operatorname{Spec}B\subseteq Y\), \(f^{-1}V=\operatorname{Spec}A'\) with \(A'\) a finite \(B\)-algebra. We use Ch. II, Ex. 5.17(e): \(f_*\) is an equivalence from quasi-coherent \(\mathcal O_X\)-modules to quasi-coherent \(\mathcal A\)-modules, with inverse \(\widetilde{\ }\); and \(f_*\) is exact on quasi-coherent sheaves (compute on affine opens \(V\), where \(f_*\widetilde M=M\) viewed as a \(B\)-module).

(a) Over \(V=\operatorname{Spec}B\), \(\mathcal G|_V=\widetilde N\), and since \(A'\) is a finitely presented \(B\)-module (\(B\) noetherian), \(\mathcal{H}om_Y(\mathcal A,\mathcal G)|_V=\operatorname{Hom}_B(A',N)^\sim\) is quasi-coherent. It is an \(\mathcal A\)-module via \((a\cdot\varphi)(b)=\varphi(ab)\) (\(a,b\in\mathcal A\)), compatibly with restrictions; so it is a quasi-coherent \(\mathcal A\)-module and \(f^!\mathcal G\) is the quasi-coherent \(\mathcal O_X\)-module with \(f_*f^!\mathcal G\cong\mathcal{H}om_Y(\mathcal A,\mathcal G)\) as \(\mathcal A\)-modules. Let \(e\colon f_*f^!\mathcal G=\mathcal{H}om_Y(\mathcal A,\mathcal G)\to\mathcal G\) be the evaluation \(\varphi\mapsto\varphi(1)\); it is \(\mathcal O_Y\)-linear and will be the “suitable map” of (c).

(b) Define \(\Theta\colon f_*\mathcal{H}om_X(\mathcal F,f^!\mathcal G)\to\mathcal{H}om_Y(f_*\mathcal F,\mathcal G)\): for \(V\subseteq Y\) open and \(\psi\in\Hom(\mathcal F|_{f^{-1}V},f^!\mathcal G|_{f^{-1}V})\), put \(\Theta(\psi)=e\circ f_*\psi\colon f_*\mathcal F|_V\to f_*f^!\mathcal G|_V\to\mathcal G|_V\). It is a natural morphism of sheaves. To see that it is an isomorphism, work over an open affine \(V=\operatorname{Spec}B\), with \(f^{-1}V=\operatorname{Spec}A'\), \(\mathcal F|=\widetilde P\) for a finitely generated \(A'\)-module \(P\) (\(A'\) is noetherian), \(\mathcal G|_V=\widetilde N\). All sheaves involved are quasi-coherent, and a morphism of quasi-coherent sheaves on an affine scheme is determined by, and an isomorphism iff it is on, global sections. As \(P\) is finitely presented over \(A'\) and over \(B\), the global sections of the two sides are \(\Hom_{A'}(P,\Hom_B(A',N))\) and \(\Hom_B(P,N)\), and \(\Theta\) becomes \(\psi\mapsto(m\mapsto\psi(m)(1))\). This is the usual adjunction isomorphism, with inverse \(\chi\mapsto(m\mapsto(a\mapsto\chi(am)))\). So \(\Theta\) is an isomorphism.

(c) Put \(\mathcal H=f^!\mathcal G\), quasi-coherent on \(X\). Choose injective resolutions \(\mathcal H\to\mathcal I^\bullet\) in \(\mathfrak{Mod}(X)\) and \(\mathcal G\to\mathcal J^\bullet\) in \(\mathfrak{Mod}(Y)\).

Claim: \(0\to f_*\mathcal H\to f_*\mathcal I^0\to f_*\mathcal I^1\to\cdots\) is exact. Left exactness of \(f_*\) gives exactness at \(f_*\mathcal H\) and \(f_*\mathcal I^0\). For exactness elsewhere, check on sections over open affines \(V\subseteq Y\), which form a base: \(\Gamma(V,f_*\mathcal I^\bullet)=\Gamma(U,\mathcal I^\bullet)\) with \(U=f^{-1}V\) affine noetherian, and \(\mathcal I^\bullet|_U\) is an injective resolution of \(\mathcal H|_U\) (Lemma 6.1), so its cohomology is \(H^i(U,\mathcal H|_U)=0\) for \(i>0\) by Thm. 3.5 (\(\mathcal H\) quasi-coherent). A complex of sheaves all of whose sections complexes over a base of opens are exact in degree \(i\) is exact there (pass to stalks, a direct limit).

Hence \(f_*\mathcal I^\bullet\) is a resolution of \(f_*\mathcal H\), and by the comparison theorem (the target \(\mathcal J^\bullet\) is an injective complex, the source an exact resolution) the map \(e\colon f_*\mathcal H\to\mathcal G\to\mathcal J^0\) extends to a chain map \(c\colon f_*\mathcal I^\bullet\to\mathcal J^\bullet\), unique up to homotopy. For any coherent \(\mathcal F\) on \(X\) define

\[ \nu_{\mathcal F}\colon\Hom_X(\mathcal F,\mathcal I^\bullet)\to\Hom_Y(f_*\mathcal F,\mathcal J^\bullet),\qquad\psi\mapsto c\circ f_*\psi, \]

a map of complexes, natural in \(\mathcal F\) (for \(\mathcal F_1\to\mathcal F_2\) the evident squares commute). Let \(\varphi_i(\mathcal F)=h^i(\nu_{\mathcal F})\colon\operatorname{Ext}^i_X(\mathcal F,f^!\mathcal G)\to\operatorname{Ext}^i_Y(f_*\mathcal F,\mathcal G)\). Up to canonical isomorphism, \(\varphi_i\) is independent of the choices (different choices change \(c\) by a homotopy), and it is natural in \(\mathcal F\). This is the composite of “apply \(f_*\)”, \(\operatorname{Ext}^i_X(\mathcal F,\mathcal H)\to\operatorname{Ext}^i_Y(f_*\mathcal F,f_*\mathcal H)\), with the map induced by \(e\colon f_*f^!\mathcal G\to\mathcal G\), as in the hint.

Compatibility with the long exact sequences in \(\mathcal F\). Let \(0\to\mathcal F'\to\mathcal F\to\mathcal F''\to0\) be exact in \(\mathfrak{Coh}(X)\). Then \(0\to f_*\mathcal F'\to f_*\mathcal F\to f_*\mathcal F''\to0\) is exact in \(\mathfrak{Coh}(Y)\), and the sequences of complexes \(0\to\Hom_X(\mathcal F'',\mathcal I^\bullet)\to\Hom_X(\mathcal F,\mathcal I^\bullet)\to\Hom_X(\mathcal F',\mathcal I^\bullet)\to0\) and the analogous one with \(\Hom_Y(f_*(\cdot),\mathcal J^\bullet)\), which define the connecting maps (Prop. 6.4), are related by the maps \(\nu_{\mathcal F''},\nu_{\mathcal F},\nu_{\mathcal F'}\), forming a morphism of short exact sequences of complexes. Hence \(\varphi_\bullet\) commutes with the connecting homomorphisms: it is a morphism of \(\delta\)-functors on \(\mathfrak{Coh}(X)\) (contravariant in \(\mathcal F\)).

(d) Now assume the hypotheses of (d) (separatedness will not actually be needed in the argument below). Write \(T^i(\mathcal F)=\operatorname{Ext}^i_X(\mathcal F,f^!\mathcal G)\), \(S^i(\mathcal F)=\operatorname{Ext}^i_Y(f_*\mathcal F,\mathcal G)\).

Step 0: \(i=0\). Identify \(T^0(\mathcal F)=\Hom_X(\mathcal F,\mathcal H)=\Gamma(Y,f_*\mathcal{H}om_X(\mathcal F,\mathcal H))\) and \(S^0(\mathcal F)=\Hom_Y(f_*\mathcal F,\mathcal G)=\Gamma(Y,\mathcal{H}om_Y(f_*\mathcal F,\mathcal G))\). For \(\psi\colon\mathcal F\to\mathcal H\subseteq\mathcal I^0\), \(\nu(\psi)=c^0f_*(\jmath\psi)\) and \(c^0|_{f_*\mathcal H}=(\mathcal G\to\mathcal J^0)\circ e\), so \(\varphi_0(\psi)=e\circ f_*\psi=\Gamma(\Theta)(\psi)\). By (b), \(\varphi_0\) is an isomorphism for every coherent \(\mathcal F\).

Step 1: \(\mathcal F\) locally free. First, \(f_*\mathcal F\) is locally free. Indeed, let \(V=\operatorname{Spec}B\subseteq Y\) be an open affine so small that \(\mathcal A|_V\) is free; then \(A'=\Gamma(f^{-1}V,\mathcal O)\cong B^m\) as \(B\)-modules. With \(\mathcal F|_{f^{-1}V}=\widetilde P\), \(P\) is a finitely generated module over \(A'\) all of whose localizations are free, hence a projective \(A'\)-module, a direct summand of some \(A'^s\cong B^{ms}\); so \(P\) is a finitely generated projective \(B\)-module and \(f_*\mathcal F|_V=\widetilde P\) is locally free.

By Lemma 6.6, \(\mathcal{H}om_X(\mathcal F,\mathcal I^j)=\mathcal F^\vee\otimes\mathcal I^j\) is injective, and \(\mathcal{H}om_X(\mathcal F,\cdot)\) is exact, so \(\mathcal{H}om_X(\mathcal F,\mathcal I^\bullet)\) is an injective resolution of \(\mathcal{H}om_X(\mathcal F,\mathcal H)\), and \(\Hom_X(\mathcal F,\mathcal I^\bullet)=\Gamma(X,\mathcal{H}om_X(\mathcal F,\mathcal I^\bullet))=\Gamma(Y,f_*\mathcal{H}om_X(\mathcal F,\mathcal I^\bullet))\). Likewise \(\Lambda^\bullet:=\mathcal{H}om_Y(f_*\mathcal F,\mathcal J^\bullet)\) is an injective resolution of \(\mathcal{H}om_Y(f_*\mathcal F,\mathcal G)\) and \(\Hom_Y(f_*\mathcal F,\mathcal J^\bullet)=\Gamma(Y,\Lambda^\bullet)\). The complex \(f_*\mathcal{H}om_X(\mathcal F,\mathcal I^\bullet)\) is a resolution of \(f_*\mathcal{H}om_X(\mathcal F,\mathcal H)\) by flasque sheaves (an injective sheaf is flasque, Lemma 2.4, and \(f_*\) preserves flasqueness); exactness is proved as in the Claim of (c), because \(\mathcal{H}om_X(\mathcal F,\mathcal H)\cong\mathcal F^\vee\otimes\mathcal H\) is quasi-coherent. Define the sheaf chain map

\[ \tilde\nu\colon f_*\mathcal{H}om_X(\mathcal F,\mathcal I^\bullet)\to\Lambda^\bullet,\qquad\psi\mapsto c\circ f_*\psi . \]

Then \(\Gamma(Y,\tilde\nu)=\nu_{\mathcal F}\), and in degree \(0\), restricted to \(f_*\mathcal{H}om_X(\mathcal F,\mathcal H)\), it equals \(\Theta\) followed by \(\mathcal{H}om(f_*\mathcal F,\mathcal G\to\mathcal J^0)\) (computation of Step 0). Thus \(\tilde\nu\) is a chain map between two resolutions of the same sheaf \(\mathcal M=\mathcal{H}om_Y(f_*\mathcal F,\mathcal G)\) (via the isomorphism \(\Theta\) of (b)), both consisting of \(\Gamma\)-acyclic (flasque) sheaves, compatible with the identification of \(f_*\mathcal{H}om_X(\mathcal F,\mathcal H)\) with \(\mathcal M\). By Prop. 1.2A (a resolution by acyclic objects computes the derived functors, via the comparison map to an injective resolution), \(\Gamma(\tilde\nu)\) induces isomorphisms \(h^i(\Gamma(Y,f_*\mathcal{H}om_X(\mathcal F,\mathcal I^\bullet)))\to h^i(\Gamma(Y,\Lambda^\bullet))\cong H^i(Y,\mathcal M)\). These are exactly the maps \(\varphi_i(\mathcal F)=h^i(\nu_{\mathcal F})\). Hence \(\varphi_i(\mathcal F)\) is an isomorphism for all \(i\ge0\) and all locally free \(\mathcal F\); in particular for \(\mathcal F=\mathcal O_X\), where the identification is \(H^i(X,f^!\mathcal G)\cong H^i(Y,f_*f^!\mathcal G)\) (this is Ex. 4.1, which our argument reproves) followed by \(H^i(Y,\mathcal{H}om(f_*\mathcal O_X,\mathcal G))\cong\operatorname{Ext}^i_Y(f_*\mathcal O_X,\mathcal G)\) (Prop. 6.7 and 6.3).

Step 2: induction on \(i\). We show by induction on \(i\) that \(\varphi_i(\mathcal F)\) is an isomorphism for all coherent \(\mathcal F\). The case \(i=0\) is Step 0. Let \(i\ge1\) and assume \(\varphi_{i-1}(\mathcal F)\) is an isomorphism for all coherent \(\mathcal F\). Let \(\mathcal F\) be coherent; since \(\mathfrak{Coh}(X)\) has enough locally frees, choose \(0\to\mathcal K\to\mathcal E\to\mathcal F\to0\) with \(\mathcal E\) locally free and \(\mathcal K\) coherent. Then \(\varphi\) gives a commutative ladder between the exact rows

\[ \begin{array}{ccccccccc} T^{i-1}(\mathcal E)&\to&T^{i-1}(\mathcal K)&\to&T^i(\mathcal F)&\to&T^i(\mathcal E)&\to&T^i(\mathcal K)\\ \downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow\\ S^{i-1}(\mathcal E)&\to&S^{i-1}(\mathcal K)&\to&S^i(\mathcal F)&\to&S^i(\mathcal E)&\to&S^i(\mathcal K) \end{array} \]

whose vertical maps are the \(\varphi\)'s. The first two verticals are isomorphisms by the induction hypothesis, and the fourth is an isomorphism by Step 1.

Injectivity of \(\varphi_i(\mathcal F)\): by the four lemma applied to the first four columns (first vertical onto, second and fourth injective) \(\varphi_i(\mathcal F)\) is injective. This holds for every coherent \(\mathcal F\), in particular for \(\mathcal K\).

Surjectivity: by the four lemma applied to the last four columns (second and fourth vertical onto, fifth injective by the preceding paragraph applied to \(\mathcal K\)), \(\varphi_i(\mathcal F)\) is surjective.

Hence \(\varphi_i\) is an isomorphism for all coherent \(\mathcal F\) and all quasi-coherent \(\mathcal G\), completing the induction. ∎

7. The Serre duality theorem

Exercise 7.1

Problem

Let \(X\) be an integral projective scheme of dimension \(\ge1\) over a field \(k\), and let \(\mathcal L\) be an ample invertible sheaf on \(X\). Then \(H^0(X,\mathcal L^{-1})=0\). (This is an easy special case of Kodaira's vanishing theorem.)

Solution

Write \(\mathcal L^{-1}=\mathcal L^\vee\). Since \(X\) is projective over \(k\), \(A:=H^0(X,\mathcal O_X)\) is a finite-dimensional \(k\)-vector space (Thm. 5.2(a)), and it is an integral domain because \(X\) is integral. A finite-dimensional domain over a field is a field, so \(A\) is a field; in particular every nonzero element of \(A\) is a unit of \(\Gamma(X,\mathcal O_X)\).

Suppose, for a contradiction, that \(0\ne s\in H^0(X,\mathcal L^{-1})\). Let \(\eta\) be the generic point and \(K=\mathcal O_{X,\eta}\) the function field. For an invertible sheaf \(\mathcal M\) on the integral scheme \(X\), \(\mathcal M_\eta\) is a one-dimensional \(K\)-vector space and \(\Gamma(X,\mathcal M)\to\mathcal M_\eta\) is injective (a section vanishing at the generic point vanishes on a dense open set, hence is zero, since \(\mathcal M\) is locally free and \(X\) is reduced). Hence a product of nonzero sections of invertible sheaves is nonzero: in \(\mathcal M_\eta\otimes\mathcal N_\eta\cong K\) the product of two nonzero elements of one-dimensional \(K\)-spaces is nonzero.

Since \(\mathcal L\) is ample, \(\mathcal L^n\) is generated by global sections for some \(n\geq 1\) (definition of ample applied to \(\mathcal F=\mathcal O_X\); II, Sect. 7). As \(X\ne\emptyset\), \(\mathcal L^n\neq0\), so there is \(0\ne t\in H^0(X,\mathcal L^n)\). Then \(s^n\in H^0(X,\mathcal L^{-n})\) is nonzero by the remark above, and so

\[ u:=t\cdot s^n\in H^0(X,\mathcal L^n\otimes\mathcal L^{-n})=H^0(X,\mathcal O_X)=A \]

is nonzero, hence a unit of \(A\).

Claim: \(s\colon\mathcal O_X\to\mathcal L^{-1}\) is an isomorphism. Let \(x\in X\) and let \(e\) generate \(\mathcal L_x\). Write \(s_x=a\,e^{-1}\), \(t_x=b\,e^{n}\) with \(a,b\in\mathcal O_{X,x}\) (where \(e^{-1}\) is the dual generator of \(\mathcal L^{-1}_x\)). Then \(u_x=a^nb\) is a unit of \(\mathcal O_{X,x}\), so \(a\) is a unit, i.e. \(s_x\) generates \(\mathcal L^{-1}_x\). Thus \(s\) is an isomorphism and \(\mathcal L\cong\mathcal O_X\).

Now ampleness of \(\mathcal L\cong\mathcal O_X\) says that every coherent sheaf on \(X\) is generated by global sections. Take a closed point \(x\in X\) (\(X\) is quasi-compact and nonempty) and let \(\mathcal I_x\subset\mathcal O_X\) be the ideal sheaf of the reduced closed subscheme \(\{x\}\). A global section of \(\mathcal I_x\) is an element of \(A\) vanishing at \(x\); a nonzero element of \(A\) is a unit and so does not vanish at \(x\). Hence \(H^0(X,\mathcal I_x)=0\), and since \(\mathcal I_x\) is generated by global sections, \(\mathcal I_x=0\). Then \(\mathcal O_X=\mathcal O_X/\mathcal I_x\) is supported at \(\{x\}\); but the stalk of \(\mathcal O_X\) at the generic point is \(K\neq0\), so \(\eta=x\) and \(X=\{x\}\) is a point, contradicting \(\dim X\ge1\). Hence \(H^0(X,\mathcal L^{-1})=0\). ∎

Exercise 7.2

Problem

Let \(f\colon X\to Y\) be a finite morphism of projective schemes of the same dimension over a field \(k\), and let \(\omega_Y^\circ\) be a dualizing sheaf for \(Y\).

  1. (a)

    Show that \(f^!\omega_Y^\circ\) is a dualizing sheaf for \(X\), where \(f^!\) is defined as in (Ex. 6.10).

  2. (b)

    If \(X\) and \(Y\) are both nonsingular, and \(k\) algebraically closed, conclude that there is a natural trace map \(t\colon f_*\omega_X\to\omega_Y\).

Solution

Let \(n=\dim X=\dim Y\). Recall (Ex. 6.10(a)): \(f\) is finite, hence affine, so quasi-coherent \(\mathcal O_X\)-modules correspond to quasi-coherent \(f_*\mathcal O_X\)-modules (II, Ex. 5.17); for a quasi-coherent \(\mathcal O_Y\)-module \(\mathcal G\), \(f^!\mathcal G\) is the quasi-coherent \(\mathcal O_X\)-module with \(f_*(f^!\mathcal G)=\mathcal Hom_{\mathcal O_Y}(f_*\mathcal O_X,\mathcal G)\).

(a) Coherence. \(f_*\mathcal O_X\) is coherent (\(f\) finite) and \(\omega^\circ_Y\) is coherent, so \(\mathcal Hom_{\mathcal O_Y}(f_*\mathcal O_X,\omega^\circ_Y)\) is coherent (Ex. 6.3(a)). For a finite morphism, an \(\mathcal O_X\)-module is coherent iff its direct image is (locally \(B\) is a finite \(A\)-algebra, and a \(B\)-module is finitely generated iff it is so over \(A\)). So \(f^!\omega_Y^\circ\) is coherent. Also \(X\) is projective over \(k\) of dimension \(n\) by hypothesis.

The natural isomorphism of Ex. 6.10(b). For \(\mathcal F\) coherent on \(X\) and \(\mathcal G\) quasi-coherent on \(Y\) there is the map

\[ f_*\mathcal Hom_X(\mathcal F,f^!\mathcal G)\longrightarrow\mathcal Hom_Y(f_*\mathcal F,\mathcal G),\qquad \varphi\longmapsto \operatorname{ev}_1\circ f_*\varphi, \]

where \(\operatorname{ev}_1\colon f_*f^!\mathcal G=\mathcal Hom_Y(f_*\mathcal O_X,\mathcal G)\to\mathcal G\) is evaluation at \(1\). It is an isomorphism: over an affine open \(\operatorname{Spec}A\subseteq Y\), with \(f^{-1}=\operatorname{Spec}B\), \(\mathcal F=\tilde M\), \(\mathcal G=\tilde N\) (\(M\) finitely generated over \(B\)), it is the sheaf associated to the usual adjunction isomorphism \(\operatorname{Hom}_B(M,\operatorname{Hom}_A(B,N))\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Hom}_A(M,N)\), \(\varphi\mapsto(m\mapsto\varphi(m)(1))\), with inverse \(\psi\mapsto(m\mapsto(b\mapsto\psi(bm)))\). Taking global sections,

\[ \operatorname{Hom}_X(\mathcal F,f^!\mathcal G)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\operatorname{Hom}_Y(f_*\mathcal F,\mathcal G),\qquad\varphi\mapsto\varphi^\flat:=\operatorname{ev}_1\circ f_*\varphi .\qquad\text{(A)} \]

The trace. Since \(f\) is finite and \(X,Y\) are noetherian and separated, \(H^i(X,\mathcal F)\cong H^i(Y,f_*\mathcal F)\) naturally in quasi-coherent \(\mathcal F\) (Ex. 4.1). Define \(t_X\colon H^n(X,f^!\omega^\circ_Y)=H^n(Y,f_*f^!\omega_Y^\circ)\to k\) as the composite

\[ H^n(Y,f_*f^!\omega^\circ_Y)\xrightarrow{H^n(\operatorname{ev}_1)}H^n(Y,\omega^\circ_Y)\xrightarrow{\ t_Y\ }k . \]

Duality. Let \(\mathcal F\) be coherent on \(X\). For \(\varphi\in\operatorname{Hom}_X(\mathcal F,f^!\omega_Y^\circ)\) and \(\xi\in H^n(X,\mathcal F)=H^n(Y,f_*\mathcal F)\) we have, by functoriality of the identification \(H^n(X,-)=H^n(Y,f_*-)\),

\[ t_X\big(H^n(\varphi)(\xi)\big)=t_Y\big(H^n(\operatorname{ev}_1)H^n(f_*\varphi)(\xi)\big)=t_Y\big(H^n(\varphi^\flat)(\xi)\big). \]

So the pairing \(\operatorname{Hom}_X(\mathcal F,f^!\omega^\circ_Y)\times H^n(X,\mathcal F)\to k\) defining the map \(\operatorname{Hom}_X(\mathcal F,f^!\omega_Y^\circ)\to H^n(X,\mathcal F)'\) is the composite of the isomorphism (A) with the corresponding pairing for \(f_*\mathcal F\) on \(Y\). Since \(f_*\mathcal F\) is coherent and \(\omega_Y^\circ\) is dualizing, \(\operatorname{Hom}_Y(f_*\mathcal F,\omega_Y^\circ)\to H^n(Y,f_*\mathcal F)'\) is an isomorphism, and therefore so is \(\operatorname{Hom}_X(\mathcal F,f^!\omega_Y^\circ)\to H^n(X,\mathcal F)'\). Thus \((f^!\omega^\circ_Y,t_X)\) is a dualizing sheaf for \(X\). (The hypothesis \(\dim X=\dim Y\) is what makes both pairings use the same \(n\).)

(b) Now let \(X,Y\) be nonsingular projective varieties of the same dimension \(n\), \(k=\bar k\). By Cor. 7.12, \(\omega_X\) and \(\omega_Y\) are dualizing sheaves, with traces \(t_X,t_Y\) (which are determined up to scalars; fix them). By (a), \(f^!\omega_Y\) is also a dualizing sheaf for \(X\), with trace \(t_Y\circ H^n(\operatorname{ev}_1)\). By Prop. 7.2 there is a unique isomorphism \(\varphi\colon f^!\omega_Y\xrightarrow{\sim}\omega_X\) compatible with the traces. Define

\[ t\colon f_*\omega_X\xrightarrow{\ f_*\varphi^{-1}\ }f_*f^!\omega_Y=\mathcal Hom_Y(f_*\mathcal O_X,\omega_Y)\xrightarrow{\ \operatorname{ev}_1\ }\omega_Y . \]

This is the natural trace map; it is \(t=(\varphi^{-1})^\flat\) in the notation of (A), and by construction \(t_Y\circ H^n(t)=t_X\) under \(H^n(Y,f_*\omega_X)=H^n(X,\omega_X)\). (One expects that on generic stalks it is the usual trace \(K(X)\to K(Y)\) of the field extension, but this is not needed and is not proved here.) ∎

Exercise 7.3

Problem

Let \(X=\mathbf P^n_k\). Show that \(H^q(X,\Omega^p_X)=0\) for \(p\ne q\), and \(k\) for \(p=q\), \(0\le p,q\le n\).

Solution

Here \(\Omega^p=\wedge^p\Omega_{X/k}\). For \(p>n\), \(\Omega^p=0\).

Step 1: an exact sequence. The Euler sequence (II, 8.13) is \(0\to\Omega^1\to\mathcal O(-1)^{n+1}\xrightarrow{\pi}\mathcal O\to0\). Let \(\mathcal F=\mathcal O(-1)^{n+1}\) and \(\mathcal F'=\Omega^1=\ker\pi\). Define \(\iota\colon\wedge^p\mathcal F\to\wedge^{p-1}\mathcal F\) (contraction with \(\pi\)) by

\[ \iota(v_1\wedge\dots\wedge v_p)=\sum_{j=1}^p(-1)^{j-1}\pi(v_j)\,v_1\wedge\dots\wedge\widehat{v_j}\wedge\dots\wedge v_p . \]

We claim that for \(p\ge1\),

\[ 0\to\wedge^p\mathcal F'\to\wedge^p\mathcal F\xrightarrow{\ \iota\ }\wedge^{p-1}\mathcal F'\to0\qquad\text{(W)} \]

is exact. This is local: on an open set where \(\pi\) is surjective with a section \(e\), \(\mathcal F=\mathcal F'\oplus\mathcal O e\) with \(\pi(e)=1\), \(\pi|_{\mathcal F'}=0\), and \(\wedge^p\mathcal F=\wedge^p\mathcal F'\oplus e\wedge\wedge^{p-1}\mathcal F'\). Then \(\iota(a)=0\) for \(a\in\wedge^p\mathcal F'\) and \(\iota(e\wedge b)=b-e\wedge\iota(b)=b\) for \(b\in\wedge^{p-1}\mathcal F'\) (as \(\iota(b)=0\)), so \(\ker\iota=\wedge^p\mathcal F'\) and \(\iota\) maps onto \(\wedge^{p-1}\mathcal F'\). (Cf. II, Ex. 5.16.) Since \(\wedge^p\mathcal F\cong\mathcal O(-p)^{\binom{n+1}{p}}\) and \(\wedge^{p-1}\mathcal F'=\Omega^{p-1}\), (W) reads

\[ 0\to\Omega^p\to\mathcal O(-p)^{\binom{n+1}{p}}\to\Omega^{p-1}\to0\qquad(p\ge1). \]

Step 2: cohomology of \(\mathcal O(-p)\). By Thm. 5.1, \(H^i(\mathbf P^n,\mathcal O(m))\neq0\) only for \(i=0,m\ge0\) or \(i=n\), \(m\le-n-1\). Hence for \(1\le p\le n\) we have \(H^i(X,\mathcal O(-p))=0\) for all \(i\ge0\) (for \(i=0\) because \(-p<0\); for \(i=n\) because \(-p\ge-n>-n-1\); for \(0<i<n\) always).

Step 3: induction. We show, for \(0\le p\le n\): \(H^q(X,\Omega^p)=k\) if \(q=p\) and \(0\) otherwise, for every \(q\ge0\). For \(p=0\), \(\Omega^0=\mathcal O\) and this is Thm. 5.1(a),(b) (with \(m=0\)). For \(1\le p\le n\), the long exact sequence of the sequence of Step 1 and the vanishing of Step 2 give \(H^0(\Omega^p)=0\) and isomorphisms (connecting maps)

\[ H^{q-1}(X,\Omega^{p-1})\xrightarrow{\sim}H^q(X,\Omega^p),\qquad q\ge1, \]

since the neighbors \(H^{q-1}(\mathcal O(-p)^N)\) and \(H^q(\mathcal O(-p)^N)\) vanish. By induction, \(H^q(\Omega^p)\cong H^{q-1}(\Omega^{p-1})\) is \(k\) iff \(q-1=p-1\), and \(0\) otherwise; and \(H^0(\Omega^p)=0\) agrees with \(q\neq p\) since \(p\ge1\). ∎

Exercise 7.4

Problem

The Cohomology Class of a Subvariety. Let \(X\) be a nonsingular projective variety of dimension \(n\) over an algebraically closed field \(k\). Let \(Y\) be a nonsingular subvariety of codimension \(p\) (hence dimension \(n-p\)). From the natural map \(\Omega_X\otimes\mathcal O_Y\to\Omega_Y\) of (II, 8.12) we deduce a map \(\Omega^{n-p}_X\to\Omega^{n-p}_Y\). This induces a map on cohomology \(H^{n-p}(X,\Omega_X^{n-p})\to H^{n-p}(Y,\Omega_Y^{n-p})\). Now \(\Omega_Y^{n-p}=\omega_Y\) is a dualizing sheaf for \(Y\), so we have the trace map \(t_Y\colon H^{n-p}(Y,\Omega_Y^{n-p})\to k\). Composing, we obtain a linear map \(H^{n-p}(X,\Omega_X^{n-p})\to k\). By (7.13) this corresponds to an element \(\eta(Y)\in H^p(X,\Omega^p_X)\), which we call the cohomology class of \(Y\).

  1. (a)

    If \(P\in X\) is a closed point, show that \(t_X(\eta(P))=1\), where \(\eta(P)\in H^n(X,\Omega^n)\) and \(t_X\) is the trace map.

  2. (b)

    If \(X=\mathbf P^n\), identify \(H^p(X,\Omega^p_X)\) with \(k\) by (Ex. 7.3), and show that \(\eta(Y)=(\deg Y)\cdot1\), where \(\deg Y\) is its degree as a projective variety (I, Sect. 7). [Hint: Cut with a hyperplane \(H\subseteq X\), and use Bertini's theorem (II, 8.18) to reduce to the case \(Y\) is a finite set of points.]

  3. (c)

    For any scheme \(X\) of finite type over \(k\), we define a homomorphism of sheaves of abelian groups \(d\log\colon\mathcal O_X^*\to\Omega_X\) by \(d\log(f)=f^{-1}df\). Here \(\mathcal O^*\) is a group under multiplication, and \(\Omega_X\) is a group under addition. This induces a map on cohomology \(\operatorname{Pic}X=H^1(X,\mathcal O_X^*)\to H^1(X,\Omega_X)\) which we denote by \(c\)—see (Ex. 4.5).

  4. (d)

    Returning to the hypotheses above, suppose \(p=1\). Show that \(\eta(Y)=c(\mathcal L(Y))\), where \(\mathcal L(Y)\) is the invertible sheaf corresponding to the divisor \(Y\).

(Starred in the book.)

Solution

Setup, conventions, and what is being assumed. Write \(\Omega^i=\Omega^i_X\). Cohomology classes of \(\Omega^\bullet\) have a cup product \(H^a(X,\Omega^i)\times H^b(X,\Omega^j)\to H^{a+b}(X,\Omega^{i+j})\) (induced by \(\wedge\)), which is graded-commutative and compatible with restriction \(i^*\colon H^a(X,\Omega^j_X)\to H^a(Y,\Omega^j_Y)\) to a closed subvariety \(i\colon Y\hookrightarrow X\) (because \(\Omega^\bullet_X\to i_*\Omega^\bullet_Y\) is a map of sheaves of graded algebras). We use that the duality isomorphism of Cor. 7.13 is given by the cup product pairing followed by the trace,

\[ H^q(X,\Omega^p)\times H^{n-q}(X,\Omega^{n-p})\xrightarrow{\ \cup\ }H^n(X,\Omega^n)\xrightarrow{\ t_X\ }k , \]

which is perfect. (This is how the isomorphism \(\Omega^{n-p}\cong(\Omega^p)^\vee\otimes\omega\) and (7.7) are combined in the proof of 7.13; that the iso of (7.7) is the cup product pairing is the standard compatibility of the maps \(\theta^i\) of Thm. 7.6 with cup/Yoneda products, which we take for granted.) Thus \(\eta(Y)\in H^p(X,\Omega^p)\) is characterized by

\[ t_X\big(\eta(Y)\cup\alpha\big)=t_Y\big(\alpha|_Y\big)\qquad\text{for all }\alpha\in H^{n-p}(X,\Omega^{n-p}) .\qquad\text{(E)} \]

For a disjoint union \(Y=\bigsqcup Y_j\) of nonsingular varieties of the same dimension we put \(t_Y=\sum t_{Y_j}\) and \(\eta(Y)=\sum\eta(Y_j)\); (E) still characterizes \(\eta(Y)\).

Normalization of the traces. By Prop. 7.2 each trace map \(t_Y\) is determined by the dualizing sheaf \(\Omega^{\dim Y}_Y\) only up to a nonzero scalar, and Hartshorne does not fix a normalization (cf. Rem. 7.14: only for curves is the trace made explicit, via residues). A statement such as “\(t_X(\eta(P))=1\)” or “\(\eta(Y)=c(\mathcal L(Y))\)” can only hold for compatible normalizations. So the exercise implicitly uses the canonical traces (residues, Grothendieck duality), and I make explicit the only two properties of them that are used:

  • •

    (T1) for \(Y=\operatorname{Spec}k\) a rational point, \(\omega_Y=\mathcal O_Y=k\) and \(t_Y=\mathrm{id}_k\);

  • •

    (T2) (compatibility with Poincare residue) if \(D\subset Z\) is a nonsingular subvariety of codimension one in a nonsingular projective variety \(Z\) of dimension \(m\ge1\), then \(t_D=-\,t_Z\circ\partial\), where \(\partial\colon H^{m-1}(D,\Omega^{m-1}_D)\to H^m(Z,\Omega^m_Z)\) is the connecting homomorphism of the residue sequence (R) below (computed with the Cech conventions fixed in (d)).

Since the traces \(t_Z\), \(t_D\) are chosen independently by Prop. 7.2, nothing in the axioms of a dualizing sheaf links them; (T2) is precisely the normalization that does, and it is an assumption (in dimension \(1\) it is the residue theorem, Rem. 7.14). Thus what is proved below from first principles in (d) is the Cech computation (D); the equality \(\eta(Y)=c(\mathcal L(Y))\) then follows only via (T2). The sign in (T2) only reflects the sign conventions for Cech cochains and for \(\operatorname{Pic}=H^1(\mathcal O^*)\) made in (d); with the opposite convention one would get \(\eta(D)=-c(\mathcal L(D))\) in (d). For a curve \(Z\) and a point \(D=P\), (T2) is the statement \(t_Z(c(\mathcal O(P)))=1\), i.e. \(\operatorname{res}_P(d\log t)=1\) for a uniformizer \(t\), Rem. 7.14. (Also the identification \(H^p(X,\Omega^p)\cong k\) in (b) is determined by Ex. 7.3 only up to a scalar; the one meant is the one in which linear subspaces have class \(1\), equivalently \(\xi\mapsto t_X(\xi\cup c(\mathcal O(1))^{n-p})\), see (b).)

(a) Here \(p=n\), \(Y=\{P\}=\operatorname{Spec}k\) (\(k=\bar k\)), \(\Omega^0_Y=\mathcal O_Y=k\) and \(t_Y=\mathrm{id}\) by (T1). The restriction \(H^0(X,\Omega^0_X)=H^0(X,\mathcal O_X)=k\to H^0(Y,\mathcal O_Y)=k\) is the identity on constants. Apply (E) with \(\alpha=1\in H^0(X,\mathcal O_X)\):

\[ t_X(\eta(P))=t_X(\eta(P)\cup1)=t_Y(1|_Y)=1 . \]

(c) Let \(f,g\) be units. Then \(d\log(fg)=(fg)^{-1}(f\,dg+g\,df)=g^{-1}dg+f^{-1}df\), so \(d\log\colon\mathcal O_X^*\to\Omega_X\) is a morphism of sheaves of abelian groups (multiplicative to additive), and it is compatible with pullback of functions and restriction to closed subschemes \(i\colon Y\to X\): \(i^*(d\log f)=d\log(i^*f)\). Hence it induces homomorphisms \(H^i(X,\mathcal O_X^*)\to H^i(X,\Omega_X)\); for \(i=1\) and \(\operatorname{Pic}X\cong H^1(X,\mathcal O_X^*)\) (Ex. 4.5) this is \(c\colon\operatorname{Pic}X\to H^1(X,\Omega_X)\). In terms of Cech cocycles for an open cover \(\mathfrak U\): if \(\mathcal L\) has trivializations \(\phi_i\colon\mathcal L|_{U_i}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal O_{U_i}\) and transition functions \(g_{ij}=\phi_i\phi_j^{-1}\in\mathcal O^*(U_{ij})\), then \(c(\mathcal L)=[\,d\log g_{ij}\,]\). Moreover \(c\) is a group homomorphism, \(c(\mathcal L\otimes\mathcal M)=c(\mathcal L)+c(\mathcal M)\), and \(i^*c(\mathcal L)=c(i^*\mathcal L)\).

(d) Let \(Y\subset X\) be a nonsingular prime divisor (\(p=1\)); put \(\mathcal L=\mathcal L(Y)=\mathcal O_X(Y)\cong\mathcal I_Y^{-1}\). By (E) and the perfectness of the pairing it suffices to show

\[ t_X\big(c(\mathcal L)\cup\alpha\big)=t_Y(\alpha|_Y)\qquad\text{for all }\alpha\in H^{n-1}(X,\Omega^{n-1}).\qquad\text{(G)} \]

The residue sequence. Since \(Y\) is nonsingular of codimension one, \(\mathcal I_Y/\mathcal I_Y^2\to\Omega_X\otimes\mathcal O_Y\to\Omega_Y\to0\) is exact on the left too (II, 8.17), with all terms locally free. Let \(x\in Y\) and \(f\) a generator of \(\mathcal I_Y\) near \(x\); then \(df\) generates the image of \(\mathcal I_Y/\mathcal I_Y^2\), which is a direct summand, so near \(x\) there is a basis \(df,dx_2,\dots,dx_n\) of \(\Omega^1_X\), and \(dx_2|_Y,\dots,dx_n|_Y\) is a basis of \(\Omega_Y^1\). Hence \(\Omega^n_X(Y)=\Omega^n_X\otimes\mathcal L\) is generated near \(x\) by \(\frac{df}{f}\wedge dx_2\wedge\dots\wedge dx_n\), every section of \(\Omega^n_X(Y)\) near \(x\) is of the form \(\frac{df}f\wedge\beta\) with \(\beta\in\Omega^{n-1}_X\), and the kernel of \(\Omega^{n-1}_X\to\Omega^{n-1}_Y\) is \(f\Omega^{n-1}_X+df\wedge\Omega^{n-2}_X\). One checks that

\[ \operatorname{Res}\colon\Omega^n_X(Y)\to i_*\Omega^{n-1}_Y,\qquad \operatorname{Res}\Big(\frac{df}{f}\wedge\beta\Big)=\beta|_Y \]

is well defined (if \(\frac{df}f\wedge\beta=\frac{df}f\wedge\beta'\) then \(\beta-\beta'\in df\wedge\Omega^{n-2}\) modulo \(f\Omega^{n-1}\), so \((\beta-\beta')|_Y=0\); and replacing \(f\) by \(uf\), \(u\) a unit, changes \(\frac{df}{f}\) by the regular form \(\frac{du}u\), which changes \(\beta\) only by terms restricting to \(0\) on \(Y\), using the same description of the kernel), \(\mathcal O_X\)-linear, surjective, with kernel \(\Omega^n_X\) (a form \(\frac{df}{f}\wedge\beta\) has residue \(0\) iff \(\beta\in f\Omega^{n-1}+df\wedge\Omega^{n-2}\) iff it lies in \(\Omega^n_X\)). This is the Poincare residue sequence

\[ 0\to\Omega^n_X\to\Omega^n_X(Y)\xrightarrow{\ \operatorname{Res}\ }i_*\Omega^{n-1}_Y\to0 ,\qquad\text{(R)} \]

all of whose terms are quasi-coherent; let \(\partial\colon H^{n-1}(Y,\Omega^{n-1}_Y)\to H^n(X,\Omega^n_X)\) be its connecting homomorphism (note \(H^{j}(X,i_*\mathcal G)=H^j(Y,\mathcal G)\), Lemma 2.10).

Computation of \(\partial(\alpha|_Y)\). \(X\) is separated, so we may compute with Cech cochains for an affine open cover \(\mathfrak U=\{U_i\}_{i\in I}\) (\(I\) totally ordered) such that \(\mathcal I_Y|_{U_i}=f_i\mathcal O_{U_i}\) (take \(f_i=1\) if \(U_i\cap Y=\emptyset\)); Cech cohomology computes sheaf cohomology compatibly with connecting maps (Thm. 4.5), and the sequence of Cech complexes of (R) is exact because the terms are quasi-coherent and the \(U_{i_0\dots i_r}\) are affine. The sheaf \(\mathcal L\) is trivialized on \(U_i\) by \(\phi_i=\) multiplication by \(f_i\) (sending the generator \(1/f_i\) of \(\mathcal O(Y)|_{U_i}\) to \(1\)), so its transition functions are \(g_{ij}=\phi_i\phi_j^{-1}=f_i/f_j\), and \(c(\mathcal L)=[d\log g_{ij}]\). Cup products of Cech cochains are \((a\cup b)_{i_0\dots i_{r+s}}=a_{i_0\dots i_r}\wedge b_{i_r\dots i_{r+s}}\).

Let \(\alpha=(\alpha_{i_0\dots i_{n-1}})\) be a Cech \((n-1)\)-cocycle of \(\Omega^{n-1}_X\). A lift of \(\alpha|_Y\) (a cocycle of \(i_*\Omega^{n-1}_Y\)) to a cochain of \(\Omega^n_X(Y)\) is

\[ \lambda_{i_0\dots i_{n-1}}=d\log f_{i_0}\wedge\alpha_{i_0\dots i_{n-1}},\qquad \operatorname{Res}\lambda=\alpha|_Y . \]

Now compute \((\delta\lambda)_{i_0\dots i_n}=\sum_{j=0}^n(-1)^j\lambda_{i_0\dots\widehat{i_j}\dots i_n}\). The term \(j=0\) is \(d\log f_{i_1}\wedge\alpha_{i_1\dots i_n}\), and the terms \(j\ge1\) have first index \(i_0\), so they give \(d\log f_{i_0}\wedge\sum_{j\ge1}(-1)^j\alpha_{\dots\widehat{i_j}\dots}=-d\log f_{i_0}\wedge\alpha_{i_1\dots i_n}\), because \(\sum_{j=0}^n(-1)^j\alpha_{\dots\widehat{i_j}\dots}=0\) (\(\alpha\) is a cocycle). Hence

\[ (\delta\lambda)_{i_0\dots i_n}=-\,d\log\!\big(f_{i_0}/f_{i_1}\big)\wedge\alpha_{i_1\dots i_n}=-\,d\log g_{i_0i_1}\wedge\alpha_{i_1\dots i_n}=-(c(\mathcal L)\cup\alpha)_{i_0\dots i_n}. \]

This is a cocycle with values in \(\Omega^n_X\) (as it must be), and by definition of the connecting homomorphism

\[ \partial(\alpha|_Y)=-\,c(\mathcal L)\cup\alpha\qquad\text{in }H^n(X,\Omega^n_X).\qquad\text{(D)} \]

By (T2) with \(Z=X\), \(D=Y\), \(t_Y=-t_X\circ\partial\), hence

\[ t_Y(\alpha|_Y)=-t_X(\partial(\alpha|_Y))=t_X(c(\mathcal L)\cup\alpha), \]

which is (G). Therefore \(\eta(Y)=c(\mathcal L(Y))\) by the perfectness of the cup product pairing. For \(Y=\bigsqcup Y_j\) a nonsingular closed subscheme all of whose components are divisors, we get the same by additivity: \(\eta(Y)=\sum c(\mathcal L(Y_j))=c(\mathcal L(Y))\), since \(\mathcal L(Y)=\bigotimes\mathcal L(Y_j)\) and \(c\) is a homomorphism. (In particular, for a curve \(X\) and a point \(P\), (a) and (d) together say \(t_X(c(\mathcal O(P)))=1\).)

(b) Let \(X=\mathbf P^n\) and \(c:=c(\mathcal O_X(1))\in H^1(X,\Omega^1)\), an element of even total degree \(2\), which therefore commutes with all cup products. We prove:

Claim A. Let \(Y\subset X\) be a nonsingular closed subscheme of pure codimension \(p<n\) (a disjoint union of nonsingular varieties), and \(H\) a hyperplane such that the scheme \(D=Y\cap H\) is nonsingular of pure codimension \(p+1\) in \(X\) (i.e. it is reduced, and \(H\) contains no component of \(Y\)). Then \(\eta(Y)\cup c=\eta(D)\).

Proof. Let \(d=n-p=\dim Y\) and \(\alpha\in H^{d-1}(X,\Omega^{d-1})\). Using (E) for \(Y\) with \(\beta=c\cup\alpha\), and the compatibility of restriction with cup products and with \(c\),

\begin{align*} t_X\big((\eta(Y)\cup c)\cup\alpha\big)&=t_X\big(\eta(Y)\cup(c\cup\alpha)\big)=t_Y\big((c\cup\alpha)|_Y\big)=t_Y\big(c(\mathcal O_X(1)|_Y)\cup\alpha|_Y\big). \end{align*}

Now \(\mathcal O_X(1)|_Y=\mathcal O_X(H)|_Y=\mathcal O_Y(D)\): a local equation of \(H\) restricts to a local equation of the scheme \(D=Y\cap H\) in \(Y\), which is a nonsingular, hence reduced, divisor \(\sum D_j\) on each component of \(Y\), so \(\mathcal O_Y(D)=\mathcal L_Y(D)\). By (d) applied to the nonsingular projective variety (each component of) \(Y\) and its nonsingular divisor \(D\) (with \(\alpha|_Y\in H^{d-1}(Y,\Omega^{d-1}_Y)\), and with \(Y\) of dimension \(d\) in place of \(X\), formula (G)),

\[ t_Y\big(c(\mathcal L_Y(D))\cup\alpha|_Y\big)=t_D\big((\alpha|_Y)|_D\big)=t_D(\alpha|_D)=t_X\big(\eta(D)\cup\alpha\big), \]

the last equality by (E) for \(D\). As \(\alpha\) is arbitrary and the pairing is perfect, \(\eta(Y)\cup c=\eta(D)\). \(\square\)

Claim B. For \(Y\subset\mathbf P^n\) nonsingular (disjoint union of varieties) of pure codimension \(p\),

\[ t_X\big(\eta(Y)\cup c^{\,n-p}\big)=\deg Y\cdot 1_k . \]

Proof. Put \(Y_0=Y\). If \(d=n-p\ge1\), by Bertini (II, 8.18; applied to each component of \(Y_0\) with its embedding in \(\mathbf P^n\); the hyperplanes with the required property form a dense open subset of the dual projective space for each component, so a common one exists) there is a hyperplane \(H_1\) containing no component of \(Y_0\) such that \(Y_1=Y_0\cap H_1\) is regular at every point, hence nonsingular (as \(k=\bar k\)) of pure dimension \(d-1\) (it is nonempty since \(d\ge1\), I, 7.2). Repeat to get \(Y_2=Y_1\cap H_2,\dots,Y_d=Y_{d-1}\cap H_d\), a finite set of reduced points of codimension \(n\) in \(X\). By Claim A used \(d\) times (and \(c^{d}\) commuting with everything), \(\eta(Y)\cup c^d=\eta(Y_d)=\sum_{P\in Y_d}\eta(P)\). By (a), \(t_X(\eta(Y)\cup c^d)=\#Y_d\cdot1_k\).

It remains to see that \(\#Y_d=\deg Y\). For a variety \(Z\) of dimension \(r\ge1\) in \(\mathbf P^n\) and a hyperplane \(H\not\supseteq Z\) with \(Z\cap H=Z_1\cup\dots\cup Z_s\) (irreducible components), I, Thm. 7.7 says \(\deg Z=\sum_j i(Z,H;Z_j)\deg Z_j\), where \(i(Z,H;Z_j)\) is the length of the local ring of the scheme \(Z\cap H\) at the generic point of \(Z_j\). Since \(Z\cap H\) is reduced here, all \(i=1\). Hence \(\deg Y_{i}=\deg Y_{i+1}\) for all \(i\) (sum over components; degree is additive for disjoint unions, being the leading coefficient of the Hilbert polynomial times \(r!\)), and finally \(\deg Y_d=\#Y_d\) (a point has Hilbert polynomial \(1\)). Thus \(\#Y_d=\deg Y\). \(\square\)

Identification of \(H^p(X,\Omega^p)\) with \(k\). By Ex. 7.3, \(H^p(X,\Omega^p)\) is a one-dimensional \(k\)-vector space (\(0\le p\le n\)), so it is isomorphic to \(k\), but not canonically. Let \(\iota\colon H^p(X,\Omega^p)\to k\), \(\iota(\xi)=t_X(\xi\cup c^{n-p})\). For a linear subspace \(L\subset X\) of codimension \(p\) (nonsingular, of degree \(1\)), Claim B gives \(\iota(\eta(L))=1\ne0\). So \(\iota\) is a nonzero linear functional on a one-dimensional space, hence an isomorphism, and it is the identification in which the class of a linear subspace is \(1\) (for \(p=n\) it is just \(t_X\), by (a)). With this identification, Claim B says

\[ \eta(Y)=\deg(Y)\cdot1 , \]

i.e. \(\iota(\eta(Y))=\deg Y\cdot1_k\); equivalently \(\eta(Y)=(\deg Y)\,\eta(L)\). (If \(\operatorname{char}k=\ell>0\), this is the statement modulo \(\ell\).) For \(p=0\), \(Y=X\), one has directly \(\eta(X)=1\in H^0(X,\mathcal O_X)=k\) by (E), \(\deg X=1\). ∎

8. Higher direct images of sheaves

Conventions and results used. For a continuous map (or morphism of ringed spaces) \(f:X\to Y\), \(R^if_*\) denotes the right derived functors of \(f_*\). By Prop. 8.4 they may be computed on \(\mathfrak{Mod}(X)\) as well as on \(\mathfrak{Ab}(X)\). We use: injective \(\mathcal O_X\)-modules are flasque (Prop. 2.4), flasque sheaves are acyclic for \(\Gamma(X,\cdot)\) (Prop. 2.5) and for \(f_*\) (Cor. 8.3), and acyclic resolutions compute derived functors (Prop. 1.2A). For an \(\mathcal O_X\)-module \(\mathcal F\) we write \(\mathcal F(l)=\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal O_X(l)\), and \(\mathcal G^{\vee}=\mathcal{H}om(\mathcal G,\mathcal O)\) (written \(\check{\ }\) in the book).

Exercise 8.1

Problem

Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a continuous map of topological spaces. Let \(\mathcal F\) be a sheaf of abelian groups on \(X\), and assume that \(R^if_*(\mathcal F)=0\) for all \(i>0\). Show that there are natural isomorphisms, for each \(i\ge 0\),

\[ H^i(X,\mathcal F)\cong H^i(Y,f_*\mathcal F). \]

(This is a degenerate case of the Leray spectral sequence—see Godement [1, II, 4.17.1].)

Solution

Choose a flasque resolution \(0\to\mathcal F\to\mathcal G^0\to\mathcal G^1\to\cdots\) of \(\mathcal F\) on \(X\) (for instance an injective resolution in \(\mathfrak{Ab}(X)\), or the Godement resolution). Each \(f_*\mathcal G^j\) is flasque on \(Y\), because \(f_*\mathcal G^j(V)=\mathcal G^j(f^{-1}V)\) and the restriction maps of \(\mathcal G^j\) are surjective.

Since flasque sheaves are acyclic for \(f_*\) (Cor. 8.3), the resolution \(\mathcal G^\bullet\) computes the derived functors of \(f_*\) (Prop. 1.2A): \(R^if_*(\mathcal F)=h^i(f_*\mathcal G^\bullet)\) for all \(i\ge0\). For \(i=0\) this is the left exactness of \(f_*\): \(\ker(f_*\mathcal G^0\to f_*\mathcal G^1)=f_*\ker(\mathcal G^0\to\mathcal G^1)=f_*\mathcal F\). For \(i>0\) it is \(R^if_*\mathcal F=0\) by hypothesis. Hence

\[ 0\to f_*\mathcal F\to f_*\mathcal G^0\to f_*\mathcal G^1\to\cdots \]

is exact, i.e. it is a flasque resolution of \(f_*\mathcal F\) on \(Y\). Flasque sheaves are \(\Gamma\)-acyclic (Prop. 2.5), so again by Prop. 1.2A

\[ H^i(Y,f_*\mathcal F)=h^i\bigl(\Gamma(Y,f_*\mathcal G^\bullet)\bigr)=h^i\bigl(\Gamma(X,\mathcal G^\bullet)\bigr)=H^i(X,\mathcal F), \]

where the middle equality is \(\Gamma(Y,f_*\mathcal G^j)=\Gamma(X,\mathcal G^j)\), compatibly with the differentials, and the last is again Prop. 1.2A, now on \(X\). The isomorphism is independent of the chosen resolution (any two flasque resolutions are homotopy equivalent over the identity of \(\mathcal F\)) and natural in \(\mathcal F\) among sheaves satisfying the hypothesis; it is the edge map \(H^i(Y,f_*\mathcal F)\to H^i(X,\mathcal F)\) of the Leray spectral sequence (whose only nonzero row is the bottom one).∎

Exercise 8.2

Problem

Let \(f:X\to Y\) be an affine morphism of schemes (II, Ex. 5.17) with \(X\) noetherian, and let \(\mathcal F\) be a quasi-coherent sheaf on \(X\). Show that the hypotheses of (Ex. 8.1) are satisfied, and hence that \(H^i(X,\mathcal F)\cong H^i(Y,f_*\mathcal F)\) for each \(i\ge0\). (This gives another proof of (Ex. 4.1).)

Solution

Recall that \(f\) affine means that \(Y\) has an open cover by affine open sets \(V_\alpha\) with \(f^{-1}(V_\alpha)\) affine. Fix \(V=V_\alpha=\operatorname{Spec}A\), so \(X_V:=f^{-1}(V)\) is an open subscheme of the noetherian scheme \(X\), hence noetherian, and it is affine. Let \(f_V:X_V\to V\) be the restriction. By Cor. 8.2,

\[ (R^if_*\mathcal F)|_V=R^i(f_V)_*(\mathcal F|_{X_V}). \]

Now \(V\) is an affine scheme, \(X_V\) is a noetherian scheme and \(\mathcal F|_{X_V}\) is quasi-coherent, so Prop. 8.5 gives \(R^i(f_V)_*(\mathcal F|_{X_V})\cong H^i(X_V,\mathcal F|_{X_V})^{\sim}\). As \(X_V\) is a noetherian affine scheme, \(H^i(X_V,\mathcal F|_{X_V})=0\) for \(i>0\) by Thm. 3.5. Therefore \((R^if_*\mathcal F)|_{V_\alpha}=0\) for all \(\alpha\) and all \(i>0\), and since the \(V_\alpha\) cover \(Y\), \(R^if_*\mathcal F=0\) for \(i>0\). This is the hypothesis of Ex. 8.1, which then yields \(H^i(X,\mathcal F)\cong H^i(Y,f_*\mathcal F)\) for all \(i\ge0\).∎

Exercise 8.3

Problem

Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a morphism of ringed spaces, let \(\mathcal F\) be an \(\mathcal O_X\)-module, and let \(\mathcal E\) be a locally free \(\mathcal O_Y\)-module of finite rank. Prove the projection formula (cf. (II, Ex. 5.1))

\[ R^if_*(\mathcal F\otimes f^*\mathcal E)\cong R^if_*(\mathcal F)\otimes\mathcal E. \]
Solution

We compare two \(\delta\)-functors \(\mathfrak{Mod}(X)\to\mathfrak{Mod}(Y)\):

\[ T^i(\mathcal F)=R^if_*(\mathcal F\otimes f^*\mathcal E),\qquad U^i(\mathcal F)=R^if_*(\mathcal F)\otimes\mathcal E . \]

Here \(R^if_*\) is the derived functor on \(\mathfrak{Mod}(X)\) (Prop. 8.4), and it forms a \(\delta\)-functor there.

Both are \(\delta\)-functors. The sheaf \(f^*\mathcal E\) is locally free of finite rank, hence flat, so \(\mathcal F\mapsto\mathcal F\otimes f^*\mathcal E\) is exact on \(\mathfrak{Mod}(X)\); composing with the \(\delta\)-functor \(R^if_*\) gives that \(T\) is a \(\delta\)-functor. Likewise \(\mathcal G\mapsto\mathcal G\otimes\mathcal E\) is exact on \(\mathfrak{Mod}(Y)\), so \(U\) is a \(\delta\)-functor.

The case \(i=0\) (II, Ex. 5.1(c)). There is a natural map \(f_*\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal E\to f_*(\mathcal F\otimes f^*\mathcal E)\), given over \(V\subseteq Y\) by \(s\otimes e\mapsto s\otimes f^{*}e\) (for \(s\in\mathcal F(f^{-1}V)\) and \(e\in\mathcal E(V)\), followed by sheafification). It is an isomorphism whenever \(\mathcal E=\mathcal O_V^{\,n}\) (both sides are \((f_*\mathcal F)^{n}\)), hence, the question being local on \(Y\), it is an isomorphism for every locally free \(\mathcal E\) of finite rank. So \(\eta^0:U^0\to T^0\) is an isomorphism of functors.

Both are effaceable for \(i>0\). Let \(\mathcal F\in\mathfrak{Mod}(X)\) and embed \(\mathcal F\hookrightarrow\mathcal I\) with \(\mathcal I\) injective in \(\mathfrak{Mod}(X)\). Then \(\mathcal I\) is flasque (Prop. 2.4), so \(R^if_*\mathcal I=0\) for \(i>0\) (Cor. 8.3), i.e. \(U^i(\mathcal I)=0\). For \(T^i\): let \(V\subseteq Y\) be open with \(\mathcal E|_V\cong\mathcal O_V^{\,n}\). Then \(f^{-1}(V)\) carries

\[ (\mathcal I\otimes f^*\mathcal E)|_{f^{-1}V}\cong(\mathcal I|_{f^{-1}V})^{\oplus n}, \]

which is flasque (restrictions of flasque sheaves to open sets are flasque, finite sums of flasque sheaves are flasque). By Cor. 8.2 and Cor. 8.3, \(T^i(\mathcal I)|_V=R^i(f|_{f^{-1}V})_*\bigl((\mathcal I\otimes f^*\mathcal E)|_{f^{-1}V}\bigr)=0\) for \(i>0\). Such \(V\) cover \(Y\), so \(T^i(\mathcal I)=0\) for \(i>0\).

Conclusion. A \(\delta\)-functor all of whose \(i\)-th terms (\(i>0\)) are effaceable is universal (Thm. 1.3A). Hence \(U\) and \(T\) are universal \(\delta\)-functors. The isomorphism \(\eta^0:U^0\to T^0\) therefore extends uniquely to a morphism of \(\delta\)-functors \(\eta:U\to T\); its inverse \((\eta^0)^{-1}\) extends uniquely to \(\theta:T\to U\), and \(\theta\eta\), \(\eta\theta\) are morphisms of \(\delta\)-functors restricting to the identity in degree \(0\), so equal the identity by uniqueness. Thus \(\eta^i:R^if_*(\mathcal F)\otimes\mathcal E\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }R^if_*(\mathcal F\otimes f^*\mathcal E)\) for all \(i\), naturally in \(\mathcal F\).∎

Exercise 8.4

Problem

Let \(Y\) be a noetherian scheme, and let \(\mathcal E\) be a locally free \(\mathcal O_Y\)-module of rank \(n+1\), \(n\ge1\). Let \(X=\mathbf P(\mathcal E)\) (II, Section 7), with the invertible sheaf \(\mathcal O_X(1)\) and the projection morphism \(\pi:X\to Y\).

  1. (a)

    Then \(\pi_*(\mathcal O(l))\cong S^l(\mathcal E)\) for \(l\ge0\), \(\pi_*(\mathcal O(l))=0\) for \(l<0\) (II, 7.11); \(R^i\pi_*(\mathcal O(l))=0\) for \(0<i<n\) and all \(l\in\mathbf Z\); and \(R^n\pi_*(\mathcal O(l))=0\) for \(l>-n-1\).

  2. (b)

    Show there is a natural exact sequence

    \[ 0\to\Omega_{X/Y}\to(\pi^*\mathcal E)(-1)\to\mathcal O\to0, \]

    cf. (II, 8.13), and conclude that the relative canonical sheaf \(\omega_{X/Y}=\wedge^n\Omega_{X/Y}\) is isomorphic to \((\pi^*\wedge^{n+1}\mathcal E)(-n-1)\). Show furthermore that there is a natural isomorphism \(R^n\pi_*(\omega_{X/Y})\cong\mathcal O_Y\) (cf. (7.1.1)).

  3. (c)

    Now show, for any \(l\in\mathbf Z\), that

    \[ R^n\pi_*(\mathcal O(l))\cong\pi_*(\mathcal O(-l-n-1))^{\vee}\otimes(\wedge^{n+1}\mathcal E)^{\vee}. \]
  4. (d)

    Show that \(p_a(X)=(-1)^np_a(Y)\) (use (Ex. 8.1)) and \(p_g(X)=0\) (use (II, 8.11)).

  5. (e)

    In particular, if \(Y\) is a nonsingular projective curve of genus \(g\), and \(\mathcal E\) a locally free sheaf of rank \(2\), then \(X\) is a projective surface with \(p_a=-g\), \(p_g=0\), and irregularity \(g\) (7.12.3). This kind of surface is called a geometrically ruled surface (V, Section 2).

Solution

Preliminaries. Let \(V=\operatorname{Spec}A\subseteq Y\) be an affine open set on which \(\mathcal E|_V\cong\mathcal O_V^{\,n+1}\). Then \(\pi^{-1}(V)\cong\mathbf P^n_A\) and \(\mathcal O_X(1)\) corresponds to \(\mathcal O(1)\) (II, Section 7); \(A\) is noetherian. Such \(V\) cover \(Y\). By Cor. 8.2 and Prop. 8.5, for every \(l\) and \(i\),

\[ (R^i\pi_*\mathcal O_X(l))|_V\cong H^i(\mathbf P^n_A,\mathcal O(l))^{\sim}\qquad\qquad(*) \]

naturally in the sheaf \(\mathcal O(l)\) (i.e. compatibly with morphisms between such sheaves, such as multiplication by a section of some \(\mathcal O(m)\)).

(a) The statements about \(\pi_*\) are given by (II, 7.11). The other statements are local on \(Y\) (Cor. 8.2), so by \((*)\) they concern \(H^i(\mathbf P^n_A,\mathcal O(l))\), \(A\) a noetherian ring. By Thm. 5.1(b) this vanishes for \(0<i<n\) and all \(l\). For \(i=n\), Thm. 5.1(d) says that

\[ H^0(\mathbf P^n_A,\mathcal O(-l-n-1))\times H^n(\mathbf P^n_A,\mathcal O(l))\to H^n(\mathbf P^n_A,\mathcal O(-n-1))\cong A \]

is a perfect pairing of finitely generated free \(A\)-modules (apply (d) with \(m=-l-n-1\) in place of \(l\)). If \(l>-n-1\) then \(-l-n-1<0\) and \(H^0(\mathbf P^n_A,\mathcal O(-l-n-1))=0\) by Thm. 5.1(a), so \(H^n(\mathbf P^n_A,\mathcal O(l))=0\). We shall also need: \(R^i\pi_*\mathcal O(l)=0\) for \(i>n\) and all \(l\); indeed \(\mathbf P^n_A\) is covered by the \(n+1\) affine open sets \(D_+(x_j)\), so the Cech complex of this cover (which computes cohomology of quasi-coherent sheaves, Thm. 4.5) vanishes in degrees \(>n\).

(b), the exact sequence. Write \(\mathcal S=\operatorname{Sym}\mathcal E\), so \(X=\operatorname{Proj}\mathcal S\). The canonical surjection \(\varepsilon:\pi^*\mathcal E\to\mathcal O_X(1)\) (II, 7.11, 7.12) twists to a surjection \(\varepsilon(-1):(\pi^*\mathcal E)(-1)\to\mathcal O_X\); let \(\mathcal K\) be its kernel, locally free of rank \(n\) (a kernel of a surjection of locally free sheaves). We construct an isomorphism \(\Omega_{X/Y}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal K\).

Work over an affine open \(V=\operatorname{Spec}A\subseteq Y\), \(M=\mathcal E(V)\), \(S=\operatorname{Sym}_A M\), \(\pi^{-1}V=\operatorname{Proj}S\). For any \(A\)-module \(M\) one has \(\Omega_{S/A}\cong S\otimes_AM\) via \(d(m)=1\otimes m\) (\(m\in M\)); write \(d:S\to S\otimes_AM\) for the universal derivation, and extend it to localizations. Let \(m\in M\) and \(U=D_+(m)\), with \(\mathcal O(U)=S_{(m)}\). For \(h=g/m^e\in S_{(m)}\) (\(g\in S_e\)) we have \(dh=(dg)/m^e-e\,g\,(1\otimes m)/m^{e+1}\), an element of \(S_m\otimes M\) all of whose \(S\)-coordinates have degree \(-1\). That is, \(dh\in\Gamma(U,\mathcal O_X(-1)\otimes\pi^*\mathcal E)\). This defines an \(A\)-linear derivation \(S_{(m)}\to\Gamma(U,(\pi^*\mathcal E)(-1))\), hence an \(\mathcal O_X\)-linear map \(\delta:\Omega_{X/Y}|_U\to(\pi^*\mathcal E)(-1)|_U\). The maps \(\delta\) agree on overlaps because they are all restrictions of the single canonical derivation \(d\), and the construction is compatible with change of affine open \(V\); so we get a global map \(\delta:\Omega_{X/Y}\to(\pi^*\mathcal E)(-1)\).

\(\delta\) lands in \(\mathcal K\). The composite of \(d\) with the multiplication map \(S\otimes M\to S\) (\(s\otimes m\mapsto sm\)) is a derivation \(S\to S\) which equals \(m\mapsto m\) on \(M\) and vanishes on \(A\); so it is the Euler derivation \(s\mapsto(\deg s)\,s\). Hence for \(h\) of degree \(0\) in \(S_m\), \(\varepsilon(-1)(dh)=0\).

\(\delta:\Omega_{X/Y}\to\mathcal K\) is an isomorphism. This is local on \(Y\), so take \(M\) free with basis \(e_0,\dots,e_n\), \(S=A[x_0,\dots,x_n]\) (\(x_j=e_j\)). On \(U_i=D_+(x_i)\), \(\Omega_{X/Y}\) is free with basis \(d(x_j/x_i)\), \(j\ne i\) (II, 8.13 / 8.1), and \(\delta(d(x_j/x_i))=x_i^{-1}e_j-x_jx_i^{-2}e_i\). On the other hand \((\pi^*\mathcal E)(-1)|_{U_i}\) is free with basis \(x_i^{-1}e_j\) (\(j=0,\dots,n\)) and \(\varepsilon(-1)(x_i^{-1}e_j)=x_j/x_i\); so \(\mathcal K|_{U_i}\) is free with basis \(x_i^{-1}e_j-(x_j/x_i)\,x_i^{-1}e_i\), \(j\ne i\), and these are exactly the images of the basis of \(\Omega_{X/Y}\). This proves that

\[ 0\to\Omega_{X/Y}\xrightarrow{\ \delta\ }(\pi^*\mathcal E)(-1)\xrightarrow{\ \varepsilon(-1)\ }\mathcal O_X\to0 \]

is exact (it is the sequence of (II, 8.13) made canonical).

(b), \(\omega_{X/Y}\). For an exact sequence \(0\to\mathcal F'\to\mathcal F\to\mathcal F''\to0\) of locally free sheaves of ranks \(r',r,r''\) we have \(\wedge^r\mathcal F\cong\wedge^{r'}\mathcal F'\otimes\wedge^{r''}\mathcal F''\) (II, Ex. 5.16(d)). Applied to the sequence above (\(r'=n\), \(r''=1\)):

\[ \omega_{X/Y}=\wedge^n\Omega_{X/Y}\cong\wedge^{n+1}\bigl((\pi^*\mathcal E)(-1)\bigr)=\pi^*(\wedge^{n+1}\mathcal E)\otimes\mathcal O_X(-n-1), \]

using \(\wedge^{r}(\mathcal G\otimes\mathcal L)=\wedge^r\mathcal G\otimes\mathcal L^{r}\) for \(\mathcal G\) locally free of rank \(r\) and \(\mathcal L\) invertible.

(b), \(R^n\pi_*\omega_{X/Y}\). Put \(G=(\pi^*\mathcal E)(-1)\) and \(\epsilon=\varepsilon(-1):G\to\mathcal O_X\), a surjection. Locally \(G=\mathcal O_X\oplus\mathcal K\) and \(\epsilon\) is the projection, so the Koszul complex

\[ 0\to K_{n+1}\to K_n\to\cdots\to K_1\to K_0\to0,\qquad K_j=\wedge^jG=\pi^*(\wedge^j\mathcal E)(-j), \]

with differential contraction with \(\epsilon\), is exact (locally it is the direct sum of the identity maps \(\wedge^{j-1}\mathcal K\to\wedge^{j-1}\mathcal K\) in the right degrees). Here \(K_0=\mathcal O_X\) and \(K_{n+1}=\omega_{X/Y}\) by the above. For \(1\le j\le n\), by Ex. 8.3 and (a),

\[ R^i\pi_*K_j\cong\wedge^j\mathcal E\otimes R^i\pi_*\mathcal O(-j)=0\quad\text{for all }i\ge0 : \]

for \(i=0\) because \(-j<0\); for \(0<i<n\) by (a); for \(i=n\) because \(-j>-n-1\); for \(i>n\) by the remark in (a). Let \(Z_j=\operatorname{im}(K_{j+1}\to K_j)\), \(0\le j\le n\); then \(Z_0=K_0\), \(Z_n\cong K_{n+1}\), and for \(1\le j\le n\) there are exact sequences \(0\to Z_j\to K_j\to Z_{j-1}\to0\). The long exact sequences, together with \(R^i\pi_*K_j=0\), show that the connecting homomorphisms are isomorphisms \(R^{i}\pi_*Z_{j-1}\xrightarrow{\sim}R^{i+1}\pi_*Z_j\) for all \(i\ge0\), \(1\le j\le n\). Composing for \(j=1,\dots,n\) (with \(i=j-1\)):

\[ \mathcal O_Y=\pi_*\mathcal O_X=\pi_*Z_0\xrightarrow{\sim}R^1\pi_*Z_1\xrightarrow{\sim}\cdots\xrightarrow{\sim}R^n\pi_*Z_n\cong R^n\pi_*(\omega_{X/Y}). \]

Here \(\pi_*\mathcal O_X=\mathcal O_Y\) by (a) (\(l=0\)). This is a natural (indeed canonical) isomorphism \(\mathcal O_Y\cong R^n\pi_*(\omega_{X/Y})\).

(c). Put \(L=\wedge^{n+1}\mathcal E\), an invertible sheaf. By (b) and Ex. 8.3,

\[ \mathcal O_Y\cong R^n\pi_*(\pi^*L\otimes\mathcal O(-n-1))\cong R^n\pi_*(\mathcal O(-n-1))\otimes L, \]

so tensoring with \(L^\vee\) gives a canonical isomorphism \(\tau:R^n\pi_*(\mathcal O(-n-1))\xrightarrow{\sim}L^\vee\). Fix \(l\in\mathbf Z\) and put \(m=-l-n-1\). Define a pairing

\[ \beta:\pi_*(\mathcal O(m))\otimes R^n\pi_*(\mathcal O(l))\longrightarrow R^n\pi_*(\mathcal O(l+m))=R^n\pi_*(\mathcal O(-n-1)) \]

as follows: for \(V\subseteq Y\) open, a section \(s\in\Gamma(V,\pi_*\mathcal O(m))=\Gamma(\pi^{-1}V,\mathcal O(m))\) gives a morphism \(\cdot s:\mathcal O(l)|_{\pi^{-1}V}\to\mathcal O(l+m)|_{\pi^{-1}V}\), hence \(R^n\pi_*(\cdot s)\) maps \(H^n(\pi^{-1}V,\mathcal O(l))\to H^n(\pi^{-1}V,\mathcal O(-n-1))\); this is bilinear and compatible with restriction, and after sheafification gives \(\beta\). Adjoining and using \(\tau\) we get

\[ \Theta:R^n\pi_*(\mathcal O(l))\to\mathcal{H}om(\pi_*\mathcal O(m),L^\vee)=(\pi_*\mathcal O(m))^\vee\otimes L^\vee , \]

the last equality because \(\pi_*\mathcal O(m)\) is \(S^m\mathcal E\) or \(0\), locally free of finite rank. To see that \(\Theta\) is an isomorphism we may work over \(V=\operatorname{Spec}A\) with \(\mathcal E|_V\) free. By \((*)\) (naturality!), over \(V\) the map \(\Theta\) corresponds to the \(A\)-linear map

\[ H^n(\mathbf P^n_A,\mathcal O(l))\to\operatorname{Hom}_A\bigl(H^0(\mathbf P^n_A,\mathcal O(m)),A\bigr) \]

adjoint to the pairing of Thm. 5.1(d), the identification of \(H^n(\mathbf P^n_A,\mathcal O(-n-1))\) with \(A\) being the one induced by the isomorphism \(\tau\) (any isomorphism with the free rank-one module will do). By Thm. 5.1(d) this adjoint map is an isomorphism of \(A\)-modules. Hence \(\Theta\) is an isomorphism of sheaves, which is (c). (For \(m<0\) both sides are \(0\), consistent with (a).)

(d). The invariants \(p_a\) and \(p_g\) are defined for projective varieties over an algebraically closed field \(k\) (\(p_g\) for nonsingular ones, as the dimension of \(\Gamma(\omega)\)), so we assume—as the exercise implicitly does—that \(Y\) is a projective variety over an algebraically closed field \(k\), nonsingular when we speak of \(p_g\), and let \(r=\dim Y\). Then \(X=\mathbf P(\mathcal E)\) is an integral scheme of dimension \(r+n\), nonsingular if \(Y\) is (it is locally \(V\times\mathbf P^n\)), and it is projective over \(k\): \(\mathcal E(m)\) is generated by global sections for \(m\gg0\), so \(X\cong\mathbf P(\mathcal E(m))\) embeds in \(\mathbf P^N_Y=\mathbf P^N\times Y\) (II, 7.9, 7.12), which is projective by the Segre embedding (I, Ex. 3.16).

\(p_a\). By (a) with \(l=0\): \(\pi_*\mathcal O_X=\mathcal O_Y\) and \(R^i\pi_*\mathcal O_X=0\) for \(i>0\) (for \(0<i<n\); for \(i=n\) since \(0>-n-1\); for \(i>n\) by the remark in (a)). By Ex. 8.1, \(H^i(X,\mathcal O_X)\cong H^i(Y,\mathcal O_Y)\) for all \(i\), so \(\chi(\mathcal O_X)=\chi(\mathcal O_Y)\). With \(p_a(Z)=(-1)^{\dim Z}(\chi(\mathcal O_Z)-1)\) (I, Ex. 7.2; Ex. 5.3),

\[ p_a(X)=(-1)^{r+n}(\chi(\mathcal O_Y)-1)=(-1)^np_a(Y). \]

\(p_g\). Let \(Y\) be nonsingular. Taking \(Z=\operatorname{Spec}k\) in (II, 8.11) we have an exact sequence \(\pi^*\Omega_{Y/k}\to\Omega_{X/k}\to\Omega_{X/Y}\to0\). The sheaves \(\Omega_{Y/k},\Omega_{X/k}\) are locally free of ranks \(r\) and \(r+n\) (II, 8.15), and \(\Omega_{X/Y}\) is locally free of rank \(n\) by (b). Hence the image of \(\pi^*\Omega_{Y/k}\) is the kernel of a surjection of locally free sheaves, hence locally free of rank \(r\); a surjection \(\pi^*\Omega_{Y/k}\to\operatorname{im}\) between locally free sheaves of the same rank \(r\) is an isomorphism. So the sequence \(0\to\pi^*\Omega_{Y/k}\to\Omega_{X/k}\to\Omega_{X/Y}\to0\) is exact and, by (II, Ex. 5.16(d)),

\[ \omega_X=\wedge^{r+n}\Omega_{X/k}\cong\pi^*\omega_Y\otimes\omega_{X/Y}\cong\pi^*(\omega_Y\otimes L)\otimes\mathcal O_X(-n-1). \]

By the projection formula (Ex. 5.1 of Ch. II, or Ex. 8.3 with \(i=0\)) and (II, 7.11),

\[ \pi_*\omega_X\cong\omega_Y\otimes L\otimes\pi_*\mathcal O_X(-n-1)=0,\qquad\text{since }-n-1<0 . \]

Therefore \(p_g(X)=\dim\Gamma(X,\omega_X)=\dim\Gamma(Y,\pi_*\omega_X)=0\).

(e). Now \(Y\) is a nonsingular projective curve of genus \(g=p_a(Y)=\dim H^1(Y,\mathcal O_Y)\) (as \(H^0(\mathcal O_Y)=k\), Ex. 5.3(a)), and \(\mathcal E\) has rank \(2\), so \(n=1\). By (d), \(X\) is a nonsingular projective variety of dimension \(2\) (a projective surface) with \(p_a(X)=(-1)^1p_a(Y)=-g\) and \(p_g(X)=0\). By Remark 7.12.3, the irregularity is \(q=p_g-p_a=0-(-g)=g\) (equivalently \(q=\dim H^1(X,\mathcal O_X)=\dim H^1(Y,\mathcal O_Y)=g\) by Ex. 8.1 as in (d)).∎

9. Flat morphisms

Conventions and results used. “Matsumura” means Commutative Ring Theory. We use the following standard facts besides those of the text. (i) A flat local homomorphism of local rings is faithfully flat, and a flat ring map \(A\to B\) satisfies going-down, i.e. every prime of \(A\) contained in \(\mathfrak q\cap A\) is of the form \(\mathfrak q'\cap A\) with \(\mathfrak q'\subseteq\mathfrak q\) (Matsumura, Thm. 9.5). (ii) Local flatness criterion (Matsumura, Thm. 22.3): let \(A\) be a noetherian ring, \(I\subseteq A\) an ideal and \(M\) an \(A\)-module which is \(I\)-adically ideal-separated (this holds if \(A\to B\) is a local homomorphism of noetherian local rings, \(I\subseteq\mathfrak m_A\) and \(M\) is a finite \(B\)-module); then \(M\) is flat over \(A\) if and only if \(M/IM\) is flat over \(A/I\) and \(\operatorname{Tor}_1^A(A/I,M)=0\). In particular, for \(A\to B\) a local homomorphism of noetherian local rings and \(M\) a finite \(B\)-module, \(M\) is \(A\)-flat iff \(\operatorname{Tor}_1^A(k,M)=0\), \(k=A/\mathfrak m_A\). (iii) Generic flatness (Matsumura, Thm. 24.1): if \(A\) is a noetherian domain, \(B\) a finitely generated \(A\)-algebra and \(M\) a finite \(B\)-module, there is \(0\ne a\in A\) with \(M_a\) free over \(A_a\). (iv) A finitely generated flat module over a noetherian local ring is free (9.1A(f)); a finitely generated module over a noetherian ring is flat iff it is locally free (9.2(e)). A localization of a \(B\)-module flat over \(A\) is flat over \(A\) (Ex. 9.1.1). A module over \(A_{\mathfrak p}\) which is flat over \(A\) is flat over \(A_{\mathfrak p}\).

Exercise 9.1

Problem

A flat morphism \(f:X\to Y\) of finite type of noetherian schemes is open, i.e. for every open subset \(U\subseteq X\), \(f(U)\) is open in \(Y\). [Hint: Show that \(f(U)\) is constructible and stable under generization (II, Ex. 3.18) and (II, Ex. 3.19).]

Solution

Let \(U\subseteq X\) be open.

\(f(U)\) is constructible. \(U\) is a constructible subset of \(X\) (it is open), and \(f\) is of finite type between noetherian schemes, so \(f(U)\) is constructible by Chevalley's theorem (II, Ex. 3.19).

\(f(U)\) is stable under generization. Let \(x\in U\), \(y=f(x)\), and let \(y'\in Y\) be a generization of \(y\), i.e. \(y\in\overline{\{y'\}}\). Put \(A=\mathcal O_{Y,y}\) and \(B=\mathcal O_{X,x}\); \(f\) flat gives a flat local homomorphism \(A\to B\). The point \(y'\) corresponds to a prime \(\mathfrak p'\subseteq A\), and by going-down for flat extensions (Matsumura, Thm. 9.5) there is a prime \(\mathfrak q'\subseteq B\) with \(\mathfrak q'\cap A=\mathfrak p'\). The prime \(\mathfrak q'\) is a point \(x'\in X\) with \(x\in\overline{\{x'\}}\) and \(f(x')=y'\). Since \(U\) is open and contains \(x\), it contains every generization of \(x\); thus \(x'\in U\) and \(y'\in f(U)\).

By (II, Ex. 3.18(c)) (the underlying space of a noetherian scheme is a Zariski space), a subset which is constructible and stable under generization is open. Hence \(f(U)\) is open.∎

Exercise 9.2

Problem

Do the calculation of (9.8.4) for the curve of (I, Ex. 3.14). Show that you get an embedded point at the cusp of the plane cubic curve.

Solution

Recall (I, Ex. 3.14(b)): \(Y=X_1\subseteq\mathbf P^3\) is the twisted cubic with parametrization \((x,y,z,w)=(t^3,t^2u,tu^2,u^3)\), \(P=(0,0,1,0)\), and the projection \(\varphi\) from \(P\) to the plane \(z=0\) is \((x:y:z:w)\mapsto(x:y:w)=(t^3:t^2u:u^3)\), whose image is the cuspidal cubic \(x^2w=y^3\), with cusp at \((0:0:1)\) (in the chart \(w=1\) it is the curve \((x,y)=(s^3,s^2)\), \(s=t/u\)). As in (9.8.3) let \(\sigma_a(x:y:z:w)=(x:y:az:w)\), \(X_a=\sigma_a(X_1)\) (\(a\ne0\)), and let \(\overline X\subseteq\mathbf P^3\times\mathbf A^1\) be the closure of \(\bigcup_{a\ne0}X_a\times\{a\}\); by (9.8) (or by the computation below) it is flat over \(\mathbf A^1\) and \(X_a\cong X_1\) for \(a\ne0\). We compute near the cusp. In the chart \(w=1\) with coordinates \((x,y,z)\) the curve \(X_1\) is \(\{(t^3,t^2,t)\}\) (put \(u=1\)), and \(X_a=\{(t^3,t^2,at)\}\).

The closure of \(\{(a,t^3,t^2,at):a\ne0\}\) in \(\mathbf A^4\) is the integral closed subscheme \(\operatorname{Spec}R\), where \(R=k[a,x,y,z]/I\) and \(I=\ker\bigl(k[a,x,y,z]\to k[a,t]\bigr)\), \(x\mapsto t^3,\ y\mapsto t^2,\ z\mapsto at\) (the map is injective on the open set \(a\ne0\) of the source, which is dense in \(\mathbf A^2_{a,t}\) since \(k[a,t]\hookrightarrow k[a,a^{-1},t]\)). Thus \(R\cong k[a,t^3,t^2,at]\subseteq k[a,t]\) is a domain, hence torsion-free over the PID \(k[a]\), hence flat over \(k[a]\) (Ex. 9.1.3).

Claim: \(I=J:=(z^2-a^2y,\ yz-ax,\ xz-ay^2,\ x^2-y^3)\).

The four generators of \(J\) visibly map to \(0\) in \(k[a,t]\) (e.g. \(z^2-a^2y\mapsto a^2t^2-a^2t^2\), \(xz-ay^2\mapsto at^4-at^4\)), so \(J\subseteq I\). For the converse use the lexicographic order with \(z>x>y>a\). The leading monomials of the four generators are \(z^2,\ yz,\ xz,\ x^2\) respectively. By the division algorithm every \(F\in k[a,x,y,z]\) can be written \(F=G+N\) with \(G\in J\) and \(N\) a linear combination of monomials divisible by none of \(z^2,yz,xz,x^2\), i.e. of the monomials \(a^iy^j\), \(a^ixy^j\), \(a^iz\) (\(i,j\ge0\)). These have images \(a^it^{2j}\), \(a^it^{2j+3}\), \(a^{i+1}t\) in \(k[a,t]\), which are pairwise distinct monomials (the exponent of \(t\) is even in the first family, odd and \(\ge3\) in the second, equal to \(1\) with \(a\)-exponent \(\ge1\) in the third). Hence if \(F\in I\) then \(N\in I\) forces \(N=0\), so \(F\in J\). This proves the claim.

Consequently \(\overline X\cap\{w\ne0\}=\operatorname{Spec}k[a,x,y,z]/J\) is flat over \(\mathbf A^1\), and the fibre over \(a=0\) is

\[ X_0=\operatorname{Spec}k[x,y,z]/I_0,\qquad I_0=(z^2,\ yz,\ xz,\ x^2-y^3). \]

Structure of \(X_0\). The radical of \(I_0\) is \(\mathfrak p=(z,x^2-y^3)\) (as \(z^2\in I_0\) and \(x^2-y^3\in I_0\)), which is prime, so the support of \(X_0\) is the cuspidal cubic \(x^2=y^3\) in the plane \(z=0\), with cusp at the origin \(0\). At a point of the support other than the origin we have \(x\ne0\) and \(y\ne0\) (as \(x=t^3,y=t^2\), \(t\ne0\)), so \(xz=0\) gives \(z=0\) in the local ring: \(X_0\) is the reduced cuspidal curve away from the cusp. At the origin: let \(\mathfrak m=(x,y,z)\) and \(B=k[x,y,z]/I_0\). In \(B\), \(z\ne0\) and \(\mathfrak m z=0\) (because \(z^2,yz,xz\in I_0\)). Moreover \(z\ne0\) even in the local ring \(B_{\mathfrak m}\): otherwise \(z\in I_0\cdot k[x,y,z]_{\mathfrak m}\subseteq\mathfrak m^2k[x,y,z]_{\mathfrak m}\) (all generators of \(I_0\) lie in \(\mathfrak m^2\)), which is absurd since \(z\in\mathfrak m\setminus\mathfrak m^2\) in the regular local ring. So \(\mathfrak m\) is an associated prime of \(B\), but it is not minimal (the only minimal prime is \(\mathfrak p\subsetneq\mathfrak m\)): the origin is an embedded point of \(X_0\). Note also that \(z\notin I_0\), so \(X_0\) is not contained in the plane \(z=0\): the nilpotent element \(z\) (\(z^2=0\)) points out of the plane. So the flat limit of the curves \(X_a\cong\) twisted cubic is the plane cuspidal cubic together with an embedded point at the cusp, exactly as for the nodal cubic in (9.8.4).

(Consistency check: the cuspidal cubic has \(p_a=1\), the twisted cubic \(p_a=0\), and the embedded point has length \(1\), so \(P_{X_0}(0)=P_{\text{cusp}}(0)+1=0+1\), matching \(P_{X_1}(0)=1-0\) as required by (9.9).)∎

Exercise 9.3

Problem

Some examples of flatness and nonflatness.

  1. (a)

    If \(f:X\to Y\) is a finite surjective morphism of nonsingular varieties over an algebraically closed field \(k\), then \(f\) is flat.

  2. (b)

    Let \(X\) be a union of two planes meeting at a point, each of which maps isomorphically to a plane \(Y\). Show that \(f\) is not flat. For example, let \(Y=\operatorname{Spec}k[x,y]\) and \(X=\operatorname{Spec}k[x,y,z,w]/(z,w)\cap(x+z,y+w)\).

  3. (c)

    Again let \(Y=\operatorname{Spec}k[x,y]\), but take \(X=\operatorname{Spec}k[x,y,z,w]/(z^2,zw,w^2,xz-yw)\). Show that \(X_{\mathrm{red}}\cong Y\), \(X\) has no embedded points, but that \(f\) is not flat.

Solution

(a) First let \(x\in X\) be a closed point, \(y=f(x)\); \(y\) is a closed point since \(f\) is of finite type over \(k\). Put \(A=\mathcal O_{Y,y}\), \(B=\mathcal O_{X,x}\). Both are regular local rings (nonsingular varieties), so \(A\) is regular and \(B\) is Cohen–Macaulay. Since \(f\) is finite and surjective, \(X\) and \(Y\) have the same dimension (integral dependence), and for closed points of varieties \(\dim A=\dim Y\), \(\dim B=\dim X\); so \(\dim B=\dim A\). The fibre ring \(B/\mathfrak m_AB\) is the local ring of the fibre \(X_y\) at \(x\), and \(X_y=\operatorname{Spec}\) of a finite \(k\)-algebra (finiteness of \(f\)), so \(\dim B/\mathfrak m_AB=0\). Hence \(\dim B=\dim A+\dim B/\mathfrak m_AB\), and the “miracle flatness” theorem (Matsumura, Thm. 23.1: for a local homomorphism \(A\to B\) of noetherian local rings with \(A\) regular and \(B\) Cohen–Macaulay, \(B\) is \(A\)-flat iff \(\dim B=\dim A+\dim B/\mathfrak m_AB\)) shows that \(B\) is flat over \(A\).

Now let \(x'\in X\) be any point; it specializes to a closed point \(x\) (every nonempty closed subset of a scheme of finite type over \(k\) contains a closed point), and \(\mathcal O_{X,x'}\) is a localization of \(B=\mathcal O_{X,x}\), hence flat over \(A=\mathcal O_{Y,f(x)}\) (Ex. 9.1.1), and it is a module over \(\mathcal O_{Y,f(x')}=A_{\mathfrak p}\), so it is flat over \(\mathcal O_{Y,f(x')}\). Thus \(f\) is flat.

(b) Let \(A_0=k[x,y]\), \(R=k[x,y,z,w]/I\) with \(I=(z,w)\cap(x+z,y+w)\), an \(A_0\)-algebra via \(x,y\). The two planes are \(\{z=w=0\}\) (coordinates \(x,y\)) and \(\{x+z=y+w=0\}\) (coordinates \(z,w\), with \(x=-z,y=-w\)); each maps isomorphically onto \(Y=\mathbf A^2\), and they meet only at the origin of \(\mathbf A^4\). The map \(R\to k[x,y,z,w]/(z,w)\times k[x,y,z,w]/(x+z,y+w)\cong A_0\times A_0\) is injective and \(A_0\)-linear (the second factor \(\cong A_0\) via \(x\mapsto-z\), \(y\mapsto-w\)), so \(R\) is a finitely generated \(A_0\)-module (\(A_0\) noetherian) and \(f\) is finite.

Suppose \(f\) were flat. Then \(R\) is a finitely generated flat \(A_0\)-module, hence locally free of constant rank on the connected scheme \(Y\) (9.2(e)); so \(\dim_{\kappa(\mathfrak p)}R\otimes\kappa(\mathfrak p)\) is the same for all primes \(\mathfrak p\) of \(A_0\). We compute it for \(\mathfrak p=(0)\) and \(\mathfrak p=(x,y)\).

At the generic point: \(K=k(x,y)\). After inverting \(A_0\setminus0\), the ideals \((z,w)\) and \((x+z,y+w)\) become comaximal (their sum contains \(x\), a unit), so by the Chinese remainder theorem \(R\otimes K\cong K[z,w]/(z,w)\times K[z,w]/(x+z,y+w)\cong K\times K\), of dimension \(2\).

At the origin: put \(u_1=x+z\), \(u_2=y+w\). Then \(z,w,u_1,u_2\) are linear coordinates on \(\mathbf A^4\), and \((z,w)\cap(u_1,u_2)=(z,w)(u_1,u_2)=(zu_1,zu_2,wu_1,wu_2)\) (a monomial computation in disjoint sets of variables). Reducing modulo \((x,y)=(u_1-z,u_2-w)\) identifies \(u_1=z\), \(u_2=w\), so

\[ R\otimes k(0)=k[z,w]/(z^2,zw,wz,w^2)=k[z,w]/(z,w)^2, \]

of dimension \(3\). Since \(2\ne3\), \(f\) is not flat.

(c) Let \(A_0=k[x,y]\), \(R=k[x,y,z,w]/J\), \(J=(z^2,zw,w^2,xz-yw)\). As \(J=(z,w)^2+(xz-yw)\), we have \(R=A_0\oplus N\) as an \(A_0\)-module, where \(N=(A_0z\oplus A_0w)/A_0(xz-yw)\) is the part of degree \(1\) in \(z,w\), and \(N^2=0\) in \(R\). The \(A_0\)-linear map \(N\to A_0\), \(az+bw\mapsto ay+bx\), is well defined (it kills \(xz-yw\mapsto xy-yx=0\)), has image the ideal \(\mathfrak m_0=(x,y)\), and is injective: the kernel of \(A_0^2\to A_0\), \((a,b)\mapsto ay+bx\), is \(\{(a,b):ay=-bx\}=A_0(x,-y)\) because \(x,y\) are coprime in the UFD \(A_0\). So \(N\cong\mathfrak m_0\), a torsion-free \(A_0\)-module.

\(X_{\mathrm{red}}\cong Y\). \(N\) is a nilpotent ideal of \(R\) and \(R/N=A_0\) is reduced, so \(N\) is the nilradical and \(X_{\mathrm{red}}=\operatorname{Spec}A_0=Y\), the map \(X_{\mathrm{red}}\to Y\) being the identity.

No embedded points. Let \(\mathfrak p=\operatorname{Ann}_R(r)\) be an associated prime, \(r=a+n\ne0\) (\(a\in A_0\), \(n\in N\)). Since \(\mathfrak p\) is prime it contains the nilradical \(N\), so for all \(\nu\in N\): \(0=\nu r=a\nu\). As \(N\cong\mathfrak m_0\) is a nonzero torsion-free module over the domain \(A_0\), \(a=0\). So \(r=n\in N\setminus0\) and \(\operatorname{Ann}_R(n)=\{a'+n':a'n=0\}=N\) by torsion-freeness. Hence \(\operatorname{Ass}(R)=\{N\}\), the unique minimal prime. So \(X\) has no embedded points.

\(f\) is not flat. \(R\) is a finitely generated \(A_0\)-module (generated by \(1,z,w\)). If it were flat it would be locally free of constant rank (as in (b)). But \(R\otimes_{A_0}K=K\oplus\mathfrak m_0\otimes K\cong K^2\), whereas \(R\otimes_{A_0}A_0/\mathfrak m_0=k\oplus\mathfrak m_0/\mathfrak m_0^2\cong k^3\). So \(f\) is not flat (it is not even flat over \(0\): \(\mathfrak m_0\) is not free).∎

Exercise 9.4

Problem

Open Nature of Flatness. Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a morphism of finite type of noetherian schemes. Then \(\{x\in X\mid f\text{ is flat at }x\}\) is an open subset of \(X\) (possibly empty)—see Grothendieck [EGA IV\(_3\), 11.1.1].

Solution

Flatness at \(x\) means that \(\mathcal O_{X,x}\) is flat over \(\mathcal O_{Y,f(x)}\).
