The assertion is local on \(X\), so we may cover \(X\) by affine opens \(\operatorname{Spec}B\) mapping into affine opens \(\operatorname{Spec}A\) of \(Y\). Thus let \(A\) be a noetherian ring, \(B\) a finitely generated \(A\)-algebra (so \(B\) is noetherian), and

\[ U=\{\mathfrak q\in\operatorname{Spec}B:\ B_{\mathfrak q}\text{ is flat over }A_{\mathfrak q\cap A}\}. \]

We prove that \(U\) is open. (The argument works verbatim for a finite \(B\)-module \(M\) in place of \(B\).)

Step 1: \(U\) is stable under generization. Let \(\mathfrak q'\subseteq\mathfrak q\) with \(\mathfrak q\in U\), \(\mathfrak p=\mathfrak q\cap A\), \(\mathfrak p'=\mathfrak q'\cap A\subseteq\mathfrak p\). \(B_{\mathfrak q'}\) is a localization of \(B_{\mathfrak q}\), which is flat over \(A_{\mathfrak p}\), so \(B_{\mathfrak q'}\) is flat over \(A_{\mathfrak p}\) (Ex. 9.1.1) and, being an \(A_{\mathfrak p'}\)-module, flat over \(A_{\mathfrak p'}\). So \(\mathfrak q'\in U\).

Step 2: local statement. Claim. If \(\mathfrak q_0\in U\) and \(Z=V(\mathfrak q_0)\subseteq\operatorname{Spec}B\), then there is a nonempty open subset \(W\subseteq Z\) with \(W\subseteq U\).

Put \(\mathfrak p_0=\mathfrak q_0\cap A\), \(A_0=A/\mathfrak p_0\) (a noetherian domain), \(\bar B=B/\mathfrak p_0B\), a finitely generated \(A_0\)-algebra. Let \(T=\operatorname{Tor}_1^A(A/\mathfrak p_0,B)\), a finite \(B\)-module (compute it with a resolution of \(A/\mathfrak p_0\) by finite free \(A\)-modules). Since \(\operatorname{Tor}\) commutes with localization, \(T_{\mathfrak q_0}=\operatorname{Tor}_1^{A_{\mathfrak p_0}}(\kappa(\mathfrak p_0),B_{\mathfrak q_0})=0\) because \(B_{\mathfrak q_0}\) is flat over \(A_{\mathfrak p_0}\). Hence \(\mathfrak q_0\notin\operatorname{Supp}T\), a closed set. By generic flatness (iii) applied to \(A_0\to\bar B\) there is \(a\in A\), \(a\notin\mathfrak p_0\), with \(\bar B_a\) free over \((A_0)_a\). Let

\[ W=\{\mathfrak q\in Z:\ \mathfrak q\notin\operatorname{Supp}T,\ a\notin\mathfrak q\}, \]

open in \(Z\) and containing \(\mathfrak q_0\) (as \(a\notin\mathfrak p_0\) and \(\mathfrak q_0\cap A=\mathfrak p_0\)); so \(W\) is nonempty. Let \(\mathfrak q\in W\) and \(\mathfrak p=\mathfrak q\cap A\supseteq\mathfrak p_0\). We apply the local flatness criterion (ii) to \(A_{\mathfrak p}\to B_{\mathfrak q}\) and the ideal \(I=\mathfrak p_0A_{\mathfrak p}\subseteq\mathfrak pA_{\mathfrak p}\): \(B_{\mathfrak q}\) is flat over \(A_{\mathfrak p}\) iff (1) \(B_{\mathfrak q}/IB_{\mathfrak q}=\bar B_{\mathfrak q}\) is flat over \(A_{\mathfrak p}/I=(A_0)_{\mathfrak p}\), and (2) \(\operatorname{Tor}_1^{A_{\mathfrak p}}(A_{\mathfrak p}/\mathfrak p_0A_{\mathfrak p},B_{\mathfrak q})=0\). Now (2) is \(T_{\mathfrak q}=0\), true since \(\mathfrak q\notin\operatorname{Supp}T\). For (1): \(a\notin\mathfrak p\), and \(\bar B_{\mathfrak q}\) is a localization of \(\bar B_a\), which is free over \((A_0)_a\); hence \(\bar B_{\mathfrak q}\) is flat over \((A_0)_a\) (Ex. 9.1.1), and being a module over \((A_0)_{\mathfrak p}\) it is flat over \((A_0)_{\mathfrak p}\). So \(\mathfrak q\in U\). This proves the claim.

Step 3: noetherian induction. Suppose \(U\) is not open. Among the closed subsets \(Z\subseteq\operatorname{Spec}B\) such that \(U\cap Z\) is not open in \(Z\) (the whole space is one) choose a minimal one, \(Z\). If \(Z=Z_1\cup Z_2\) with \(Z_i\subsetneq Z\) closed, then \(Z_i\setminus U\) is closed in \(Z_i\), hence in \(Z\), so \(Z\setminus U=(Z_1\setminus U)\cup(Z_2\setminus U)\) is closed in \(Z\) and \(U\cap Z\) is open in \(Z\), a contradiction. So \(Z\) is irreducible, with generic point \(\mathfrak q_0\). If \(\mathfrak q_0\notin U\), then \(U\cap Z=\emptyset\) by Step 1 (every point of \(Z\) specializes \(\mathfrak q_0\)), which is open in \(Z\): contradiction. So \(\mathfrak q_0\in U\) and Step 2 provides \(W\subseteq U\cap Z\) nonempty open in \(Z\). Then \(Z\setminus W\subsetneq Z\) is closed, so by minimality \(U\cap(Z\setminus W)\) is open in \(Z\setminus W\), i.e. \((Z\setminus W)\setminus U\) is closed in \(Z\setminus W\), hence in \(Z\). But \(Z\setminus U=(Z\setminus W)\setminus U\) because \(W\subseteq U\); so \(U\cap Z\) is open in \(Z\), contradicting the choice of \(Z\). Hence \(U\) is open.∎

Exercise 9.5

Problem

Very Flat Families. For any closed subscheme \(X\subseteq\mathbf P^n\), we denote by \(C(X)\subseteq\mathbf P^{n+1}\) the projective cone over \(X\) (I, Ex. 2.10). If \(I\subseteq k[x_0,\dots,x_n]\) is the (largest) homogeneous ideal of \(X\), then \(C(X)\) is defined by the ideal generated by \(I\) in \(k[x_0,\dots,x_{n+1}]\).

  1. (a)

    Give an example to show that if \(\{X_t\}\) is a flat family of closed subschemes of \(\mathbf P^n\), then \(\{C(X_t)\}\) need not be a flat family in \(\mathbf P^{n+1}\).

  2. (b)

    To remedy this situation, we make the following definition. Let \(X\subseteq\mathbf P^n_T\) be a closed subscheme, where \(T\) is a noetherian integral scheme. For each \(t\in T\), let \(I_t\subseteq S_t=k(t)[x_0,\dots,x_n]\) be the homogeneous ideal of \(X_t\) in \(\mathbf P^n_{k(t)}\). We say that the family \(\{X_t\}\) is very flat if for all \(d\ge0\), \(\dim_{k(t)}(S_t/I_t)_d\) is independent of \(t\). Here \((\ )_d\) means the homogeneous part of degree \(d\).

  3. (c)

    If \(\{X_t\}\) is a very flat family in \(\mathbf P^n\), show that it is flat. Show also that \(\{C(X_t)\}\) is a very flat family in \(\mathbf P^{n+1}\), and hence flat.

  4. (d)

    If \(\{X_{(t)}\}\) is an algebraic family of projectively normal varieties in \(\mathbf P^n_k\), parametrized by a nonsingular curve \(T\) over an algebraically closed field \(k\), then \(\{X_{(t)}\}\) is a very flat family of schemes.

Solution

Conventions: “the homogeneous ideal of a closed subscheme \(X'\subseteq\mathbf P^n_K\) (\(K\) a field)” is the largest homogeneous ideal defining it, namely \(I(X')=\bigoplus_{d\ge0}\Gamma(\mathbf P^n_K,\mathcal I_{X'}(d))\) (II, Ex. 5.10(c),(d); it is the saturated ideal). So \((S/I)_d\) is the image of \(\Gamma(\mathbf P^n,\mathcal O(d))\to\Gamma(X',\mathcal O_{X'}(d))\) (injective on \((S/I)_d\) by left exactness of \(\Gamma\) applied to \(0\to\mathcal I(d)\to\mathcal O(d)\to\mathcal O_{X'}(d)\to0\)). For \(d\gg0\) this map is surjective (\(H^1(\mathcal I_{X'}(d))=0\), Thm. 5.2(b)) and \(\dim\Gamma(X',\mathcal O_{X'}(d))=P_{X'}(d)\), the Hilbert polynomial of \(X'\) (Thm. 5.2, Ex. 5.2). Hence

\[ \dim_K(S/I)_d=P_{X'}(d)\quad(d\gg0)\qquad\qquad(\dagger) \]

(a) Take \(k\) algebraically closed, \(T=\mathbf A^1_k=\operatorname{Spec}k[t]\), \(n=2\), and the three sections \(P_1=(1:0:0)\), \(P_2=(0:1:0)\), \(P_3(t)=(1:1:t)\) of \(\mathbf P^2_T\to T\). They are pairwise disjoint (at every \(t\in k\) the three points are distinct), so \(X=s_1(T)\sqcup s_2(T)\sqcup s_3(T)\subseteq\mathbf P^2_T\) is a closed subscheme isomorphic to three disjoint copies of \(T\): it is flat over \(T\), with \(X_t=\{P_1,P_2,P_3(t)\}\) three reduced points. For \(t\ne0\) these points are not collinear (the determinant of the matrix with rows \((1,0,0)\), \((0,1,0)\), \((1,1,t)\) is \(t\)), while for \(t=0\) they lie on the line \(z=0\).

The Hilbert function \(h_t(d)=\dim(S_t/I_t)_d\) of three distinct points is the rank of the evaluation map \(S_{t,d}\to k^3\) at the three points. \(h_t(0)=1\); \(h_t(1)\) is the rank of the \(3\times3\) matrix of the coordinates of the points, i.e. \(3\) for \(t\ne0\) and \(2\) for \(t=0\); and \(h_t(d)=3\) for \(d\ge2\) (for each \(i\) take the product \(\ell_i\lambda^{d-2}\) with \(\ell_i\) a product of two linear forms each vanishing at one of the two other points but not at \(P_i\), and \(\lambda\) a linear form not vanishing at \(P_i\): it takes a nonzero value at \(P_i\) and vanishes at the other two points). So

\[ h_t=(1,3,3,3,\dots)\ (t\ne0),\qquad h_0=(1,2,3,3,\dots). \]

The cone \(C(X_t)\subseteq\mathbf P^3\) is defined by \(I_tS'_t\), \(S'_t=S_t[x_3]\), and \((S_t'/I_tS_t')_d=\bigoplus_{j=0}^d(S_t/I_t)_jx_3^{d-j}\) has dimension \(\sum_{j\le d}h_t(j)\), which is \(3d+1\) for \(t\ne0\) and \(3d\) for \(t=0\) (\(d\ge1\)). By (\(\dagger\)) the Hilbert polynomials of the cones are \(3d+1\) (\(t\ne0\)) and \(3d\) (\(t=0\)). By Thm. 9.9, no closed subscheme of \(\mathbf P^3_T\) with fibres \(C(X_t)\) is flat over \(T\). (Geometrically: the cone over three non-collinear points is three non-coplanar concurrent lines, \(p_a=0\); over three collinear points it is three coplanar concurrent lines, \(p_a=1\).)

(b) This is a definition (with \(I_t\) as in the conventions above).

(c) Very flat \(\Rightarrow\) flat. If the \(\dim(S_t/I_t)_d\) are independent of \(t\), then by (\(\dagger\)) the Hilbert polynomials \(P_t\) of the fibres are independent of \(t\), so \(X\) is flat over \(T\) by Thm. 9.9 (\(T\) is integral noetherian).

The cone. Let \(X\subseteq\mathbf P^n_T\). The cone \(C(X)\subseteq\mathbf P^{n+1}_T\) is defined over an affine open \(\operatorname{Spec}A\subseteq T\) as \(\operatorname{Proj}A[x_0,\dots,x_{n+1}]/\mathfrak aA[x_0,\dots,x_{n+1}]\) for a homogeneous ideal \(\mathfrak a\subseteq A[x_0,\dots,x_n]\) with \(\operatorname{Proj}A[x_0,\dots,x_n]/\mathfrak a=X\cap\mathbf P^n_{A}\); it is independent of the choice of \(\mathfrak a\) (two such ideals agree in all large degrees, \(\mathfrak a_d=\Gamma(\tilde{\mathfrak a}(d))\) for \(d\gg0\), hence so do their extensions), so these glue, and the fibre over \(t\) is \(C(X_t)\) (Proj commutes with base change, and the image of \(\mathfrak a\) in \(S_t\) agrees with \(I_t\) in large degrees).

Let now \(\{X_t\}\) be very flat. Fix \(t\) and write \(S=S_t\), \(I=I_t\), \(R=S/I\), \(S'=S[x_{n+1}]\), \(R'=S'/IS'=R[x_{n+1}]\). Since \(I\) is saturated, \(R\) has no nonzero element killed by a power of \(\mathfrak m=(x_0,\dots,x_n)\). If \(g=\sum g_jx_{n+1}^j\in R'\) is killed by a power of \((x_0,\dots,x_{n+1})\), then \(x_i^Ng=0\) for all \(i\le n\), so \(x_i^Ng_j=0\) for all \(i,j\) and hence \(g_j=0\). So \(IS'\) is saturated, i.e. it is the homogeneous ideal of \(C(X_t)\) (II, Ex. 5.10(c),(d)). Then

\[ \dim_{k(t)}(S'/IS')_d=\sum_{j=0}^d\dim_{k(t)}(S_t/I_t)_j \]

is independent of \(t\), so \(\{C(X_t)\}\) is very flat, hence flat by the first part (applied in \(\mathbf P^{n+1}_T\)).

(d) Let \(X\subseteq\mathbf P^n_T\) be the closed subscheme giving the family, \(f:X\to T\). Since projective normality implies normality (I, Ex. 3.18(a)), the \(X_{(t)}\) are normal varieties, so by Thm. 9.11 \(f\) is flat and \(X_t=X_{(t)}\) for every closed point \(t\) (the scheme-theoretic fibre is the variety). Let \(\eta\) be the generic point of \(T\), \(K=k(\eta)\), and \(s_t(d)=\dim_{k(t)}(S_t/I_t)_d\) for \(t\in T\). We show \(s_t(d)=s_\eta(d)\) for all closed \(t\) and all \(d\ge0\).

Let \(\mathcal J\subseteq\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^n_T}\) be the ideal sheaf of \(X\). Fix a nonempty affine open \(\operatorname{Spec}A\subseteq T\); \(A\) is a regular noetherian domain of dimension \(1\), hence a Dedekind domain. Let \(S_A=A[x_0,\dots,x_n]\), \(\mathfrak a_d=\Gamma(\mathbf P^n_A,\mathcal J(d))\subseteq(S_A)_d\) and \(R_d=(S_A)_d/\mathfrak a_d\).

(1) Fibres of \(\mathcal J\). \(\mathcal O_X\) is flat over \(A\), so \(\operatorname{Tor}_1^A(\mathcal O_X,k(t))=0\) and \(0\to\mathcal J\otimes k(t)\to\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^n_{k(t)}}\to\mathcal O_{X_t}\to0\) is exact for every \(t\) (also for \(t=\eta\)): \(\mathcal J\otimes k(t)=\mathcal I_{X_t}\). Hence the image of \(\mathfrak a_d\) in \((S_t)_d\) lies in \(I_{t,d}=\Gamma(\mathbf P^n,\mathcal I_{X_t}(d))\), and \(R_d\otimes k(t)=(S_t)_d/\overline{\mathfrak a_d}\twoheadrightarrow(S_t/I_t)_d\). Thus \(s_t(d)\le\dim_{k(t)}R_d\otimes k(t)\).

(2) \(R_d\) is locally free. \(R_d\hookrightarrow\Gamma(X,\mathcal O_X(d))\) (left exactness), and \(\Gamma(X,\mathcal O_X(d))\) is torsion-free over \(A\) since \(\mathcal O_X(d)\) is \(A\)-flat. So \(R_d\) is a finitely generated torsion-free module over the Dedekind domain \(A\), hence projective, of some constant rank \(r_d\). Therefore \(\dim_{k(t)}R_d\otimes k(t)=r_d\) for all \(t\in\operatorname{Spec}A\).

(3) Generic point. By flat base change (Prop. 9.3) \(\Gamma(\mathbf P^n_K,\mathcal I_{X_\eta}(d))=\mathfrak a_d\otimes_AK\), so \((S_\eta/I_\eta)_d=R_d\otimes K\) and \(s_\eta(d)=r_d\). With (1) and (2): \(s_t(d)\le s_\eta(d)\) for all closed \(t\in\operatorname{Spec}A\).

(4) The reverse inequality. Let \(t\) be closed. Since \(X_t\) is projectively normal, Ex. II.5.14(d) says that \(\Gamma(\mathbf P^n,\mathcal O(d))\to\Gamma(X_t,\mathcal O_{X_t}(d))\) is surjective for all \(d\ge0\); with the injectivity noted in the conventions, \(s_t(d)=h^0(X_t,\mathcal O_{X_t}(d))\). Now \(\mathcal O_X(d)\) is flat over \(T\) and \(f\) is projective, so \(s\mapsto h^0(X_s,\mathcal O_{X_s}(d))\) is upper semicontinuous on \(T\) (Thm. 12.8). The closed set \(\{s:h^0(X_s,\mathcal O(d))\ge h^0(X_\eta,\mathcal O(d))\}\) contains \(\eta\), hence is all of \(T\). Also \((S_\eta/I_\eta)_d\subseteq\Gamma(X_\eta,\mathcal O(d))\). Therefore \(s_t(d)=h^0(X_t,\mathcal O(d))\ge h^0(X_\eta,\mathcal O(d))\ge s_\eta(d)\).

Together, \(s_t(d)=s_\eta(d)\) for every closed point \(t\) and every \(d\ge0\), i.e. the family is very flat.∎

Exercise 9.6

Problem

Let \(Y\subseteq\mathbf P^n\) be a nonsingular variety of dimension \(\ge2\) over an algebraically closed field \(k\). Suppose \(\mathbf P^{n-1}\) is a hyperplane in \(\mathbf P^n\) which does not contain \(Y\), and such that the scheme \(Y'=Y\cap\mathbf P^{n-1}\) is also nonsingular. Prove that \(Y\) is a complete intersection in \(\mathbf P^n\) if and only if \(Y'\) is a complete intersection in \(\mathbf P^{n-1}\). [Hint: See (II, Ex. 8.4) and use (9.12) applied to the affine cones over \(Y\) and \(Y'\).]

Solution

Recall (II, Ex. 8.4) that a closed subscheme \(Z\subseteq\mathbf P^N\) of codimension \(r\) is a (strict, global) complete intersection if its homogeneous ideal \(I(Z)\subseteq k[x_0,\dots,x_N]\) (the largest homogeneous ideal defining \(Z\)) can be generated by \(r\) elements.

Setup. Choose coordinates with \(H=\mathbf P^{n-1}=\{x_n=0\}\); \(S=k[x_0,\dots,x_n]\), \(S'=S/(x_n)=k[x_0,\dots,x_{n-1}]\), \(\delta=\dim Y\ge2\), \(r=n-\delta=\operatorname{codim}(Y,\mathbf P^n)\). Let \(I=I(Y)\), a homogeneous prime ideal, and \(R=S/I\), a domain of dimension \(\delta+1\) (the coordinate ring of the affine cone). As \(Y\not\subseteq H\), \(x_n\notin I\), so \(x_n\) is a nonzerodivisor on \(R\). We have \(R/x_nR=S'/J_0\), where \(J_0\subseteq S'\) is the image of \(I\), and \(\operatorname{Proj}(S'/J_0)=Y\cap H=Y'\) as schemes. By hypothesis \(Y'\) is nonsingular; it is connected by Cor. 7.9 and Remark 7.9.1 (\(Y'\) is a hyperplane section of \(Y\), \(\dim Y\ge2\)), hence integral, of dimension \(\delta-1\ge1\), and \(\operatorname{codim}(Y',\mathbf P^{n-1})=n-\delta=r\). Let \(I'=I(Y')\subseteq S'\) (a homogeneous prime). Then \(J_0\subseteq I'\) and \(J_0,I'\) agree in large degrees (they define the same subscheme).

(\(\Rightarrow\)) Let \(I=(f_1,\dots,f_r)\). Since \(S\) is Cohen–Macaulay and \(\operatorname{ht}I=r\), the \(f_i\) form a regular sequence (Matsumura, Thm. 17.4), so \(R\) is Cohen–Macaulay. As \(x_n\) is a nonzerodivisor on \(R\), \(R/x_nR=S'/J_0\) is Cohen–Macaulay of dimension \(\delta\ge2\), in particular of depth \(\ge1\). Hence the irrelevant ideal \(\mathfrak m'\) of \(S'\) is not an associated prime of \(S'/J_0\), i.e. \(J_0\) is saturated, and a saturated ideal defining \(Y'\) is the largest homogeneous ideal: \(J_0=I'\) (II, Ex. 5.10). But \(J_0=(\bar f_1,\dots,\bar f_r)\) is generated by \(r=\operatorname{codim}(Y',\mathbf P^{n-1})\) elements. So \(Y'\) is a complete intersection.

(\(\Leftarrow\)) Assume \(I'=(g_1,\dots,g_r)\). Let \(\mathfrak m=R_+\) be the irrelevant maximal ideal of \(R\) and apply Lemma 9.12 to

\[ A=R_{\mathfrak m},\qquad t=x_n\in\mathfrak m . \]

\(A\) is a local noetherian domain which is a localization of a finitely generated \(k\)-algebra. Let \(\mathfrak p\subseteq R\) be the kernel of \(R\to R/x_nR=S'/J_0\to S'/I'\) (a prime of \(R\) with \(R/\mathfrak p=S'/I'\), the coordinate ring of the cone over \(Y'\)).

(1) \(tA\) has a unique minimal prime \(\mathfrak pA\). The minimal primes of \(x_nR\) correspond to the minimal primes of \(J_0\), i.e. to the irreducible components of the cone \(V(J_0)\subseteq\mathbf A^n\). This cone is a union of lines through \(0\); \(V(J_0)\setminus\{0\}\) is the cone over \(Y'\) minus the vertex, which is irreducible since \(Y'\) is; and every component contains the origin and has positive dimension since \(Y'\ne\emptyset\), so every component meets \(V(J_0)\setminus\{0\}\). Hence \(V(J_0)\) is irreducible, with prime ideal \(I'\), so \(\mathfrak p\) is the only minimal prime of \(x_nR\).

(2) \(t\) generates the maximal ideal of \(A_{\mathfrak p}=R_{\mathfrak p}\). Choose \(i<n\) with \(x_i\notin I'\). The ring \((S'/J_0)_{x_i}\) is the Laurent extension \(\mathcal O(Y'\cap D_+(x_i))[x_i,x_i^{-1}]\), which is reduced because \(Y'\) is nonsingular (reduced). \(R_{\mathfrak p}/x_nR_{\mathfrak p}\) is a localization of this ring, hence reduced; it is a zero-dimensional local ring (\(\mathfrak p\) is minimal over \(x_nR\)), hence a field. So \(x_nR_{\mathfrak p}=\mathfrak pR_{\mathfrak p}\).

(3) \(A/\mathfrak pA\) is normal. \(A/\mathfrak pA=(S'/I')_{\mathfrak m'}\). Now \(Y'\) is a nonsingular (hence normal) complete intersection of dimension \(\ge1\) in \(\mathbf P^{n-1}\), hence projectively normal (II, Ex. 8.4(b)), i.e. \(S'/I'\) is an integrally closed domain; so is its localization.

Lemma 9.12 gives \(\mathfrak pA=x_nA\) (and \(A\) normal). Both \(x_nR\subseteq\mathfrak p\) are homogeneous ideals of \(R\), and \(N=\mathfrak p/x_nR\) is a finitely generated graded \(R\)-module with \(N_{\mathfrak m}=0\). Since a nonzero homogeneous element has a proper homogeneous annihilator, contained in \(\mathfrak m=R_+\), \(N_{\mathfrak m}=0\) forces \(N=0\). Hence \(x_nR=\mathfrak p\), i.e. \(R/x_nR=S'/I'\), i.e. \(J_0=I'=(g_1,\dots,g_r)\).

Now choose homogeneous \(G_i\in I\) with image \(g_i\) in \(S'\). For \(f\in I\) write \(\bar f=\sum c_ig_i\) in \(S'\), lift \(c_i\) to \(C_i\in S\); then \(f-\sum C_iG_i\in x_nS\cap I=x_nI\) (since \(I\) is prime and \(x_n\notin I\)). So \(I=(G_1,\dots,G_r)+x_nI\). The graded \(S\)-module \(I/(G_1,\dots,G_r)\) is finitely generated and equal to \(x_n\) times itself, so it is \(0\) by (graded) Nakayama's lemma. Thus \(I=(G_1,\dots,G_r)\), with \(r=\operatorname{codim}(Y,\mathbf P^n)\): \(Y\) is a complete intersection.∎

Exercise 9.7

Problem

Let \(Y\subseteq X\) be a closed subscheme, where \(X\) is a scheme of finite type over a field \(k\). Let \(D=k[t]/t^2\) be the ring of dual numbers, and define an infinitesimal deformation of \(Y\) as a closed subscheme of \(X\), to be a closed subscheme \(Y'\subseteq X\times_kD\), which is flat over \(D\), and whose closed fibre is \(Y\). Show that these \(Y'\) are classified by \(H^0(Y,\mathcal N_{Y/X})\), where \(\mathcal N_{Y/X}=\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal O_Y}(\mathcal I/\mathcal I^2,\mathcal O_Y)\).

Solution

Write \(\mathcal I\) for the ideal sheaf of \(Y\) in \(X\) and \(\varepsilon\) for \(t\). The scheme \(X\times_kD\) has the same underlying space as \(X\) and structure sheaf \(\mathcal O_X[\varepsilon]=\mathcal O_X\oplus\varepsilon\mathcal O_X\). A closed subscheme \(Y'\) is given by an ideal sheaf \(\mathcal I'\subseteq\mathcal O_X[\varepsilon]\); “closed fibre is \(Y\)” means \(Y'\cap(X\times\operatorname{Spec}k)=Y\), i.e. \(\mathcal I'+(\varepsilon)=\mathcal I+(\varepsilon)\), i.e. the image of \(\mathcal I'\) under \(\varepsilon\mapsto0\) is \(\mathcal I\).

Flatness over \(D\). For a \(D\)-module \(Q\): \(Q\) is flat over \(D\) iff for each ideal \(\mathfrak b\) of \(D\), \(\mathfrak b\otimes_DQ\to Q\) is injective (9.1A(a)). The ideals of \(D\) are \(0,(\varepsilon),D\), and \((\varepsilon)\cong D/(\varepsilon)=k\), so \((\varepsilon)\otimes Q=Q/\varepsilon Q\to Q\) is multiplication by \(\varepsilon\). Hence \(Q\) is \(D\)-flat iff \(\ker(\varepsilon:Q\to Q)=\varepsilon Q\).

Local computation. Let \(W=\operatorname{Spec}P\subseteq X\) be an affine open set, \(I=\mathcal I(W)\subseteq P\), and \(I'=\mathcal I'(W)\subseteq P[\varepsilon]\) with image \(I\) in \(P\) (closed fibre \(Y\)). Put \(J'=\{a\in P:\varepsilon a\in I'\}\), an ideal of \(P\). For \(f\in I\) pick \(g\) with \(f+\varepsilon g\in I'\); then \(\varepsilon f=\varepsilon(f+\varepsilon g)\in I'\), so \(f\in J'\): \(I\subseteq J'\). Let \(Q=P[\varepsilon]/I'\). Then \(\ker(\varepsilon|Q)=\{a+\varepsilon b:\varepsilon a\in I'\}=\{a+\varepsilon b:a\in J'\}+I'/I'\), and \(\varepsilon Q=\{\varepsilon c\}+I'/I'\). As \(\varepsilon\cdot\varepsilon Q=0\), always \(\varepsilon Q\subseteq\ker\varepsilon\). The reverse inclusion says: for each \(a\in J'\) and \(b\in P\), \(a+\varepsilon b\in\varepsilon P+I'\), i.e. \(a\in\) image of \(I'\) in \(P\) \(=I\). So

\[ Q\text{ is flat over }D\iff J'\subseteq I\iff J'=I . \]

Assume \(J'=I\). For \(f\in I\) the element \(g\) with \(f+\varepsilon g\in I'\) is unique modulo \(J'=I\), so we get a well defined map \(\varphi:I\to P/I\), \(\varphi(f)=g\bmod I\). It is additive and \(P\)-linear (\(p(f+\varepsilon g)=pf+\varepsilon pg\in I'\)), and it kills \(I^2\) since \(P/I\) is killed by \(I\) (\(\varphi(ff')=f\varphi(f')=0\)). So \(\varphi\in\operatorname{Hom}_P(I/I^2,P/I)=\operatorname{Hom}_{P/I}(I/I^2,P/I)\). Moreover \(I'=\{f+\varepsilon g:f\in I,\ g\bmod I=\varphi(f)\}\), since \(I'\supseteq\varepsilon I\) (as \(J'=I\)) and \(I'\) maps onto \(I\).

Conversely, given \(\varphi\in\operatorname{Hom}_P(I/I^2,P/I)\), the set \(I'_\varphi=\{f+\varepsilon g:f\in I,\ \bar g=\varphi(f)\}\) is an ideal of \(P[\varepsilon]\): \((p+\varepsilon q)(f+\varepsilon g)=pf+\varepsilon(pg+qf)\) with \(qf\in I\), and \(\overline{pg+qf}=p\varphi(f)=\varphi(pf)\). It maps onto \(I\), and \(J'=I\) (if \(\varepsilon a\in I'_\varphi\) then \(0\in I\), \(\bar a=\varphi(0)=0\)). So \(P[\varepsilon]/I'_\varphi\) is \(D\)-flat with closed fibre \(P/I\). We have shown: on the affine open \(W\), deformations \(\leftrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{P/I}(I/I^2,P/I)=\Gamma(W\cap Y,\mathcal N_{Y/X})\) (the Hom sheaf of the coherent sheaves \(\mathcal I/\mathcal I^2\), \(\mathcal O_Y\) over an affine is the Hom of modules).

Globalization. The construction \(\mathcal I'\mapsto\varphi\) commutes with restriction to smaller affine opens (localize \(I'\) at \(h\in P\): \((I')_h\) and \(J'_h\), \(\varphi_h\)), so a deformation \(\mathcal I'\) yields compatible local sections of \(\mathcal N_{Y/X}\), i.e. a global section; conversely a global section \(\varphi\) yields compatible local ideals \(I'_{\varphi|W}\) which glue to a sheaf of ideals, whose flatness and closed fibre are checked locally. The two constructions are inverse. Hence the set of infinitesimal deformations of \(Y\) in \(X\) is in canonical bijection with \(H^0(Y,\mathcal N_{Y/X})\), the trivial deformation \(Y\times_kD\) corresponding to \(\varphi=0\).∎

Exercise 9.8

Problem

Let \(A\) be a finitely generated \(k\)-algebra. Write \(A\) as a quotient of a polynomial ring \(P\) over \(k\), and let \(J\) be the kernel:

\[ 0\to J\to P\to A\to0. \]

Consider the exact sequence of (II, 8.4A)

\[ J/J^2\to\Omega_{P/k}\otimes_PA\to\Omega_{A/k}\to0. \]

Apply the functor \(\operatorname{Hom}_A(\cdot,A)\), and let \(T^1(A)\) be the cokernel:

\[ \operatorname{Hom}_A(\Omega_{P/k}\otimes A,A)\to\operatorname{Hom}_A(J/J^2,A)\to T^1(A)\to0. \]

Now use the construction of (II, Ex. 8.6) to show that \(T^1(A)\) classifies infinitesimal deformations of \(A\), i.e., algebras \(A'\) flat over \(D=k[t]/t^2\), with \(A'\otimes_Dk\cong A\). It follows that \(T^1(A)\) is independent of the given representation of \(A\) as a quotient of a polynomial ring \(P\). (For more details, see Lichtenbaum and Schlessinger [1].) (Starred in the book.)

Solution

Let \(P=k[x_1,\dots,x_m]\), \(P_D=P\otimes_kD=P[t]/t^2\), and let \(\mathrm{Def}(A)\) be the set of isomorphism classes of pairs \((A',\alpha)\), \(A'\) a \(D\)-algebra flat over \(D\) and \(\alpha:A'/tA'\xrightarrow{\sim}A\) an isomorphism of \(k\)-algebras; an isomorphism of pairs is a \(D\)-algebra isomorphism compatible with the \(\alpha\)'s. We construct a bijection \(\mathrm{Def}(A)\to T^1(A)\). Note that \(\operatorname{Hom}_A(\Omega_{P/k}\otimes A,A)=\operatorname{Der}_k(P,A)\) and that the first map above sends a derivation \(\delta\) to \(\delta|_J:J\to A\) (this is \(P\)-linear and kills \(J^2\) because \(\delta(fg)=\bar f\delta g+\bar g\delta f=0\) for \(f\in J\), \(g\in J\), and \(\delta(pf)=\bar p\delta f\) for \(f\in J\)).

Step 1: lifting. Let \((A',\alpha)\) be a deformation and identify \(A'/tA'=A\). Since \(P\) is a polynomial ring and \(A'\to A\) is surjective, there is a \(k\)-algebra homomorphism \(\psi:P\to A'\) lifting \(P\to A\) (lift the images of the \(x_i\)). It extends to a \(D\)-algebra map \(\Psi:P_D\to A'\). It is surjective: \(A'=\psi(P)+tA'\) and \(tA'=t\psi(P)\) (as \(t^2=0\)), so \(A'=\psi(P)+t\psi(P)=\Psi(P_D)\). Put \(I'_\psi=\ker\Psi\), so \(A'\cong P_D/I'_\psi\). This is flat over \(D\) with closed fibre \(P_D/(I'_\psi+tP_D)=A=P/J\); so \(I'_\psi\) is exactly an infinitesimal deformation of the closed subscheme \(\operatorname{Spec}A\subseteq\mathbf A^m_k\) in the sense of Ex. 9.7 (with \(X=\mathbf A^m_k\), \(Y=\operatorname{Spec}A\)). By the computation in Ex. 9.7 it corresponds to \(\varphi_\psi\in\operatorname{Hom}_A(J/J^2,A)\) defined by: for \(f\in J\) and \(f+tg\in I'_\psi\), \(\varphi_\psi(f)=\bar g\) (the class of \(g\) in \(A\)); every \(\varphi\) arises from a unique ideal.

Step 2: change of lifting. Because \(A'\) is \(D\)-flat, multiplication by \(t\) gives an isomorphism \(A=A'/tA'\xrightarrow{\sim}tA'\) (tensor \(0\to k\xrightarrow{t}D\to k\to0\) with \(A'\)). If \(\psi,\psi'\) are two lifts, \(\psi'-\psi\) takes values in \(tA'\), so \(\psi'=\psi+t\delta\) for a unique \(\delta:P\to A\), and expanding \(\psi'(pq)=\psi'(p)\psi'(q)\) (with \(t^2=0\)) shows \(\delta(pq)=\bar p\delta(q)+\bar q\delta(p)\): \(\delta\in\operatorname{Der}_k(P,A)\). Conversely every \(\delta\in\operatorname{Der}_k(P,A)\) gives a lift \(\psi+t\delta\) by the same computation. Now let \(f\in J\) and \(f+tg\in I'_\psi\), i.e. \(\psi(f)=-t\bar g\). Then

\[ \Psi'(f+tg')=\psi(f)+t\delta(f)+t\bar g'=t(\delta(f)+\bar g'-\bar g) \]

vanishes iff \(\bar g'=\bar g-\delta(f)\). Hence

\[ \varphi_{\psi'}=\varphi_\psi-\delta|_J . \]

Therefore the class \([\varphi_\psi]\in T^1(A)=\operatorname{Hom}_A(J/J^2,A)/\{\delta|_J\}\) does not depend on \(\psi\); call it \(\Phi(A',\alpha)\).

Step 3: \(\Phi\) is well defined on isomorphism classes. If \(\sigma:A'_1\to A'_2\) is an isomorphism of deformations and \(\psi_1\) is a lift for \(A_1'\), then \(\psi_2=\sigma\psi_1\) is a lift for \(A_2'\) and \(\ker\Psi_2=\ker\Psi_1\); so \(\varphi_{\psi_2}=\varphi_{\psi_1}\).

Step 4: surjectivity. Given \(\varphi\in\operatorname{Hom}_A(J/J^2,A)\), Ex. 9.7 gives an ideal \(I'_\varphi\subseteq P_D\) with \(P_D/I'_\varphi\) flat over \(D\) and \(P_D/(I'_\varphi+tP_D)=P/J=A\); this deformation has \(\Phi=[\varphi]\) (use \(\psi=\) the canonical map \(P\to P_D/I'_\varphi\)).

Step 5: injectivity. Let \(\Phi(A'_1)=\Phi(A'_2)\). Choose lifts \(\psi_1,\psi_2\); then \(\varphi_{\psi_1}-\varphi_{\psi_2}=\delta|_J\) for some \(\delta\in\operatorname{Der}_k(P,A)\); replacing \(\psi_2\) by \(\psi_2'=\psi_2-t\delta\) we get \(\varphi_{\psi_2'}=\varphi_{\psi_2}+\delta|_J=\varphi_{\psi_1}\) (apply the formula of Step 2). By Ex. 9.7 the ideal is determined by \(\varphi\), so \(I'_{\psi_1}=I'_{\psi_2'}\), and \(\Psi_2'\circ\Psi_1^{-1}:A'_1=P_D/I'_{\psi_1}\to A'_2\) is an isomorphism of \(D\)-algebras compatible with the identifications of closed fibres with \(A\).

Hence \(\Phi:\mathrm{Def}(A)\to T^1(A)\) is a bijection. In particular \(T^1(A)\), which was defined using the representation \(A=P/J\), is in bijection with \(\mathrm{Def}(A)\), a set defined only in terms of \(A\); this identification is natural, and \(T^1(A)\) is independent (up to canonical bijection, indeed up to canonical \(k\)-isomorphism for its natural \(A\)-module structure) of the presentation.∎

Exercise 9.9

Problem

A \(k\)-algebra \(A\) is said to be rigid if it has no infinitesimal deformations, or equivalently, by (Ex. 9.8) if \(T^1(A)=0\). Let \(A=k[x,y,z,w]/(x,y)\cap(z,w)\), and show that \(A\) is rigid. This corresponds to two planes in \(\mathbf A^4\) which meet at a point.

Solution

Let \(P=k[x,y,z,w]\) and \(J=(x,y)\cap(z,w)=(xz,xw,yz,yw)\) (the intersection of monomial ideals is generated by the common multiples of the generators, which are the products). Put \(g_1=xz\), \(g_2=xw\), \(g_3=yz\), \(g_4=yw\). We must show that every \(\varphi\in\operatorname{Hom}_A(J/J^2,A)=\operatorname{Hom}_P(J,A)\) is induced by a derivation \(\delta\in\operatorname{Der}_k(P,A)\), i.e. \(\varphi(f)=\delta(f)\) for \(f\in J\); then the cokernel \(T^1(A)\) is \(0\).

Description of \(A\). The map \(f\mapsto(f(0,0,z,w),f(x,y,0,0))\) identifies \(A\) with the subring of \(k[z,w]\times k[x,y]\) of pairs \((u,v)\) with \(u(0,0)=v(0,0)\) (injective since \(J=(x,y)\cap(z,w)\); the image is as stated, since \((u,v)\) is the image of \(u(z,w)+v(x,y)-u(0,0)\)). We write \(a=(a^{(1)},a^{(2)})\) with \(a^{(1)}\in k[z,w]\) (restriction to the plane \(x=y=0\)) and \(a^{(2)}\in k[x,y]\) (restriction to \(z=w=0\)).

Homomorphisms. Let \(\varphi\in\operatorname{Hom}_P(J,A)\) and \(a_i=\varphi(g_i)\in A\). The syzygies \(wg_1=zg_2\), \(wg_3=zg_4\), \(yg_1=xg_3\), \(yg_2=xg_4\) give in \(A\):

\[ wa_1=za_2,\quad wa_3=za_4,\quad ya_1=xa_3,\quad ya_2=xa_4 . \]

Applying the two restriction maps: the first two relations are trivial on \(z=w=0\) (both sides vanish), and the last two are trivial on \(x=y=0\). So we get

\[ wa_1^{(1)}=za_2^{(1)},\quad wa_3^{(1)}=za_4^{(1)}\ \text{in }k[z,w];\qquad ya_1^{(2)}=xa_3^{(2)},\quad ya_2^{(2)}=xa_4^{(2)}\ \text{in }k[x,y]. \]

Since \(z,w\) are coprime in the UFD \(k[z,w]\), there are \(u,u'\in k[z,w]\) with \((a_1^{(1)},a_2^{(1)})=(zu,wu)\) and \((a_3^{(1)},a_4^{(1)})=(zu',wu')\); similarly there are \(v,v'\in k[x,y]\) with \((a_1^{(2)},a_3^{(2)})=(xv,yv)\) and \((a_2^{(2)},a_4^{(2)})=(xv',yv')\). Thus

\[ a_1=(zu,xv),\quad a_2=(wu,xv'),\quad a_3=(zu',yv),\quad a_4=(wu',yv') \]

(all with zero constant terms, so these are indeed elements of \(A\)).

Derivations. A derivation \(\delta\in\operatorname{Der}_k(P,A)\) is given by arbitrary elements \(\alpha_x=\delta(x)\), \(\alpha_y\), \(\alpha_z\), \(\alpha_w\in A\). Write \(\alpha_x=(p_x,q_x)\), etc. Then

\[ \delta(g_1)=z\alpha_x+x\alpha_z=(zp_x,\ xq_z),\quad\delta(g_2)=w\alpha_x+x\alpha_w=(wp_x,\ xq_w), \]
\[ \delta(g_3)=z\alpha_y+y\alpha_z=(zp_y,\ yq_z),\quad\delta(g_4)=w\alpha_y+y\alpha_w=(wp_y,\ yq_w), \]

because \(x,y\) vanish on the plane \(x=y=0\) and \(z,w\) on the plane \(z=w=0\). Choose \(\alpha_x=(u,u(0))\), \(\alpha_y=(u',u'(0))\), \(\alpha_z=(v(0),v)\), \(\alpha_w=(v'(0),v')\) (pairs with equal constant terms, so elements of \(A\)). Then \(\delta(g_1)=(zu,xv)=a_1\), \(\delta(g_2)=(wu,xv')=a_2\), \(\delta(g_3)=(zu',yv)=a_3\), \(\delta(g_4)=(wu',yv')=a_4\). So \(\varphi\) and \(\delta|_J\) agree on the generators \(g_i\) of \(J\), hence \(\varphi=\delta|_J\).

Therefore \(\operatorname{Der}_k(P,A)\to\operatorname{Hom}_A(J/J^2,A)\) is surjective, \(T^1(A)=0\), and \(A\) is rigid.∎

Exercise 9.10

Problem

A scheme \(X_0\) over a field \(k\) is rigid if it has no infinitesimal deformations.

  1. (a)

    Show that \(\mathbf P^1_k\) is rigid, using (9.13.2).

  2. (b)

    One might think that if \(X_0\) is rigid over \(k\), then every global deformation of \(X_0\) is locally trivial. Show that this is not so, by constructing a proper, flat morphism \(f:X\to\mathbf A^2\) over \(k\) algebraically closed, such that \(X_0\cong\mathbf P^1_k\), but there is no open neighborhood \(U\) of \(0\) in \(\mathbf A^2\) for which \(f^{-1}(U)\cong U\times\mathbf P^1\).

  3. (c)

    Show, however, that one can trivialize a global deformation of \(\mathbf P^1\) after a flat base extension, in the following sense: let \(f:X\to T\) be a flat projective morphism, where \(T\) is a nonsingular curve over \(k\) algebraically closed. Assume there is a closed point \(t\in T\) such that \(X_t\cong\mathbf P^1_k\). Then there exists a nonsingular curve \(T'\), and a flat morphism \(g:T'\to T\) whose image contains \(t\), such that if \(X'=X\times_TT'\) is the base extension, then the new family \(f':X'\to T'\) is isomorphic to \(\mathbf P^1_{T'}\to T'\).

(Part (c) is starred in the book.)

Solution

(a) Let \(k\) be algebraically closed (as in 9.13.2). By (9.13.2) the set of infinitesimal deformations of the nonsingular variety \(X=\mathbf P^1\), up to isomorphism, is in bijection with \(H^1(X,\mathcal T_X)\), where \(\mathcal T_X=\mathcal{H}om(\Omega_X,\mathcal O_X)\). For \(\mathbf P^1\), \(\Omega\cong\mathcal O(-2)\) (the canonical sheaf of \(\mathbf P^n\) is \(\mathcal O(-n-1)\), II, 8.20.1), so \(\mathcal T\cong\mathcal O(2)\). By Thm. 5.1(d), \(H^1(\mathbf P^1,\mathcal O(2))\) is dual to \(H^0(\mathbf P^1,\mathcal O(-4))=0\). So \(H^1(\mathcal T)=0\): there is only the trivial infinitesimal deformation, and \(\mathbf P^1\) is rigid.

(b) Here \(k\) is algebraically closed (also in (c)). Let \(T=\mathbf A^2=\operatorname{Spec}k[a,b]\) and let \(X\subseteq\mathbf P^2_T=\operatorname{Proj}k[a,b][x,y,z]\) be the conic bundle

\[ X:\ F=x^2+xy+a\,y^2-(1+b)\,z^2=0 ,\qquad f:X\to T . \]

Then \(f\) is projective, hence proper. For every point \(s\in T\) (closed or not), \(F_s\in k(s)[x,y,z]\) is a nonzero form of degree \(2\) (the coefficient of \(x^2\) is \(1\)), so \(X_s\) is a plane conic and its Hilbert polynomial is \(2m+1\); since \(T\) is integral noetherian, \(f\) is flat by Thm. 9.9.

The fibre over \(0\). \(X_0=\{x^2+xy-z^2=0\}\subseteq\mathbf P^2_k\) is a smooth conic: the partial derivatives \(2x+y,\ x,\ -2z\) vanish simultaneously only if \(x=0,y=0\) and then \(z=0\) when \(\operatorname{char}k\ne2\); in characteristic \(2\) the partials are \(y,x,0\), which vanish only at \(x=y=0\), where \(F=z^2\) forces \(z=0\). A smooth plane conic over an algebraically closed field is isomorphic to \(\mathbf P^1\) (e.g. project from one of its points), so \(X_0\cong\mathbf P^1_k\).

No trivialization. Suppose \(U\ni0\) is open in \(\mathbf A^2\) and \(f^{-1}(U)\cong U\times\mathbf P^1\) as \(U\)-schemes. Then \(f^{-1}(U)\to U\) has a section, hence the generic fibre \(X_\eta\) (over \(K=k(a,b)\)) has a \(K\)-rational point: there is \((x:y:z)\in\mathbf P^2(K)\) with \(F=0\). We show this is impossible. Put \(q=1+b\), so \(k[a,b]=k[a,q]\) is a polynomial ring (a UFD). Clearing denominators and dividing by common factors, we may assume \(x,y,z\in k[a,q]\) have no common prime factor and \(x^2+xy+ay^2=qz^2\). Reduce modulo \(q\): with \(\bar x,\bar y\in k[a]\) we get \(\bar x^2+\bar x\bar y+a\bar y^2=0\). If \(\bar y\ne0\) let \(g=\gcd(\bar x,\bar y)\), \(\bar x=gu\), \(\bar y=gv\), \(\gcd(u,v)=1\); then \(u^2+uv+av^2=0\), so \(v\mid u^2\), hence \(v=c\in k^*\) and \(u^2+cu+ac^2=0\) in \(k[a]\). If \(u\) is constant, the left side has degree \(1\) in \(a\); if \(\deg u\ge1\), \(\deg(u^2+cu)=2\deg u\ge2>1\). Contradiction. So \(\bar y=0\), then \(\bar x^2=0\), \(\bar x=0\): \(q\mid x\) and \(q\mid y\). Then \(q^2\mid qz^2\), so \(q\mid z\), contradicting the choice of \(x,y,z\). Hence \(X_\eta\) has no \(K\)-point, and no neighborhood \(U\) of \(0\) has \(f^{-1}(U)\cong U\times\mathbf P^1\) over \(U\). (Of course \(f\) is a smooth conic bundle over a neighborhood of \(0\), since \(X_0\) is smooth and \(f\) is flat; the obstruction to Zariski local triviality is arithmetic: the generic conic has no rational point.)

(c) Step 1: shrinking \(T\). Since \(X_t\) is smooth over \(k\) and \(f\) is flat, \(f\) is smooth at every point of \(X_t\) (EGA IV\(_4\) 17.5.1; this is the local form of Thm. 10.2). The smooth locus \(V\subseteq X\) is open and contains \(X_t\), and \(f\) is closed, so \(T_1=T\setminus f(X\setminus V)\) is an open neighborhood of \(t\) with \(f^{-1}(T_1)\subseteq V\). By Thm. 12.8 the functions \(s\mapsto h^i(X_s,\mathcal O_{X_s})\) are upper semicontinuous, and at \(t\) we have \(h^0=1\), \(h^1=0\); shrinking \(T_1\) we get \(h^0(\mathcal O_{X_s})\le1\) and \(h^1(\mathcal O_{X_s})=0\) for all \(s\in T_1\). Since \(f\) is flat and \(T_1\) is connected, \(\dim X_s=\dim X_t=1\) for all \(s\in T_1\) (Cor. 9.10), in particular \(X_s\ne\emptyset\) and \(h^0(\mathcal O_{X_s})=1\). Thus for each closed point \(s\in T_1\), \(X_s\) is a smooth (it lies in the smooth locus \(V\)) projective connected curve of genus \(0\), i.e. \(X_s\cong\mathbf P^1_k\). Replace \(T\) by \(T_1\); then \(f\) is smooth, projective, with all closed fibres \(\mathbf P^1\).

Step 2: a finite flat cover over which \(f\) has a section. Let \(X\subseteq\mathbf P^N_T\) (projective morphism). \(X_t\subseteq\mathbf P^N_k\) is an irreducible curve; choose a hyperplane \(H\subseteq\mathbf P^N_k\) not containing it. Then \(X_t\cap H\) is nonempty (a projective variety of dimension \(\ge1\) meets every hypersurface, Ex. I.3.7) and finite. Let \(C=X\cap(H\times T)\). By upper semicontinuity of fibre dimension (II, Ex. 3.22) and properness of \(f\), shrinking \(T\) around \(t\) we may assume \(C\to T\) has finite fibres, hence is finite (\(C\) is proper over \(T\)). Let \(c\in C_t\). Since \(f\) is flat with fibre dimension \(1\), \(\dim\mathcal O_{X,c}=1+1=2\) (Prop. 9.5), and \(C\) is defined near \(c\) by one equation in \(X\), so \(\dim\mathcal O_{C,c}\ge1\). Choose a prime \(\mathfrak q\) of \(\mathcal O_{C,c}\) with \(\dim\mathcal O_{C,c}/\mathfrak q\ge1\) and let \(C_1\subseteq C\) be the closure of the corresponding point with the reduced structure: an integral closed subscheme through \(c\) of dimension \(\ge1\). \(f(C_1)\) is an irreducible closed subset of \(T\) containing \(t\); if it were \(\{t\}\) then \(C_1\subseteq C_t\) would be finite, contradicting \(\dim C_1\ge1\). So \(f(C_1)=T\) and \(C_1\) is an integral curve, finite and surjective over \(T\). Let \(T'\to C_1\) be its normalization: a nonsingular curve, finite over \(C_1\), and \(g:T'\to T\) is finite and surjective. \(T'\) is integral and dominates the nonsingular curve \(T\), so \(\mathcal O_{T'}\) is torsion-free, hence flat, over the local rings of \(T\) (Dedekind): \(g\) is flat, and its image is all of \(T\ni t\).

Let \(X'=X\times_TT'\), \(f':X'\to T'\). It is smooth and projective, and for each closed \(s'\in T'\) the fibre is \(X_{g(s')}\cong\mathbf P^1_k\). The morphism \(T'\to C_1\hookrightarrow X\) together with \(\mathrm{id}_{T'}\) defines a section \(\sigma:T'\to X'\) of \(f'\).

Step 3: \(X'\) is a \(\mathbf P^1\)-bundle. \(X'\) is regular (smooth over the regular scheme \(T'\)), so the closed integral curve \(D=\sigma(T')\) is an effective Cartier divisor (II, 6.11); put \(\mathcal M=\mathcal O_{X'}(D)\). \(D\) meets each closed fibre \(X'_{s'}\) transversally in the single reduced point \(\sigma(s')\) (\(D\to T'\) is an isomorphism), so \(\mathcal M|_{X'_{s'}}\cong\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^1}(\sigma(s'))\cong\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^1}(1)\). Hence \(h^0(X'_{s'},\mathcal M_{s'})=2\) and \(h^1=0\) for all closed \(s'\), hence (semicontinuity; a nonempty closed subset of \(T'\) contains a closed point) for all points \(s'\in T'\). By Cor. 12.9, \(\mathcal E'=f'_*\mathcal M\) is locally free of rank \(2\) and \(\mathcal E'\otimes k(s')\cong H^0(X'_{s'},\mathcal M_{s'})\). The evaluation map \(f'^*\mathcal E'\to\mathcal M\) is surjective on each closed fibre (\(H^0(\mathcal O(1))\otimes\mathcal O\to\mathcal O(1)\) is surjective), so by Nakayama it is surjective on a neighborhood of every closed fibre, hence everywhere (the support of the cokernel is closed and its image in \(T'\) is closed and contains no closed point). By (II, 7.12) it defines a \(T'\)-morphism \(\psi:X'\to\mathbf P(\mathcal E')\) with \(\psi^*\mathcal O(1)=\mathcal M\), which on each closed fibre is the morphism \(\mathbf P^1\to\mathbf P(H^0(\mathbf P^1,\mathcal O(1)))=\mathbf P^1\) given by the complete linear system \(|\mathcal O(1)|\), an isomorphism.

\(\psi\) is an isomorphism. Indeed \(\psi\) is proper, and all its fibres over closed points of \(\mathbf P(\mathcal E')\) are single points; by semicontinuity of fibre dimension the locus of points of \(X'\) where \(\psi\) has positive-dimensional fibre is closed, and its image is a closed subset of \(\mathbf P(\mathcal E')\) containing no closed point, hence empty. So \(\psi\) is proper and quasi-finite, hence finite. Put \(\mathcal A=\psi_*\mathcal O_{X'}\), a coherent \(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P(\mathcal E')}\)-algebra, flat over \(T'\) (as \(\psi\) is finite and \(X'\) is \(T'\)-flat). The map \(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P(\mathcal E')}\to\mathcal A\) becomes an isomorphism after \(\otimes k(s')\) for each closed \(s'\) (the fibre of \(\psi\) over \(s'\)). By Nakayama its cokernel vanishes near the fibres over closed points, hence everywhere; for its kernel \(\mathcal K\), flatness of \(\mathcal A\) over \(T'\) gives that \(\mathcal K\otimes k(s')\to\mathcal O\otimes k(s')\to\mathcal A\otimes k(s')\) is exact on the left, so \(\mathcal K\otimes k(s')=0\) and again \(\mathcal K=0\). So \(\psi\) is an isomorphism: \(X'\cong\mathbf P(\mathcal E')\) over \(T'\).

Step 4: trivialization. Pick a closed point \(t'\in g^{-1}(t)\). The locally free sheaf \(\mathcal E'\) is free on a neighborhood \(T''\subseteq T'\) of \(t'\). Then \(X'|_{T''}\cong\mathbf P(\mathcal O_{T''}^{2})=\mathbf P^1_{T''}\). The morphism \(T''\hookrightarrow T'\xrightarrow{g}T\) is flat (open immersion followed by a flat map), \(T''\) is a nonsingular curve, its image contains \(t\), and the base extension \(X\times_TT''=X'|_{T''}\) is isomorphic to \(\mathbf P^1_{T''}\to T''\). This proves (c).

(Remark: the base extension \(g\) constructed is finite and surjective onto a neighborhood of \(t\) before the final shrinking; the family becomes trivial near a point over \(t\). In contrast, (b) shows that over a two-dimensional base even this kind of shrinking cannot trivialize the family on a Zariski neighborhood of the point itself.)∎

Exercise 9.11

Problem

Let \(Y\) be a nonsingular curve of degree \(d\) in \(\mathbf P^n_k\), over an algebraically closed field \(k\). Show that

\[ 0\le p_a(Y)\le\tfrac12(d-1)(d-2). \]

[Hint: Compare \(Y\) to a suitable projection of \(Y\) into \(\mathbf P^2\), as in (9.8.3) and (9.8.4).]

Solution

Here a curve is an integral (equivalently, connected nonsingular) projective scheme of dimension \(1\), \(p_a(Z)=1-P_Z(0)\) for a \(1\)-dimensional \(Z\) (I, Ex. 7.2; Ex. III 5.3), where \(P_Z(m)=dm+(1-p_a)\) is the Hilbert polynomial. Let \(g=p_a(Y)\).

Lower bound. \(H^0(Y,\mathcal O_Y)=k\) since \(Y\) is integral projective over the algebraically closed \(k\), so \(p_a(Y)=1-\chi(\mathcal O_Y)=h^1(\mathcal O_Y)\ge0\).

Upper bound. If \(n=1\), then \(Y=\mathbf P^1\), \(d=1\), \(g=0\). If \(n=2\), \(Y\) is a plane curve of degree \(d\) and the sequence \(0\to\mathcal O(-d)\to\mathcal O\to\mathcal O_Y\to0\) gives \(\chi(\mathcal O_Y(m))=\chi(\mathcal O(m))-\chi(\mathcal O(m-d))=dm+1-\tfrac12(d-1)(d-2)\), so \(g=\frac12(d-1)(d-2)\). Let \(n\ge3\).

Step A: a good projection. Claim: there is a linear subspace \(L\subseteq\mathbf P^n\) of dimension \(n-3\) with \(L\cap Y=\emptyset\) such that the projection \(\pi_L:Y\to\mathbf P^2\) from \(L\) is birational onto its image \(\Gamma\), which is an integral plane curve. It suffices to project successively from general points: let \(\Gamma_0\subseteq\mathbf P^m\) (\(m\ge3\)) be an integral curve. For a point \(P\in\mathbf P^m\setminus\Gamma_0\) the image of \(\pi_P\) is a curve (not a point: otherwise \(\Gamma_0\) lies in a line through \(P\), hence is that line, contradicting \(P\notin\Gamma_0\)). Let \(N\) be the (dense open) nonsingular locus of \(\Gamma_0\). The union \(S\) of the secant lines \(\overline{xx'}\), \(x\ne x'\in N\), is a constructible set of dimension \(\le3\), and the union of the tangent lines to \(\Gamma_0\) at points of \(N\) has dimension \(\le2\). If \(m\ge4\), a general \(P\) lies on no secant and no tangent line. If \(m=3\), a general \(P\) lies on no tangent line and on only finitely many secant lines (the incidence variety \(\{(x,x',P)\}\) has dimension \(3\) so its fibres over a general \(P\in\mathbf P^3\) are finite, or empty). Fix such a general \(P\) (also \(P\notin\Gamma_0\)) and put \(\Gamma_1=\pi_P(\Gamma_0)\). Then: (i) there is a finite set \(F\subseteq N\) such that \(\pi_P\) is injective on \(N\setminus F\) (take \(F\) to be the points lying on the finitely many secant lines through \(P\) if \(m=3\), and \(F=\emptyset\) if \(m\ge4\)); hence the finite surjective map \(\pi_P:\Gamma_0\to\Gamma_1\) has exactly one point in the fibre over every point of the dense open set \(\Gamma_1\setminus\pi_P(F\cup(\Gamma_0\setminus N))\), so the field extension \(k(\Gamma_0)/k(\Gamma_1)\) has trivial separable degree; (ii) since \(P\) lies on no tangent line at a point of \(N\), the differential of \(N\to\mathbf P^{m-1}\) is injective at each \(x\in N\), i.e. \(\Omega_{N/\mathbf P^{m-1}}=0\), and since \(\Omega_{N/\Gamma_1}\) is a quotient of \(\Omega_{N/\mathbf P^{m-1}}\) (II, 8.11) we get \(\Omega_{k(\Gamma_0)/k(\Gamma_1)}=0\), so the extension is separable. Hence \(k(\Gamma_0)=k(\Gamma_1)\) and \(\pi_P:\Gamma_0\to\Gamma_1\) is birational. Iterating from \(\mathbf P^n\) down to \(\mathbf P^2\) (\(n-2\) projections) and composing gives the claim, the composite being the projection from a linear space \(L\) of dimension \(n-3\) disjoint from \(Y\).

Choose coordinates such that \(L=\{x_0=x_1=x_2=0\}\), so \(\pi_L(x_0:\dots:x_n)=(x_0:x_1:x_2)\), defined on all of \(Y\).

Step B: the family (cf. 9.8.3). For \(a\in k^*\) let \(\sigma_a(x_0:\dots:x_n)=(x_0:x_1:x_2:ax_3:\dots:ax_n)\), an automorphism of \(\mathbf P^n\). The map

\[ \Phi:Y\times\mathbf A^1\to\mathbf P^n\times\mathbf A^1,\qquad(y,a)\mapsto(\sigma_a(y),a) \]

(the formula \((x_0:x_1:x_2:ax_3:\dots:ax_n)\) makes sense for \(a=0\) too, because \(x_0,x_1,x_2\) have no common zero on \(Y\)) is a morphism over \(\mathbf A^1\), hence proper. Its image is closed and irreducible, and it is the closure of \(\Phi(Y\times\mathbf G_m)\) (\(Y\times\mathbf G_m\) is dense in \(Y\times\mathbf A^1\)). Let \(\overline X\subseteq\mathbf P^n\times\mathbf A^1\) be this image with its reduced structure; \(\overline X\) is an integral closed subscheme, hence flat over \(\mathbf A^1\) (Ex. 9.1.3, \(k[a]\) a PID), and:

  • •

    for \(a\ne0\), \(\overline X_a=\sigma_a(Y)\cong Y\) (over \(a\ne0\), \(\Phi\) is an isomorphism of \(Y\times\mathbf G_m\) onto \(\overline X|_{a\ne0}\), since \((y,a)\mapsto(\sigma_a y,a)\) is the restriction of an automorphism of \(\mathbf P^n\times\mathbf G_m\));

  • •

    the underlying set of \(\overline X_0\) is \(\Phi(Y\times\{0\})=\pi_L(Y)=\Gamma\subseteq\mathbf P^2\subseteq\mathbf P^n\).

By Thm. 9.9 all fibres have Hilbert polynomial \(P_Y(m)=dm+1-g\); in particular \(\overline X_0\) has Hilbert polynomial \(dm+1-g\).

Step C: the multiplicity of \(\overline X_0\) along \(\Gamma\) is \(1\). The map \(\Phi:Y\times\mathbf A^1\to\overline X\) is proper, quasi-finite (the fibre over \((z,0)\) is \(\{y:\pi_L(y)=z\}\), finite since \(\pi_L:Y\to\Gamma\) is a non-constant morphism of curves) and birational (an isomorphism over \(a\ne0\)), hence finite and birational. As \(Y\times\mathbf A^1\) is normal, it is the normalization of \(\overline X\). Let \(\xi\) be the generic point of \(\Gamma\) in \(\overline X_0\) and \(\mathcal O=\mathcal O_{\overline X,\xi}\), a one-dimensional local domain, in which \(a\ne0\) is a nonzerodivisor with \(\mathcal O/a\mathcal O=\mathcal O_{\overline X_0,\xi}\). The normalization \(B\) of \(\mathcal O\) is the semi-local ring of \(Y\times\mathbf A^1\) at the points lying over \(\xi\). A point of \(Y\times\{0\}\) over \(\xi\) must (by finiteness, dimensions) be the generic point \(\eta_Y\) of \(Y\times\{0\}\), so \(B=\mathcal O_{Y\times\mathbf A^1,(\eta_Y,0)}=k(Y)[a]_{(a)}\), a DVR with uniformizer \(a\) and residue field \(k(Y)\). Since \(\pi_L\) is birational onto \(\Gamma\), \(k(Y)=k(\Gamma)=\kappa(\xi)\): the residue fields of \(B\) and \(\mathcal O\) coincide. \(B/\mathcal O\) is a finitely generated torsion \(\mathcal O\)-module over a one-dimensional local domain, hence of finite length; multiplication by \(a\) on \(0\to\mathcal O\to B\to B/\mathcal O\to0\) and the snake lemma (for a finite-length module \(Q\), \(\ell(\ker a|_Q)=\ell(Q/aQ)\)) give \(\ell(\mathcal O/a\mathcal O)=\ell(B/aB)=\ell(\kappa(B))=1\). So \(\mathcal O_{\overline X_0,\xi}=\mathcal O/a\mathcal O\) is the field \(\kappa(\xi)\): \(\overline X_0\) is generically reduced along \(\Gamma\).

Step D: comparison of genera. Let \(\mathcal H\subseteq\mathcal O_{\overline X_0}\) be the subsheaf of sections supported on a finite set of points (a coherent ideal with finite support, of length \(\ell\ge0\)) and \(C\subseteq\overline X_0\) the closed subscheme with \(\mathcal O_C=\mathcal O_{\overline X_0}/\mathcal H\). Then \(C\) has no embedded or isolated points, its support is \(\Gamma\) (irreducible), and by Step C it is reduced at the generic point of \(\Gamma\); a scheme with no embedded points that is reduced at its generic points is reduced, so \(C\) is the reduced integral curve \(\Gamma\), a plane curve contained in \(\mathbf P^2\subseteq\mathbf P^n\). From \(0\to\mathcal H\to\mathcal O_{\overline X_0}\to\mathcal O_\Gamma\to0\): \(\chi(\mathcal O_{\overline X_0}(m))=\chi(\mathcal O_\Gamma(m))+\ell\), i.e.

\[ dm+1-g=P_\Gamma(m)+\ell . \]

So \(P_\Gamma\) has leading coefficient \(d\): \(\Gamma\) is a plane curve of degree \(d\) (a plane curve of degree \(e\) has leading coefficient \(e\), by the computation for \(n=2\) above), and \(P_\Gamma(0)=1-g-\ell\). But \(P_\Gamma(0)=1-\tfrac12(d-1)(d-2)\) by the plane case. Therefore

\[ g=\tfrac12(d-1)(d-2)-\ell\ \le\ \tfrac12(d-1)(d-2). \]

(The number \(\ell\) measures the embedded points of the flat limit, as in (9.8.4) and Ex. 9.2.) Together with the lower bound, \(0\le p_a(Y)\le\tfrac12(d-1)(d-2)\).∎

10. Smooth morphisms

Conventions. As in the text, all schemes are of finite type over a field \(k\). The definition of “smooth of relative dimension \(n\)” makes sense verbatim for a scheme of finite type over any field \(K\) (flatness is automatic; condition (2) says every irreducible component has dimension \(n\); condition (3) says \(\dim_{k(x)}\Omega_{X/K}\otimes k(x)=n\) for all points \(x\)), and it is used in this form for fibres \(X_y\) over \(K=k(y)\). For a morphism \(f:X\to Y\) and \(x\in X\), \(y=f(x)\), base change (II, 8.10) gives \(\Omega_{X_y/k(y)}=\Omega_{X/Y}|_{X_y}\), hence

\[ \Omega_{X/Y}\otimes k(x)=\Omega_{X_y/k(y)}\otimes k(x). \]

The sheaf \(\Omega_{X/Y}\) is coherent, so by Nakayama \(\Omega_{X/Y}=0\) if and only if \(\Omega_{X/Y}\otimes k(x)=0\) for all \(x\), and for each \(r\) the set \(\{x:\dim_{k(x)}\Omega_{X/Y}\otimes k(x)\ge r\}\) is closed (II, Ex. 5.8).

Exercise 10.1

Problem

Over a nonperfect field, smooth and regular are not equivalent. For example, let \(k_0\) be a field of characteristic \(p>0\), \(k=k_0(t)\), and let \(X\subseteq\mathbf A^2_k\) be the curve defined by \(y^2=x^p-t\). Show that every local ring of \(X\) is a regular local ring, but \(X\) is not smooth over \(k\).

Solution

Regularity. Put \(f=y^2-x^p+t\) and \(S=k_0[t]\setminus\{0\}\). Since \(k[x,y]=S^{-1}k_0[t][x,y]\) and localization commutes with quotients,

\[ \mathcal O(X)=k[x,y]/(f)=S^{-1}\bigl(k_0[x,y,t]/(t-(x^p-y^2))\bigr). \]

The ring \(k_0[x,y,t]/(t-x^p+y^2)\) is isomorphic to \(k_0[x,y]\) by \(t\mapsto x^p-y^2\). Under this isomorphism \(S\) goes to the multiplicative set \(S'=\{g(x^p-y^2): 0\ne g\in k_0[T]\}\), which does not contain \(0\) because \(x^p-y^2\) is transcendental over \(k_0\). Hence

\[ \mathcal O(X)\cong S'^{-1}k_0[x,y] \]

is a localization of the polynomial ring \(k_0[x,y]\). So \(X\) is integral and each local ring \(\mathcal O_{X,x}\) is a localization of \(k_0[x,y]\) at a prime ideal, hence a regular local ring (a polynomial ring over a field is regular at its maximal ideals, and a localization of a regular local ring at a prime ideal is regular by Serre's theorem, Matsumura, Thm. 19.3). The function field of \(X\) is \(k_0(x,y)\), of transcendence degree \(2-1=1\) over \(k=k_0(t)\) (as \(t=x^p-y^2\) there), so \(\dim X=1\).

Non-smoothness. Let \(A=\mathcal O(X)\). By (II, 8.4A) we have the exact sequence \((f)/(f^2)\to\Omega_{k[x,y]/k}\otimes A\to\Omega_{A/k}\to0\) with \(\Omega_{k[x,y]/k}=k[x,y]\,dx\oplus k[x,y]\,dy\). Since \(k\)-derivations kill \(t\in k\) and \(p=0\),

\[ df=2y\,dy-px^{p-1}dx+dt=2y\,dy,\qquad\text{so}\qquad \Omega_{X/k}\cong A\,dx\oplus (A/2yA)\,dy . \]

The polynomial \(x^p-t\) is irreducible over \(k\) (\(t\) is not a \(p\)-th power in \(k_0(t)\): if \(th^p=g^p\) then comparing \(t\)-adic valuations gives \(1\equiv0\pmod p\)). Hence \(\mathfrak m=(y,x^p-t)\) is a maximal ideal of \(k[x,y]\), and \(f\in\mathfrak m\), so \(\mathfrak m\) gives a closed point \(x_0\in X\) with \(y(x_0)=0\). Then \(2y\,dy\otimes k(x_0)=0\), whence

\[ \dim_{k(x_0)}\Omega_{X/k}\otimes k(x_0)=2 . \]

If \(X\) were smooth of relative dimension \(n\) over \(k\), condition (2) (with \(Y=\Spec k\), \(X\) integral) would force \(n=\dim X=1\), and condition (3) would force this dimension to be \(1\) at every point. It is \(2\) at \(x_0\), so \(X\) is not smooth over \(k\). (For \(p=2\) the dimension is \(2\) at every point.) ∎

Exercise 10.2

Problem

Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a proper, flat morphism of varieties over \(k\). Suppose for some point \(y\in Y\) that the fibre \(X_y\) is smooth over \(k(y)\). Then show that there is an open neighborhood \(U\) of \(y\) in \(Y\) such that \(f:f^{-1}U\to U\) is smooth.

Solution

Here varieties are integral, separated, of finite type over an algebraically closed field \(k\), and “\(X_y\) smooth over \(k(y)\)” means smooth of relative dimension \(n\) over \(k(y)\) for some \(n\) (see the conventions).

Lemma A. Let \(Z\) be a scheme of finite type over a field \(K\) and \(z\in Z\). Then \(\dim_{k(z)}\Omega_{Z/K}\otimes k(z)\ge \dim_zZ\), where \(\dim_zZ\) is the maximum of the dimensions of the irreducible components of \(Z\) through \(z\).

Proof. Let \(Z'\) be such a component with its reduced structure; it is integral of finite type over \(K\) and \(\dim Z'=\operatorname{tr.deg}_KK(Z')\). By (II, 8.6A), the coherent sheaf \(\Omega_{Z'/K}\) has rank \(\dim_{K(Z')}\Omega_{K(Z')/K}\ge\dim Z'\), so it needs at least \(\dim Z'\) generators at every point (its stalk at \(z\) surjects onto its generic stalk after tensoring). By (II, 8.12) \(\Omega_{Z/K}\otimes k(z)\to\Omega_{Z'/K}\otimes k(z)\to0\) is surjective. \(\square\)

If \(X_y=\emptyset\), then \(U=Y\setminus f(X)\) works, because \(f\) is proper, so \(f(X)\) is closed; \(f^{-1}U=\emptyset\). So assume \(X_y\ne\emptyset\).

Step 1: all nonempty fibres are equidimensional of dimension \(e:=\dim X-\dim Y\), and \(n=e\). Let \(y'\in Y\) and let \(x\) be a closed point of the fibre \(X_{y'}\); thus \(k(x)\) is finite over \(k(y')\). By (9.5), \(\dim\mathcal O_{X_{y'},x}=\dim\mathcal O_{X,x}-\dim\mathcal O_{Y,y'}\). By (II, Ex. 3.20), \(\dim\mathcal O_{X,x}=\dim X-\dim\overline{\{x\}}\) and \(\dim\mathcal O_{Y,y'}=\dim Y-\dim\overline{\{y'\}}\). Since \(k(x)/k(y')\) is finite, \(\dim\overline{\{x\}}=\operatorname{tr.deg}_kk(x)=\operatorname{tr.deg}_kk(y')=\dim\overline{\{y'\}}\). Hence \(\dim\mathcal O_{X_{y'},x}=e\) for every closed point of every nonempty fibre. If \(Z\) is an irreducible component of \(X_{y'}\), choose a closed point \(x\in Z\) lying on no other component; then \(\dim Z=\dim\mathcal O_{Z,x}=\dim\mathcal O_{X_{y'},x}=e\). So all irreducible components of all fibres have dimension \(e\). Applied to the fibre \(X_y\), which is smooth of relative dimension \(n\), this gives \(n=e\).

Step 2: shrinking. Let \(W=\{x\in X:\dim_{k(x)}\Omega_{X/Y}\otimes k(x)\ge n+1\}\), a closed subset of \(X\). For \(x\in X_y\) we have \(\Omega_{X/Y}\otimes k(x)=\Omega_{X_y/k(y)}\otimes k(x)\) of dimension \(n\) (condition (3) for \(X_y\)), so \(W\cap X_y=\emptyset\), i.e. \(y\notin f(W)\). As \(f\) is proper, \(f(W)\) is closed. Put \(U=Y\setminus f(W)\), an open neighborhood of \(y\), and \(X_U=f^{-1}U\), a nonempty open subset of \(X\).

Step 3: \(X_U\to U\) is smooth of relative dimension \(n\). (1) \(f\) is flat, so is its restriction. (2) \(X_U\) and \(U\) are integral, hence are their own unique irreducible components, and \(\dim X_U=\dim X=\dim Y+n=\dim U+n\) (nonempty opens of varieties have the full dimension). (3) Let \(x\in X_U\), \(y'=f(x)\). Since \(x\notin W\), \(\dim\Omega_{X/Y}\otimes k(x)\le n\). On the other hand, with \(Z=X_{y'}\), Lemma A and Step 1 give \(\dim_{k(x)}\Omega_{X/Y}\otimes k(x)=\dim\Omega_{Z/k(y')}\otimes k(x)\ge\dim_xZ=e=n\). So the dimension equals \(n\) at every point. ∎

Exercise 10.3

Problem

A morphism \(f:X\to Y\) of schemes of finite type over \(k\) is etale if it is smooth of relative dimension \(0\). It is unramified if for every \(x\in X\), letting \(y=f(x)\), we have \(\fm_y\cdot\mathcal O_x=\fm_x\), and \(k(x)\) is a separable algebraic extension of \(k(y)\). Show that the following conditions are equivalent:

  1. (i)

    \(f\) is etale;

  2. (ii)

    \(f\) is flat, and \(\Omega_{X/Y}=0\);

  3. (iii)

    \(f\) is flat and unramified.

Solution

Fix \(x\in X\), \(y=f(x)\), \(K=k(y)\), \(L=k(x)\), and let \(B_0=\mathcal O_x/\fm_y\mathcal O_x=\mathcal O_{X_y,x}\), a local \(K\)-algebra with residue field \(L\) and maximal ideal \(\fm_0\). Note that \(L\) is a finitely generated field extension of \(k\), hence of \(K\).

Claim. \((\Omega_{X/Y})_x=0\) if and only if \(f\) is unramified at \(x\) (i.e. \(\fm_y\mathcal O_x=\fm_x\) and \(L/K\) is separable algebraic).

(\(\Leftarrow\)) If \(\fm_y\mathcal O_x=\fm_x\) then \(B_0=L\). As \(L/K\) is finite separable, \(\Omega_{L/K}=0\) by (II, 8.6A) (it is separably generated of transcendence degree \(0\)). Using the base change (II, 8.10), \((\Omega_{X/Y})_x\otimes k(x)=\Omega_{B_0/K}\otimes L=\Omega_{L/K}=0\), so \((\Omega_{X/Y})_x=0\) by Nakayama.

(\(\Rightarrow\)) If \((\Omega_{X/Y})_x=0\) then \(\Omega_{B_0/K}=0\) (base change). By (II, 8.12) applied to \(B_0\to L\) the sequence \(\fm_0/\fm_0^2\to\Omega_{B_0/K}\otimes L\to\Omega_{L/K}\to0\) is exact, so \(\Omega_{L/K}=0\). By (II, 8.6A), \(L\) is separably generated over \(K\) of transcendence degree \(\le\dim\Omega_{L/K}=0\); so \(L/K\) is finite and separable. Now let \(B'=B_0/\fm_0^2\), an artinian local \(K\)-algebra with residue field \(L\). By (II, 8.25A) (or Hensel's lemma, as \(B'\) is complete and \(L/K\) is finite separable), \(B'\) contains a field of representatives \(L'\supseteq K\). We have \(\Omega_{B'/K}\otimes L\) a quotient of \(\Omega_{B_0/K}\otimes L=0\) (II, 8.12), and \(\Omega_{B'/K}\to\Omega_{B'/L'}\) is surjective (II, 8.3A for \(K\to L'\to B'\)), so \(\Omega_{B'/L'}=0\). By (II, 8.7), \(\fm'/\fm'^2\cong\Omega_{B'/L'}\otimes L=0\) where \(\fm'=\fm_0/\fm_0^2\); since \(\fm'^2=0\) this gives \(\fm'=0\), i.e. \(\fm_0=\fm_0^2\), so \(\fm_0=0\) by Nakayama. Thus \(B_0=L\), i.e. \(\fm_y\mathcal O_x=\fm_x\). \(\square\)

(ii)\(\Leftrightarrow\)(iii): immediate from the Claim, applied at every point \(x\) (and \(\Omega_{X/Y}=0\) iff all stalks vanish).

(i)\(\Rightarrow\)(ii): \(f\) is flat by definition, and condition (3) with \(n=0\) says \(\Omega_{X/Y}\otimes k(x)=0\) for all \(x\); since \(\Omega_{X/Y}\) is coherent, Nakayama gives \(\Omega_{X/Y}=0\).

(ii)\(\Rightarrow\)(i): \(f\) is flat and, by (ii), condition (3) holds with \(n=0\). It remains to check condition (2): let \(X'\subseteq X\) and \(Y'\subseteq Y\) be irreducible components with \(f(X')\subseteq Y'\); we must show \(\dim X'=\dim Y'\). Let \(\xi'\), \(\eta'\) be their generic points. Since \(f(\xi')\in Y'\), \(\eta'\) is a generization of \(f(\xi')\); by flatness generizations lift (going down, Matsumura, Thm. 9.5), so there is a generization \(x_1\) of \(\xi'\) with \(f(x_1)=\eta'\). But \(\xi'\) is a generic point of an irreducible component, so \(x_1=\xi'\) and \(f(\xi')=\eta'\). By the Claim, \(f\) is unramified at \(\xi'\), so \(k(\xi')\) is finite over \(k(\eta')\). Therefore \(\operatorname{tr.deg}_kk(\xi')=\operatorname{tr.deg}_kk(\eta')\), i.e. \(\dim X'=\dim Y'\) (I, 1.8A, for the integral schemes \(X'_{\mathrm{red}}\), \(Y'_{\mathrm{red}}\)). Hence \(f\) is smooth of relative dimension \(0\). ∎

Exercise 10.4

Problem

Show that a morphism \(f:X\to Y\) of schemes of finite type over \(k\) is etale if and only if the following condition is satisfied: for each \(x\in X\), let \(y=f(x)\). Let \(\hat{\mathcal O}_x\) and \(\hat{\mathcal O}_y\) be the completions of the local rings at \(x\) and \(y\). Choose fields of representatives (II, 8.25A) \(k(x)\subseteq\hat{\mathcal O}_x\) and \(k(y)\subseteq\hat{\mathcal O}_y\), so that \(k(y)\subseteq k(x)\) via the natural map \(\hat{\mathcal O}_y\to\hat{\mathcal O}_x\). Then our condition is that for every \(x\in X\), \(k(x)\) is a separable algebraic extension of \(k(y)\), and the natural map

\[ \hat{\mathcal O}_y\otimes_{k(y)}k(x)\to\hat{\mathcal O}_x \]

is an isomorphism.

Solution

Remarks on the statement. (a) If \(k(x)/k(y)\) is algebraic then it is finite, since \(k(x)\) is finitely generated over \(k\), hence over \(k(y)\). (b) Existence of compatible fields of representatives: \(\hat{\mathcal O}_y\) contains a field, so by (II, 8.25A) applied with the prime field (over which every extension is separably generated) it has a field of representatives \(K\cong k(y)\). Its image in \(\hat{\mathcal O}_x\) is a subfield \(K'\) mapping onto the copy of \(k(y)\) in \(k(x)\). If \(k(x)/k(y)\) is finite separable, (II, 8.25A) applied to \(\hat{\mathcal O}_x\) and the subfield \(K'\) gives a field of representatives \(L\supseteq K'\) of \(\hat{\mathcal O}_x\). So the choices described exist whenever \(k(x)/k(y)\) is separable algebraic, and below any such compatible choice may be used. Then \(\hat{\mathcal O}_y\otimes_{k(y)}k(x)\) is a finite free \(\hat{\mathcal O}_y\)-module (call it \(T\)), hence a complete local ring with maximal ideal \(\fm_yT\) (since \(T/\fm_yT=k(x)\)) and residue field \(k(x)\).

Lemma B. Let \(A\to B\) be a local homomorphism of noetherian local rings. Then \(B\) is flat over \(A\) if and only if \(\hat B\) is flat over \(\hat A\).

Proof. Let \(\kappa=A/\fm_A\). By the local criterion of flatness (Matsumura, Thm. 22.3; \(B\) is ideal-separated for \(\fm_A\) since \(\fm_AB\subseteq\fm_B\) and Krull's theorem applies to the finitely generated \(B\)-modules \(B\otimes_AJ\)), \(B\) is flat over \(A\) iff \(\Tor_1^A(\kappa,B)=0\); likewise \(\hat B\) is flat over \(\hat A\) iff \(\Tor_1^{\hat A}(\kappa,\hat B)=0\). Since \(\hat A\) is flat over \(A\) with \(\hat A\otimes_A\kappa=\kappa\), \(\Tor_1^{\hat A}(\kappa,\hat B)=\Tor_1^A(\kappa,\hat B)\), and since \(\hat B\) is flat over \(B\), this equals \(\Tor_1^A(\kappa,B)\otimes_B\hat B\). As \(\hat B\) is faithfully flat over \(B\), the latter vanishes iff \(\Tor_1^A(\kappa,B)=0\). \(\square\)

Now fix \(x\), \(y=f(x)\), \(A=\mathcal O_y\), \(B=\mathcal O_x\), \(K=k(y)\), \(L=k(x)\). By Ex. 10.3, \(f\) is etale iff \(f\) is flat and unramified.

(\(\Rightarrow\)) Let \(f\) be etale. Then \(f\) is unramified, so \(L/K\) is separable algebraic (finite) and \(\fm_AB=\fm_B\). Choose compatible fields of representatives \(K\subseteq\hat A\), \(L\subseteq\hat B\), and let \(T=\hat A\otimes_KL\), with the natural map \(\varphi:T\to\hat B\) (an \(\hat A\)-algebra map and \(L\)-linear). We have \(\fm_{\hat A}\hat B=\fm_A\hat B=\fm_B\hat B=\fm_{\hat B}\).

\(\varphi\) is surjective. \(T\) is complete for the topology defined by its maximal ideal \(\fn=\fm_AT\), and \(\varphi(\fn)\hat B=\fm_{\hat B}\). Consider \(\hat B\) as a \(T\)-module via \(\varphi\): it is separated for the \(\fn\)-adic topology (as that is the \(\fm_{\hat B}\)-adic topology), and \(\hat B/\fn\hat B=\hat B/\fm_{\hat B}=L\) is generated by the image of \(1\) (indeed \(L\subseteq T\) maps isomorphically onto \(\hat B/\fm_{\hat B}\)). By the complete Nakayama lemma (Matsumura, Thm. 8.4), \(\hat B=T\cdot1=\varphi(T)\).

\(\varphi\) is injective. \(\hat B\) is flat over \(\hat A\) (Lemma B, \(f\) being flat), and \(T\) is free over \(\hat A\); \(\hat B\) is a finitely generated \(\hat A\)-module since it is a quotient of \(T\). Let \(N=\Ker\varphi\), finitely generated over \(\hat A\). Tensoring \(0\to N\to T\to\hat B\to0\) with \(\kappa=\hat A/\fm_{\hat A}\), and using \(\Tor_1^{\hat A}(\kappa,\hat B)=0\), gives the exact sequence \(0\to N/\fm_{\hat A}N\to T/\fm_{\hat A}T=L\to\hat B/\fm_{\hat A}\hat B=L\to0\), in which the last map is an isomorphism. Hence \(N/\fm_{\hat A}N=0\) and \(N=0\) by Nakayama.

So \(\varphi\) is an isomorphism.

(\(\Leftarrow\)) Suppose \(L/K\) is separable algebraic and \(\varphi:T=\hat A\otimes_KL\to\hat B\) is an isomorphism, for all \(x\). Then \(\hat B\cong T\) is free over \(\hat A\), so \(B\) is flat over \(A\) by Lemma B. Moreover \(\fm_A\hat B\) corresponds to \(\fm_AT=\fm_T\), the maximal ideal of \(T\), so \(\fm_A\hat B=\fm_{\hat B}=\fm_B\hat B\). By faithful flatness of \(B\to\hat B\) we have \(I\hat B\cap B=I\) for ideals \(I\) of \(B\); applying this to \(I=\fm_AB\) and \(I=\fm_B\) gives \(\fm_AB=\fm_B\). Thus \(f\) is flat at every \(x\) and unramified, i.e. etale by Ex. 10.3. ∎

Exercise 10.5

Problem

If \(x\) is a point of a scheme \(X\), we define an etale neighborhood of \(x\) to be an etale morphism \(f:U\to X\), together with a point \(x'\in U\) such that \(f(x')=x\). As an example of the use of etale neighborhoods, prove the following: if \(\mathcal F\) is a coherent sheaf on \(X\), and if every point of \(X\) has an etale neighborhood \(f:U\to X\) for which \(f^*\mathcal F\) is a free \(\mathcal O_U\)-module, then \(\mathcal F\) is locally free on \(X\).

Solution

Let \(x\in X\) and let \(f:U\to X\), \(x'\mapsto x\), be an etale neighborhood with \(f^*\mathcal F\) free. Put \(A=\mathcal O_{X,x}\), \(B=\mathcal O_{U,x'}\), \(M=\mathcal F_x\), a finitely generated \(A\)-module (\(\mathcal F\) is coherent). Since \(f\) is etale it is flat, so \(A\to B\) is a flat local homomorphism of noetherian local rings, hence faithfully flat (Matsumura, Thm. 7.2). The stalk \((f^*\mathcal F)_{x'}=M\otimes_AB\) is free over \(B\), of finite rank \(r\) since it is finitely generated.

\(M\) is flat over \(A\). Let \(N'\hookrightarrow N\) be an injection of \(A\)-modules. Then \((N'\otimes_AM)\otimes_AB=(N'\otimes_AB)\otimes_B(M\otimes_AB)\to(N\otimes_AB)\otimes_B(M\otimes_AB)\) is injective, since \(N'\otimes_AB\to N\otimes_AB\) is injective (\(B\) flat over \(A\)) and \(M\otimes_AB\) is free over \(B\). By faithful flatness, \(N'\otimes_AM\to N\otimes_AM\) is injective.

A finitely generated flat module over a noetherian local ring is free (Matsumura, Thm. 7.10), so \(M=\mathcal F_x\cong A^s\). Take sections \(s_1,\dots,s_s\) of \(\mathcal F\) over a neighbourhood \(V\) of \(x\) whose germs generate \(\mathcal F_x\) and (shrinking \(V\), using coherence) such that \(\mathcal O_V^s\to\mathcal F|_V\) is surjective. Its kernel \(\mathcal K\) is coherent with \(\mathcal K_x=\Ker(A^s\to A^s)=0\) (the map \(A^s\to M\cong A^s\) is a surjective endomorphism of a finitely generated module, hence an isomorphism). Since \(\operatorname{Supp}\mathcal K\) is closed and misses \(x\), after shrinking \(V\) we get \(\mathcal F|_V\cong\mathcal O_V^s\). So \(\mathcal F\) is locally free. ∎

Exercise 10.6

Problem

Let \(Y\) be the plane nodal cubic curve \(y^2=x^2(x+1)\). Show that \(Y\) has a finite etale covering \(X\) of degree \(2\), where \(X\) is a union of two irreducible components, each one isomorphic to the normalization of \(Y\) (Fig. 12).

Solution

We assume \(\operatorname{char}k\ne2\) (as is implicit in the picture: in characteristic \(2\) the singularity \(y^2=x^2(x+1)\) is a cusp). \(k\) need not be algebraically closed.

The curve \(Y\) and its normalization. Let \(A=k[x,y]/(y^2-x^2(x+1))\), a domain (since \(x^2(x+1)\) is not a square in \(k(x)\)). Define \(A\to k[t]\) by \(x\mapsto u:=t^2-1\), \(y\mapsto v:=t^3-t=tu\). This is injective (\(A\) is a domain of dimension \(1\) and the image contains the non-constant \(u\)). Its image is

\[ k[u,v]=\{g\in k[t]:g(1)=g(-1)\}=k+(t^2-1)k[t]. \]

Indeed “\(\subseteq\)” is clear; conversely, if \(g(1)=g(-1)\) then \(g-g(1)\) vanishes at \(\pm1\) (which are distinct as \(\operatorname{char}k\ne2\)) so lies in \((t^2-1)k[t]\), and writing \(h=h_e(t^2)+t\,h_o(t^2)\) we get \((t^2-1)h=(u)h_e(u+1)+v\,h_o(u+1)\in k[u,v]\). We henceforth identify \(A\) with this subring of \(k[t]\). As \(k[t]\) is integrally closed, with the same fraction field \(k(t)=k(x,y)\) (\(t=y/x\)) and integral over \(A\) (\(t^2=x+1\)), \(\mathbf A^1=\Spec k[t]\to Y\) is the normalization.

The covering. Let

\[ R=\{(g_1,g_2)\in k[t]\times k[t]:\ g_1(1)=g_2(-1),\ g_1(-1)=g_2(1)\},\qquad X=\Spec R, \]

i.e. two copies of \(\mathbf A^1\) glued crosswise at \(t=\pm1\) (two ordinary double points). Map \(A\to R\) by \(g\mapsto(g,g)\); this lands in \(R\) since \(g(1)=g(-1)\) for \(g\in A\). Let \(\pi:X\to Y\) be the corresponding morphism.

Components. The projections \(p_i:R\to k[t]\) are surjective (for given \(g_1\) choose \(g_2\) with the prescribed values at \(\pm1\)). Put \(I_1=\Ker p_1=\{(0,g_2)\}\) and \(I_2=\Ker p_2\). Then \(I_1\cap I_2=0\) and \(I_1I_2=0\), so \(X=V(I_1)\cup V(I_2)\) with \(V(I_i)\cong\Spec k[t]=\mathbf A^1\), and \(R\) is reduced; hence \(X\) has exactly two irreducible components, each isomorphic to the normalization of \(Y\), and \(\pi\) restricts to the normalization map on each.

Finiteness. \(k[t]^2\) is a finite module over \(k[x]\) (\(t^2=x+1\)), so \(R\) (a \(k[x]\)-submodule) is finite over \(k[x]\) and hence over \(A\).

Etaleness. The elements \(x=u\) and \(x+1=t^2\) of \(A\) generate the unit ideal (\((x+1)-x=1\)), so \(Y=D(x)\cup D(x+1)\); since by Ex. 10.3 “etale” means “flat and \(\Omega_{X/Y}=0\)”, which are conditions local on \(Y\), it suffices to show that \(\pi^{-1}D(x)\to D(x)\) and \(\pi^{-1}D(x+1)\to D(x+1)\) are etale.

Over \(D(x)\). Inverting \(u=t^2-1\): the conditions at \(t=\pm1\) disappear. Indeed \(R_x\subseteq k[t]_u\times k[t]_u\), and any \((h_1,h_2)\) in the right side satisfies \((u^Nh_1,u^Nh_2)\in k[t]^2\) with all values at \(\pm1\) equal to \(0\) (for \(N\ge1\) large), so is in \(R\), giving \((h_1,h_2)=(u^Nh_1,u^Nh_2)/(u,u)^N\in R_x\). In the same way \(A_x=k[t]_u\). So \(R_x=A_x\times A_x\) and \(\pi^{-1}D(x)=D(x)\sqcup D(x)\to D(x)\) is the trivial double cover, which is etale (an open immersion is smooth of relative dimension \(0\) by Prop. 10.1(a)).

Over \(D(x+1)\). Put \(A'=A_{x+1}\), \(R'=R_{x+1}\). As above (inverting \(t^2\), which equals \(1\) at \(t=\pm1\)) we get

\[ A'=\{h\in k[t,t^{-1}]:h(1)=h(-1)\},\qquad R'=\{(g_1,g_2)\in k[t,t^{-1}]^2:\ g_1(1)=g_2(-1),\ g_1(-1)=g_2(1)\}. \]

Let \(w=(t,-t)\in R'\) (it lies in \(R\): \(g_1(1)=1=g_2(-1)\), \(g_1(-1)=-1=g_2(1)\)); then \(w^2=(t^2,t^2)=x+1\). Claim: \(R'=A'\oplus A'w\) (free with basis \(1,w\)). Given \((g_1,g_2)\in R'\) put \(a=\tfrac12(g_1+g_2)\), \(b=\tfrac1{2t}(g_1-g_2)\) in \(k[t,t^{-1}]\); then \((g_1,g_2)=a\cdot1+b\cdot w\). We check \(a,b\in A'\):

\[ a(1)=\tfrac12(g_1(1)+g_2(1))=\tfrac12(g_2(-1)+g_1(-1))=a(-1), \]
\[ b(-1)=\tfrac1{-2}(g_1(-1)-g_2(-1))=\tfrac1{-2}(g_2(1)-g_1(1))=\tfrac12(g_1(1)-g_2(1))=b(1). \]

The sum is direct: \(a+bw=(a+bt,a-bt)=0\) forces \(a=b=0\). Therefore \(R'\cong A'[W]/(W^2-(x+1))\) (a surjection between free \(A'\)-modules of rank \(2\)), which is free, hence flat, over \(A'\), and \(\Omega_{R'/A'}\) is generated by \(dW\) with \(2W\,dW=0\); since \(W^2=x+1\) is a unit in \(A'\), \(W\) and \(2\) are units, so \(\Omega_{R'/A'}=0\). By Ex. 10.3 ((ii)\(\Rightarrow\)(i)), \(\Spec R'\to\Spec A'\) is etale.

Thus \(\pi\) is finite and etale; it is locally free of rank \(2\) (trivial over \(D(x)\), free over \(D(x+1)\)), so it is a finite etale covering of degree \(2\) with the required properties. ∎

Exercise 10.7

Problem

(Serre). A linear system with moving singularities. Let \(k\) be an algebraically closed field of characteristic \(2\). Let \(P_1,\dots,P_7\in\mathbf P^2_k\) be the seven points of the projective plane over the prime field \(\mathbb F_2\subseteq k\). Let \(\mathfrak d\) be the linear system of all cubic curves in \(X\) passing through \(P_1,\dots,P_7\).

  1. (a)

    \(\mathfrak d\) is a linear system of dimension \(2\) with base points \(P_1,\dots,P_7\), which determines an inseparable morphism of degree \(2\) from \(X-\{P_i\}\) to \(\mathbf P^2\).

  2. (b)

    Every curve \(C\in\mathfrak d\) is singular. More precisely, either \(C\) consists of \(3\) lines all passing through one of the \(P_i\), or \(C\) is an irreducible cuspidal cubic with cusp \(P\ne\) any \(P_i\). Furthermore, the correspondence \(C\mapsto\) the singular point of \(C\) is a \(1\)-\(1\) correspondence between \(\mathfrak d\) and \(\mathbf P^2\). Thus the singular points of elements of \(\mathfrak d\) move all over.

Solution

Here \(X=\mathbf P^2_k\) with homogeneous coordinates \(x,y,z\) (an evident misprint: the curves lie in \(\mathbf P^2\)). Put

\[ F_1=x^2y+xy^2,\quad F_2=y^2z+yz^2,\quad F_3=z^2x+zx^2 . \]

(a) The system. A cubic form \(G=\sum c_m m\) over \(k\) (sum over the \(10\) monomials of degree \(3\)) vanishes at the seven points of \(\mathbf P^2(\mathbb F_2)\) iff the function \(\mathbb F_2^3\setminus0\to k\) it defines is zero. As functions on \(\mathbb F_2^3\), \(x^3,y^3,z^3,xyz\) are \(x,y,z,xyz\), the two monomials \(x^2y,xy^2\) both give \(xy\), the monomials \(y^2z,yz^2\) both give \(yz\), and \(z^2x,zx^2\) both give \(zx\). The functions \(x,y,z,xy,yz,zx,xyz\) are linearly independent over \(k\) on \(\mathbb F_2^3\setminus0\): the eight multilinear monomials \(1,x,\dots,xyz\) form a basis of all \(k\)-valued functions on \(\mathbb F_2^3\), and the only linear combinations vanishing off the origin are multiples of \((1+x)(1+y)(1+z)=1+x+y+z+xy+yz+zx+xyz\), which has a nonzero constant term. Consequently the evaluation map from cubic forms to \(k^7\) is onto, with kernel of dimension \(10-7=3\), namely the span of \(x^2y+xy^2,\ y^2z+yz^2,\ z^2x+zx^2\). So \(\mathfrak d=\{aF_1+bF_2+cF_3\}\cong\mathbf P^2\), a linear system of dimension \(2\).

Base points. \(F_1=xy(x+y)\), \(F_2=yz(y+z)\), \(F_3=zx(z+x)\). If \(x=0\) then \(F_1=F_3=0\) and \(F_2=0\) gives \(y=0\), \(z=0\) or \(y=z\): the points \((0:0:1),(0:1:0),(0:1:1)\). If \(y=0\) (and \(x\ne0\)), \(F_1=F_2=0\) and \(F_3=0\) gives \(z=0\) or \(z=x\): \((1:0:0),(1:0:1)\). If \(xy\ne0\) and \(x=y\), then \(F_2=0\) gives \(z=0\) or \(z=y\): \((1:1:0),(1:1:1)\) (and then \(F_3=0\) holds). So the base locus is exactly \(\{P_1,\dots,P_7\}\).

The morphism. On \(X-\{P_i\}\) the three cubics have no common zero, so \(\varphi=(F_1:F_2:F_3):X-\{P_i\}\to\mathbf P^2\) is a morphism. Use \(z=1\) on the source and the affine coordinates \(U=F_1/F_3\), \(V=F_2/F_3\) on the target; with \(K=k(x,y)\), \(L=k(U,V)\subseteq K\):

\[ U=\frac{x^2y+xy^2}{x^2+x},\qquad V=\frac{y^2+y}{x^2+x}. \]

Compute \(Vx+U=\dfrac{(y^2+y)x+x^2y+xy^2}{x^2+x}=\dfrac{xy(1+x)}{x(x+1)}=y\). Squaring, \(y^2=V^2x^2+U^2\); inserting this into \(y^2+y=V(x^2+x)\) gives \(V^2x^2+U^2+Vx+U=Vx^2+Vx\), i.e.

\[ x^2=\frac{U^2+U}{V^2+V}\in L,\qquad y=Vx+U\in L(x). \]

Hence \(K=L(x)\) with \(x^2\in L\): \(K/L\) is purely inseparable of degree \(1\) or \(2\). It is not of degree \(1\): in characteristic \(2\) one computes \(d(x^2y+xy^2)=x^2dy+y^2dx\) and \(d(x^2+x)=dx\), giving

\[ dU=x^2\,\frac{(x^2+x)\,dy+(y^2+y)\,dx}{(x^2+x)^2}=x^2\,dV . \]

If \(L=K\), then \(\Omega_{K/k}\) would be generated by \(dU,dV\), hence by \(dV\), a space of dimension \(\le1\); but \(\Omega_{K/k}=K\,dx\oplus K\,dy\) has dimension \(2\) (II, 8.6A). So \([K:L]=2\): \(\varphi\) is dominant of degree \(2\) and the extension of function fields is purely inseparable, i.e. \(\varphi\) is inseparable of degree \(2\).

(b) Singular points. Let \(G=aF_1+bF_2+cF_3\), \((a,b,c)\ne0\), \(C=V(G)\). Since \(\operatorname{char}k=2\),

\[ G_x=ay^2+cz^2,\qquad G_y=ax^2+bz^2,\qquad G_z=by^2+cx^2, \]

and Euler's relation gives \(xG_x+yG_y+zG_z=3G=G\). So \(G\) lies in the ideal of its partials and, by the Jacobian criterion, the singular points of \(C\) are the common zeros of \(G_x,G_y,G_z\). These are linear equations in \((x^2,y^2,z^2)\) with matrix \(\begin{pmatrix}0&a&c\\a&0&b\\c&b&0\end{pmatrix}\); its \(2\times2\) minors include \(a^2,b^2,c^2\) (up to sign), so the rank is \(\ge2\), while \((x^2,y^2,z^2)=(b,c,a)\) is a solution. So the solution space is \(1\)-dimensional and

\[ C\text{ has exactly one singular point},\qquad S(C)=(\sqrt b:\sqrt c:\sqrt a). \]

In particular \(G\) is squarefree (a multiple component would give infinitely many singular points), so \(C\) is a reduced cubic, singular. As \(k\) is perfect, \((a:b:c)\mapsto(\sqrt b:\sqrt c:\sqrt a)\) is a bijection \(\mathfrak d\cong\mathbf P^2\to\mathbf P^2\). This proves the last assertion: \(C\mapsto S(C)\) is a bijection between \(\mathfrak d\) and \(\mathbf P^2\).

The shape of \(C\). Write \(S=(s_1:s_2:s_3)=S(C)\), so \((a,b,c)=(s_3^2,s_1^2,s_2^2)\). Note: \(g\in GL_3(\mathbb F_2)\) permutes the \(P_i\), so \(G\mapsto G\circ g\) preserves \(\mathfrak d\) and carries the singular point \(S\) to \(g^{-1}S\); the group acts transitively on the seven points and on the seven \(\mathbb F_2\)-lines of \(\mathbf P^2\).

(i) \(S\) is one of the \(P_i\). By transitivity it suffices to take \(S=(0:0:1)\), i.e. \((a,b,c)=(1,0,0)\), \(G=F_1=xy(x+y)\): three lines through \((0:0:1)\). So \(C\) is three lines through a single \(P_i\) (this is the first alternative of the printed statement).

(ii) \(S\notin\mathbf P^2(\mathbb F_2)\), but \(S\) lies on an \(\mathbb F_2\)-rational line. By transitivity take the line \(z=0\): \(s_3=0\), \(s_1s_2(s_1+s_2)\ne0\), \((a,b,c)=(0,s_1^2,s_2^2)=:(0,\beta,\alpha)\) with \(\alpha\beta\ne0\), \(\alpha\ne\beta\). Then \(G=\beta F_2+\alpha F_3=z\,Q\) with \(Q=\alpha x^2+\beta y^2+z(\alpha x+\beta y)\). So \(C=\{z=0\}\cup V(Q)\) is reducible. The partials \(Q_x=\alpha z\), \(Q_y=\beta z\), \(Q_z=\alpha x+\beta y\) vanish only at \((\beta:\alpha:0)\), where \(Q=\alpha\beta(\alpha+\beta)\ne0\); so \(Q\) is a smooth, irreducible conic. On \(z=0\), \(Q=(\sqrt\alpha x+\sqrt\beta y)^2\), so the line is tangent to \(Q\) at \(S\). Thus these curves are a line plus a smooth conic tangent to it.

(iii) \(S\) lies on no \(\mathbb F_2\)-rational line. Then \(C\) is irreducible. Otherwise \(G=\ell\cdot Q'\) with \(\ell\) a line. Then \(\emptyset\ne\ell\cap V(Q')\subseteq\operatorname{Sing}C=\{S\}\), so \(S\in\ell\) and \(\ell\) is not \(\mathbb F_2\)-rational. A non-rational line contains at most one point of \(\mathbf P^2(\mathbb F_2)\) (two such points span a rational line), so the conic \(Q'\) passes through at least six of the seven \(P_i\). Omitting at most one point of the Fano plane leaves a full \(\mathbb F_2\)-line \(L_0\) of three points on \(V(Q')\), whence \(L_0\mid Q'\) and \(L_0\) is a component of \(C\). Applying the same argument to this component gives \(S\in L_0\), a contradiction.

Now we show \(C\) is a cusp at \(S\). Expanding \(G\) at \(S\) monomial by monomial (e.g. \((s_1+v_1)^2(s_2+v_2)=s_1^2s_2+s_1^2v_2+s_2v_1^2+v_1^2v_2\) in characteristic \(2\)), one finds
