\[ G(S+v)=G(S)+\sum G_i(S)v_i+Q_S(v)+G(v), \]
\[ Q_S(v)=s_2s_3(s_2+s_3)v_1^2+s_3s_1(s_3+s_1)v_2^2+s_1s_2(s_1+s_2)v_3^2 . \]

Here \(G(S)=0\) and \(G_i(S)=0\). \(Q_S\) has no cross terms, so it is the square of a linear form \(m\). The coefficients vanish simultaneously iff all ratios \(s_i/s_j\in\{0,1,\infty\}\), i.e. iff \(S\) is rational; so \(m\ne0\) for \(S\) non-rational. Also \(Q_S(S)=s_1s_2s_3\cdot2(\cdots)=0\) so \(m(S)=0\). Choose a complement \(W\) of \(kS\) in \(k^3\). Then \(m|_W\ne0\) (otherwise \(W\subseteq\ker m\) and \(S\in\ker m\) would give \(m=0\)), so we may take coordinates \((u,v)\) on \(W\) with \(u=m|_W\). The points \([S+w]\), \(w\in W\), form an affine plane \(\mathbf A^2\subseteq\mathbf P^2\) with coordinates \((u,v)\) centred at \(S\), and by the expansion above \(C\cap\mathbf A^2\) has the exact equation

\[ u^2+g(u,v)=0,\qquad g=G|_W=\alpha u^3+\beta u^2v+\gamma uv^2+\delta v^3 . \]

If \(\delta=0\) this would factor as \(u\,(u+\alpha u^2+\beta uv+\gamma v^2)\), and \(C\) (the closure of this dense open piece) would be reducible, contrary to (iii). So \(\delta\ne0\). Thus \(S\) is a double point with tangent cone the double line \(u^2=0\), and the tangent line \(u=0\) meets \(C\) at \(S\) with multiplicity \(3\). To see that this is a cusp, project from \(S\): the line \(u=sv\) meets \(C\) where \(s^2v^2+v^3g(s,1)=0\), i.e. at \(S\) (doubly) and at \(v=s^2/g(s,1)\), where \(g(s,1)=\alpha s^3+\beta s^2+\gamma s+\delta\) and \(g(0,1)=\delta\ne0\). So near \(s=0\) the curve is parametrized by \((u,v)=(sv,v)\) with \(v=s^2\cdot(\text{unit})\), i.e. \(v\) has order \(2\) and \(u\) order \(3\) in the local parameter \(s\). Hence \(C\) is unibranch at \(S\) and \(\widehat{\mathcal O}_{C,S}=k[[s^2,s^3]]\) (the normalization is \(\mathbf P^1\), via \(s\)): \(S\) is a cusp. So \(C\) is an irreducible cuspidal cubic with cusp \(S\ne P_i\) (this is the second alternative of the printed statement).

Remark (a correction). Case (ii) really occurs: for example take \(\omega\in\mathbb F_4\setminus\mathbb F_2\) and \(G=z(x^2+\omega y^2+zx+\omega zy)\), which vanishes at all seven \(\mathbb F_2\)-points (checked directly) and lies in \(\mathfrak d\) (\(a=0\)). So the dichotomy printed in the book omits the case of a line plus a tangent smooth conic. Every other assertion in (b) is unaffected: every member is singular, has a unique singular point, and \(C\mapsto S(C)\) is a bijection \(\mathfrak d\to\mathbf P^2\); the cuspidal members correspond exactly to points of \(\mathbf P^2\) not on the seven \(\mathbb F_2\)-lines. ∎

Exercise 10.8

Problem

A linear system with moving singularities contained in the base locus (any characteristic). In affine \(3\)-space with coordinates \(x,y,z\), let \(C\) be the conic \((x-1)^2+y^2=1\) in the \(xy\)-plane, and let \(P\) be the point \((0,0,t)\) on the \(z\)-axis. Let \(Y_t\) be the closure in \(\mathbf P^3\) of the cone over \(C\) with vertex \(P\). Show that as \(t\) varies, the surfaces \(\{Y_t\}\) form a linear system of dimension \(1\), with a moving singularity at \(P\). The base locus of this linear system is the conic \(C\) plus the \(z\)-axis.

Solution

Hypothesis. I assume \(\operatorname{char}k\ne2\), with \(k\) algebraically closed. This is necessary: in characteristic \(2\) the equation \((x-1)^2+y^2=1\) reads \((x+y)^2=0\), a double line, and the computation below degenerates (the second generator \(B=2xz\) vanishes, all the \(Y_t\) coincide). The heading “any characteristic” is to be understood as “any characteristic \(\ne2\)” (including \(0\)).

Use homogeneous coordinates \((x:y:z:w)\) on \(\mathbf P^3\) (affine part \(w=1\)) and set

\[ A=x^2+y^2-2xw,\qquad B=2xz,\qquad F_t=tA+B=t(x^2+y^2)-2txw+2xz . \]

\(Y_t=V(F_t)\) for \(t\ne0\). The points of the line through \(P=(0,0,t)\) and \((q_1,q_2,0)\in C\) are \((sq_1,sq_2,t(1-s))\), and

\[ F_t(sq_1,sq_2,t(1-s),1)=ts^2(q_1^2+q_2^2)-2tsq_1+2sq_1t(1-s)=ts^2(q_1^2+q_2^2-2q_1)=0, \]

since \(q\in C\). So the cone, hence \(Y_t\), lies in \(V(F_t)\). With \(z'=z-tw\) we get \(F_t=t(x^2+y^2)+2xz'\), a quadratic form in \(x,y,z'\) only, with Gram matrix \(\begin{pmatrix}t&0&1\\0&t&0\\1&0&0\end{pmatrix}\) of determinant \(-t\ne0\). So \(V(F_t)\) is an irreducible quadric cone of rank \(3\), with unique singular point \(x=y=z'=0\), i.e. \(P_t=(0:0:t:1)=P\), the vertex. \(Y_t\) is an irreducible closed surface (closure of the image of \(C\times\mathbf A^1\)) contained in the irreducible surface \(V(F_t)\), so \(Y_t=V(F_t)\).

Linear system. Thus \(\{Y_t\}_{t\ne0}\) are members of the pencil \(\Lambda=\{\mu A+\nu B\}\) spanned by the linearly independent quadrics \(A\) and \(B\) (\(\operatorname{char}\ne2\)): a linear system of dimension \(1\). (The remaining members are \(t=\infty\), the cylinder \(A=0\) over \(C\) with vertex \((0:0:1:0)\), and \(t=0\), the reducible quadric \(xz=0\) containing the plane \(z=0\) of \(C\).) Any member has the form \(\mu A+\nu B\), \((\mu:\nu)\in\mathbf P^1\).

Moving singularity. By the above, for each \(t\ne0\) the surface \(Y_t\) is singular exactly at \(P_t=(0:0:t:1)\), and \(P_t\) moves along the \(z\)-axis as \(t\) varies.

Base locus. A point lies on all \(Y_t\) (\(t\ne0\)) iff \(t_1A+B=t_2A+B=0\) for two distinct values, i.e. iff \(A=B=0\). Now \(B=2xz=0\) means \(x=0\) or \(z=0\). If \(z=0\) then \(A=0\) is the conic \(C\) (closure of \(C\) in the plane \(z=0\) of \(\mathbf P^3\)). If \(x=0\) then \(A=y^2=0\), so \(x=y=0\): the \(z\)-axis (the line \(x=y=0\) in \(\mathbf P^3\), including its point at infinity). Hence the base locus is \(C\cup\{z\text{-axis}\}\), as claimed; note that every \(P_t\) lies in it. (Scheme-theoretically the \(z\)-axis occurs with multiplicity \(2\), because \(x=0\) forces \(y^2=0\).) This is consistent with Bertini (Cor. 10.9, characteristic \(0\)): the singularities of the general member can only occur in the base locus. ∎

Exercise 10.9

Problem

Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a morphism of varieties over \(k\). Assume that \(Y\) is regular, \(X\) is Cohen–Macaulay, and that every fibre of \(f\) has dimension equal to \(\dim X-\dim Y\). Then \(f\) is flat. [Hint: Imitate the proof of (10.4), using (II, 8.21A).]

Solution

Put \(n=\dim X-\dim Y\). Flatness means \(\mathcal O_x\) is flat over \(\mathcal O_{f(x)}\) for all \(x\in X\). As in the proof of (10.4), it suffices to treat closed points \(x\in X\): a general point \(x'\) specializes to a closed point \(x\) with \(\mathcal O_{x'}\) a localization of \(\mathcal O_x\) and \(\mathcal O_{f(x')}\) a localization of \(\mathcal O_{f(x)}\), and flatness localizes.

Let \(x\in X\) be closed and \(y=f(x)\), also closed (\(k(x)\) is finite over \(k\), so \(k(y)\subseteq k(x)\) is finite over \(k\)). Let \(A=\mathcal O_{Y,y}\) and \(B=\mathcal O_{X,x}\) with \(r=\dim A=\dim Y\) and \(\dim B=\dim X=r+n\) (closed points of varieties, I, 1.8A / II, Ex. 3.20). \(A\) is a regular local ring, and \(B\) is Cohen–Macaulay by hypothesis. Let \(t_1,\dots,t_r\) be a regular system of parameters of \(A\), so \(\fm_y=(t_1,\dots,t_r)\) and \(A/(t_1,\dots,t_r)=k(y)=k\); they form an \(A\)-regular sequence by (II, 8.21A(a),(c)), since \(\dim A/(t_1,\dots,t_r)=0=\dim A-r\). Denote by the same letters their images in \(\fm_x\subseteq B\).

The \(t_i\) form a \(B\)-regular sequence. \(B/(t_1,\dots,t_r)B=B/\fm_yB=\mathcal O_{X_y,x}\), the local ring of the fibre \(X_y\) at \(x\), whose dimension is \(\le\dim X_y=n\) by hypothesis. By Krull's height theorem \(\dim B/(t_1,\dots,t_r)B\ge\dim B-r=n\). So \(\dim B/(t_1,\dots,t_r)B=\dim B-r\), and since \(B\) is Cohen–Macaulay, (II, 8.21A(c)) says that \(t_1,\dots,t_r\) is a \(B\)-regular sequence.

Flatness by descending induction. For \(1\le i\le r+1\) let \(A_i=A/(t_1,\dots,t_{i-1})\) and \(B_i=B/(t_1,\dots,t_{i-1})B\); so \(A_1=A\), \(B_1=B\), and \(A_i\to B_i\) is a local homomorphism of noetherian local rings. We show by descending induction that \(B_i\) is flat over \(A_i\). For \(i=r+1\): \(A_{r+1}=k(y)\) is a field, so every module over it is flat. Suppose \(B_{i+1}=B_i/t_iB_i\) is flat over \(A_{i+1}=A_i/t_iA_i\). The element \(t_i\in A_i\) is a nonunit and a non-zerodivisor (\(t_1,\dots,t_r\) is \(A\)-regular), and it is a non-zerodivisor on \(B_i\) (\(t_1,\dots,t_r\) is \(B\)-regular). Apply Lemma 10.3.A to \(A_i\to B_i\), \(M=B_i\) (a finitely generated \(B_i\)-module), \(t=t_i\): conditions (1) and (2) hold, so \(B_i\) is flat over \(A_i\). At \(i=1\) we get that \(B=\mathcal O_x\) is flat over \(A=\mathcal O_y\).

Hence \(f\) is flat at all closed points, hence everywhere. ∎

11. The theorem on formal functions

Throughout this section, “Thm. 11.1”, “Cor. 11.2–11.5” refer to the text, “12.8–12.11” to the next section (Semicontinuity, Grauert, cohomology and base change), and “II, Ex. \(\dots\)” etc. to exercises of other chapters. A closed point of a scheme of finite type over an algebraically closed field \(k\) has residue field \(k\). The following standard fact is used repeatedly (and its proof is repeated each time it is needed in a slightly different form): a scheme of finite type over a field is a Jacobson scheme, so a nonempty closed subset contains a closed point, and the closed points are dense in every closed subset.

Exercise 11.1

Problem

Show that the result of (11.2) is false without the projective hypothesis. For example, let \(X=\mathbf A^n_k\), let \(P=(0,\dots,0)\), let \(U=X-P\), and let \(f\colon U\to X\) be the inclusion. Then the fibres of \(f\) all have dimension \(0\), but \(R^{n-1}f_*\mathcal O_U\neq0\).

Solution

We assume \(n\ge2\) (needed: then \(n-1>0=r\); for \(n=1\) the statement \(R^{0}f_*\mathcal O_U\ne0\) is no contradiction to (11.2), which concerns \(R^i\) with \(i>0\)).

Fibres. \(f\) is an open immersion, hence injective on points, and the fibre over \(x\in U\) is \(\Spec k(x)\) (an open immersion is a monomorphism, so \(U\times_X\Spec k(x)=\Spec k(x)\)); the fibre over \(P\) is empty. So every fibre has dimension \(0\) (or is empty), i.e. \(r=0\), and (11.2) would give \(R^if_*\mathcal O_U=0\) for all \(i>0\).

The higher direct image. Write \(X=\Spec S\), \(S=k[x_1,\dots,x_n]\). Then \(U=\bigcup_{i=1}^nU_i\) with \(U_i=D(x_i)\). Since \(X\) is affine, \(R^if_*\mathcal O_U=\widetilde{H^i(U,\mathcal O_U)}\) (Prop. 8.5). The scheme \(U\) is separated and the open sets \(U_{i_0\cdots i_p}=D(x_{i_0}\cdots x_{i_p})\) are affine, so \(H^i(U,\mathcal O_U)\) is computed by the Cech complex of the covering \(\mathfrak U=\{U_1,\dots,U_n\}\) (Thm. 4.5). This complex has \(C^p=\bigoplus_{i_0<\dots<i_p}S_{x_{i_0}\cdots x_{i_p}}\) for \(0\le p\le n-1\). In the top degree \(p=n-1\) there is the single term \(S_{x_1\cdots x_n}\), and \(d\colon C^{n-2}\to C^{n-1}\) maps the summand \(S_{x_1\cdots\widehat{x_i}\cdots x_n}\) into it by \(\pm\) the inclusion. Hence

\[ H^{n-1}(U,\mathcal O_U)=S_{x_1\cdots x_n}\Big/\sum_{i=1}^nS_{x_1\cdots\widehat{x_i}\cdots x_n}. \]

The ring \(S_{x_1\cdots x_n}\) has the \(k\)-basis of Laurent monomials \(x^a=x_1^{a_1}\cdots x_n^{a_n}\), \(a\in\Z^n\), and the subring \(S_{x_1\cdots\widehat{x_i}\cdots x_n}\) is spanned by those with \(a_i\ge0\). So the quotient has the basis of monomials with all \(a_i<0\); it is nonzero (it contains the class of \((x_1\cdots x_n)^{-1}\)). Therefore \(R^{n-1}f_*\mathcal O_U=\widetilde{H^{n-1}(U,\mathcal O_U)}\ne0\) for \(n\ge 2\), although all fibres have dimension \(0\). Of course \(f\) is not projective (not even proper) here. ∎

Exercise 11.2

Problem

Show that a projective morphism with finite fibres (\(=\) quasi-finite (II, Ex. 3.5)) is a finite morphism.

Solution

As throughout Section 11 we assume the base \(Y\) is noetherian. Let \(f\colon X\to Y\) be projective with finite fibres. By Cor. 11.5 (Stein factorization) \(f=g\circ f'\) with \(f'\colon X\to Y'=\Spec f_*\mathcal O_X\) projective, \(f'_*\mathcal O_{X}=\mathcal O_{Y'}\), with connected fibres, and \(g\colon Y'\to Y\) finite. It suffices to show that \(f'\) is an isomorphism.

\(f'\) is surjective. \(f'\) is projective, hence proper, hence closed. If \(y'\in Y'\) were not in the closed set \(f'(X)\), there would be an open neighborhood \(V\ni y'\) with \(f'^{-1}(V)=\emptyset\), so \(0=\mathcal O_X(f'^{-1}V)=(f'_*\mathcal O_X)(V)=\mathcal O_{Y'}(V)\ne0\), a contradiction.

\(f'\) is injective. Let \(y'\in Y'\) and \(y=g(y')\). Then \(f'^{-1}(y')\subseteq f^{-1}(y)\) and the latter is a finite set. Its topology is that of the underlying space of the fibre \(X_y\) (II, Ex. 3.10), a scheme of finite type over \(k(y)\) with finitely many points; such a space is discrete: if some point \(x_1\) were not closed in \(X_y\), then \(\overline{\{x_1\}}\) would be an integral scheme of finite type over \(k(y)\) of dimension \(\ge1\), and it has infinitely many points (by Noether normalization it maps finitely and surjectively onto some \(\mathbf A^d_{k(y)}\), \(d\ge1\), which has infinitely many points). So all points of \(X_y\) are closed, and a finite \(T_1\) space is discrete. So a connected subset of \(f^{-1}(y)\) is a single point; by Cor. 11.5, \(f'^{-1}(y')\) is connected and nonempty, so it is a point.

Hence \(f'\) is a continuous, closed, bijective map, i.e. a homeomorphism, and \(f'^\#\colon\mathcal O_{Y'}\to f'_*\mathcal O_X\) is an isomorphism of sheaves. A homeomorphism of ringed spaces with an isomorphism \(f'^\#\) is an isomorphism of locally ringed spaces, hence of schemes. So \(f=g\circ f'\) is finite. ∎

Exercise 11.3

Problem

Let \(X\) be a normal, projective variety over an algebraically closed field \(k\). Let \(\mathfrak d\) be a linear system (of effective Cartier divisors) without base points, and assume that \(\mathfrak d\) is not composite with a pencil, which means that if \(f\colon X\to\mathbf P^n_k\) is the morphism determined by \(\mathfrak d\), then \(\dim f(X)\ge2\). Then show that every divisor in \(\mathfrak d\) is connected. This improves Bertini's theorem (10.9.1). [Hints: Use (11.5), (Ex. 5.7) and (7.9).]

Solution

Let \(f\colon X\to\mathbf P^n=\mathbf P^n_k\) be the morphism determined by \(\mathfrak d\). It is projective (X is projective over \(k\) and \(\mathbf P^n\) is separated over \(k\); II, Ex. 4.9). Every \(D\in\mathfrak d\) is \(f^*H=\operatorname{div}(f^*h)\) for a hyperplane \(H=\{h=0\}\), so \(|D|=f^{-1}(H)\), and \(f^*h\neq0\) since \(D\) is an effective Cartier divisor.

Step 1: Stein factorization. By Cor. 11.5, \(f=g\circ f'\) with \(f'\colon X\to Y'=\Spec f_*\mathcal O_X\) projective, \(f'_*\mathcal O_X=\mathcal O_{Y'}\), with connected fibres (in particular \(f'\) is surjective, as in Ex. 11.2) and \(g\colon Y'\to\mathbf P^n\) finite. Let \(Z=f(X)=g(Y')\), a closed integral subscheme (reduced structure) of dimension \(\ge2\).

Step 2: \(Y'\) is an integral normal projective variety of dimension \(\ge2\). Let \(V\subseteq\mathbf P^n\) be an affine open. Then \(g^{-1}V=\Spec B_V\) with \(B_V=\mathcal O_X(f^{-1}V)\). As \(X\) is integral, \(B_V\) is a domain, and \(B_V=\bigcap_{x\in f^{-1}V}\mathcal O_{X,x}\subseteq K(X)\). Since \(X\) is normal, every \(\mathcal O_{X,x}\) is integrally closed in \(K(X)\), so \(B_V\) is integrally closed in \(K(X)\), hence in its own fraction field. Thus \(Y'\) is integral and normal, and it is of finite type over \(k\) (finite over \(\mathbf P^n\)), separated, and proper. Since \(Y'\) is reduced, \(g\) factors through \(Z\), and \(g\colon Y'\to Z\) is finite and surjective; hence \(\dim Y'=\dim Z\ge2\). The sheaf \(\mathcal O_Z(1)\) is ample on \(Z\) (Ex. 5.7(a)), so \(g^*\mathcal O_Z(1)=g^*\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^n}(1)\) is ample on \(Y'\) (Ex. 5.7(d), \(Y'\) and \(Z\) being proper over \(k\)), and therefore (II, 7.6) some power of it is very ample and \(Y'\) is projective over \(k\).

Step 3: \(g^{-1}(H)\) is connected. Let \(D\in\mathfrak d\) correspond to \(h\) as above. The section \(\sigma=g^*h\) of the ample invertible sheaf \(\mathcal M=g^*\mathcal O(1)\) on the integral scheme \(Y'\) is nonzero (otherwise \(f^*h=f'^*\sigma=0\)). Its zero scheme is therefore an effective Cartier divisor \(E\) with \(\mathcal O_{Y'}(E)\cong\mathcal M\) ample and \(|E|=g^{-1}(H)\). It is nonempty (if \(E=\emptyset\) then \(\mathcal M\cong\mathcal O_{Y'}\) would be ample on a projective variety of positive dimension, impossible), hence a closed subset of codimension \(1\). By Cor. 7.9, applied to the integral, normal, projective variety \(Y'\) of dimension \(\ge2\), the set \(S:=|E|=g^{-1}(H)\) is connected.

Step 4: \(f'^{-1}(S)=|D|\) is connected. Indeed \(f'^{-1}(g^{-1}(H))=f^{-1}(H)=|D|\). Suppose \(f'^{-1}(S)=A\sqcup B\) with \(A,B\) disjoint closed. Every fibre \(f'^{-1}(s)\), \(s\in S\), is connected, so lies in \(A\) or in \(B\). Hence \(S=f'(A)\sqcup f'(B)\), a disjoint union (a point cannot have its fibre in both), and \(f'(A),f'(B)\) are closed because \(f'\) is proper. As \(S\) is connected, one of them is empty, say \(A=\emptyset\). So \(|D|\) is connected. ∎

Exercise 11.4

Problem

Principle of Connectedness. Let \(\{X_t\}\) be a flat family of closed subschemes of \(\mathbf P^n_k\) parametrized by an irreducible curve \(T\) of finite type over \(k\). Suppose there is a nonempty open set \(U\subseteq T\), such that for all closed points \(t\in U\), \(X_t\) is connected. Then prove that \(X_t\) is connected for all \(t\in T\).

Solution

Reading of the statement. \(k\) is algebraically closed (as in the surrounding discussion); the family is a closed subscheme \(X\subseteq\mathbf P^n_T\) flat over \(T\), and “for all \(t\in T\)” is proved for the closed points \(t\) (at the end we remark on the generic point). A connected space is nonempty.

Step 0: reduction to \(T\) a nonsingular affine curve. The base change \(X\times_T T_{\rm red}\to T_{\rm red}\) is still a flat family with the same fibres at closed points, and so is its pullback to the normalization \(\nu\colon\widetilde T\to T_{\rm red}\) (\(\nu\) is finite and surjective, \(\widetilde T\) is an integral normal curve of finite type over \(k\)). For a closed point \(\tilde t\) of \(\widetilde T\) lying over \(t\), the residue fields are both \(k\) and the fibre is unchanged: \(\widetilde X_{\tilde t}=X_t\). The set \(\nu^{-1}(U)\) is nonempty and open. Given a closed point \(t_0\in T\) choose a closed point \(\tilde t_0\in\nu^{-1}(t_0)\) and an affine open neighborhood \(\Spec R\) of \(\tilde t_0\) in \(\widetilde T\). So we may assume: \(T=\Spec R\), \(R\) a Dedekind domain which is a finitely generated \(k\)-algebra, \(K=\operatorname{Frac}R\), \(U\ne\emptyset\) open (so \(T\setminus U\) is a finite set of closed points), and we must show that \(X_{t_0}\) is connected for a given closed point \(t_0\) (\(\mathfrak m_t\) denotes the maximal ideal of a closed point \(t\)).

Step 1: connected components of closed fibres. Let \(p\colon X\to T\) be the projection (projective), and let \(B=\Gamma(X,\mathcal O_X)=\Gamma(T,p_*\mathcal O_X)\), a finite \(R\)-algebra. Cor. 11.5 gives \(p=g\circ p'\) with \(p'\colon X\to T'=\Spec B\) projective, surjective, with connected fibres, and \(g\colon T'\to T\) finite. For a closed point \(t\), \(g^{-1}(t)\) is a finite set of closed points and \(X_t=\bigsqcup_{t'\in g^{-1}(t)}p'^{-1}(t')\) is a finite disjoint union of nonempty closed connected subsets, which are therefore its connected components. Hence

\[ (*)\qquad X_t\text{ is connected}\iff g^{-1}(t)\text{ is a single point}\iff \Spec B\text{ has exactly one point over }t . \]

Step 2: algebra. \(X\) is flat over the Dedekind domain \(R\), so \(\mathcal O_X\) is torsion-free over \(R\) and so is \(B\); thus \(B\subset B\otimes_RK\). Let \(N\) be the nilradical of \(B\) and \(C=B/N\). \(C\) is \(R\)-torsion-free: if \(a\in R\setminus0\), \(c\in B\) and \(ac\in N\), then \(a^mc^m=0\) for some \(m\), so \(c^m=0\) and \(c\in N\). Hence \(C\) is a finite torsion-free reduced \(R\)-algebra, \(\Spec C=\Spec B\) as spaces, and \(L:=C\otimes_RK\supseteq C\) is a finite reduced \(K\)-algebra, i.e. \(L=\prod_{i=1}^sL_i\) with \(L_i\) finite field extensions of \(K\). Let \(\widetilde C=\prod\widetilde C_i\) be the integral closure of \(R\) in \(L\) (\(\widetilde C_i\) = integral closure of \(R\) in \(L_i\)). It is a finite \(R\)-module (I, 3.9A), \(C\subseteq\widetilde C\), \(\Spec\widetilde C\to\Spec C\) is surjective (lying over), and \(\widetilde C/C\) is a finite torsion \(R\)-module, annihilated by some \(a\ne0\). So the set \(S\) of closed points where \(a\) vanishes is finite, and for closed \(t\notin S\), \(C\) and \(\widetilde C\) coincide after localizing at \(\mathfrak m_t\), so they have the same number of primes over \(\mathfrak m_t\).

By hypothesis and \((*)\), \(\Spec C\) has exactly one point over each closed \(t\in U\). Choose a closed \(t_1\in U\setminus S\) (possible: \(U\setminus S\) is the complement of a finite set in the infinite set \(T\), and nonempty open sets are cofinite). We derive two facts.

(i) \(s=1\). Each \(\widetilde C_i\) is a domain, finite over \(R\) and containing \(R\), so \(\Spec\widetilde C_i\to T\) is surjective, giving at least one point over \(t_1\) in each of the \(s\) components. As \(\Spec\widetilde C\) has exactly one point over \(t_1\), \(s=1\) and \(L\) is a field.

(ii) \(L/K\) is purely inseparable. Let \(L_s\) be the separable closure of \(K\) in \(L\), \(n_s=[L_s:K]\). Write \(L_s=K(\alpha)\) with \(\alpha\) integral over \(R\) and let \(h\in R[x]\) be its minimal polynomial (monic, in \(R[x]\) since \(R\) is normal), \(\delta=\operatorname{disc}(h)\neq0\). Replace \(t_1\) by a closed point of \(U\setminus S\) with \(\delta(t_1)\ne0\) (we excluded finitely many more points). The fibre of \(R[\alpha]=R[x]/(h)\) over \(t_1\) is \(k[x]/(\bar h)\), where \(\bar h\) has \(n_s\) distinct roots in \(k\) (since \(\operatorname{disc}\bar h=\delta(t_1)\ne0\) and \(k\) is algebraically closed), i.e. \(R[\alpha]\) has \(n_s\) points over \(t_1\). Now \(R[\alpha]\subseteq\widetilde C\) is an integral extension, so \(\Spec\widetilde C\to\Spec R[\alpha]\) is surjective and \(\widetilde C\) has \(\ge n_s\) points over \(t_1\). Hence \(n_s=1\).

So \(L/K\) is purely inseparable: there is \(q\) (a power of the characteristic, or \(q=1\) in characteristic \(0\)) with \(x^q\in K\) for all \(x\in L\).

Step 3: the special point. Let \(P_1,P_2\) be primes of \(\widetilde C\) over \(\mathfrak m_{t_0}\). For \(x\in\widetilde C\), \(x^q\in K\) is integral over \(R\), so \(x^q\in R\) (\(R\) normal). Then \(x\in P_j\iff x^q\in P_j\cap R=\mathfrak m_{t_0}\). Thus \(P_1=P_2\): \(\Spec\widetilde C\) has exactly one point over \(t_0\), and as \(\Spec\widetilde C\to\Spec C\) is surjective, \(\Spec C=\Spec B\) has at most one (and at least one, as \(R\subseteq C\) is integral) point over \(t_0\). By \((*)\), \(X_{t_0}\) is connected.

Remark (generic point). The argument also shows \(L\) is a field, and \(H^0(X_\eta,\mathcal O_{X_\eta})=B\otimes K\) has nilradical quotient \(L\), so it is a local ring; hence \(X_\eta\) has no nontrivial idempotent global function, i.e. it is connected. Thus all fibres, closed or not, are connected. ∎

Exercise 11.5

Problem

Let \(Y\) be a hypersurface in \(X=\mathbf P^N_k\) with \(N\ge4\). Let \(\widehat X\) be the formal completion of \(X\) along \(Y\) (II, Section 9). Prove that the natural map \(\operatorname{Pic}\widehat X\to\operatorname{Pic}Y\) is an isomorphism. [Hint: Use (II, Ex. 9.6), and then study the maps \(\operatorname{Pic}X_{n+1}\to\operatorname{Pic}X_n\) for each \(n\) using (Ex. 4.6) and (Ex. 5.5).] (Starred in the book.)

Solution

Let \(Y=V(F)\), \(F\) a form of degree \(d\ge1\), \(\mathcal I=F\cdot\mathcal O_X(-d)\cong\mathcal O_X(-d)\) its ideal sheaf, and \(X_n=(Y,\mathcal O_X/\mathcal I^n)\) the \(n\)-th infinitesimal neighborhood, \(X_1=Y\). Each \(X_n\) is projective over \(k\), so the Mittag-Leffler condition of (II, Ex. 9.6(d)) holds and \(\operatorname{Pic}\widehat X=\varprojlim\operatorname{Pic}X_n\). The natural map to \(\operatorname{Pic}Y\) is the projection to the term \(n=1\). So it suffices to show that \(\operatorname{Pic}X_{n+1}\to\operatorname{Pic}X_n\) is an isomorphism for every \(n\ge1\).

Let \(\mathcal J_n=\mathcal I^n/\mathcal I^{n+1}\subseteq\mathcal O_{X_{n+1}}\); as \(2n\ge n+1\), \(\mathcal J_n^2=0\), and \(\mathcal O_{X_{n+1}}/\mathcal J_n=\mathcal O_{X_n}\). By (Ex. 4.6) there is an exact sequence

\[ H^1(X_{n+1},\mathcal J_n)\to\operatorname{Pic}X_{n+1}\to\operatorname{Pic}X_n\to H^2(X_{n+1},\mathcal J_n), \]

where the cohomology of \(\mathcal J_n\) is that of an \(\mathcal O_Y\)-module on \(Y\). Since \(\mathcal I^n=\mathcal O_X(-nd)\), we have \(\mathcal J_n=\mathcal O_X(-nd)/\mathcal O_X(-(n+1)d)\cong\mathcal O_Y(-nd)\), so it suffices to prove \(H^i(Y,\mathcal O_Y(m))=0\) for \(i=1,2\) and all \(m\in\Z\). Use \(0\to\mathcal O_X(m-d)\xrightarrow{F}\mathcal O_X(m)\to\mathcal O_Y(m)\to0\) and Thm. 5.1(b): \(H^i(X,\mathcal O_X(j))=0\) for \(0<i<N\), all \(j\). The long exact sequence gives \(H^i(Y,\mathcal O_Y(m))=0\) whenever \(i\ge1\) and \(i+1<N\), i.e. \(1\le i\le N-2\). Since \(N\ge4\) this includes \(i=1,2\). (This is Ex. 5.5(c), \(Y\) being a complete intersection of dimension \(N-1\ge3\).) Therefore \(\operatorname{Pic}X_{n+1}\to\operatorname{Pic}X_n\) is an isomorphism for all \(n\ge1\), and \(\operatorname{Pic}\widehat X\to\operatorname{Pic}X_1=\operatorname{Pic}Y\) is an isomorphism. ∎

Exercise 11.6

Problem

Again let \(Y\) be a hypersurface in \(X=\mathbf P^N_k\), this time \(N\ge2\).

  1. (a)

    If \(\mathcal F\) is a locally free sheaf on \(X\), show that the natural map \(H^0(X,\mathcal F)\to H^0(\widehat X,\widehat{\mathcal F})\) is an isomorphism.

  2. (b)

    Show that the following conditions are equivalent: (i) for each locally free sheaf \(\mathfrak J\) on \(\widehat X\), there exists a coherent sheaf \(\mathcal F\) on \(X\) such that \(\mathfrak J\cong\widehat{\mathcal F}\) (i.e., \(\mathfrak J\) is algebraizable); (ii) for each locally free sheaf \(\mathfrak J\) on \(\widehat X\), there is an integer \(n_0\) such that \(\mathfrak J(n)\) is generated by global sections for all \(n\ge n_0\). [Hint: For (ii)\(\Rightarrow\)(i), show that one can find sheaves \(\mathcal E_0,\mathcal E_1\) on \(X\), which are direct sums of sheaves of the form \(\mathcal O(-q_i)\), and an exact sequence \(\widehat{\mathcal E}_1\to\widehat{\mathcal E}_0\to\mathfrak J\to0\) on \(\widehat X\). Then apply (a) to the sheaf \(\mathcal Hom(\mathcal E_1,\mathcal E_0)\).]

  3. (c)

    Show that the conditions (i) and (ii) of (b) imply that the natural map \(\operatorname{Pic}X\to\operatorname{Pic}\widehat X\) is an isomorphism.

Note. In fact, (i) and (ii) always hold if \(N\ge3\). This fact, coupled with (Ex. 11.5) leads to Grothendieck's proof [SGA 2] of the Lefschetz theorem which says that if \(Y\) is a hypersurface in \(\mathbf P^N\) with \(N\ge4\), then \(\operatorname{Pic}Y\cong\Z\), and it is generated by \(\mathcal O_Y(1)\). See Hartshorne [5, Ch. IV] for more details.

Solution

Notation: \(Y=V(F)\), \(\deg F=d\ge1\), \(\mathcal I=\mathcal O_X(-d)\subseteq\mathcal O_X\), \(\mathcal F_n=\mathcal F/\mathcal I^n\mathcal F\) and \(\widehat{\mathcal F}=\varprojlim\mathcal F_n\), so \(\Gamma(\widehat X,\widehat{\mathcal F})=\varprojlim H^0(Y,\mathcal F_n)\). For a sheaf \(\mathfrak J\) on \(\widehat X\), \(\mathfrak J(n)=\mathfrak J\otimes\widehat{\mathcal O_X(n)}\), and \(\widehat{\mathcal F}(n)=\widehat{\mathcal F(n)}\). Completion is an exact functor on coherent sheaves (II, Section 9).

(a) Let \(\mathcal F\) be locally free. Then \(\mathcal I^n\mathcal F=\mathcal F(-nd)\) and there is an exact sequence \(0\to\mathcal F(-nd)\to\mathcal F\to\mathcal F_n\to0\), whence

\[ 0\to H^0(X,\mathcal F(-nd))\to H^0(X,\mathcal F)\to H^0(\mathcal F_n)\to H^1(X,\mathcal F(-nd)). \]

(1) \(H^1(X,\mathcal F(-nd))=0\) for \(n\gg0\) by Cor. 7.8 (\(X=\mathbf P^N\) is normal projective of dimension \(N\ge2\)). (2) \(H^0(X,\mathcal F(-nd))=0\) for \(n\gg0\): the sheaf \(\mathcal F^\vee(q)\) is generated by global sections for some \(q\) (II, 5.17), so there is a surjection \(\mathcal O_X^s\to\mathcal F^\vee(q)\); dualizing the surjection of locally free sheaves gives an injection \(\mathcal F(-q)\hookrightarrow\mathcal O_X^s\), so \(H^0(\mathcal F(-q-j))\subseteq H^0(\mathcal O(-j))^s=0\) for \(j\ge1\).

Hence there is \(n_0\) such that for \(n\ge n_0\) the map \(H^0(X,\mathcal F)\to H^0(\mathcal F_n)\) is bijective. These maps are compatible with the transition maps \(H^0(\mathcal F_{n+1})\to H^0(\mathcal F_n)\), which are therefore bijective for \(n\ge n_0\), and \(\varprojlim H^0(\mathcal F_n)=H^0(\mathcal F_{n_0})\cong H^0(X,\mathcal F)\) compatibly with the natural map. So \(H^0(X,\mathcal F)\to H^0(\widehat X,\widehat{\mathcal F})\) is an isomorphism.

(b) (i)\(\Rightarrow\)(ii). Let \(\mathfrak J\cong\widehat{\mathcal F}\) with \(\mathcal F\) coherent. By II, 5.17 there is \(n_0\) with \(\mathcal F(n)\) generated by global sections for \(n\ge n_0\), i.e. there is a surjection \(\mathcal O_X^s\to\mathcal F(n)\). Completion is exact, so \(\mathcal O_{\widehat X}^s\to\widehat{\mathcal F(n)}=\mathfrak J(n)\) is surjective: \(\mathfrak J(n)\) is generated by global sections for \(n\ge n_0\).

(ii)\(\Rightarrow\)(i). Let \(\mathfrak J\) be locally free on \(\widehat X\). By (ii), \(\mathfrak J(n_0)\) is generated by global sections, hence (II, Ex. 9.5) by finitely many of them: there is a surjection \(\mathcal O_{\widehat X}^s\to\mathfrak J(n_0)\), i.e. \(\widehat{\mathcal E}_0\to\mathfrak J\to0\) with \(\mathcal E_0=\mathcal O_X(-n_0)^{s}\). Its kernel \(\mathfrak J'\) is coherent (II, Cor. 9.9) and locally free (the kernel of a surjection of locally free sheaves is locally a direct summand). Applying (ii) to \(\mathfrak J'\) we find in the same way a surjection \(\widehat{\mathcal E}_1\to\mathfrak J'\) with \(\mathcal E_1=\bigoplus\mathcal O_X(-q_j)\) a finite direct sum. Composing, we get an exact sequence \(\widehat{\mathcal E}_1\xrightarrow{\varphi}\widehat{\mathcal E}_0\to\mathfrak J\to0\).

The sheaf \(\mathcal H=\mathcal Hom(\mathcal E_1,\mathcal E_0)=\bigoplus\mathcal O_X(q_j-n_0)\) is locally free, and \(\widehat{\mathcal H}=\bigoplus\mathcal O_{\widehat X}(q_j-n_0)=\mathcal Hom(\widehat{\mathcal E}_1,\widehat{\mathcal E}_0)\). So \(\varphi\in H^0(\widehat X,\widehat{\mathcal H})\) and by (a) there is \(u\in H^0(X,\mathcal H)=\Hom(\mathcal E_1,\mathcal E_0)\) with \(\widehat u=\varphi\). Put \(\mathcal F=\operatorname{coker}(u)\), a coherent sheaf on \(X\). By exactness of completion, \(\widehat{\mathcal F}=\operatorname{coker}(\widehat u)=\operatorname{coker}\varphi\cong\mathfrak J\). This proves (i).

(c) The natural map sends \(\mathcal L\in\operatorname{Pic}X\) to \(\widehat{\mathcal L}\), which is invertible on \(\widehat X\) (II, Section 9). Recall \(\operatorname{Pic}X=\Z\cdot[\mathcal O(1)]\) (II, 6.4).

Injectivity. If \(\widehat{\mathcal O(m)}\cong\mathcal O_{\widehat X}\), then \(H^0(\widehat X,\widehat{\mathcal O(m)})\) contains a nowhere vanishing section, so is nonzero, and by (a) \(H^0(X,\mathcal O(m))\ne0\), i.e. \(m\ge0\). Dualizing, \(\widehat{\mathcal O(-m)}\cong\mathcal O_{\widehat X}\) and the same argument gives \(-m\ge0\). So \(m=0\).

Surjectivity. Let \(\mathfrak J\) be an invertible sheaf on \(\widehat X\). By (i) there is a coherent \(\mathcal F\) on \(X\) with \(\widehat{\mathcal F}\cong\mathfrak J\). Locally on \(X=\bigcup\Spec A\) (we may take \(X\) covered by such) \(\mathcal F=\widetilde M\) and \(\widehat{\mathcal F}\) corresponds to the \(\mathfrak a\)-adic completion \(\widehat M=M\otimes_A\widehat A\) over \(\widehat A\) (\(\mathfrak a\) the ideal of \(Y\)); that \(\widehat{\mathcal F}\) is invertible means \(\widehat M\) is locally free of rank one. For a prime \(P\supseteq\mathfrak a\) (a point of \(Y\)), \(\mathfrak a\subseteq P\) gives \(\widehat A/P\widehat A=A/P\), so \(P\widehat A\) is prime, and \(A_P\to\widehat A_{P\widehat A}\) is a flat local, hence faithfully flat, homomorphism. As \(M_P\otimes_{A_P}\widehat A_{P\widehat A}=\widehat M_{P\widehat A}\) is free of rank one, \(M_P\) is free of rank one (faithfully flat descent for a finitely generated module). So \(\mathcal F\) is invertible at every point of \(Y\), hence on an open set \(W\supseteq Y\) (the locally free locus of a coherent sheaf is open).

The closed set \(X\setminus W\) is disjoint from \(Y\). It has dimension \(0\): an irreducible closed subset \(C\subseteq X\setminus W\) of positive dimension (with its reduced structure) would be a projective variety contained in the affine scheme \(X\setminus Y=D_+(F)\), impossible, since a closed subscheme of an affine scheme which is proper over \(k\) is finite. So \(X\setminus W\) is a finite set of closed points, of codimension \(N\ge2\). Since \(X\) and \(W\) are regular, (II, 6.5 and 6.16) give \(\operatorname{Pic}X=\operatorname{Cl}X\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\operatorname{Cl}W=\operatorname{Pic}W\). Hence there is \(\mathcal M\in\operatorname{Pic}X\) with \(\mathcal M|_W\cong\mathcal F|_W\). Completion along \(Y\subseteq W\) only involves \(W\), so \(\widehat{\mathcal M}\cong\widehat{\mathcal F}\cong\mathfrak J\). ∎

Remark used in Ex. 11.7. The surjectivity argument shows, for \(N\ge2\) and without assuming (i) or (ii): if an invertible sheaf \(\mathfrak J\) on \(\widehat X\) is isomorphic to \(\widehat{\mathcal F}\) for a coherent \(\mathcal F\), then \(\mathfrak J\cong\widehat{\mathcal M}\) for some \(\mathcal M\in\operatorname{Pic}X\).

Exercise 11.7

Problem

Now let \(Y\) be a curve in \(X=\mathbf P^2_k\).

  1. (a)

    Use the method of (Ex. 11.5) to show that \(\operatorname{Pic}\widehat X\to\operatorname{Pic}Y\) is surjective, and its kernel is an infinite-dimensional vector space over \(k\).

  2. (b)

    Conclude that there is an invertible sheaf \(\mathfrak L\) on \(\widehat X\) which is not algebraizable.

  3. (c)

    Conclude also that there is a locally free sheaf \(\mathfrak J\) on \(\widehat X\) so that no twist \(\mathfrak J(n)\) is generated by global sections. Cf. (II, 9.9.1).

Solution

Notation as in Ex. 11.5: \(Y=V(F)\), \(\deg F=d\ge1\), \(\mathcal I=\mathcal O_X(-d)\), \(X_n\), \(\mathcal J_n=\mathcal I^n/\mathcal I^{n+1}\cong\mathcal O_Y(-nd)\) (\(n\geq 1\)), and \(\operatorname{Pic}\widehat X=\varprojlim\operatorname{Pic}X_n\) (II, Ex. 9.6(d)).

Cohomology of \(\mathcal O_Y(m)\). From \(0\to\mathcal O_X(m-d)\to\mathcal O_X(m)\to\mathcal O_Y(m)\to0\) and Thm. 5.1 (\(H^1(\mathcal O_X(j))=0\) for all \(j\); \(H^0(\mathcal O_X(j))=0\) for \(j<0\); \(H^2(\mathcal O_X(-j))\) is dual to \(H^0(\mathcal O_X(j-3))\), so has dimension \(\binom{j-1}{2}\) for \(j\ge1\), where \(\binom{0}{2}=\binom{1}{2}=0\)):

  • •

    \(H^2(Y,\mathcal O_Y(m))=0\) for all \(m\) (indeed \(\dim Y=1\), Grothendieck vanishing, 2.7).

  • •

    \(H^0(Y,\mathcal O_Y)=k\), and \(H^0(Y,\mathcal O_Y(-m))=0\) for \(m\ge1\) (as \(H^0(\mathcal O_X(-m))=0=H^1(\mathcal O_X(-m-d))\)).

  • •

    For \(m\ge1\): \(0=H^1(\mathcal O_X(-m))\to H^1(\mathcal O_Y(-m))\to H^2(\mathcal O_X(-m-d))\to H^2(\mathcal O_X(-m))\to H^2(\mathcal O_Y(-m))=0\), so with \(c(j)=\binom{j-1}{2}=\dim H^2(\mathcal O_X(-j))\) (this is \(0\) for \(j=1,2\)),

    \[ h^1(Y,\mathcal O_Y(-m))=c(m+d)-c(m). \]

    For \(m=nd\): \(h^1(Y,\mathcal O_Y(-nd))=\tfrac12\big[((n+1)d-1)((n+1)d-2)-(nd-1)(nd-2)\big]=\tfrac12 d\big((2n+1)d-3\big)\), which tends to \(\infty\) with \(n\).

The groups \(\operatorname{Pic}X_n\). Put \(V_n=H^1(Y,\mathcal J_n)=H^1(Y,\mathcal O_Y(-nd))\), a \(k\)-vector space with \(\dim V_n\to\infty\). We claim \(H^0(X_n,\mathcal O_{X_n})=k\) for all \(n\ge1\): from \(0\to\mathcal J_n\to\mathcal O_{X_{n+1}}\to\mathcal O_{X_n}\to0\) and \(H^0(\mathcal J_n)=0\), \(H^0(\mathcal O_{X_{n+1}})\hookrightarrow H^0(\mathcal O_{X_n})\), and induction from \(H^0(\mathcal O_Y)=k\) (the constants \(k\) lie in all of them). Therefore \(H^0(X_{n+1},\mathcal O^*)=k^*\to H^0(X_n,\mathcal O^*)=k^*\) is bijective. Ex. 4.6 (\(\mathcal J_n^2=0\)) gives the exact sequence

\[ k^*\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }k^*\to V_n\to\operatorname{Pic}X_{n+1}\to\operatorname{Pic}X_n\to H^2(Y,\mathcal J_n)=0, \]

so the connecting map \(k^*\to V_n\) is zero and

\[ 0\to V_n\to\operatorname{Pic}X_{n+1}\to\operatorname{Pic}X_n\to0\quad\text{is exact}\quad(n\ge1). \]

(a) The transition maps \(\operatorname{Pic}X_{n+1}\to\operatorname{Pic}X_n\) are surjective, so the projection \(\operatorname{Pic}\widehat X=\varprojlim\operatorname{Pic}X_n\to\operatorname{Pic}X_1=\operatorname{Pic}Y\) is surjective (lift step by step). Let \(K_n=\ker(\operatorname{Pic}X_n\to\operatorname{Pic}Y)\) and \(K=\ker(\operatorname{Pic}\widehat X\to\operatorname{Pic}Y)=\varprojlim K_n\). Since \(\operatorname{Pic}X_{n+1}\to\operatorname{Pic}X_n\) is onto and \(V_n\) maps to \(0\in\operatorname{Pic}Y\), we get that \(K_{n+1}\to K_n\) is surjective with kernel \(V_n\). Hence \(K\to K_n\) is surjective for every \(n\), and \(V_{n-1}\subseteq K_n\), so \(K\) surjects onto groups containing vector spaces of arbitrarily large dimension: \(K\) is infinite-dimensional in the following sense.

\(K\) is a \(k\)-vector space of infinite dimension if \(\operatorname{char}k=0\). Let \(\mathfrak n_n=\mathcal I/\mathcal I^n\subseteq\mathcal O_{X_n}\), a nilpotent ideal. Then \(\mathcal O^*_{X_n}\to\mathcal O^*_Y\) is surjective with kernel \(1+\mathfrak n_n\), and as \(H^0(\mathcal O^*_{X_n})=k^*\to H^0(\mathcal O_Y^*)=k^*\) is bijective, \(K_n=H^1(Y,1+\mathfrak n_n)\). In characteristic \(0\) the truncated logarithm and exponential series give isomorphisms of sheaves of abelian groups \(1+\mathfrak n_n\cong\mathfrak n_n\), compatible with the ring maps \(\mathcal O_{X_{n+1}}\to\mathcal O_{X_n}\), so \(K_n\cong H^1(Y,\mathfrak n_n)\) is a \(k\)-vector space, the transition maps are \(k\)-linear, and \(K=\varprojlim K_n\) is a \(k\)-vector space, which surjects onto \(K_n\supseteq V_{n-1}\) with \(\dim V_{n-1}\to\infty\). So \(\dim_kK=\infty\).

Honest remark. In characteristic \(p>0\), \(1+\mathfrak n_n\) need not be killed by \(p\) (e.g. \((1+a\varepsilon)^2=1+a^2\varepsilon^2\) in characteristic \(2\)), so \(K\) is in general an abelian group, an inverse limit of successive extensions of \(k\)-vector spaces of unbounded dimension, not obviously a \(k\)-vector space; the statement “vector space” is proved above in characteristic \(0\). In all characteristics we obtain what is needed below: \(K\) is uncountable. Indeed, \(V_n\ne0\) for all large \(n\), so in the inverse system of surjections \(K_{n+1}\to K_n\) the fibres over every element have \(\ge2\) elements for large \(n\), and one builds an injection \(\{0,1\}^{\mathbf N}\hookrightarrow K\).

(b) \(\operatorname{Pic}X=\Z\) (II, 6.4) is countable, while \(\operatorname{Pic}\widehat X\supseteq K\) is uncountable. So there is \(\mathfrak L\in\operatorname{Pic}\widehat X\) not in the image of \(\operatorname{Pic}X\to\operatorname{Pic}\widehat X\). If \(\mathfrak L\) were algebraizable, \(\mathfrak L\cong\widehat{\mathcal F}\) with \(\mathcal F\) coherent, then by the Remark at the end of Ex. 11.6(c) (valid for \(N=2\)) \(\mathfrak L\cong\widehat{\mathcal M}\) with \(\mathcal M\in\operatorname{Pic}X\), a contradiction. So \(\mathfrak L\) is not algebraizable.

(c) First, the sheaves \(\mathcal O_{\widehat X}(j)=\widehat{\mathcal O_X(j)}\), \(j\ge0\), are generated by global sections (\(\mathcal O_X^{3}\to\mathcal O_X(j)\) by \(x_0^j,x_1^j,x_2^j\) is surjective, and completion is exact). If \(\mathfrak J(n)\) is generated by global sections, then so is \(\mathfrak J(n+j)=\mathfrak J(n)\otimes\mathcal O_{\widehat X}(j)\) for all \(j\ge0\) (a tensor product of sheaves generated by global sections is generated by the products of the sections). Hence “some twist \(\mathfrak J(n)\), \(n\in\Z\), is generated by global sections” implies “\(\mathfrak J(n)\) is generated by global sections for all \(n\ge n_0\)”.

Suppose every locally free \(\mathfrak J\) on \(\widehat X\) had some twist generated by global sections. Then condition (ii) of Ex. 11.6(b) holds, hence so does (i), and the invertible sheaf \(\mathfrak L\) of (b) would be algebraizable, contradicting (b). Hence there is a locally free sheaf \(\mathfrak J\) on \(\widehat X\) such that no twist \(\mathfrak J(n)\), \(n\in\Z\), is generated by global sections. ∎

Exercise 11.8

Problem

Let \(f\colon X\to Y\) be a projective morphism, let \(\mathcal F\) be a coherent sheaf on \(X\) which is flat over \(Y\), and assume that \(H^i(X_y,\mathcal F_y)=0\) for some \(i\) and some \(y\in Y\). Then show that \(R^if_*(\mathcal F)\) is \(0\) in a neighborhood of \(y\).

Solution

(\(Y\) is noetherian, as throughout.) Let \(A=\mathcal O_{Y,y}\), \(\mathfrak m\) its maximal ideal, \(k(y)=A/\mathfrak m\). By the flat base change \(\Spec A\to Y\) (Prop. 9.3) we may replace \(Y\) by \(\Spec A\), \(X\) by \(X\times_Y\Spec A\) and \(\mathcal F\) by its pull-back (still coherent and flat over \(A\), and the fibre over \(y\) is unchanged). Let \(X_n=X\times\Spec A/\mathfrak m^n\), \(\mathcal F_n=\mathcal F\otimes_AA/\mathfrak m^n\) (a sheaf on the space \(X_y\)), so \(X_1=X_y\), \(\mathcal F_1=\mathcal F_y\).

Claim: \(H^i(X_n,\mathcal F_n)=0\) for all \(n\ge1\). For \(n=1\) this is the hypothesis. Tensoring \(0\to\mathfrak m^n/\mathfrak m^{n+1}\to A/\mathfrak m^{n+1}\to A/\mathfrak m^n\to0\) with the flat \(A\)-module \(\mathcal F\) gives an exact sequence of sheaves on \(X_y\):

\[ 0\to\mathcal F\otimes_A(\mathfrak m^n/\mathfrak m^{n+1})\to\mathcal F_{n+1}\to\mathcal F_n\to0 . \]

As \(\mathfrak m^n/\mathfrak m^{n+1}\) is a finite-dimensional \(k(y)\)-vector space, say of dimension \(r\), the left term is \(\mathcal F_y^{\oplus r}\), whose \(H^i\) is \(0\). The exact sequence \(H^i(\mathcal F_y^{\oplus r})\to H^i(\mathcal F_{n+1})\to H^i(\mathcal F_n)\) and induction on \(n\) give the claim.

Now by Thm. 11.1, \((R^if_*\mathcal F)^{\wedge}_y=\varprojlim H^i(X_n,\mathcal F_n)=0\). The stalk \(M=(R^if_*\mathcal F)_y\) is a finitely generated \(A\)-module (Prop. 8.8(b)) and its \(\mathfrak m\)-adic completion \(M\otimes_A\widehat A\) is \(0\); since \(A\to\widehat A\) is faithfully flat, \(M=0\). A coherent sheaf with zero stalk at \(y\) vanishes in a neighborhood of \(y\). (Alternatively: \(H^i(X_y,\mathcal F_y)=0\) makes \(\varphi^i(y)\colon R^if_*\mathcal F\otimes k(y)\to H^i(X_y,\mathcal F_y)\) surjective, hence an isomorphism by Cor. 12.11(a); so \(R^if_*\mathcal F\otimes k(y)=0\) and Nakayama gives the same conclusion.) ∎

12. The semicontinuity theorem

Notation as in the text: for \(f\colon X\to Y\) projective and \(\mathcal F\) coherent and flat over \(Y\), \(h^i(y,\mathcal F)=\dim_{k(y)}H^i(X_y,\mathcal F_y)\), and \(\varphi^i(y)\colon R^if_*\mathcal F\otimes k(y)\to H^i(X_y,\mathcal F_y)\) is the natural map. Thm. 12.8 says that \(h^i(y,\mathcal F)\) is upper semicontinuous on \(Y\), so by Remark 12.7.1 each set \(\{y\mid h^i(y,\mathcal F)\ge n\}\) is closed. We use freely that on a scheme of finite type over a field \(k\) every nonempty closed subset contains a closed point, so that a closed subset containing all closed points is everything.

Exercise 12.1

Problem

Let \(Y\) be a scheme of finite type over an algebraically closed field \(k\). Show that the function \(\varphi(y)=\dim_k(\mathfrak m_y/\mathfrak m_y^2)\) is upper semicontinuous on the set of closed points of \(Y\).

Solution

Let \(y\) be a closed point. Then \(A=\mathcal O_{Y,y}\) is a local ring containing \(k\) with residue field \(A/\mathfrak m_y=k\) (\(k\) algebraically closed, \(y\) closed). By (II, Prop. 8.7) the map \(d\colon\mathfrak m_y/\mathfrak m_y^2\to\Omega_{A/k}\otimes_Ak\) is an isomorphism. Moreover \(\Omega_{A/k}=(\Omega_{Y/k})_y\) (II, 8.2A), so

\[ \varphi(y)=\dim_k\big(\Omega_{Y/k}\otimes k(y)\big). \]

Since \(Y\) is of finite type over \(k\), \(\Omega_{Y/k}\) is coherent (II, Rem. 8.9.1), and by Example 12.7.2 the function \(y\mapsto\dim_{k(y)}(\Omega_{Y/k}\otimes k(y))\) is upper semicontinuous on \(Y\). Its restriction to the subspace of closed points is therefore upper semicontinuous (the induced topology on the closed points consists of the intersections with open sets of \(Y\)). ∎

Exercise 12.2

Problem

Let \(\{X_t\}\) be a family of hypersurfaces of the same degree in \(\mathbf P^n_k\). Show that for each \(i\), the function \(h^i(X_t,\mathcal O_{X_t})\) is a constant function of \(t\).

Solution

Interpretation: \(t\) runs through the points of a parameter scheme \(T\) (of finite type over \(k\), say) and \(X_t\subseteq\mathbf P^n_{k(t)}\) is the hypersurface \(V(f_t)\) defined by a nonzero form \(f_t\) of the fixed degree \(d\ge1\) (\(f_t\) in \(k(t)[x_0,\dots,x_n]\)); \(h^i\) is computed over \(k(t)\). We show that \(h^i(X_t,\mathcal O_{X_t})\) depends only on \(n,d,i\) (and not on \(f_t\), on \(t\), or on whether the family is flat).

Fix a field \(K=k(t)\), \(P=\mathbf P^n_K\), \(X=V(f)\), \(f\ne0\) homogeneous of degree \(d\ge1\). Multiplication by \(f\) is injective on \(\mathcal O_P(-d)\), so \(0\to\mathcal O_P(-d)\to\mathcal O_P\to\mathcal O_X\to0\) is exact. By Thm. 5.1: \(H^0(\mathcal O_P)=K\) and \(H^j(\mathcal O_P)=0\) for \(j>0\); \(H^0(\mathcal O_P(-d))=0\); \(H^j(\mathcal O_P(-d))=0\) for \(0<j<n\); and \(H^n(\mathcal O_P(-d))\) is dual to \(H^0(\mathcal O_P(d-n-1))\), of dimension \(\binom{d-1}{n}\).

If \(n=1\): \(0\to K\to H^0(\mathcal O_X)\to H^1(\mathcal O_P(-d))\to H^1(\mathcal O_P)=0\), so \(h^0=1+(d-1)=d\) and \(h^i=0\) for \(i\ge1\) (as \(\dim X=0\)).

If \(n\ge2\): \(H^1(\mathcal O_P(-d))=0\), so \(H^0(\mathcal O_X)\cong H^0(\mathcal O_P)=K\) and \(h^0=1\). For \(1\le i\le n-2\): \(H^i(\mathcal O_P)=0=H^{i+1}(\mathcal O_P(-d))\), so \(h^i=0\). For \(i=n-1\): \(0=H^{n-1}(\mathcal O_P)\to H^{n-1}(\mathcal O_X)\to H^n(\mathcal O_P(-d))\to H^n(\mathcal O_P)=0\), so \(h^{n-1}=\binom{d-1}{n}\); and \(h^i=0\) for \(i\ge n\) (\(\dim X=n-1\)).

In all cases the values depend only on \((n,d,i)\), so each \(h^i(X_t,\mathcal O_{X_t})\) is constant in \(t\). ∎

Exercise 12.3

Problem

Let \(X_1\subseteq\mathbf P^4_k\) be the rational normal quartic curve (which is the \(4\)-uple embedding of \(\mathbf P^1\) in \(\mathbf P^4\)). Let \(X_0\subseteq\mathbf P^3_k\) be a nonsingular rational quartic curve, such as the one in (I, Ex. 3.18b). Use (9.8.3) to construct a flat family \(\{X_t\}\) of curves in \(\mathbf P^4\), parametrized by \(T=\mathbf A^1\), with the given fibres \(X_1\) and \(X_0\) for \(t=1\) and \(t=0\). Let \(\mathcal I\subseteq\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^4\times T}\) be the ideal sheaf of the total family \(X\subseteq\mathbf P^4\times T\). Show that \(\mathcal I\) is flat over \(T\). Then show that

\[ h^0(t,\mathcal I)=\begin{cases}0&t\ne0\\1&t=0\end{cases}\qquad\text{and also}\qquad h^1(t,\mathcal I)=\begin{cases}0&t\ne0\\1&t=0.\end{cases} \]

This gives another example of cohomology groups jumping at a special point.

Solution

Misprint. As printed (with the untwisted ideal sheaf) the displayed values are false: \(H^0(\mathbf P^4,\mathcal I_t)=0\) always (it consists of constants vanishing on a nonempty set) and, as computed below, \(H^1(\mathbf P^4,\mathcal I_t)=0\) for all \(t\), because \(H^0(\mathcal O_{X_t})=k\) for every \(t\). The statement is true, and is what the example is meant to illustrate, for the twist \(\mathcal I(1)\): \(h^0(t,\mathcal I(1))=\) number of hyperplanes containing \(X_t\), which jumps at \(t=0\) because \(X_0\) lies in a hyperplane. We prove the statement with \(\mathcal I(1)\) (and we also verify the untwisted vanishing).

Construction of the family. Let \(\nu\colon\mathbf P^1\xrightarrow{\sim}X_0\subseteq\mathbf P^3\), \(\nu=(g_0:g_1:g_2:g_3)\) with \(g_i\) binary quartic forms in \(v,w\) (for the quartic of I, Ex. 3.18b: \(g=(v^4,v^3w,vw^3,w^4)\)). Since \(\nu^*\mathcal O(1)=\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^1}(4)\) (the curve has degree \(4\)), \(g_0,\dots,g_3\) are sections of \(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^1}(4)\); they are linearly independent, since \(X_0\) is not contained in a plane (a plane quartic has arithmetic genus \(3\), while \(X_0\cong\mathbf P^1\)). Complete them by a form \(g_4\) to a basis of the \(5\)-dimensional space \(H^0(\mathbf P^1,\mathcal O(4))\) (for the standard example \(g_4=v^2w^2\)). Then \((g_0:\dots:g_4)\) is the complete linear system \(|\mathcal O(4)|\), i.e. the \(4\)-uple embedding followed by a linear change of coordinates, and its image is a rational normal quartic \(X_1\subseteq\mathbf P^4\) (we choose the coordinates \(z_0,\dots,z_4\) of \(\mathbf P^4\) so that this is the given \(X_1\)). The point \(Q=(0:0:0:0:1)\) is not on \(X_1\) (the \(g_0,\dots,g_3\) have no common zero), and the projection of \(X_1\) from \(Q\) to the hyperplane \(H=\{z_4=0\}\cong\mathbf P^3\) is \(X_0\).

As in (9.8.3), for \(t\in k^*\) (or \(t\) a point of \(\mathbf G_m=\mathbf A^1\setminus0\)) let \(\sigma_t(z_0:\dots:z_3:z_4)=(z_0:\dots:z_3:tz_4)\) and \(X_t=\sigma_t(X_1)\). Over \(\mathbf G_m\) the automorphism \(\sigma\colon\mathbf P^4\times\mathbf G_m\to\mathbf P^4\times\mathbf G_m\), \((z,t)\mapsto(\sigma_tz,t)\), carries \(X_1\times\mathbf G_m\) to a closed subscheme \(X'\cong X_1\times\mathbf G_m\), flat over \(\mathbf G_m\), with fibres \(X_t=\sigma_t(X_1)\cong X_1\), and \(X'\) is reduced and irreducible. By Prop. 9.8 (with \(Y=T=\mathbf A^1\), \(P=0\)) the scheme-theoretic closure \(X\) of \(X'\) in \(\mathbf P^4_T\) is flat over \(T\) (and is the unique such extension); \(X_1\) is the fibre at \(t=1\).

The fibre at \(0\). Let \(\Phi\colon\mathbf P^1\times\mathbf A^1\to\mathbf P^4\times\mathbf A^1\), \(((v:w),t)\mapsto((g_0:g_1:g_2:g_3:tg_4),t)\) (a morphism since \(g_0,\dots,g_3\) have no common zero). It is proper, so its image \(Z\) is closed and irreducible, and contains \(X'=\Phi(\mathbf P^1\times\mathbf G_m)\) as a dense subset. Hence \(|X|=Z\) and \(|X_0|=Z\cap\{t=0\}=\Phi(\mathbf P^1\times\{0\})=\{(g_0:\dots:g_3:0)\}=X_0^{\rm set}\), the set underlying the curve \(X_0\subseteq H\subseteq\mathbf P^4\). (We write \(C\) for \(X_0\) regarded as a reduced subscheme of \(\mathbf P^4\), and \(X^{\rm fib}_0\) for the fibre at \(0\) of the family just constructed; so far we know \(X_0^{\rm fib}\) has underlying set \(C\).) The scheme \(X^{\rm fib}_0\) has the same Hilbert polynomial as \(X_1\) (Thm. 9.9): \(P(m)=\chi(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^1}(4m))=4m+1\). Since \(C\) is reduced with the same support, \(C\subseteq X_0^{\rm fib}\) is the reduced subscheme, with ideal \(\mathcal N\) (nilpotent). Also \(C\cong\mathbf P^1\) and \(\mathcal O_C(1)=\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^1}(4)\), so \(P_C(m)=4m+1\). From \(0\to\mathcal N\to\mathcal O_{X_0^{\rm fib}}\to\mathcal O_C\to0\) we get \(P_{\mathcal N}=0\); but a nonzero coherent sheaf \(\mathcal N\) has \(\chi(\mathcal N(m))=h^0(\mathcal N(m))>0\) for \(m\gg0\) (Serre). So \(\mathcal N=0\) and

\[ X_0^{\rm fib}=C=X_0 . \]

Thus \(\{X_t\}_{t\in\mathbf A^1}\) is a flat family of curves in \(\mathbf P^4\) with fibres \(X_1\) at \(t=1\) and \(X_0\) at \(t=0\) (here \(X_0\) is viewed in \(\mathbf P^3\subseteq\mathbf P^4\)), and \(X_t\cong\) rational normal quartic for \(t\ne0\).

Flatness of \(\mathcal I\). In \(0\to\mathcal I\to\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^4_T}\to\mathcal O_X\to0\) both \(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^4_T}\) and \(\mathcal O_X\) are flat over \(T\), so \(\mathcal I\) is flat over \(T\) (Prop. 9.1A(e)). Moreover, tensoring with \(k(t)\) over \(\mathcal O_T\) and using \(\Tor_1^{\mathcal O_T}(\mathcal O_X,k(t))=0\) gives for every point \(t\in T\) the exact sequence \(0\to\mathcal I_t\to\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^4_{k(t)}}\to\mathcal O_{X_t}\to0\), i.e. \(\mathcal I_t\) is the ideal sheaf of the fibre \(X_t\) and \(h^i(t,\mathcal I(m))=\dim H^i(\mathbf P^4_{k(t)},\mathcal I_t(m))\). (Below \(k(t)=k\) for closed \(t\); the generic point is treated identically over \(k(t)\).)

Untwisted groups. From \(0\to H^0(\mathcal I_t)\to H^0(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^4})=k\to H^0(\mathcal O_{X_t})\to H^1(\mathcal I_t)\to H^1(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^4})=0\), and \(H^0(\mathcal O_{X_t})=k\) for every \(t\) (each fibre is \(\cong\mathbf P^1\), including \(X_0=C\)), we get \(h^0(t,\mathcal I)=h^1(t,\mathcal I)=0\) for all \(t\).

Twisted groups. Twisting by \(\mathcal O(1)\): \(0\to H^0(\mathcal I_t(1))\to H^0(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^4}(1))\xrightarrow{\ \rho_t\ }H^0(\mathcal O_{X_t}(1))\to H^1(\mathcal I_t(1))\to H^1(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^4}(1))=0\). Here \(H^0(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^4}(1))\) has dimension \(5\) (basis \(z_0,\dots,z_4\)), and \(X_t\cong\mathbf P^1\) with \(\mathcal O_{X_t}(1)\cong\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^1}(4)\), so \(h^0(\mathcal O_{X_t}(1))=5\).

  • •

    \(t\ne0\): \(\rho_t\) sends \(z_i\mapsto g_i\) (\(i\le3\)) and \(z_4\mapsto tg_4\). As \(g_0,\dots,g_4\) is a basis of \(H^0(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^1}(4))\) and \(t\neq0\) in \(k(t)\), \(\rho_t\) is an isomorphism. So \(h^0(t,\mathcal I(1))=0\) and \(h^1(t,\mathcal I(1))=0\).

  • •

    \(t=0\): \(\rho_0\) sends \(z_i\mapsto g_i\) (\(i\le3\)) and \(z_4\mapsto0\), so \(\ker\rho_0=k\cdot z_4\) is \(1\)-dimensional and the image is \(4\)-dimensional, so the cokernel is \(1\)-dimensional. So \(h^0(0,\mathcal I(1))=1\) and \(h^1(0,\mathcal I(1))=1\).

This is the stated jump: \(h^0(t,\mathcal I(1))=h^1(t,\mathcal I(1))=0\) for \(t\ne0\) and \(=1\) for \(t=0\). (Consistent with Thm. 12.8, and with \(\chi\) constant: \(h^0-h^1=0\) for all \(t\).) ∎

Exercise 12.4

Problem

Let \(Y\) be an integral scheme of finite type over an algebraically closed field \(k\). Let \(f\colon X\to Y\) be a flat projective morphism whose fibres are all integral schemes. Let \(\mathcal L,\mathcal M\) be invertible sheaves on \(X\), and assume for each \(y\in Y\) that \(\mathcal L_y\cong\mathcal M_y\) on the fibre \(X_y\). Then show that there is an invertible sheaf \(\mathcal N\) on \(Y\) such that \(\mathcal L\cong\mathcal M\otimes f^*\mathcal N\). [Hint: Use the results of this section to show that \(f_*(\mathcal L\otimes\mathcal M^{-1})\) is locally free of rank \(1\) on \(Y\).]

Solution

(We use the hypothesis \(\mathcal L_y\cong\mathcal M_y\) only for the closed points \(y\).) Put \(\mathcal G=\mathcal L\otimes\mathcal M^{-1}\), an invertible sheaf on \(X\), flat over \(Y\) (locally \(\cong\mathcal O_X\), and \(X\) is flat over \(Y\)). By hypothesis \(\mathcal G_y\cong\mathcal O_{X_y}\) for all closed \(y\in Y\). Every fibre is integral, in particular nonempty.

Step 1: \(h^0(y,\mathcal G)=1\) for all \(y\in Y\). For \(y\) closed, \(X_y\) is an integral projective scheme over \(k(y)=k\), so \(H^0(X_y,\mathcal O_{X_y})\) is a finite-dimensional \(k\)-algebra which is a domain, hence a field finite over \(k\), hence \(=k\). So \(h^0(y,\mathcal G)=1\) for every closed point \(y\). By Thm. 12.8 the set \(\{y\mid h^0(y,\mathcal G)\ge2\}\) is closed and contains no closed point, hence is empty; the set \(\{y\mid h^0(y,\mathcal G)\ge1\}\) is closed and contains all closed points, hence equals \(Y\). So \(h^0(y,\mathcal G)=1\) for all \(y\in Y\).

Step 2: \(\mathcal N:=f_*\mathcal G\) is invertible. \(Y\) is integral and \(h^0(y,\mathcal G)\) is constant. By Cor. 12.9, \(f_*\mathcal G\) is locally free on \(Y\) and \(\varphi^0(y)\colon f_*\mathcal G\otimes k(y)\to H^0(X_y,\mathcal G_y)\) is an isomorphism for every \(y\). Hence \(\mathcal N\) is locally free of rank \(h^0(y,\mathcal G)=1\).

Step 3: \(f^*\mathcal N\cong\mathcal G\). Consider the natural map \(\alpha\colon f^*f_*\mathcal G\to\mathcal G\), a map of invertible sheaves on \(X\); it suffices to show it is surjective (a surjective map of invertible sheaves is an isomorphism). Its cokernel has closed support; if nonempty, it contains a closed point \(x\in X\) (finite type over \(k\)), lying over a closed point \(y=f(x)\). Restricted to the fibre \(X_y\), \(\alpha\) becomes the map \(\mathcal O_{X_y}\otimes_k(\mathcal N\otimes k(y))\to\mathcal G_y\) adjoint to \(\varphi^0(y)\), i.e. it sends a generator \(e\) of \(\mathcal N\otimes k(y)\) to the section \(\varphi^0(y)(e)\in H^0(X_y,\mathcal G_y)\), which is nonzero because \(\varphi^0(y)\) is an isomorphism. Now \(\mathcal G_y\cong\mathcal O_{X_y}\) and \(H^0(X_y,\mathcal O_{X_y})=k\), so this section is a nonzero constant times a generator, hence a unit: \(\alpha|_{X_y}\) is an isomorphism, in particular surjective at \(x\). By Nakayama \(\alpha\) is surjective at \(x\), a contradiction. So \(\alpha\) is surjective, hence an isomorphism, and \(\mathcal G\cong f^*\mathcal N\), i.e.

\[ \mathcal L\cong\mathcal M\otimes f^*\mathcal N . \]

Remark. Every step is local on \(Y\) (semicontinuity, Grauert's corollary, the formation of \(f_*\)), so the argument applies equally if \(f\) is only locally projective over \(Y\); this is used in Ex. 12.5. ∎

Exercise 12.5

Problem

Let \(Y\) be an integral scheme of finite type over an algebraically closed field \(k\). Let \(\mathcal E\) be a locally free sheaf on \(Y\), and let \(X=\mathbf P(\mathcal E)\)—see (II, Section 7). Then show that \(\operatorname{Pic}X\cong(\operatorname{Pic}Y)\times\Z\). This strengthens (II, Ex. 7.9).

Solution

Hypothesis. We assume \(\operatorname{rank}\mathcal E=r\ge2\), as in (II, Ex. 7.9): for \(r=1\), \(X=Y\) and the statement is false (it would give \(\operatorname{Pic}Y\cong\operatorname{Pic}Y\times\Z\) for, e.g., \(Y=\Spec k\)). Let \(\pi\colon X\to Y\) be the projection and \(\mathcal O_X(1)\) the tautological invertible sheaf (II, 7.11). Locally on \(Y\) (where \(\mathcal E\) is free), \(X\cong\mathbf P^{r-1}_V\), so \(\pi\) is flat, locally projective, with all closed fibres \(X_y\cong\mathbf P^{r-1}_k\) and \(\mathcal O_X(1)|_{X_y}=\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^{r-1}}(1)\); also \(\pi_*\mathcal O_X=\mathcal O_Y\).

Define \(\psi\colon\operatorname{Pic}Y\times\Z\to\operatorname{Pic}X\), \(\psi(\mathcal N,m)=\pi^*\mathcal N\otimes\mathcal O_X(m)\), clearly a homomorphism. We show it is bijective.

Injective. Suppose \(\pi^*\mathcal N\otimes\mathcal O_X(m)\cong\mathcal O_X\). Restrict to the fibre over a closed point \(y\): \(\pi^*\mathcal N\) is trivial on \(X_y\), so \(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^{r-1}}(m)\cong\mathcal O\), forcing \(m=0\) since \(r-1\ge1\) (II, 6.4). Then \(\pi^*\mathcal N\cong\mathcal O_X\); applying \(\pi_*\) and the projection formula, \(\mathcal N\cong\pi_*\pi^*\mathcal N\cong\mathcal N\otimes\pi_*\mathcal O_X\) and \(\pi_*\pi^*\mathcal N\cong\pi_*\mathcal O_X=\mathcal O_Y\). So \(\mathcal N\cong\mathcal O_Y\).

Surjective. Let \(\mathcal L\in\operatorname{Pic}X\). For a closed point \(y\in Y\), \(\mathcal L|_{X_y}\cong\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^{r-1}}(m_y)\) for a unique \(m_y\in\Z\) (II, 6.4).

Claim: \(m_y\) is independent of \(y\). For \(c\in\Z\) let \(A_c=\{y\in Y\mid h^0(y,\mathcal L(-c))\ge1\}\) and \(B_c=\{y\in Y\mid h^0(y,\mathcal L^{-1}(c))\ge1\}\). These are closed in \(Y\) by Thm. 12.8 (semicontinuity is local on \(Y\), and \(\mathcal L(-c)\), \(\mathcal L^{-1}(c)\) are invertible and flat over \(Y\)). For closed \(y\), \(h^0(y,\mathcal L(-c))=h^0(\mathbf P^{r-1},\mathcal O(m_y-c))\ge1\iff m_y\ge c\), and \(y\in B_c\iff m_y\le c\). Hence \(A_c\cup B_{c-1}\) is closed and contains all closed points, so equals \(Y\); as \(Y\) is irreducible, \(Y=A_c\) or \(Y=B_{c-1}\). Fix a closed \(y_0\) and take \(c=m_{y_0}\): \(y_0\notin B_{c-1}\), so \(Y=A_c\), whence \(m_y\ge m_{y_0}\) for all closed \(y\). Exchanging the roles of the points, \(m_{y_0}\ge m_y\). So \(m_y=m\) for all closed \(y\).

Now \(\mathcal L(-m)=\mathcal L\otimes\mathcal O_X(-m)\) restricts to \(\mathcal O\) on every closed fibre \(\mathbf P^{r-1}_k\), i.e. it agrees on closed fibres with \(\mathcal M=\mathcal O_X\). All fibres are integral, so Ex. 12.4 (applied with \(\mathcal L(-m)\) and \(\mathcal O_X\), which only needs the closed fibres and works locally on \(Y\)) gives \(\mathcal N\in\operatorname{Pic}Y\) with \(\mathcal L(-m)\cong\pi^*\mathcal N\), i.e. \(\mathcal L\cong\psi(\mathcal N,m)\).

Hence \(\operatorname{Pic}X\cong\operatorname{Pic}Y\times\Z\). ∎

Exercise 12.6

Problem

Let \(X\) be an integral projective scheme over an algebraically closed field \(k\), and assume that \(H^1(X,\mathcal O_X)=0\). Let \(T\) be a connected scheme of finite type over \(k\).

  1. (a)

    If \(\mathcal L\) is an invertible sheaf on \(X\times T\), show that the invertible sheaves \(\mathcal L_t\) on \(X_t=X\times\{t\}\) are isomorphic, for all closed points \(t\in T\).

  2. (b)

    Show that \(\operatorname{Pic}(X\times T)=\operatorname{Pic}X\times\operatorname{Pic}T\). (Do not assume that \(T\) is reduced!) Cf. (IV, Ex. 4.10) and (V, Ex. 1.6) for examples where \(\operatorname{Pic}(X\times T)\ne\operatorname{Pic}X\times\operatorname{Pic}T\). [Hint: Apply (12.11) with \(i=0,1\) for suitable invertible sheaves on \(X\times T\).]

(Starred in the book.)

Solution

Let \(p\colon X\times T\to T\) be the second projection: it is projective and flat (base change of \(X\to\Spec k\)), \(T\) is noetherian, and every invertible sheaf on \(X\times T\) is flat over \(T\). For a closed point \(t\) we have \(k(t)=k\) and \(X_t=X\). Since \(X\) is integral and projective over the algebraically closed field \(k\), \(H^0(X,\mathcal O_X)=k\) (a finite-dimensional domain over \(k\) is \(k\)). Also: a nonzero section of an invertible sheaf on an integral scheme is nonzero at the generic point.

Lemma. Let \(\mathcal G\) be an invertible sheaf on \(X\times T\) and \(t_1\in T\) a closed point with \(\mathcal G_{t_1}\cong\mathcal O_X\). Then there is an open neighborhood \(W\) of \(t_1\) such that \(p_*\mathcal G|_W\) is invertible, and \(\varphi^0(t)\colon p_*\mathcal G\otimes k(t)\to H^0(X_t,\mathcal G_t)\) is an isomorphism for all \(t\in W\); in particular \(h^0(t,\mathcal G)=1\) for all \(t\in W\).

Proof. \(H^1(X_{t_1},\mathcal G_{t_1})=H^1(X,\mathcal O_X)=0\), so \(\varphi^1(t_1)\) is surjective, hence (Cor. 12.11(a)) an isomorphism near \(t_1\), and \(R^1p_*\mathcal G\otimes k(t_1)=0\); by Nakayama (\(R^1p_*\mathcal G\) is coherent) \(R^1p_*\mathcal G=0\) in a neighborhood of \(t_1\), in particular it is locally free there. Now Cor. 12.11(b) with \(i=1\) (\(\varphi^1(t_1)\) surjective and \(R^1p_*\mathcal G\) locally free near \(t_1\)) shows that \(\varphi^0(t_1)\) is surjective, hence an isomorphism by 12.11(a) (for \(i=0\)), and the same holds for all \(t\) in a neighborhood \(W\) of \(t_1\). Also \(p_*\mathcal G\) is locally free near \(t_1\): since \(\varphi^0(t_1)\) is surjective, \(T^0\) is right exact at \(t_1\) (Prop. 12.10); it is always left exact (\(T^0(M)=H^0(X,\mathcal G\otimes M)\) and \(\mathcal G\) is flat), hence exact at \(t_1\), so \(T^0(A)=H^0(X,\mathcal G)\) is projective locally at \(t_1\) (Cor. 12.6, 12.7), and \(p_*\mathcal G=\widetilde{T^0(A)}\) is locally free near \(t_1\). Its rank is \(\dim_kH^0(X_{t_1},\mathcal G_{t_1})=\dim H^0(X,\mathcal O_X)=1\). Shrinking \(W\) gives the claim. ∎

(a) Fix a closed point \(t_0\) and put \(\mathcal M=\mathcal L_{t_0}\) (an invertible sheaf on \(X\)) and \(\mathcal G=\mathcal L\otimes p_1^*\mathcal M^{-1}\), so \(\mathcal G_t=\mathcal L_t\otimes\mathcal M^{-1}\) for closed \(t\) and \(\mathcal G_{t_0}\cong\mathcal O_X\). We must show \(\mathcal G_t\cong\mathcal O_X\) for all closed \(t\).

For an invertible sheaf \(\mathcal H\) on \(X\): \(\mathcal H\cong\mathcal O_X\) if and only if \(H^0(X,\mathcal H)\ne0\) and \(H^0(X,\mathcal H^{-1})\ne0\). (If \(s,s'\) are nonzero sections, then \(ss'\in H^0(\mathcal O_X)=k\) is nonzero at the generic point, hence a nonzero constant, so \(s\) is nowhere zero and trivializes \(\mathcal H\); the converse is clear.) Let

\[ Z=\{t\in T\mid h^0(t,\mathcal G)\ge1\text{ and }h^0(t,\mathcal G^{-1})\ge1\}, \]

a closed subset of \(T\) (Thm. 12.8). For closed \(t\): \(t\in Z\iff\mathcal G_t\cong\mathcal O_X\).

Let \(t_1\) be a closed point of \(Z\). The Lemma applied to \(\mathcal G\) and to \(\mathcal G^{-1}\) (both trivial at \(t_1\)) gives an open \(W_{t_1}\ni t_1\) with \(h^0(t,\mathcal G)=h^0(t,\mathcal G^{-1})=1\) on \(W_{t_1}\), so \(W_{t_1}\subseteq Z\). Let \(W=\bigcup W_{t_1}\) over all closed \(t_1\in Z\): it is open, \(W\subseteq Z\), and \(Z\) minus \(W\) is a closed subset of \(T\) containing no closed point, hence empty. So \(Z=W\) is open and closed, nonempty (\(t_0\in Z\)), and \(T\) is connected: \(Z=T\). Thus \(\mathcal L_t\cong\mathcal L_{t_0}\) for all closed \(t\).

(b) Let \(\psi\colon\operatorname{Pic}X\times\operatorname{Pic}T\to\operatorname{Pic}(X\times T)\), \(\psi(\mathcal M,\mathcal N)=p_1^*\mathcal M\otimes p_2^*\mathcal N\) (\(p_2=p\)), a homomorphism. We show it is bijective.

Injective. Let \(p_1^*\mathcal M\otimes p_2^*\mathcal N\cong\mathcal O_{X\times T}\). Restricting to \(X\times\{t_0\}=X\) (\(t_0\) a closed point; \(T\neq\emptyset\)) gives \(\mathcal M\otimes(\text{trivial sheaf})\cong\mathcal O_X\), so \(\mathcal M\cong\mathcal O_X\). Then \(p_2^*\mathcal N\cong\mathcal O_{X\times T}\). Applying \(p_{2*}\) and the projection formula, \(\mathcal N\otimes p_{2*}\mathcal O_{X\times T}\cong p_{2*}\mathcal O_{X\times T}\), and \(p_{2*}\mathcal O_{X\times T}=H^0(X,\mathcal O_X)\otimes_k\mathcal O_T=\mathcal O_T\) by flat base change (Prop. 9.3: over an affine open \(\Spec B\subseteq T\), \(H^0(X\times\Spec B,\mathcal O)=H^0(X,\mathcal O_X)\otimes_kB=B\)). So \(\mathcal N\cong\mathcal O_T\). (This part uses neither \(H^1(X,\mathcal O_X)=0\) nor any reducedness of \(T\).)

Surjective. Let \(\mathcal L\in\operatorname{Pic}(X\times T)\). By (a), \(\mathcal L_t\cong\mathcal L_{t_0}=:\mathcal M\) for all closed \(t\). Put \(\mathcal G=\mathcal L\otimes p_1^*\mathcal M^{-1}\); then \(\mathcal G_t\cong\mathcal O_X\) for every closed \(t\in T\). By the Lemma, every closed point \(t_1\) has an open neighborhood \(W_{t_1}\) on which \(p_*\mathcal G\) is invertible and \(\varphi^0(t)\) is an isomorphism. The union of the \(W_{t_1}\) is an open set containing all closed points, so equals \(T\). Therefore \(\mathcal N:=p_*\mathcal G\) is an invertible sheaf on \(T\) (no reducedness of \(T\) is used: the rank is read off from the fibre at closed points, and the Lemma gives local freeness directly from 12.11), and \(\varphi^0(t)\) is an isomorphism for every closed \(t\).

Consider \(\alpha\colon p^*\mathcal N=p^*p_*\mathcal G\to\mathcal G\), a map of invertible sheaves on \(X\times T\). Its cokernel has closed support in \(X\times T\) (finite type over \(k\)); if nonempty it contains a closed point \(x\), lying over a closed point \(t\) of \(T\). On the fibre \(X_t=X\), \(\alpha\) is \(\mathcal O_X\otimes_k(\mathcal N\otimes k(t))\to\mathcal G_t\cong\mathcal O_X\), sending a generator to \(\varphi^0(t)(\text{generator})\), a nonzero element of \(H^0(X,\mathcal G_t)=k\cdot(\text{generator of }\mathcal G_t)\), hence a unit multiple of a generator. So \(\alpha|_{X_t}\) is an isomorphism, and by Nakayama \(\alpha\) is surjective at \(x\), a contradiction. So \(\alpha\) is a surjection of invertible sheaves, hence an isomorphism: \(\mathcal G\cong p_2^*\mathcal N\), i.e.

\[ \mathcal L\cong p_1^*\mathcal M\otimes p_2^*\mathcal N . \]

Hence \(\psi\) is surjective, and \(\operatorname{Pic}(X\times T)=\operatorname{Pic}X\times\operatorname{Pic}T\). ∎
